    Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya" (p. 218, folio 200) — and then destroyed the proceeding by deference: he "ran to
    receive him and carry his shoes", saying that Jalalu'd-Din "had served his pir for seven years and
    therefore it was fitting for him to use the dust from Shaikh Jalalu'd-Din's feet as an eye-wash" (p. 218,
    folio 200).
  - **Sparing with praise, with one exception.** "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya, not well-known for his
    praise of other sufis, was a great admirer of Shaikh Ahmad" — the weaver Shaikh Ahmad Naharwani (p. 216,
    folio 198).
  - **A friendship with the Chishti leader at Multan.** Khwaja Qutbu'd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki, leaving Baghdad,
    "travelled through Khurasan to reach Multan. There he established a friendship with Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din
    Zakariyya" (p. 153, folio 135).

- **Shrine / Darbar, khanqah and economy**
  - **The economy of his khanqah, stated twice and consistently.** "The prosperity of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din
    Zakariyya's khanqah depended mainly on gifts from merchants and trade guilds, for even local governors
    were often short of funds. As many merchants traded outside India, to a large extent, they were
    independent of the government and could not be prevented from giving futuh" (p. 242, folio 222); and of
    the Suhrawardis generally, "They accepted state patronage, received huge gifts from merchants and artisan
    guilds, and treasured riches as future assets" (p. 236, folio 216).
  - **The end of that prosperity, dated by an event and not by a year.** "This marked the end of the
    prosperity of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya's khanqah in Multan. The Suhrawardi silsila, however, started
    flourishing in other areas, while Multan became a mere shadow of its former glory" (p. 235, folio 215) —
    **the referent of "This" is the execution of Shaikh Hud and the seizure of the khanqah property (p. 234,
    folio 214).**
  - **Hospitality rationed and charity fixed by him personally.** "The food in Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din
    Zakariyya's khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] was abundant, but was offered to only a select few. Often the Shaikh
    ordered his servants to pay fixed amounts in charity to those he considered deserving and only the sum he
    specified was paid" (p. 236, folio 216).
  - **His khanqah described at second hand, and the one detail is about his slave girls.** Nizamu'd-Din, at
    twelve, "first heard of Baba Farid through a qawwal (a musician) who had been to both Multan and Ajodhan.
    The gawwal [OCR?] [qawwal] first related to Nizamu'd-Din the intensely religious atmosphere in the
    khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya', where even his slave girls performed constant
    zikr" (p. 173, folio 155). **The book does not there say where that khanqah was; see Doubts.**
  - **His khanqah took in a wandering qalandar party and kept one of them.** The qalandars "reached Multan
    where they stayed with Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din"; when the party dispersed in a storm "'Iraqi returned to the
    Shaikh's khangah [OCR?] [khanqah]" and "began living in a cell which the Shaikh had assigned him, as a
    spiritual retreat" (p. 224, folio 204).
  - **The estate he left, and the number of heirs.** "Shaikh Sadru'd-Din had six brothers, but he alone
    inherited from his father property worth 700,000 tankas. According to Jamali, he immediately gave the
    entire amount to the poor, for like his father he believed he was unable to spend it judiciously"
    (p. 220, folio 202). **A rival account stands on the same page — see Doubts.** Rizvi's conclusion: "it
    seems clear that wealth accumulated by Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din did not long remain with his sons" (p. 220,
    folio 202).
  - **His descendants as a political grievance after his time** — Shaikh Hud's alleged war-cry was "the
    ill-treatment of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya's descendants by the Sultan" (p. 234, folio 214).

- **Practices & Events**
  - **He barred prostration before himself and required prayers first — directly relevant to what is done at
    his shrine.** "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya did not encourage his disciples to perform sajda. They
    greeted him with: as-salam alaikum (Peace be with you). He also expected his disciples to finish their
    obligatory religious duties first and to greet him afterwards" (p. 240, folio 220).
  - **The negative on faith-cures, and it is the author's inference.** "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya was
    concerned only with the élite and it is unlikely that people visited his khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] for
    faith cures. By contrast, the Chishti jama'at-khanas were filled with people wanting amulets" (p. 240,
    folio 220). **Note the hedge "it is unlikely"; Rizvi cites no source.**
  - **His food doctrine, quoted from the Qurʾan.** "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya was a lively host and
    followed literally the Qur'anic injunction: 'eat of what is pure and act righteously.'" (p. 237,
    folio 217).
  - **He refused qalandars — and broke the rule twice.** "Qalandars were intolerable to Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din"
    (p. 236, folio 216); "As also mentioned previously, Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din disliked qalandars intensely and
    certainly never encouraged their unwelcome visits." (p. 328, folio 306) — yet "Among the Suhrawardiyyas,
    Shaikh Fakhru'd-Din 'Iraqi was a qalandar and as described earlier, even after his initiation by Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya he continued to live as a wandering dervish" (p. 328, folio 306), and he adopted
    Lal Shahbaz as his disciple and named him (p. 328, folio 306). **The book flags the two as exceptions and
    still states the dislike absolutely — see Doubts.**
  - **His position on poetry and music, and the exception he made.** "Baha'u'd-Din was opposed to poetry and
    music, but he tolerated its indulgence by Fakhru'd-Din Ibrahim" (p. 224, folio 204). His other disciples
    "were shocked to learn that this ghazal was being sung in local taverns to the accompaniment of the harp
    and zither. They complained to the Shaikh" (p. 224, folio 204) — and he ruled for 'Iraqi.
  - **He insisted on single initiation, and the rule broke after him.** "Although Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din
    Zakariyya had insisted during his lifetime that sufis join one order only, from the fourteenth century
    sufis were often initiated into both the Chishtiyya and the Suhrawardiyya" (p. 292, folio 272).
  - **Multan as his seat and as the qalandars' route into India.** "Mongol domination of Central Asia and Iran
    facilitated the movement of the Qalandariyyas and the Jawaliqi groups from Turkey and Egypt to India.
    Passing through Multan, they often came into conflict with Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya" (p. 325,
    folio 303), "but such experiences were counteracted by the generosity of Baba Farid" (p. 325, folio 303).

- **The dispute about whether a sufi may hold wealth — the fullest treatment of shrine wealth in the book**
  - "In order to ascertain the attitude of the Suhrawardi order towards wealth, Shaikh Hamidu’d-Din wrote a
    number of letters to Shaikh Baha’u'd-Din Zakariyya… Although the latter replied, Shaikh Hamidu'd-Din was
    not completely satisfied with the answers received" (p. 146, folio 128). The two met at Delhi "during the
    mahzar4 to resolve the charges against Shaikh Jalalu’d-Din Tabrizi" (p. 146, folio 128), *mahzar* glossed
    as "An assembly of ‘ulama’ and sufis to decide an important dispute; literally the word means either a
    royal court or any document attested by witnesses" (p. 146, folio 128, footnote 4). **No date for the
    mahzar — (not stated).**
  - **His defence, in his own reported words.** "Although wealth is a serpent, someone who has learnt the
    incantation to overcome the venom, need not have any fear from the serpent"; and, pressed, "Your
    achievement as a dervish is not endowed by such beauty and elegance as to be immune from the evil eye, but
    our achievements as dervishes are endowed with such great beauty as to gain no harm even if the whole of
    the world’s blackness were applied to its face" (pp. 146–147, folios 128–129). **The book twice records
    that he lost the exchange:** "Shaikh Baha’u’d-Din was speechless" and "Shaikh Baha’u’d-Din Zakariyya
    Suhrawardi was unable to reply" (p. 147, folio 129), sourced "Sururu's-Sudur, pp. 86-7".
  - **A son carried the quarrel to Ajmer.** "Controversy over the acquisition of material wealth by members of
    the Suhrawardi order annoyed one of Shaikh Baha’u’d-Din’s sons, who visited Ajmer. Discovering that Shaikh
    Hamidu’d-Din did not attend Friday congregational prayers, he used this as a pretext to attack the Shaikh
    and gained some support from a group of ‘ulama’ who demanded that the Shaikh fulfil his religious duties"
    (p. 147, folio 129). **The son is not named — (not stated).**

- **Succession and disciples**
  - **Hereditary succession, arranged by himself.** "He appointed his own son, Sadru'd-Din, known as 'Arif
    (Gnostic), his khalifa" (p. 220, folio 202).
  - **ʿIraqi's deputisation, in four instruments and in order.** He "declared 'Iraqi's training to be
    complete. He ordered him to give up retirement and meditation, presented him with his own khirga [OCR?]
    [khirqa] to wear and later gave him his daughter in marriage. Before he died, Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din
    appointed him his khalifa" (pp. 224–225, folios 204–205). **Robe, marriage into the family, and the
    khalifa appointment — no document of any kind is mentioned (not stated).** And the succession broke at
    once: "after his death, the jealousy of other disciples forced 'Iraqi to leave Multan about 1263. It would
    appear he had been there since about 1239" (p. 225, folio 205) — **a twenty-four-year residence given as
    two approximations.**
  - **Rizvi names the poet as the chief reason for his scholarly fame.** "The main reason for Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya's fame in the scholarly world, however, was due to the achievements of his
    disciple, Shaikh Fakhru'd-Din Ibrahim, popularly known as 'Iraqi" (p. 224, folio 204). A devotional verse
    of ʿIraqi's names him as the guide of the world: "'If thou shouldst ask of the world 'Who is the guide of
    men?' / Thou wilt hear from heaven no other answer than 'Zakariyya'.'" (p. 225, folio 205); and in the
    Kanzu'r-Rumuz "Husaini praised the Shaikhs, Shihabu'd-Din Suhrawardi, Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya and
    Sadru'd-Din" (p. 226, folio 206).
  - **A named disciple who is himself an archive row.** "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya had many famous
    disciples, one was Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din Bukhari, also called Jalal Surkh (Red)" (p. 223, folio 203) — see
    `shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari` below. He is also named in one clause as the
    pir behind the Uch line: "a disciple of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya" (p. 299, folio 277).
  - **His illiterate disciple, and the boast attached to him.** "One of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din's disciples, Hasan
    Afghan, was illiterate. The Shaikh was proud of Hasan Afghan and used to say that if God asked him what he
    had brought from the world, he would present Hasan as a gift" (p. 223, folio 203).
  - **Two authorities attach Amir Husain to him and Rizvi refutes both on chronology.** "Jamali describes him
    in connection with the disciples of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya"; "Modern scholars also describe him as
    Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din's disciple" (p. 226, folio 206); Husain "must have been born about 671/1272-73, while
    Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya died in December 1262, ten years earlier" (p. 226, folio 206).

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **A prenatal prophecy about his own grandson, and an endorsement against the child's father.** He
    "prophesied his prominence while he was still in his mother's womb" (p. 230, folio 210); and "at the age of
    four, Ruknu'd-Din donned Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din's turban, and although his father objected strongly, Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din approved of the child's action" (p. 231, folio 211). **His turban then becomes succession
    regalia two generations on: "After Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din succeeded his father, he wore the same turban and
    Shaikh Sadru'd-Din's khirga [OCR?] [khirqa]" (p. 231, folio 211).**
  - **A frontier of spiritual authority shared with Baba Farid** — beyond a certain water tank "began the area
    of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din whose prayers he should attain" (p. 211, folio 193).
  - **Guru Nanak's reported discussion with him at Multan, which the book reads as a conversation with the
    dead.** "At Ajodhan and Multan, Guru Nanak was reported to have had discussions with Baba Farid and Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya… The suggestion is far-fetched although Guru Nanak did undoubtedly enjoy the
    company of sufis in these towns" (p. 413, folio 389).

- **Significance**
  - **The book's Multan is his Multan**: the Suhrawardi seat, the qalandars' obstacle, the merchant-funded
    khanqah, and the point from which the order's Indian history is traced (p. 220, folio 202). **It gives the
    shrine nothing: no tomb, no fabric, no ʿurs, no custodian, no endowment — all (not stated).**

### data-darbar — Data Darbar

**38 bullets across chunks 002, 003, 004, 006, 007, 008, 011, 020, 026 and 027, and they fall into two
sharply different kinds.** For most of the book Hujwiri is a *text*, cited at second hand through
Nicholson's translation; in chunk 007 (pp. 130–131, folios 112–113) he becomes a *person*, with a
birthplace, a teacher, a journey, a city that disliked him, a tomb and three irreconcilable death
dates. The book's names for him: "‘Ali bin ‘Usman al-Jullabi al-Hujwiri" (p. 41, folio 25),
"Abu'l-Hasan ʿAli bin ʿUsman bin ʿAli al-Ghaznawi al-Jullabi al-Hujwiri" (p. 80, folio 64), "Shaikh
‘Ali Hujwiri" (p. 139, folio 121), "Shaikh Hujwiri" (p. 130, folio 112) and, most often, plain
"Hujwiri".

- **Saint**
  - **Birth place and year, given approximately.** "As his name implies, Hujwiri was born in the Ghazna
    suburb, about 1009" (p. 130, folio 112). **The suburb is not named beyond what the nisba implies —
    (not stated).**
  - **His teachers, named from his own book.** "He studied under several sufi masters, some of whom he
    names in his Kashf al-Mahjub were Abu'l-Qasim Gurgani, Khwaja Muzaffar, Abu'l-‘Abbas Ahmad bin
    Muhammad al-Ashqani. However, Khattali of Syria was his main teacher" (p. 130, folio 112) — the
    teacher being "Abu'l-Fazl Muhammad bin al-Hasan Khattali, a disciple of Husri (d. 371/981-82) of the
    school of Junaid" (p. 130, folio 112). The index carries the teacher too: "Abu'l-Fazl Muhammad b. …
    al-Hasan … Khattali, 111" (p. 458, folio 434, the entry scrambled by the OCR's column reading).
  - **Sent to Lahore against his own objection.** His pir "asked a young disciple … to follow Husain
    Zinjani" to the Panjab; "Hujwiri objected on the grounds that already an eminent sufi was there. His
    pir, however, ordered him to obey" (p. 130, folio 112).
  - **The arrival, dated by inference, and the death that met it.** "Hujwiri reached Lahore after dark.
    In the morning Shaikh Husain Zinjani's coffin was carried out for burial—he had died during the
    night" (p. 130, folio 112); "It would seem that Hujwiri probably reached Lahore in c. 1035, while
    Sultan Mas‘ud I (1031-41) reigned in Ghazna" (p. 130, folio 112). **Rizvi marks the date as
    inference ("It would seem … probably").**
  - **A trans-regional life after settling, given in numbers.** "Even after settling in Lahore, Hujwiri
    kept in contact with sufis in Transoxiana, Khurasan and Syria. He made long trips at least twice
    during his lifetime, each for more than two years. When Khattali died in 1065, Hujwiri was by his
    side. After 1067, Hujwiri returned to Lahore, remaining there until his death" (p. 130, folio 112).
    **So the "settling" of c. 1035 is interrupted by at least one long absence; see Doubts.**
  - **Three incompatible death dates in one paragraph, one of them read off the tomb, and the archive
    row prints a fourth position as settled.** "According to the tablet on his tomb, the saint died in
    465/1072-73. Nicholson believed that Hujwiri died sometime between 465 and 469/1076—77. However, the
    Kashf al-Mahjub recorded Khwaja ‘Abdullah Ansari, who died in 1089, as amongst the contemporary
    saints. It would seem, therefore, that Hujwiri died some time after that date" (p. 130, folio 112).
    **Rizvi's own conclusion is the latest of the three: after 1089** — which is also what the index
    prints, "Hujwiri Abu'l Hasan ‘Ali b. “Usman (d. after 1089), 29, 34, 43, 45, 52. 61, 64, 90, 92,
    97-99, 101, 111-13, 121, 302" (p. 468, folio 444). **The archive row carries 1072, i.e. the tomb
    tablet, the one reading Rizvi argues against.** See Doubts.
  - **The title, its meaning, and that it is posthumous.** "Later Muslims posthumously conferred on
    Shaikh Hujwiri the title, Data Ganj Bakhsh, ‘Distributor of (Unlimited) Treasure’" (p. 130,
    folio 112). **The index carries the archive's own name only as a bare cross-reference with no
    numbers of its own: "Data Ganj Bakhsh, see Hujwiri" (p. 464, folio 440).**

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The tomb as a standing, visitable site, and as a place of ascetic practice.** "His tomb has always
    been greatly venerated by sufis and Muslims alike. Among early mystics who undertook hard ascetic
    exercises in Lahore at the Shaikh's tomb was Khwaja Mui‘nu'd-Din [OCR?] [Muʿinuʾd-Din] Chishti, the
    founder of the leading Indian order, the Chishtiyya" (p. 130, folio 112). **No location within
    Lahore, no builder, no date and no description of the tomb are given — all (not stated). This is the
    only sentence in the book that links the region's two most-visited shrines, and it is a claim about
    ascetic practice at a grave.**
  - **The two Lahore tombs together.** Khwaja Muʿinuʾd-Din "reached Lahore and stayed near the tombs of
    Shaikh Husain Zanjani and Shaikh ‘Ali Hujwiri" (p. 139, folio 121). **Four positions on whether he
    arrived at a tomb or at a living man are printed on that one page — see Doubts and the
    `shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib` subsection.** Rizvi's own ruling: "Fawaidu'l Fu‘ad's
    statement appears to be correct and it is likely that the Khwaja stayed near the two tombs" (p. 139,
    folio 121, footnote 3).
  - **"Data Darbar" as such, and any tomb, ʿurs or custodian at Lahore, is not an index headword** —
    (not stated) (p. 468, folio 444; p. 464, folio 440).

- **Lahore, and the hostility of his own city**
  - **He was opposed there, and said so.** "The Kashf al-Mahjub suggests that in the eleventh century a
    number of mystics and scholars who had settled in Lahore were strongly hostile to the views of its
    author." An unnamed "expert in Qur'anic commentaries, sharply disagreed with his interpretation of
    fana' and baqa.'"; "Some Lahore sufis believed that gnosis emanated from inspiration; Hujwiri
    disparagingly called this view ‘Brahmanical.’"; and "These developments so distressed Hujwiri that he
    considered himself a ‘captive among uncongenial folk’ in Lahore." (p. 131, folio 113), footnoted
    "Nicholson, p. 91. The original does not indicate that he was actually imprisoned, see Nicholson's
    preface p. X." **Rizvi explicitly cancels a literal reading of "captive".**
  - **The one Lahore imprint in the bibliography is his book, not his shrine.** "Hujwiri,
    Kashfu'l-Mahjub, Lahore, 1923, English tr. R.A. Nicholson, London, 1936." (p. 442, folio 418).

- **The Kashf al-Mahjub, and what Rizvi uses it for**
  - **Rizvi's assessment, which is the warrant for citing him throughout.** "Hujwiri's Kashf al-Mahjub is
    also the first known manual of sufism written in Persian. Composed towards the end of his life, the
    work draws upon the vast source material available in Arabic and is a most authoritative exposition
    of sufism according to Junaid's school"; "Hujwiri however, is not merely a compiler. Firstly, he
    explains the doctrines of various schools, then he relates and assesses them. His comments form the
    most authoritative classification of sufi thoughts and practices" (p. 131, folio 113). Also "Another
    prolific sufi author was Abu'l-Hasan ʿAli bin ʿUsman bin ʿAli al-Ghaznawi al-Jullabi al-Hujwiri. Of
    his many works only his greatest, the Kashf al-Mahjub, has survived" (p. 80, folio 64), in the
    section headed "Sufi Literature" (p. 78, folio 62), between Qushairi and Ghazali. **No date of
    composition, no patron, no place of writing — (not stated).** The index gives the book its own
    headword: "Kashf al-Mahjub, 28-29, 37, 47, 64, 90, 111-13, 171" (p. 471, folio 447).
  - **Eight works listed from the Kashf al-Mahjub itself, in a numbered list whose numerals 4–8 are
    destroyed.** "1. Minhaj al-Din, a work on sufi practices, containing a detailed account of the Ahl-i
    Suffa and a full biography of Husain bin Mansur al-Hallaj. 2. Asrar al-khiraq wa'l-ma‘unat, a work on
    the patched garments worn by sufis. 3. The Kitab-i fana' wa baqa' written, the author relates, ‘in
    the vanity and rashness of youth.’"; then, un-numbered in the OCR, "A work of which no title is
    given, in which the sayings of al-Hallaj are explained", "The Kitab al-Bayan li-Ahl al-‘Iyan, a work
    on the union with God", "The Bahr al-Qulub", "Al-Ri‘ayat li-Huqug [OCR?] [li-Huquq] Allah on Divine
    Unity", "A work on faith of which the title is not specified" (p. 131, folio 113). **The count of
    eight is the Pass 1 worker's reading of a damaged list — see Doubts.**
  - **The four Righteous Caliphs as sufi exemplars, all from his book** (p. 41, folio 25): Abu Bakr as "a
    paragon of voluntary poverty to the sufis" who "taught them to renounce all their material goods for
    Islam" (footnote 1, to the Kashf al-Mahjub, pp. 70-1); ‘Umar's "patched gown or muraqqa’ [OCR?]
    [muraqqaʿ]" as "a symbol of his self-denial", with the doctrine that "spiritualists outwardly were a
    part of mankind, but inwardly their hearts clung to God" (footnote 2, "ibid. p. 72"); ‘Usman's life
    as "the best of all possible examples in resignation during a crisis" (footnote 3, "ibid, pp. 73-4");
    and ‘Ali as "their Shaikh (leader or teacher) in both the theory and practice of sufism", a model "in
    respect to the truths of outward expressions and the subtleties of inward meanings, the stripping of
    one's self of all property either of this world or of the next, and consideration of divine
    providence" (footnote 4, "ibid, p. 74").
  - **The Uways al-Qarani material** — the saying that safety lay in solitude because "the heart of the
    solitary one was free from thoughts of others" carries footnote 5, "Sibid" [OCR?] [ibid] (p. 41,
    folio 25). **The prayer of al-Baqir** — "O my God and my Lord, night has come, and the power of
    monarchs has ceased" — is footnoted to the Kashf al-Mahjub, p. 78 (pp. 42–43, folios 26–27).
  - **Two episodes recorded in his own voice.** "The following conversation was recorded in the Kashf
    al-Mahjub between a Bedouin and Hasan on the idea of sabr (patience)" (p. 44, folio 28) — ending
    "This is the mark of true sincerity" and "Association with the wicked produces suspicion of the
    good"; and "The following passage from Hujwiri's Kashf al-Mahjub, relates the significance of
    sincerity and devotion in prayers, rather than language and form" (p. 45, folio 29) — the
    Hasan-of-Basra-and-Habib dream, quoted under Legends below.
  - **He is the authority on a Balkh Sufi's lost books** — "According to Hujwiri, Shaqiq wrote a number
    of books on Sufism but none are in existence" (p. 51, folio 35).
  - **He summarises the malāmatī path, with a verse.** Of Hamdun al-Qassar's doctrine of blame: "Hujwiri
    sums it up like this: 'In true love there is nothing sweeter than blame, because blame of the Beloved
    makes no impression on the lover's heart: he heeds not what strangers say, for his heart is ever
    faithful to the object of his love.' / ''Tis sweet to be reviled for passion's sake.'" (p. 59,
    folio 43), footnoted "Nicholson, pp. 67, 125-26; NU, p. 60" — **the siglum `NU` is nowhere expanded
    in the book; see Doubts.** Restated later: "As Hujwiri explained at great length, malamat (blame) was
    the distinctive practice of the Qassaris" (p. 324, folio 302), footnoted "Nicholson, pp. 183-84, See
    Chapter One. pp. 41-42, supra." **This is the book's doctrinal background for the archive's only
    Malamati row, `madho-lal-hussain`, and the book makes no connection between the two — see Doubts.**
  - **He glosses a saying of Shah ibn Shujaʿ of Kirman in his own voice.** "Hujwiri records an interesting
    sentence Abu'l Fawaris is reported to have expressed, and makes the following comment: '"The eminent
    have eminence until they see it, and the saints have saintship until they see it," that is, whoever
    regards his eminence loses its reality, and whoever regards his saintship loses its reality.'"
    (p. 61, folio 45).
  - **He settles fanaʾ and baqaʾ against two named errors.** "Hujwiri comments: 'In annihilation there is
    no love or hate, and in subsistence there is no consciousness of union or separation. Some wrongly
    imagine that annihilation signifies loss of essence and destruction of personality, and that
    subsistence indicates the subsistence of God in Man; both these notions are absurd.'" (p. 68,
    folio 52; footnoted "Nicholson, p. 243").
  - **He is the source for the eternity of the spirit and for the Hululis.** "Discussing the theory of the
    eternity of the spirit, Hujwiri related that Sunnis also believed that the spirit was non-eternal
    (muhdas), that it existed prior to the formation of the body, but that it could not be transferred
    from one body to another" (p. 77, folio 61); "Hujwiri reminds us that Hululis and other followers of
    metempsychosis who believed that the spirit was an eternal characteristic of God, stressed that he
    could never become an attribute of His creatures" (p. 77, folio 61); his own words, "'The spirit is
    created and is under God's command. Anyone who holds another belief is in flagrant error and cannot
    distinguish what is non-eternal from what is eternal. No saint, if his saintship be sound, can
    possibly be ignorant of the attributes of God.'" (p. 77, folio 61; "Nicholson, pp. 263-64"). **Note
    the internal tangle in the middle sentence — see Doubts.** The back-reference in Chapter Six places
    him among the *evidence for Hindu–sufi interaction*: "However Hujwiri's disappointment with certain
    sufis of his own time, whom he called Hululis, has already been mentioned, as has his assessment of
    current Brahmanical influence on sufi thought in Islam" (p. 345, folio 323), footnoted "Nicholson,
    pp. 236, 243, 260-66, 271".
  - **He is the arbiter of which sufi sects are legitimate.** "According to Hujwiri the sufi sects named
    after the above Masters were approved ones. He gives a detailed description of each in the Kashf
    al-Mahjub; Nicholson, pp. 176-260" (p. 77, folio 61, footnote 1); the Masters and cities in the
    sentence the note hangs on are "al-Hakim in Tirmiz, Abu'l-ʿAbbas Sayyar in Marw, Qassar in Nishapur,
    Bayazid in Bastam, Khafif in Shiraz, Sahl in Tustar, and around Kharraz, Muhasibi, Junaid and Nuri in
    Baghdad" (p. 77, folio 61).
  - **Rizvi ranks him above Suhrawardi on samaʿ, outright.** "However, the Shaikh was unable to excel
    Hujwiri's unique means of defending sama', who says:" followed by the passage on *pay-bazi* and
    *iztirab* (pp. 110–111, folios 92–93). His book is also the benchmark for Suhrawardi's ethics:
    Suhrawardi's section "was a marked improvement over related chapters in Hujwiri's Kashf al-Mahjub;
    Suhrawardi could make an outstanding contribution on this very important subject for by the twelfth
    century khanqah life was at its highest peak" (p. 109, folio 91); and he is the source of the
    two-fold division the whole hospitality code rests on — "Like Hujwiri, Suhrawardi divided the people
    of khanqahs into two: residents and travellers" (p. 109, folio 91).
  - **The khirqa's prophetic warrant and its allegorical reading.** "According to Hujwiri, the wearing of
    the muragqa [OCR?] [muraqqa] or khirqa was practised by Muhammad and his companions" (p. 120,
    folio 102); "Hujwiri gives the following allegorical interpretation of the cloak's significance: ‘Its
    collar is annihilation of intercourse (with men), its two sleeves are observance (hifz) and
    continence (‘ismat), its two gussets are poverty and purity, its belt is persistence in
    contemplation, its hem (Kursi) is tranquillity in (God's) presence, and its fringe is settlement in
    the abode of union.’" (p. 120, folio 102).
  - **The novice's three-year probation — the one initiation rule in Chapter One with a named source and
    a number.** "According to Hujwiri, sufi Shaikhs prescribed a probationary period to novices of three
    years before instructing them in the real secrets of the Path (Tariga [OCR?] [Tariqa]). The first
    year of sufi apprenticeship was devoted to serving the people; the second, to the service of God and
    the third to guarding the heart" (p. 117, folio 99), sourced only as "Nicholson, p. 54".
  - **His book is a model of literary style for Amir Khusraw.** "1. The style of sufis and saints, which
    is of two kinds: (a) Of men of "gravity and stations," like that to be met with in Kashfu'l-Mahjub,
    Suluku'l-Musafirin, and other similar works" (p. 189, folio 171). **The person is not named there at
    all — the mapping is on the title; see Doubts.**
  - **The Kashf al-Mahjub is twice quoted without its author being named.** "This following quotation from
    the Kashf al-Mahjub is from amongst Ahmad's more important sayings" (p. 53, folio 37), introducing
    Ahmad ibn Khazruya's saying that "the way to God is like the blazing sun; do thou seek thyself, for
    when thou hast found thyself thou art come to thy journey's end, inasmuch as God is too manifest to
    admit of His being sought"; and "In this passage from the Kashfu al-Mahjub he says" (p. 62, folio 46),
    introducing Sahl al-Tustari on Law and Truth (p. 63, folio 47). **The title is spelled two ways,
    "Kashf al-Mahjub" (p. 53) and "Kashfu al-Mahjub" (p. 62).**

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The hierarchy and cosmic function of the saints, with Hujwiri's claim of direct sight.** "Hujwiri
    saw in sufi saints a vehicle by which 'the Truth and the proof of Muhammad's veracity' could be
    continued. Drawing upon Tirmizi's thesis of sainthood, he outlined the hierarchy of saints" (p. 115,
    folio 97): "(God) has made the Saints the governors of the universe"; "Through the blessing of their
    advent the rain falls from heaven, and through the purity of their lives the plants spring up from
    the earth, and through their spiritual influence the Muslims gain victories over the unbelievers";
    "Among them there are four thousand who are concealed and do not know one another"; and the graded
    officers — "three hundred, called Akhyar, and forty, called Abdal, and seven, called Abrar, and four,
    called Awtad, and three, called Nugaba [OCR?] [Nuqaba], and one, called Qutb or Ghaws. All these know
    one another and cannot act save by mutual consent." — closing with "I myself—God be praised!—have had
    ocular experience (khabar-i 'iyan) of this matter." (pp. 115–116, folios 97–98). **On the bare name
    "Tirmizi" see Doubts.**
  - **Hasan of Basra corrected by God in a dream, from his book.** "The same night, Hasan dreamed that he
    saw God and said to Him: ‘O Lord, wherein does Thy good pleasure consist?' and that God answered: ‘O
    Hasan, you found My good pleasure, but did not know its value: if ... you had said your prayers after
    Habib, and if the rightness of his intention had restrained you from taking offence at his
    pronunciation, I should have been well pleased with you'" (p. 45, folio 29).

- **Significance**
  - **Rizvi treats him as the authority who classifies Sufism and as a saint whose grave drew the founder
    of the Chishtiyya** (pp. 130–131, folios 112–113). **Everything else the archive would want — the
    shrine's fabric, its ʿurs, its custodians, its income — is (not stated) in 491 pages**, and every
    quotation from him in this book is at second hand, through Nicholson's English.

### shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam — Shrine of Shah Rukn-e-Alam

**28 bullets across chunks 013, 014, 018 and 027.** The book's names: **"Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din Abu'l Fath"**
(p. 230, folio 210) — the *kunya* that matches the archive row's own parenthetical "Rukn-ud-Din Abul
Fath" — "Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din" throughout, and once "Shaikhu'l-Islam Ruknu'l Haqq Wa'd-Din" (p. 234,
folio 214). **The book never prints "Rukn-e-Alam" anywhere.** Four other men in this book are called
Ruknuʾd-Din and the bare short form must never be mapped — see Doubts.

- **Saint**
  - **Descent, three generations of it.** "The successor of Shaikh Sadru'd-Din was his son, Shaikh
    Ruknu'd-Din Abu'l Fath" (p. 230, folio 210); son of Shaikh Sadruʾd-Din ʿArif, grandson of Shaikh
    Bahaʾuʾd-Din Zakariyya, and "his grandson" in so many words (p. 236, folio 216); "The latter's mother
    was a most pious lady" (p. 230, folio 210), **unnamed (not stated)**.
  - **Death** — "Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din died in 735/1334-35" (p. 234, folio 214), **against the archive row's
    `figure_died` 1335; the book's own conversion of 735 AH is "1334-35", so the disagreement is
    apparent, not real, and neither has been altered.** The index carries "Ruknu'd-Din Multani, Shaikh
    (d. 1334—35), 163, 183, 206, 210-6 [OCR?] [210-16?], 277" and, separately and without linking them,
    "Ruknu'd-Din Abu'l-Fath, 282" (p. 482, folio 458). **This row is mapped on both headwords and neither
    is merged into the other — see Doubts.**
  - **Multan as his seat, and his death as the hinge of the order's decline there.** The Sultan "Marching
    towards Multan he was met by Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din" (p. 233, folio 213); "As discussed in the previous
    chapter, after the death of Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din the order declined in Multan only to be rejuvenated in
    Uch" (p. 299, folio 277).
  - **He is the initiating pir of Makhdum-i Jahaniyan** — "Later he obtained initiation into the
    Suhrawardiyya order from Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din" (p. 299, folio 277). **Since the book dates Makhdum-i
    Jahaniyan's birth to 707/1308, this puts Ruknuʾd-Din alive well into the 1320s or later, which is
    consistent with the row's 1335; but the book prints no date for him there and the inference is not
    the book's.**
  - **His writings are recorded as lost.** "Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Haqq mentions a number of works containing the
    teachings of Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din, including his Malfuzat; none of these however still exist" (p. 233,
    folio 213).
  - **He is physically infirm and the book says so.** "Although Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din had a serious problem
    with his leg, he ordered his men to lift him from his palanquin so he could show his respect for
    Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din, but the latter prevented him from doing so" (p. 231, folio 211).
  - **His brother is named two incompatible ways** — "Shaikh Maulana 'IImu'd-Din [OCR?] ['Ilmu'd-Din]" in
    the text against "the Siyvaru'l-Auliya' [OCR?] [Siyaru'l-Auliya'] gives the name of Shaikh
    Ruknu'd-Din's brother as Maulana '*Imadu'l-Millat wa'd-Din Isma'il. SA. p. 137. *Imadu'd-Din seems to
    be his title" (p. 231, folio 211, footnote 4) — and the brother is killed as the Sultan's double:
    "In a ruse to confuse the enemy the Sultan placed Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din's brother, Shaikh 'Imadu'd-Din,
    who closely resembled himself, under the royal canopy. 'Imadu'd-Din was killed" (p. 233, folio 213).
  - **Many biographers make him, not his father, the pir of Amir Husain.** "As he was about fourteen years
    old when Shaikh Sadru'd-Din died, many biographers believe he was in fact the disciple of Shaikh
    Ruknu'd-Din, the son of Shaikh Sadru'd-Din" (p. 226, folio 206).

- **Shrine / Darbar, khanqah and economy — the densest economy-of-religion material in the book**
  - **The largest endowment recorded anywhere in this book: one hundred villages to a khanqah.** "The
    Sultan awarded Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din one hundred villages for the upkeep of his khangah [OCR?]
    [khanqah]" (p. 233, folio 213). **No date, no names of villages, no deed — (not stated).**
  - **The sums a Sultan paid him per visit, and what he did with them.** "In the reign of Sultan
    'Ala'u'd-Din Khalji, Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din visited Delhi twice. The Sultan himself went to welcome him
    and rode back with him to the city. During each visit he paid 200,000 tankas at the Shaikh's arrival
    and 500,000 tankas at his departure. The Shaikh always distributed the entire amount among Delhi's
    population and naturally his visits were a source of great jubilation to the people" (p. 231,
    folio 211). **700,000 tankas per visit, twice, redistributed in full — but the book's own count of his
    visits does not add up; see Doubts before any total is computed.**
  - **His khanqah as an international institution.** "Following the tradition set by his father and
    grandfather, the Shaikh's khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] was a busy rendezvous for distinguished visitors
    from many countries west of-the Indus" (p. 233, folio 213). **"his father" is not named in that chunk
    — (not stated).**
  - **Fame carried by merchants as far as Alexandria, and a dated visit by Ibn Battuta.** "Shaikh
    Ruknu'd-Din's fame reached as far as Alexandria, being spread by merchants who visited Multan, and Ibn
    Battuta was recommended to see him. In 1333, after reaching Multan Ibn Battuta stayed with one of the
    Shaikh's disciples, and it is recorded that he had an interesting conversation with Shaikh
    Ruknu'd-Din, which exter.d2d [OCR?] [extended] even to political matters" (p. 233, folio 213).
  - **He refuses futuh and passes it to his brother.** "Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din's servant, Iqbal, presented
    Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din with several pieces of fine cloth and a purse of gold coins from his master, but
    the latter would not accept them and asked his brother, Maulana 'IImu'd-Din [OCR?] ['Ilmu'd-Din] to
    accept them instead" (p. 232, folio 212).
  - **The doctrine that a Shaikh should be rich, in his own reported words — the book's most quotable
    defence of shrine wealth.** Shaikh Nasiruʾd-Din Chiragh Dihlawi relates: "When Shaikhu'l-Islam
    Ruknu'l Haqq Wa'd-Din visited Delhi, qgalandars [OCR?] [qalandars] and jwalgis [OCR?] [reading not
    resolved] would make demands of him, the former requesting sherbet and the latter, money. The
    Shaikhu'l-Islam would satisfy them and remark: 'The leaders of the community should possess three
    things. Firstly, they should have property in order to satisfy their demands… Secondly, the leaders
    should have knowledge inorder thatthey mighthave scholarly discussions with 'ulama.' Thirdly, they
    should be endowed with ha/ [OCR?] [hal] (mystical enlightenment) so that they might impress
    dervishes.'" — with the Chishti rebuttal, "Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din added that money was not necessary—only
    knowledge and hal were essential" (p. 234, folio 214). **Rizvi frames it as "the ideological
    differences between Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din and his Chishti counterparts". The quotation has lost its word
    spaces and cannot be entered verbatim — see Doubts.**
  - **He tolerated qalandars where his grandfather did not.** "Qalandars were intolerable to Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din but the attitude of his grandson, Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din, was different. He tolerated them as
    he considered that their persistent demands and those of other visitors justified sufi Shaikhs
    possessing wealth" (p. 236, folio 216).
  - **The succession after him ends in confiscation and an execution.** "According to Jamali, his nephew,
    Shaikh Isma'il, succeeded him. Ibn Battuta says that Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din nominated his grandson, Shaikh
    Hud, as his successor, but his nephew challenged the claim. The dispute was laid before Muhammad bin
    Tughluq who gave his verdict in favour of Shaikh Hud. After some time, the governor of Sind accused
    Shaikh Hud of misappropriating the income of Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din's khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] for his own
    personal use. The Sultan ordered the governor to seize the entire property and the Shaikh was reduced
    to a miserable condition" (p. 234, folio 214); Shaikh Hud was then "executed on a trumped-up charge of
    attempting to mastermind a Mongol invasion of India, using as a war-cry the ill-treatment of Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya's descendants by the Sultan" (p. 234, folio 214). **Two incompatible successions
    printed side by side and left unresolved.**
  - **A robe-and-turban investiture of a disciple.** "Shaikh 'Usman Sayyah of Sunnam", enrolled "near
    Kilukhari when the latter was performing prayers on the banks of the Jumna", taught "the
    'Awarifu'l-Ma'arif" at Multan, and was "given the honour of being presented with Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din's
    own garment and turban" on returning from seven years' travel (p. 235, folio 215). **A garment and a
    turban, not a khirqa and not a document.**
  - **Two garments carry his own succession.** "he wore the same turban and Shaikh Sadru'd-Din's khirga
    [OCR?] [khirqa]" (p. 231, folio 211).

- **Practices & Events, and the state**
  - **A petition-broker to the throne, with the mechanism described.** On the palanquin "there was a pile
    of papers. Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din mentioned that they were petitions from the needy and had been given to
    him so that he could recommend them to the Sultan" (p. 232, folio 212); and systematically, "whenever
    Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din went to the court of Sultan Qutbu'd-Din, the people of Delhi would place petitions
    inside the Shaikh's takht-i rawan (a type of palanquin similar to a moveable throne)… The petitions
    would then be brought in and, after having read them, the Sultan would write sympathetic replies.
    According to a sufi tradition, the Shaikh would remain at court until all the requests had been
    granted" (p. 232, folio 212), with a Sultan who "would squat respectfully at his feet".
  - **A Sultan tried to use him against a rival Shaikh and failed.** "His three visits to the capital
    during Sultan Qutbu'd-Din's reign were, however, sponsored by the Sultan who wished that Shaikh
    Ruknu'd-Din's presence might eclipse that of Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din Auliya'. But the close friendship
    between the two great Shaikhs frustrated the Sultan's hopes and he could not arouse the tiniest
    jealousy between them" (p. 231, folio 211); asked who had met him first, "Unhesitatingly the Shaikh
    replied that the leading figure amongst the élite was the one who had met him first, that is, Shaikh
    Nizamu'd-Din" (p. 231, folio 211).
  - **A hajj obtained by visiting a saint instead of travelling.** On "the tenth Zu'lhijja, the day when
    pilgrimages to Mecca were performed", with Nizamu'd-Din ill, "Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din decided instead to
    visit the Shaikh in order to gain from him the blessings of a hajj" (p. 232, folio 212).
  - **Cordial relations with the next Sultan, contrasted with the Chishtis'.** "Unlike his relations with
    the Chishti sufis of Delhi, those between Sultan Muhammad bin Tughluq and Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din were most
    cordial. In 1327-28, Bahram Aiba Kishlu Khan, the Governor of Multan," — **the sentence is cut off at
    the chunk boundary (p. 232, folio 212), and it is the only place in that chunk where he and Multan
    stand in one sentence.**
  - **His teaching, summarised and quoted.** He "exhorted his disciples to abandon cruelty, oppression,
    avarice and greed, as he believed such vices rendered human beings to the level of beasts.
    Self-purification came only through humility and prayer", citing "'I do not exculpate myself. Lo! the
    (human) soul enjoineth unto evil, save whereon my Lord hath mercy. Lo! My Lord is Forgiving and
    Merciful.'" (p. 233, folio 213); and "purification of the self in the final analysis depended on divine
    grace", on "'Had it not been for the grace of Allah and His mercy unto you, not one of you would ever
    have grown pure.'", with "the mark of divine grace and mercy emanated from an insight into one's
    faults" (p. 234, folio 214).

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **A prenatal prophecy by his grandfather, and the turban at the age of four** (pp. 230–231, folios
    210–211).
  - **A prophecy of victory sealed by a Sultan kissing his feet.** "The Sultan kissed the Shaikh's feet and
    he in turn prophesied victory. The battle took place near Abuhar" (p. 233, folio 213) — **the obeisance
    that the same chunk elsewhere makes a controversy.**
  - **Seven days' meditation during the massacre of Multan — a story Rizvi rejects.** The Sultan "flayed
    the Qazi alive and ordered a general massacre of the population. According to 'Isami, Shaikh
    Ruknu'd-Din retreated into meditation for seven days, while the inhabitants were massacred; and
    emerging from isolation, he then interceded with the Sultan in an effort to save further lives. This
    story, however, would appear apocryphal. Barani says the Shaikh approached the Sultan soon after he
    learnt of his intention to massacre the population, and that the Sultan relented on his request for
    clemency" (p. 233, folio 213). **Both accounts stand; Rizvi prefers Barani and gives no reason.**
  - **He leaves the collapsing palace at Afghanpur minutes before a Sultan is killed in it.** He "went to
    receive Sultan Ghiyasu'd-Din Tughluq at the village of Afghanpur, about five miles from the city. He
    lunched with the Sultan in a specially constructed palace and left to perform afternoon prayers at the
    suggestion of Muhammad bin Tughlugq [OCR?] [Tughluq], the Sultan's son. After the Shaikh left, the
    palace collapsed and Sultan Ghiyasu'd-Din was crushed to death under its roof" (p. 232, folio 212).
    **The book draws no miraculous inference and names no agency — (not stated).**

- **Significance**
  - **The book makes him the richest and most political of the Multan Suhrawardis** — a hundred villages,
    700,000 tankas a visit, a palanquin full of petitions, Ibn Battuta at his door in 1333 — **and gives
    his shrine nothing at all: no tomb, no fabric, no builder, no ʿurs, no custodian, no sajjada nashin —
    all (not stated).**

### gorakhnath-goraknath-temple — Gorakhnath (Goraknath) Temple

**Eleven bullets across chunks 001, 021, 022, 023, 024, 026 and 027, and they divide into figure
evidence and site evidence — this is the only archive row in the book that has both.** The book calls
him "Gorakhnath", once "Goraknath [OCR?] [Gorakhnath]" (p. 355, folio 333) and once "Gorakh" (p. 393,
folio 369). **Under the many-row-person rule of 22 September 2026 Gorakhnath takes both of his rows;
the same material stands under `tilla-jogian` below, and the two-row hedge the Pass 1 workers left open
is closed.**

- **Saint**
  - **Birth legend and maiming.** "Gorakhnath, Matsyendra's disciple, believed to have been born from
    the sweat of Shiva's breast, saved his master, in whose service he had earlier lost an eye. Much
    mystery and legend also surround Gorakhnath's personality, but we know he wrote some treatises in
    Sanskrit" (p. 353, folio 331). **No birth date, no death date, no burial, no tomb — (not stated).**
    **Rizvi withholds historicity from the nine Naths and the eighty-four Siddhas but not from him:**
    "The traditions venerate nine Naths and eighty-four Siddhas but this does not imply the historical
    authenticity of these figures. The eighty-four Siddhas represent the 'totality of a revelation'"
    (p. 354, folio 332).
  - **Head of the Nath transmission, in a trinity with a deity.** "The transmission of the Nath doctrine
    however is founded on the trinity: Shiva, Matsyendranath and Gorakhnath" (p. 354, folio 332); "The
    Adi Nath, the First Lord of the Naths, is the Shiva of the Hindus" (p. 353, folio 331).
  - **Founder of the Kanphata order.** "The framework of the order of the Kanphata (Split Ear Yogis) or
    the Naths founded by Gorakhnath serves to illustrate the latter's remarkable organizational capacity
    and foresight" (p. 354, folio 332). The index cross-refers "Kan-phata yogis, see Nath Yogis"
    (p. 471, folio 447).
  - **Works, surviving and lost.** "Of the Sanskrit treatises by Gorakhnath, unlike the Hatha-Yoga, the
    Goraksha-Sataka has survived and has been edited and translated into English" (p. 354, folio 332);
    "Gorakhnath's disciples were the authors of a number of treatises on magic, alchemy and left-handed
    occultism and Hatha-Yoga" (pp. 353–354, folios 331–332); "A number of verses in Panjabi and Hindi
    also attributed to him were written by successive generations of disciples using the name Gorakhnath
    in order to establish a spiritual link with their master" (p. 353, folio 331) — **the same pen-name
    practice the book finds in the Baba Farid corpus (p. 353, folio 331)**; "Some verses ascribed to
    Gorakhnath in the Hindi Gorakhbani challenge qazis for mechanically crying the name 'Muhammad'"
    (p. 361, folio 339); and "One of the more important seventeenth century contributions is the
    Goraksha-Bijay, based on the Sanskrit work said to have been composed by Gorakhnath" (p. 374,
    folio 352), which Rizvi's own footnote assigns to another man — "Written by Shaikh Faizu'llah"
    (p. 374, folio 352, footnote 3).
  - **Doctrine, summarised at length as the chapter's bridge to sufism.** Ultimate Reality "could not be
    conceived by logical reasoning; it was a super sensuous, super intellectual, direct experience in the
    state of samadhi (trance)" (p. 355, folio 333); "Sat-Cit-Ananda-Murti (One who reveals Himself as
    Being, Consciousness and Bliss), Goraknath [OCR?] [Gorakhnath] believed was the highest form of God,
    the self-manifestation of the Formless and Manifestationless One—Brahma, Shiva, Parmatma,
    Parmeswara, the holiest names of the Nameless One" (p. 355, folio 333); "To Gorakhnath and the
    Siddhas the phenomenal cosmic system was not false or illusory" (p. 355, folio 333); "Adhars,
    according to Gorakhnath, are the main sources of the vital and psychical functions which have to be
    controlled" (pp. 356–357, folios 334–335); "According to Gorakhnath the Absolute Truth realized in
    the highest spiritual experience is above the concept of bhava (existence) and abhava (negation of
    existence)" (p. 361, folio 339).
  - **His name used by a Chishti shaikh as a term for God — the strongest shared-ground evidence in the
    book.** "Of the many verses in the Rushd-Nama there are six references to either 'Gorakhnath,' 'Shri
    Gorakh,' 'Nath' and 'O! Nath.' As in many Nath texts, these words at five different places throughout
    the work imply Ultimate Reality and Absolute Truth, while in the sixth place, the word refers to the
    Perfect Siddha or Perfect Man" (p. 358, folio 336); "In the words of Gorakh, Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus
    warns yogis that wearing rings in their ears and rosaries of Eleocarpus ganitrus around their necks,
    or the recitation of sakhi and sabad (Nath poetry), fails to make one a yogi" (p. 360, folio 338);
    "All the verses by Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus relating to the simile of the mirror and the polishing of
    the heart are based on ideas expressed by Ibn al-'Arabi and Gorakhnath" (p. 363, folio 341).
  - **Gorakh as the Mahdi's counterpart, stated flatly.** "a yogi becomes a Siddha only after meeting
    Gorakh, a counterpart of the Mahdi. Both Gorakh and Siddhas exhibit the distinctive features of the
    Mahdi" (p. 393, folio 369); and "they had failed to inherit Gorakh's real absorption with the"
    (p. 401, folio 377) — **the sentence is cut at the chunk boundary.**
  - **The yogis' claim that he is master of the gods and of the prophets, held in secret.** "They
    considered Brahma, Vishnu and Mahesh to be both angels and disciples of Gorakhnath. They also claimed
    all the prophets and apostles as his disciples. To yogis the Prophet Muhammad was a pupil and
    disciple of Gorakhnath but they concealed this belief for fear of Muslim retribution" (p. 376,
    folio 354), sourced "Dabistan-i Mazahib, pp. 179-80". **And a second figure is identified with him:
    "Baba Ratan Haijji [OCR?] [Hajji] was identified with Gorakhnath and it was asserted that the Prophet
    had learnt Yoga through the Baba" (p. 376, folio 354).**

- **Shrine / site — the row's location, and the only Pakistani site this book describes as a
  shared-ground place**
  - **Peshawar named as the order's centre.** "From the eleventh century the Nath yogis began to spread
    throughout northern India, and from their centre at Peshawar moved to all parts of Central Asia and
    Iran, at the same time influencing both qalandars and sufis" (p. 355, folio 333). **The book does not
    name the Gorakhnath temple, Gor Khatri, or any building at Peshawar in that sentence — (not stated).**
  - **The site itself, in three spellings, located in Peshawar, and its history.** "After his return from
    the Middle East, Guru Nanak seems to have remained in the Panjab, occasionally visiting Ajodhan,
    Multan and a place called Gorakhtari. According to Trumpp the existence of such a town has not been
    substantiated by modern geographers. McLeod rightly locates the site in Peshawar. Babur mentions his
    visit to a place which according to Persian script is either 'Gorkhattari' or perhaps 'Gorakhtari.'
    According to Dani the spot was associated with 'the tower of Buddha's bowl.' Like other Buddhist
    sites occupied by yogis, it became a centre for their activities. In the reign of Akbar and Jahangir
    it was an important yogi centre of pilgrimage" (p. 413, folio 389), sourced to "A.H. Dani, Peshawar,
    historic city of the Frontier, Peshawar, 1969, p. 36" and "S.A.A. Rizvi, Babur Nama in Mughal kalin
    Bharat-Babur, Aligarh. 1965, pp. 109-10". **A Buddhist relic-site taken over by yogis, become a yogi
    pilgrimage centre under two named Mughal emperors, and visited by the founder of Sikhism. The word
    "temple" is never used of it, no deity is named there, and Gorakhnath is not named in that passage —
    (not stated). The book also preserves a dispute about whether the place exists at all.**
  - **The site in the plate list and in the index.** The illustration caption "Babur visiting Gorkhattri"
    (p. 392, folio not stated) — **a caption and nothing else: no temple, no Gorakhnath, no jogi, no
    custodian; the picture itself cannot be read from the OCR, so what it shows of the site is (not
    stated)**; the caption spelling "Gorakhattri" in the illustration list (p. 10, folio not stated); and
    the index's "Gorakhtari, 389" and "Gorkhattari, 389" (p. 466, folio 442). **All four are converted to
    this row under ruling (a): Gor Khatri, Peshawar, is this row's own location.**
  - **The index entry for the figure, which serves both his rows.** "Gorakhnath, 331-33, 340-41, 352-54,
    369" (p. 466, folio 442); around it "Gorakhbani, 339", "Goraksha-Bijay, 352", "Goraksha-Sataka, 332"
    (p. 466, folio 442) and "Philosophy of Gorakhnath, 333-34" (p. 480, folio 456). The general headword
    "Nath yogi, 111, 304, 308, 318, 320. 327. 332-33, 338, 351-54, 359, 368-69, 374-75, 387, 389, 390,
    393" (p. 478, folio 454) is **the longest yogic entry in the index**. Two bibliography entries name
    him in full: "Raghaw, S., Gorakhnath aur unka yug, Delhi, 1963." (p. 449, folio 425) and "Banerjea,
    A.K., Philosophy of Gorakhnath, Gorakhpur, n.d." (p. 450, folio 426) — **the latter with no date at
    all.**

- **Practices & Events**
  - **The Kanphata initiation rite is described as still current** (p. 354, folio 332), and the Naths'
    settlement pattern is given generically: they "lived in forests, wandered in towns and also
    established permanent monasteries" (p. 376, folio 354). **No monastery is named or located — (not
    stated).**
  - **Bodily immortality as the yogi's attainment.** "The perfect yogi can transform his body according
    to his will and is therefore free of all diseases and death. Siddhas, such as the disciples of
    Gorakhnath and others are believed to have achieved the practical aspects of this philosophy while in
    the foothills of the Himalayas and came to be known as the jivan-mukta (liberated while living)"
    (p. 357, folio 335).

- **Significance**
  - **The book's Naths are a Peshawar-centred movement in continuous doctrinal exchange with Chishti
    sufis** (p. 355, folio 333), and Gorakhnath's name is used by a Chishti shaikh as a name for God
    (p. 358, folio 336). **On the temple itself the book is silent: no deity, no building, no custodian,
    no festival — all (not stated).**

### tilla-jogian — Tilla Jogian

**Person-level only.** Under the many-row-person rule of 22 September 2026 Guru Gorakhnath, this row's
principal figure, takes both of his rows, so **the whole Gorakhnath block in the
`gorakhnath-goraknath-temple` subsection above stands equally under this id** (pp. 353–363, 374, 376,
393, 401; folios 331–341, 352, 354, 369, 377; index p. 466, folio 442). **What the book does NOT give
this row: it never names Tilla Jogian, the Salt Range, Jhelum or Dina anywhere in 491 pages, and "Tilla
Jogian" is not an index headword** — all (not stated). **The site evidence in the Gorakhnath subsection
(Gorakhtari / Gorkhattari / Gor Khatri, Peshawar) belongs to the Peshawar row and NOT to this one; it
must never be read as describing Tilla Jogian.** The Naths' only site-language reachable for this row is
generic: they "lived in forests, wandered in towns and also established permanent monasteries" (p. 376,
folio 354), and "Siddhas… achieved the practical aspects of this philosophy while in the foothills of the
Himalayas" (p. 357, folio 335).

### gurudwara-janam-asthan-nankana-sahib — Gurudwara Janam Asthan Nankana Sahib

**The only Guru Nanak row with real site evidence in this book, and it is one sentence.**

- **Saint**
  - **Birth, place, old name, distance and direction.** "Guru Nanak was born in 1469 in the village of
    Talwandi later known as Nankana Sahib, about forty miles south-west of Lahore, now part of Pakistan"
    (p. 407, folio 383); "Most of his life occurred during the reign of the Lodis" (p. 407, folio 383).
    **The index agrees on the dates: "Guru Nanak (b. 1469, d. 1539), 329, 331, 346, 383-95" (p. 466,
    folio 442).** **No gurdwara, no building, no shrine and no date of construction at Nankana Sahib —
    all (not stated).** The index's bare "Talwandi, 383" (p. 489, folio 465) **is not glossed as Nankana
    Sahib anywhere in the book, and the equation has not been supplied here.**
  - **Family and childhood at Talwandi.** "The parents of Guru Nanak came from the Khattri community
    which was closely connected with the Afghan rulers in the fields of commerce and administration. Guru
    Nanak's father, Kalu, was a village accountant and as was customary with members of that profession
    he supplemented his income through agriculture" (p. 408, folio 384); "When seven years old, Nanak was
    taken by his father to receive instruction in Hinduism. Two years later, attempts were made toteach
    [OCR?] [to teach] him Persian"; "It would, however, seem that he learnt both Sanskrit and Persian. In
    those days Sa'di's Karima and the Gulistan, as well as general sufi verses were taught to both Hindu
    and Muslim youths"; "When about sixteen, Nanak was married by his parents and under pressure from his
    family he later became a trader and farmer. Nevertheless most of Nanak's time was spent with yogis
    who lived in the surrounding jungles" (p. 408, folio 384). **The mother is not named; the wife is not
    named; the marriage is dated only by his age — (not stated).** The index carries the father as a bare
    name, "Kalu, 384" (p. 471, folio 447), with "Jai Ram, 384" (p. 470, folio 446) and "Lehna, 394"
    (p. 473, folio 449).
  - **Itinerary, as the book gives it** — Sultanpur, Panipat, Delhi, eastern India/Assam, Saiyidpur
    (Amanabad), Ceylon, Kashmir, Mecca, Baghdad, Ajodhan, Multan, Gorakhtari, Kartarpur (pp. 409–418,
    folios 385–394). **Hasan Abdal, Sukkur, Shikarpur, Quetta, Farooqabad, Rohtas, Eminabad and Saidpur
    are not named — (not stated).**
  - **Rizvi's own disclaimer, the most important sentence in the book for every Nanak row.** "Our
    analysis of Guru Nanak's teachings in the light of sufi beliefs should not be construed as an
    assertion that the great Guru borrowed his ideas from sufis. It would indicate that Guru Nanak,
    through his own meditation, arrived at the same conclusions as had already been reached by sufis such
    as Rumi, Sa'di, 'Iraqi, Jami and Hafiz" (p. 420, folio 396); "Although some hymns in the Adi-Granth
    read like portions of the Masnawi of Maulana Rumi, which Jami called 'the Qur'an in Persian,' there
    is nothing to indicate that Guru Nanak was imitating the great poet" (p. 420, folio 396).
  - **Against McLeod, on sufi contact.** "If by classical sufism, McLeod was referring to the thoughts of
    sufis such as 'Attar, Rumi and Sa'di who were also poets in the classical Persian mould all literate
    Panjabis, both Hindus and Muslims, at the time of Guru Nanak were conversant with them. Certainly the
    Guru belonged to a literate family" (p. 420, folio 396); "Although the Janam-Sakhis of the
    seventeenth century do not mention Guru Nanak's visit to these personalities, it is unbelievable that
    he made 'informal contacts with ordinary Muslims' alone and not with the great sufi saints of his own
    days" (p. 420, folio 396); "Guru Nanak undoubtedly criticized sufis at several places as suggested by
    McLeod but he intended to denounce only impostors and charlatans who made a living out of mysticism.
    Great sufis also attacked such people, along with mullas and qazis. Nanak did likewise" (p. 420,
    folio 396).
  - **The one comparative sentence in Chapter Six.** "As we shall see, Guru Nanak exhibited more
    equanimity and resignation in the divine will than Shaikh ʿAbduʾl-Quddus" (p. 368, folio 346) — **the
    forward promise is not redeemed in pp. 366–382.** He is also named as the climax of Nirguna Bhakti
    (p. 397, folio 373) and as the visitor to Shaikh Ibrahim at Ajodhan (p. 351, folio 329).
  - **The monographs about him, in the bibliography** — seven entries on pp. 451–455 (folios 427–431),
    including "McLeod, W.H., Guru Nanak and the Sikh religion, Oxford, 1968" (pp. 409, 411, 412, 417;
    folios 385, 387, 388, 393), cited also as "Guru Nanak and the Sikhs, p. 65" (p. 413, folio 389) —
    **two titles for what may be one book.** The index carries "Guru Nanak and the Sikh religion, 385,
    387-96", "Guru Granth, 328-29, 356, 374, 383-84", "Granth-Sahib, see Guru Granth", "Guru Ka Sahad
    [OCR?] [Guru Ka Sabad], 391" (p. 466, folio 442), "Janam-Sakhi, 383-84, 387-94", "Japji, 390"
    (p. 470, folio 446), "Macauliffe, M.A., 356, 359, 375-82, 386-94" and "McLeod, W.H., 383, 385, 387.97
    [OCR?] [387-97]" (p. 475, folio 451). **The index names no gurdwara at all.**

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The Kaʿba that turns towards him, and the book's explanation of it.** "The legend that the Ka'ba or
    the mihrab of the principal mosque, moved in the direction of the Guru's feet need not be interpreted
    literally. The story is based on the sufi belief that great saints did not need to circumambulate the
    Ka'ba, for whichever way they looked it would appear before them. In fact sufis believed that eminent
    saints and holy men were recipients of the divine light, while the K'aba [OCR?] [Kaʿba] itself was a
    building of mere stone and rubble" (p. 412, folio 388); the Janam-Sakhi authors "had no real
    knowledge of the fact that the Ka'ba was a cube-like building, in the middle of the mosque at Mecca.
    There was no question of a mihrab" (p. 412, folio 388, footnote 4).
  - **Conversation with dead saints, as the shared sufi-and-Nath doctrine.** "the Janam-Sakhis expressed
    the sufi and Nath belief that great saints did not die but remained accessible to important mystics
    in later ages through spiritual conversation" (p. 413, folio 389); "when the Janam-Sakhis describe
    Guru Nanak's conversation with the saints of the past, this should invariably be interpreted as a
    mystic experience or, in the sufi sense, a chat with the spirits, although presented as if taken
    place by two living people" (p. 414, folio 390); "At Panipat Nanak also had discussions with the
    spirit of Shaikh Sharafu'd-Din Abu 'Ali Qalandar" (p. 409, folio 385).
  - **The failure of miracle against Babur's army, in his own hymn.** "'Millions of priests tried by their
    miraculous power to restrain the Emperor when they heard of his approach. He burned houses, mansions,
    and palaces; he cut princes to pieces, and had them rolled in the dust. No Mughal hath become blind;
    no priest hath wrought a miracle'" (p. 410, folio 386); and "so impressed with the Guru's hymns was he
    that Guru Nanak was released along with some other prisoners" (p. 410, folio 386).
  - **Debates on a legendary mountain, which Rizvi says "need not be accepted literally"** — "the
    legendary Mount Sumeru or Maru where he was alleged to have held debates with Naths and Siddhas"
    (pp. 411–412, folios 387–388).
  - **Transmission of light at the succession.** "posterity recognized him as the personification of the
    light of God. It came to be believed that the mingling of 'the light' of the Guru with that of Guru
    Angad was in accordance with God's Riza" (p. 419, folio 395).

- **Significance / negatives**
  - **The book denies that he left any material memorial.** "Guru Nanak, preoccupied with discovering and
    disseminating Truth, would not have cared about a lasting material memorial to himself and certainly
    nothing of this kind was left by him in his own country" (p. 413, folio 389). **Rizvi's own judgement,
    offered against Sikh scholars seeking epigraphic proof of the Baghdad journey; it is the only thing
    the book says about memorials to Guru Nanak, and an entry quoting it must say so.**

### gurdwara-darbar-sahib-kartarpur — Gurdwara Darbar Sahib Kartarpur

- **Saint** — **"Guru Nanak died at Kartarpur in 1539. Before his death he appointed Lehna his
  successor"** (p. 418, folio 394). **Place of death plus year, matching the row's `figure_died` 1539, and
  confirmed by the index's "Guru Nanak (b. 1469, d. 1539)" (p. 466, folio 442).** The succession is
  described as a sufi-style humiliation of the heir: "Although Lehna came from a rich family, after
  becoming the Guru's disciple, in accordance with sufi traditions, he was made to perform such
  humiliating tasks as carrying loads of wet grass" (p. 418, folio 394); "As the First Guru he was known
  as Guru Angad (1539-52)" (p. 419, folio 395) — **"First" contradicts the book's own numbering of Arjan
  as fifth; see Doubts.**
- **Shrine / Darbar** — **no samadhi, no tomb, no shrine and no building at Kartarpur is mentioned** —
  (not stated) — and the book's flat denial that Guru Nanak left any memorial (p. 413, folio 389) stands
  against its own record of his death there. **All the person-level Guru Nanak material in the
  `gurudwara-janam-asthan-nankana-sahib` subsection above also stands under this id.**

### lal-shahbaz-qalandar — Lal Shahbaz Qalandar

**Four bullets, all from chunk 019 plus one index line, and all in one paragraph of the book.**

- **Saint**
  - **Personal name and native place.** "Lal Shahbaz's real name was Mir Saiyid 'Usman and he was a
    native of Sehwan in the Sind" (p. 328, folio 306). **The book writes "Mir Saiyid ʿUsman", NOT "Usman
    Marwandi"; the nisba "Marwandi" appears nowhere, and no death date is given anywhere — the archive
    row's 1274 is not in this book.** See Doubts.
  - **Initiation, as an exception to a rule.** "Another exception was Shaikh Lal Shahbaz Qalandar… Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din disliked qalandars intensely and certainly never encouraged their unwelcome visits. Lal
    Shahbaz was an exception and was adopted as his disciple." (p. 328, folio 306). He is introduced
    inside a run of qalandars "Among the Suhrawardiyyas" (p. 328, folio 306). **The row's `silsila` is
    Qalandari; the book gives him a Suhrawardi initiation and no silsila name of his own.**
  - **The two halves of the name, each given an origin, one a conferred title.** "Often he wore a red
    dress, hence his name lal (red). Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya gave him the title 'Shahbaz,' the
    noblest species of falcon." (p. 328, folio 306).
- **Practices** — "Following the Shari'a held no interest for Lal Shahbaz who like the malamatis, indulged
  in ecstatic dancing." (p. 328, folio 306).
- **Shrine / Darbar and Legends** — **the tomb is named as a working miracle-site, in the present tense.**
  "A large number of miracles are said to have been performed by him and even his tomb in Sind is known
  for innumerable miraculous feats." (p. 328, folio 306), sourced only as "Ma'ariju'l-Wilayat, f. 542b".
  **The book locates the tomb as "in Sind" and does NOT name Sehwan as the tomb's place — Sehwan is given
  only as his birthplace. No death date, no ʿurs, no shrine building, no custodian — all (not stated).**
- **Index** — "Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, Shaikh, 306" (p. 473, folio 449): **a single folio, no dates, no
  place, no order.** The general headword "Qalandar, 1, 5, 14, 16, 142-43, 145, 150, 193, 204, 214, 216,
  270, 271, 278, 287, 301-21, 354, 356, 389" (p. 480, folio 456) supplies the run folio 306 sits in, and
  "Rasuli Qalandars, 306" (p. 482, folio 458) and "Haidaris, 306-11, 410" (p. 467, folio 443) share it.
  The place-name is indexed separately: "Sehwan, 153, 277, 306" and "Siwistan, 153, 277" (pp. 484, 486;
  folios 460, 462) — **converted to this row and to `shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar` under ruling (a); see
  the place-level run below.**

### shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari — Shrine of Jalaluddin Surkh-Posh Bukhari (Jalaluddin Bukhari)

- **Saint** — **the whole of what the book says about him in his own right is three sentences.** "Shaikh
  Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya had many famous disciples, one was Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din Bukhari, also called Jalal
  Surkh (Red). He first migrated from Bukhara to Bhakkar, however local jealousy forced him to migrate to
  Uch. He remained there until his death" (p. 223, folio 203), sourced only as "3AA, p. 61". **The book
  prints "Jalal Surkh (Red)", never "Surkh-Posh"** (p. 223, folio 203). **No death date, no year, no tomb,
  no shrine building, no khalifas — all (not stated); the row's `figure_died` 1291 is neither confirmed nor
  contradicted, because the book prints no date for him at all.**
- **Family** — "He was a grandson of Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din Surkh (Red), a disciple of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din
  Zakariyya. Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din Surkh had three sons: Saiyid Ahmad Kabir, Saiyid Baha'u'd-Din and Saiyid
  Muhammad" (p. 299, folio 277). **"Saiyid Baha'u'd-Din" here is NOT `tomb-of-baha-al-halim-uch-sharif`;
  see Doubts.**
- **Silsila** — his affiliation as the book gives it is to Bahaʾuʾd-Din Zakariyya, which matches the row's
  Suhrawardi cell, **but the book does not use the word "Suhrawardiyya" of him — (not stated)** (p. 223,
  folio 203).
- **Index** — "Saiyid Jalalu’d-Din Surkh (Bukhari), 203, 277" (p. 483, folio 459), **the archive's
  principal figure under the archive's own epithet "Surkh"**. But the index's cross-reference "Jalalu’d-Din
  Bukhari, see Makhdum Jahaniyan" (p. 470, folio 446) **sends the shorter form of his name to the other
  Uch row's man — do not follow it blindly; see Doubts.**

### shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib — Shrine of Miran Hussain Zanjani (Zanjani Sahib)

- **Saint** — the book calls him "Shaikh Husain Zinjani", then "Zinjani" (p. 130, folio 112), "Shaikh
  Husain Zanjani" and, in Abuʾl-Fazl's report, "Shaikh Husaini Zanjani" (p. 139, folio 121). **The row
  writes "Syed Miran Hussain Zanjani"; the book prints neither "Syed" nor "Miran" anywhere.** He was a
  disciple of Khattali "ordered … to move to the Panjab" before Hujwiri (p. 130, folio 112).
- **Shrine / Darbar — the burial place is the archive row's own location, and that is what makes the
  mapping.** "In the morning Shaikh Husain Zinjani's coffin was carried out for burial—he had died during
  the night. Zinjani was buried in Chah Miran, which is now a suburb of Lahore" (p. 130, folio 112).
  **His death is dated only by Hujwiri's arrival, which the book puts at "c. 1035" (p. 130, folio 112),
  itself hedged; the row's `figure_died` 1042 is not in the book.** **No year of birth, no silsila, no
  honorific, no tomb building, no ʿurs — all (not stated).**
- **The two Lahore tombs, and the four positions on whether he was alive.** Khwaja Muʿinuʾd-Din "stayed
  near the tombs of Shaikh Husain Zanjani and Shaikh ‘Ali Hujwiri" (p. 139, folio 121). On one page the
  book prints: Jamali, that Hujwiri died the year the Khwaja reached Lahore "but that Shaikh Husain
  Zanjani was still alive"; the *Fawaʿidu'l-Fuʾad*, that "Hujwiri reached Lahore in the year Shaikh Husain
  Zanjani died"; "According to Abu'l-Fazl, Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din lived with Shaikh Husaini Zanjani. Ain, II]
  [OCR?] [III], p. 168" — **a statement that would require him to be alive**; and Firishta, who "simply
  mentions that after leaving Ghazna, the Khwaja stayed at Lahore" (p. 139, folio 121, footnote 3).
  Rizvi's ruling: "Fawaidu'l Fu‘ad's statement appears to be correct and it is likely that the Khwaja
  stayed near the two tombs" (p. 139, folio 121, footnote 3).
- **Index** — "Husain Zinjani [OCR?] [Zanjani], Shaikh, 111, 121" (p. 468, folio 444): **two folios, a
  corrupted nisba in the headword, no dates.** The row's location is an index headword too, with its
  number string destroyed: "Chah Miran, I11 [OCR?] [number unreadable; possibly 111, but not repaired]"
  (p. 463, folio 439) — **converted to this row under ruling (a); folio 111 is where the man and the place
  may meet, and that has not been checked or asserted.**

### tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak — Tomb of Qutbuddin Aibak

**Mapped on the person only; the book gives him no tomb, no death date and no Lahore anywhere.** In this
book "Qutbuʾd-Din" names at least nine different men and the bare short form is never mapped — see Doubts.

- **Saint** — "Mu‘izzu’d-Din Muhammad bin Sam had conquered Delhi and, departing for Ghazni, left his
  favourite slave, Qutbu’d-Din Aibak, as head of his forces in the occupied city" (p. 139, folio 121);
  "Sultan Qutbu'd-Din Aibak, also a former slave, during a visit to Ghazna after his Anhilwara expedition
  in 1196, took a fancy to Iltutmish and was able to buy him in Delhi, and between 1197 or 1198 to 1206
  Iltutmish rose to prominence" (p. 153, folio 135, footnote 2). **The date 1206 in that sentence closes a
  range describing Iltutmish's rise, not Aibak's life.**
- **Acts** — "Qutbu’d-Din had appointed Saiyid Husain Mashhadi as the darogha (military governor) of the
  area. The Saiyid served the Khwaja with great devotion" (p. 140, folio 122); "Occupying troops stationed
  at Indraprastha near Delhi under Qutbu’d-Din Aibak were forced to make several invasions in the Doab and
  the regions around Ajmer to consolidate their power" (p. 140, folio 122).
- **Index** — "Qutbu’d-Din Aibak (1206-1210), 122, 135" (p. 481, folio 457). **The printed dates are a
  reign span, not a death year; the archive's `figure_died` is 1210, the span's terminal year.** Two Delhi
  monuments stand near him — "Qutb Minar, 247", "Qubbatu’l-Islam mosque, 247", "Quwatu'l-Islam mosque, 247"
  (p. 481, folio 457) — **but the index names no tomb of Aibak and no Lahore location for him — (not
  stated).**
- **Negative** — the row is categorised "Secular / Memorial" at Anarkali, Lahore; **this book places Aibak
  at Delhi and Ghazni only and attaches no tomb to him at all.**

### dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee — Dargah Pir Ratan Nath Jee

**Three bullets, and the weakest-evidenced mapping in this file.** The book calls him "Ratannath".

- **Saint** — **the whole of what the main text says is one clause.** "Ratannath, a contemporary of
  Gorakhnath, and the subject of many miraculous tales" (p. 354, folio 332), inside a list of "Other names
  associated with the Nath cult". **No place, no dargah, no dates, no order beyond "the Nath cult", and no
  miracle actually narrated — all (not stated); the tales are named and never told.**
- **A corrective footnote of Rizvi's own.** "In fact Ratannath, a contemporary of Gorakhnath, was a very
  important figure of the movement" (p. 376, folio 354, footnote 1) — **a bare name in an editorial aside:
  no Peshawar, no Jhanda Bazar, no dargah, no dates, no lineage. It sits in tension with the main text's
  "Baba Ratan Haijji [Hajji] was identified with Gorakhnath" on the same line, and the Pass 1 worker read
  Rizvi as distinguishing two figures; "Baba Ratan Haijji" is left unmapped — see Doubts.**
- **Index** — "Ratannath, 332" (p. 482, folio 458), printed inside the index's run of Nath names
  ("Dandanath", "Dayanath", "Dharamanath", "Gahininath", "Guganath", "Minanath", "Nimanath",
  "Nivrittinath", "Parasnath", "Ratannath"). A possibly related work is headed "Risala-i Rataniyya, 320"
  (p. 482, folio 458). **The index gives no "Pir", no "Baba", no Peshawar and no date — (not stated).**
- **Place-level** — the row is one of the ten Peshawar rows, and the book places the Naths' centre at
  Peshawar (p. 355, folio 333); see the place-level run below.

### gurdwara-rori-sahib — Gurdwara Rori Sahib

**This row has two principal figures, "Guru Nanak; Bhai Mardana", and the book names both.**

- **Bhai Mardana** — "There the minstrel, Bhai Mardana, a favourite friend of the Guru's, joined him" — at
  Sultanpur, in Daulat Khan's commissariat (p. 409, folio 385); "Soon afterwards, accompanied by Bhai
  Mardana, Nanak left Sultanpur" (p. 409, folio 385); and **"Guru Nanak and Mardana witnessed the massacre
  of the inhabitants of Saiyidpur (Amanabad) and were taken captive"** (p. 410, folio 386), an episode the
  chunk dates 926/1520 (p. 409, folio 385). **The book gives Mardana no death date, no tomb and no place of
  burial — (not stated).** The index carries him as a bare heading: "Bhai Mardana, 384-85" (p. 462,
  folio 438). **The adjacent index line "Amnabad, 385" (p. 462, folio 438) may or may not be the row's
  Eminabad; the identification has NOT been made — see Doubts.**
- **Guru Nanak** — all the person-level material in the `gurudwara-janam-asthan-nankana-sahib` subsection
  above stands equally under this id.
- **Negative** — **the book names no gurdwara at Eminabad and no site of any kind for this row — (not
  stated).**

### gurdwara-dera-sahib — Gurdwara Dera Sahib

**Person-level only: Guru Arjan Dev, under the many-row-person rule of 22 September 2026.** The book calls
him "The fifth Guru, Arjan Dev" and "the Fifth Guru, Arjan".

- **Saint** — **"The fifth Guru, Arjan Dev (1563-81-—1606) [OCR?] [the printed date string is damaged —
