  see Doubts] collected the inspiring hymns written by Guru Nanak and his disciples in the Sikh 'Bible,'
  the Adi-Granth which is also known as the Granth-Saheb or the Guru-Granth. He also went to the extent of
  incorporating the authentic poems of the fourteen bhaktas preceding Guru Nanak. This appendix to the
  Guru-Granth is the most authentic anthology of the movement and a memorable collection of medieval
  devotional literature. The Guru-Granth was completed at Amritsar in 1604"** (p. 407, folio 383); and
  **"After the death of the Fifth Guru, Arjan, at the hands of the Mughal Jahangir, the Sikhs dropped
  their pacifist policy and adopted their famous martial spirit"** (p. 419, folio 395). Also "more than a
  hundred s/okas [slokas] ascribed to Baba Farid Ganj-i Shakar in the Guru Garanth [Guru Granth], compiled
  by the fifth Sikh Guru, Arjan, in 1604" (p. 350, folio 328).
- **Index** — "Guru, Arjan Dev (b. 1563 guru 1581-1606), 324, 383, 394" (p. 466, folio 442), **the printed
  string carrying a stray comma in the headword and giving his guruship as a span; this row's
  `figure_died` is 1606, which the string's terminal year matches.** Beside it "Guru Angad (1539-52), 394"
  (p. 466, folio 442).
- **Negatives** — **the book does not say where Arjan died, gives no year for his death in the second
  passage, and names no shrine, gurdwara or Lahore location for him — all (not stated).** The damaged date
  string has not been resolved and no date has been asserted from it.

**Person-level only.** All the Guru Arjan Dev material in the `gurdwara-dera-sahib` subsection above
(p. 407, folio 383; p. 419, folio 395; p. 350, folio 328; index p. 466, folio 442) stands equally under

### mazar-e-iqbal — Tomb of Allama Iqbal (Mazar-e-Iqbal)

- **Author-only mentions, both for philosophy and never for poetry.** "Iqbal, Muhammad, The reconstruction
  of religious thought in Islam, rep. Lahore, 1944." and "The development of metaphysics in Persia,
  London, 1908." (p. 452, folio 428). **No dates, no Lahore tomb, no Urdu or Persian verse title, nothing
  but two English books — all else (not stated).** Mapped under the ruled 18 September 2026 convention,
  which names Iqbal by way of example.
- **Two further "Muhammad Iqbal" strings in the bibliography are editorial, not authorial, and are NOT
  mapped** — "Sadr al-Din 'Ali Husaini, Akhbar al-Daula al-Saljugivva, ed. Muhammad lIybal [OCR?] [Iqbal].,
  Lahore, 1933." and "Rawandi, Rahat al-Sudur wa-A yat al-Surur,ed. Muhammad Iqbal, London, 1921."
  (p. 446, folio 422). **Whether the editor of two Seljuq chronicles is the same man is not stated by the
  book and has not been decided here.**
- **The index's bare "Iqbal, 163, 213" (p. 469, folio 445) is NOT mapped** — a surname with no initials,
  whose immediate neighbour is "Iqbal b. Sabig-i Sistani, 249"; folios 163 and 213 must be opened by a
  human before that entry is attached to this row. See Doubts.
- One of the 35 Lahore rows on the place-level line below.

### ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed — Darbar Ghazi Ilm Din Shaheed

- **A declined mapping, kept as a finding.** The index prints "‘Ilmu’d-Din, 211-12" (p. 469, folio 445) —
  **a bare homonym with no dates; the archive's figure was executed in 1929 and the index gives the
  headword nothing to identify it by. Not mapped; folios 211–212 must be opened by a human.**
- One of the 35 Lahore rows and one of the four Miani Sahib rows; see the place-level run below.

### mazar-of-bulleh-shah — Mazar of Bulleh Shah

- **The book's single sharpest negative on a Punjabi archive figure.** **"Bulleh Shah" is absent from the
  index.** "Its place is between 'Bulgarian mat, 230' and 'Burchhardt, T., 104' (p. 463, folio 439) and
  nothing stands there." **Kasur appears only buried inside another man's name — "Khweshgi, Ghulam
  Mu‘inu‘d-Din *Abdu'llah Khweshgi of Qasur, 14" (p. 473, folio 449) — never as a headword.** "The index
  of a standard history of Indian Sufism has no entry for Bulleh Shah."
- **Place-level, converted under ruling (a).** The book locates the town once: "Qasur, thirty miles
  south-east of Lahore" (p. 28, folio 14), where it places the scholar Ghulam Muʿinuʾd-Din ʿAbduʾllah
  Khweshgi. **The book spells it "Qasur" against the archive's "Kasur" and attaches no shrine, saint or
  ʿurs to it.** Qasur appears again, bare, at p. 157 (folio 139), where Baba Farid's father "moved to
  Qasur, south-east of Lahore". **Under ruling (a) both mentions take this row, which is the only archive
  row at Kasur; the material is place-level and says nothing about the mazar.**

### sakhi-sarwar — Sakhi Sarwar

- **A negative finding, checked headword by headword.** **"Sakhi Sarwar" is absent from the index** — "its
  place is between 'Sakhi, 375' and 'Salah Mukhlis Dawud Khani, 8' (p. 483, folio 459), the chunk's last
  page, and nothing stands there." **The shrine's own place-name, Dera Ghazi Khan, falls under D and is
  also absent: the D run has "Deccan", "Deh", "Delhi", "Deogir", "Devagaon" (p. 464, folio 440).** All
  (not stated).

### shergarh — Shergarh

- **A negative finding.** **Daud Bandagi Kirmani is absent.** "The index's Dawud run is 'Dawud Khaki, 290 /
  Dawud, Maulana, 364-66 / Dawud Mishkati, 290' (p. 464, folio 440) — none is Daud Bandagi. Only the group
  name 'Kirmanis, 158-59' (p. 472, folio 448) is near, and that is not a mapping." Earlier chunks record
  the same refusal on "Baba Dawud Khaki Kashmiri Suhrawardi" (pp. 25–26, folios 11–12), "Salah Mukhlis
  Dawud Khani" (p. 22, folio 8) and "Shaikh Auhadu’d-Din Kirmani" (p. 138, folio 120). **No mapping, and
  none should be inferred from the shared elements "Dawud" and "Kirmani."**

### langer-makhdoom — Langer Makhdoom

- **A collision case rather than a clean absence, and the row is reported absent.** The index prints seven
  Burhanuʾd-Dins in one block: "Burhanu'd-Din b. Jamalu'd-Din, 153", "Burhanu’d-Din Gharib, 183, 251",
  "Burhanu'd-Din Kaziruni (Ts wan-chow-fu), 410", "Burhanu'd-Din Nasafi, Maulana, 219", "Burhanu'd-Din,
  Maulana, 155", "Burhanu'd-Din Sagharji, 315", "Burhanu'd-Din Shaikh, 178" (p. 463, folio 439); an eighth,
  "Qutb-i ‘Alam, Burhanu'd-Din (d. 1453), 282-84", stands at p. 481 (folio 457). **The archive's figure is
  "Makhdoom Burhan-ud-din" of Chiniot, died 1245. None of the eight is identified as of Chiniot and none
  carries that date, so none is mapped.** The book's fullest Burhanuʾd-Din — the khalifa of Nizamuʾd-Din
  Auliyaʾ who was denounced at ʿAlaʾuʾd-Din Khalji's court, was "aged over ninety" and was forced to
  Daulatabad (p. 201, folio 183) — **is chronologically incompatible with 1245, though that reasoning uses
  the archive's date and not the book's, since the book gives him no dates at all.** Chiniot is not named
  anywhere in the book — (not stated).

**Place-level attachments under ruling (a)**

*Every bullet below is a toponym the book names without attaching a shrine, tomb, saint, ʿurs or custodian
to it. Under ruling (a) of 21 September 2026 the toponym takes the id of every archive row in that place,
joined on `location_short`; the ids are printed in full on the head line of each bullet. **This material
is place-level. It is never the book describing any of these sites**, and an entry that uses it must say
so. Where the Pass 1 notes hedged these as "under interim convention (a) it takes no id" or "if the ruling
reverses, the rows are …", the hedge has been deleted and the mapping made.*

- **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi, bibi-pak-daman, darbar-abul-muali-qadri, data-darbar, ganj-e-inayat-sarkar, ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed, gurdwara-babay-nanki, gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore, gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan, gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang, gurdwara-chowmala-sahib, gurdwara-dera-sahib, gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji, gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore, gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh, gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian, jain-mandir-lahore, khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz, krishna-mandir-ravi-road, loh-temple-lava-temple, madho-lal-hussain, mazar-e-iqbal, peer-makki, samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh, shah-jamal, shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh, shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal, shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari, shrine-of-mian-mir, shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib, shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri, tahir-bandagi-qadri, tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak, valmik-mandir-naqi-road, wasif-ali-wasif** — **LAHORE, the archive's 35 rows.** What the book actually attaches to the bare name: an eleventh-century city hostile to its own saint — "a number of mystics and scholars who had settled in Lahore were strongly hostile to the views of its author", "Some Lahore sufis believed that gnosis emanated from inspiration", "a ‘captive among uncongenial folk’ in Lahore" (p. 131, folio 113); a route terminus, "one of the main routes from Multan to Lahore and Delhi" (p. 158, folio 140) and "Qazi Shu'aib, went to Lahore" (p. 157, folio 139); a capital, "Lahore had become the capital", with "the Khwaja reached Delhi from Lahore" and "His father, Ahmad, migrated from Lahore to Delhi" (pp. 135, 140, 145; folios 117, 122, 127); a city destroyed and a merchant colony — "the destruction of Lahore, by the Mongols in 1241" and "A group of Lahore merchants visited Gujarat" (pp. 241–242, folios 221–222); a city Babur conquered in 1523-24 (p. 367, folio 345); a compass bearing, "about forty miles south-west of Lahore" (p. 407, folio 383); and one named building that is not a shrine, "the Padishahi mosque at Lahore" (p. 339, folio 317, footnote 3), inside Goldziher on relic worship. **Chunk 001's Doubts records four further Lahore mentions in pp. 1–29 whose individual page references are not carried in the Pass 1 text; the conversion is recorded at chunk level (pp. 1–29) and a human must recover the pages.** **NOT converted: the two Lahore-as-imprint mentions — "published in Lahore in 1909" and "Oriental College Magazine, Lahore X/1" (pp. 300, 310; folios 278, 288), and the Lahore imprints throughout the bibliography (pp. 442, 446, 452, 454; folios 418, 422, 428, 430) — which are publication data, not places in the narrative.**
- **prahladpuri-temple, shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya, shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani, shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi, shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam, shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari, shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez, shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** — **MULTAN, the archive's 8 rows, and the most substantive place-level material in the book.** "Multan had become a dependency of the Fatimids of Egypt"; Mahmud's blow to Ismaʿili power there; "in 1175 Mu‘izzu'd-Din Muhammad bin Sam seized Multan"; "by the middle of the eleventh century, sufism had penetrated into the areas surrounding Multan" (p. 128, folio 110); "the news of Sultan Mahmud's conquest of Multan" (p. 129, folio 111). Later: the ʿulamaʾ of Multan refusing a fatwa (pp. 158–159, folios 140–141); a Malik "of Uch and Multan" (p. 172, folio 154); Qutbuʾd-Din Kashani's madrasa, "the Governor of Multan", "the Qazi of Multan", "the Multan fort" and the townfolk (pp. 209–210, folios 191–192); "governor of Multan" and "Maulana Husamu'd-Din of Multan" (pp. 187, 197; folios 169, 179); two bare mentions at pp. 285 and 287 (folios 265, 267); "he went to Multan and Uch" (p. 337, folio 315); "Seizing Multan and Delhi" (p. 335, folio 313); "visited Ajodhan and Multan" (p. 366, folio 344); "(at Delhi from Uch, near Multan)" (p. 258, folio 238); and the index headword "Multan, … 179--80, … 203 5, …" (p. 477, folio 453), **its number string damaged and not repaired.** **Two Multan mentions in pp. 1–29 are recorded by chunk 001 without page references and are converted at chunk level.** **NOT converted: "the Multani dialect" (pp. 350–351, folios 328–329), which is a language, and the nisba "Taju'd-Din Multani" (p. 240, folio 220), which is a private individual.**
- **shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari, shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht, tomb-of-baha-al-halim-uch-sharif, tomb-of-javindi-bibi, tomb-of-ustad-nuriya** — **UCH SHARIF, the archive's 5 rows, and the single most valuable place-level conversion in this book: a foundation legend for an archive town.** "the Shaikh founded a town later called Uch" (p. 129, folio 111), of Shaikh Safiuʾd-Din Kaziruni, who "ordered him to mount a camel and travel in whatever direction the animal led him; he was then to remain where the camel finally halted", the halt being "in the middle of a desert" (p. 129, folio 111) — Rizvi's verdict, "Although this story would appear mythical, it is typical of medieval tales relating to sufis." Also: "The Baba found a lonely mosque in Uch" (p. 158, folio 140); "a Malik of Uch and Multan" (p. 172, folio 154); "(at Delhi from Uch, near Multan)" (p. 258, folio 238); "he went to Multan and Uch. At Uch it is possible that he was formally initiated into the Suhrawardiyya order as is suggested by local legends" (p. 337, folio 315) — **a Suhrawardi initiation attached to the town itself**; the flying contest with the yogi "at Uch" (pp. 129–130, folios 111–112); the whole Uch section's statement that "all the stories dealing with three generations of these 'sufis in Uch recount conflicts with Hindu Yogis" (p. 304, folio 282); Uch as an economic statement in the Conclusion (p. 421, folio not stated); and the index headword "Uch, 110, 140, 154, 240, 267, 273, 277-82, 285, 291, 316, 320, 396" (p. 489, folio 465). **`tomb-of-javindi-bibi` and `tomb-of-ustad-nuriya` get nothing else at all: the book's own Uch section (pp. 299–304, folios 277–282) names no woman and no builder or craftsman — a substantive negative.**
- **dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee, gorakhnath-goraknath-temple, gurdwara-bhai-beba-singh, gurdwara-bhai-joga-singh, kali-bari-mandir, panj-tirath, rahman-baba-mausoleum-rehman-baba-shrine, shrine-of-akhund-darweza-baba, shrine-of-mian-umar-baba-chamkani, wadpagga-sharif** — **PESHAWAR, the archive's 10 rows.** "From the eleventh century the Nath yogis began to spread throughout northern India, and from their centre at Peshawar moved to all parts of Central Asia and Iran, at the same time influencing both qalandars and sufis" (p. 355, folio 333); and the Gorakhtari passage, "McLeod rightly locates the site in Peshawar" (p. 413, folio 389). **The Peshawar rows other than `gorakhnath-goraknath-temple` and `dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee` gain nothing but the place: Rahman Baba, Akhund Darweza, Mian Umar Baba, Panj Tirath, the Kali Bari and the two gurdwaras are named nowhere in the book** (not stated). **"Rahman, M.A., 202" (p. 482, folio 458) is a modern scholar and is NOT Rahman Baba.**
- **bhagnari-mandir, darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir, jhollay-lal-mandir, shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi, shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge, shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi, shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi, shri-varun-dev-mandir, shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi, shrine-of-pir-mangho, shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** — **KARACHI, the archive's 11 rows.** The Pass 1 notes for chunk 027 name a "Karachi" index headword among the bare toponyms withheld under the old convention (chunk 027, pp. 463–483, folios 439–459) **but do not give the headword's printed page or its number string in the extract available to me; the conversion is recorded at chunk level and a human must supply the page before anything is cited from it.** **Elsewhere Karachi appears only as a publisher's imprint (p. 375, folio 353), which is not a place in the narrative and is not converted.** The ruled count for Karachi is **11 rows**; the Pass 1 note says "the eight Karachi rows", which is a miscount of the join and has been corrected to the ruled list.
- **lal-shahbaz-qalandar, shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** — **SEHWAN SHARIF, the archive's 2 rows.** "Siwistan (Sehwan) in Sind" as a posting for Shaikh ʿArif (p. 172, folio 154); and, with an institution and an endowment attached to somebody else, "Sultan Muhammad bin Tughluq appointed him the Shaikhu'l-Islam and made him the head of a khangah [khanqah] in Siwistan (Sehwan) known as the Khanqah-i Muhammadi to which several villages were attached" (p. 299, folio 277). **That khanqah is Makhdum-i Jahaniyan's, not Lal Shahbaz's or Lakhi Shah Saddar's, and must never be read as either.** Index: "Sehwan, 153, 277, 306" and "Siwistan, 153, 277" (pp. 484, 486; folios 460, 462).
- **allo-mahar, gurdwara-babay-de-ber, jagannath-temple-sialkot, shahwala-teja-singh-mandir, shrine-of-imam-ali-ul-haq** — **SIALKOT, the archive's 5 rows.** The book names the town once, in Guru Nanak's itinerary (p. 409, folio 385). **Nothing else: no shrine, no temple, no saint, no date** (not stated).
- **gurdwara-balila-sahib-bal-lila-sahib, gurdwara-malji-sahib, gurdwara-panjvi-chati-patshahi, gurdwara-patti-sahib, gurdwara-sri-kiara-sahib, gurdwara-tambo-sahib, gurudwara-janam-asthan-nankana-sahib** — **NANKANA SAHIB, the archive's 7 rows.** "the village of Talwandi later known as Nankana Sahib, about forty miles south-west of Lahore, now part of Pakistan" (p. 407, folio 383), and the index headword named by chunk 027 among the withheld toponyms (chunk 027, pp. 463–483, folios 439–459) **whose printed page the extract does not give.** **The book names no gurdwara at Nankana Sahib** (not stated).
- **gurdwara-darbar-sahib-kartarpur** — **KARTARPUR, the archive's single row.** "Guru Nanak died at Kartarpur in 1539" (p. 418, folio 394), plus the index headword named by chunk 027 (pp. 463–483) **without a printed page in the extract.** **No building, no samadhi, no gurdwara** (not stated).
- **mazar-of-bulleh-shah** — **KASUR, the archive's single row.** "Qasur, thirty miles south-east of Lahore" (p. 28, folio 14) and "Qasur, south-east of Lahore" (p. 157, folio 139). **The book spells it "Qasur"; it attaches no shrine, saint or ʿurs to it** (not stated).
- **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib** — **CHAH MIRAN, the archive's single row.** "Zinjani was buried in Chah Miran, which is now a suburb of Lahore" (p. 130, folio 112) — **here the toponym carries a burial and is already mapped through the person** — and the index headword "Chah Miran, I11 [OCR?] [number unreadable]" (p. 463, folio 439).
- **gorakhnath-goraknath-temple** — **GOR KHATRI, the archive's single row.** "Gorakhattri" in the illustration list (p. 10, folio not stated); "Babur visiting Gorkhattri" as a plate caption (p. 392, folio not stated); "a place called Gorakhtari… either 'Gorkhattari' or perhaps 'Gorakhtari'" (p. 413, folio 389); and the index's "Gorakhtari, 389" / "Gorkhattari, 389" (p. 466, folio 442).
- **amb-temples-amb-sharif, bhagnari-mandir, chandragup-baba-chandragup, katas-raj-temples, shahwala-teja-singh-mandir, shiv-mandir-chiti-ghati, shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi, umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir** — **SHIVA, the archive's 8 rows, under the many-row-person rule of 22 September 2026.** The book names him as a theological principle throughout Chapter Six: "Isma‘ili propagandists evolved a belief for Hindu converts that ‘Ali was the tenth incarnation of Vishnu, that Adam was another aspect of Siva and that Muhammad was in fact Brahma" (p. 128, folio 110) — **immediately qualified by Rizvi, "This is, however, not necessarily representative of early Isma‘ili approaches to proselytization"**; "The region from the head to the navel is associated with Shiva, while that below the navel is connected with Shakti" (p. 161, folio 143); "The Adi Nath, the First Lord of the Naths, is the Shiva of the Hindus", "the esoteric doctrines of the Nath which the Lord Shiva was imparting to his spouse, Parvati", "born from the sweat of Shiva's breast" (p. 353, folio 331); "the trinity: Shiva, Matsyendranath and Gorakhnath" (p. 354, folio 332); "Brahma, Shiva, Parmatma, Parmeswara, the holiest names of the Nameless One" (p. 355, folio 333); "In Nath terminology the Absolute Spirit is called Shiva and His Unique Power is Shakti … Shiva is the father of the universe and Shakti is the mother", "The human body is the microcosm of the entire cosmic body of Shiva", "the plane of blissful union of Shakti and Shiva", "Para-Siva (Supreme Spirit)" (p. 356, folio 334); "the union of Shakti, the sun, and Shiva, the moon" (p. 358, folio 336); "the union between Shakti and Shiva" (p. 360, folio 338); "the Supreme Reality, identified as Shiva", "Shiva is diffused throughout the universe", "In one Shiva and Universe and Soul" (pp. 371–372, folios 349–350); "Mahesh" for Shiva and "he did not reject devotion to Shiva" (pp. 376, 377; folios 354, 355); Shiva and Shakti in Qutban (pp. 389–390, folios 367–368); "Brahma, Vishnu and the Shiva" as created beings in a Nanak hymn (p. 416, folio 392); and the index, "Shiva, 331-34, 337, 349-50, 355, 365, 368" and "Siva, 109" (p. 486, folio 462), with "Mahesh" (p. 474, folio 450). **Not one temple, site or act of worship at a place is attached to any of it; this is doctrine, and the eight rows are temples.** **"Shakti, 34, 337, 366, 368-69" (p. 485, folio 461) is the Nath and Tantric principle and is NOT the Shaktipeeth.**
- **krishna-mandir-kabari-bazar, krishna-mandir-ravi-road** — **KRISHNA, the archive's 2 rows, under the many-row-person rule.** Named on seven pages of Chapter Six, with a glossary entry to himself (pp. 376, 377, 378, 379, 380, 381, 382; folios 354, 355, 356, 357, 358, 359, 360), including "adored in the form of two of his ten incarnations, Rama and Krishna" (p. 376, folio 354) and "Chaitanya's emotional attachment to Krishna led to prolonged spells of ecstasy and epileptic fits" (p. 379, folio 357); also pp. 383, 384, 401 (folios 361, 362, 377) and the index (p. 473, folio 449). **Doctrine and devotion, never a temple** (not stated).
- **ram-mandir-saidpur-ram-kund-mandir** — **RAMA.** "adored in the form of two of his ten incarnations, Rama and Krishna" (p. 376, folio 354); "devotion to the incarnation of Vishnu in the form of Rama, and of his consort Sita" (p. 379, folio 357); "A Hindi scholar identified Pitambar Pir with the Hindu god, Rama", and Kabir's "Nirguna Rama" against "Ram Chandar" (pp. 398, 401; folios 374, 377); "Ram and Rahim were the same Being" (p. 412, folio 388); "traditional terms used by Hindus and Muslims to invoke God, such as Allah, Khuda, Sahib, Hari and Rama" (p. 415, folio 391); index "Rama" (p. 482, folio 458). **Rizvi presents "Hari" and "Rama" in Guru Nanak's usage as names for a single formless God, not as the deities of cult sites, and the Pass 1 worker judged these weak candidates — recorded with the conversion.**
- **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge** — **LAKSHMI-NARAYAN.** "Thou who art wedded to lady Lakshmi" (p. 378, folio 356), beside "the worship of Vishnu or Narayana" and "sang in praise of Narayana" (pp. 376, 377; folios 354, 355); "the holy Lord of Lakshmi" (p. 400, folio 376); index "Narayana" (p. 478, folio 454). **The one deity row this book satisfies on both halves of its name, and no temple is named** (not stated).
- **jagannath-temple-sialkot** — **JAGANNATH**, as one of Kabir's names for God (p. 401, folio 377) and as an index headword (p. 470, folio 446). **No temple, no Sialkot** (not stated).
- **shri-varun-dev-mandir** — **VARUNA**, named once inside a quoted Rigvedic hymn (p. 34, folio not stated) and once as an index headword, "Varuna, 18" (p. 490, folio 466). **One clause of one quoted hymn, and no temple, place or observance** (not stated). Note the false friend: "Lakshinkara, a legendary princess" (p. 353, folio 331) **is not Lakshmi.**
- **gori-temple-gori-jo-mandar, jain-mandir-lahore, nagarparkar-jain-temples-nagarparkar-cultural-landscape** — **THE JAIN TIRTHANKARAS.** "Parasnath, probably Parsava, the twenty-third Jain tirthankara" (p. 354, folio 332) — **the ordinal matches `gori-temple-gori-jo-mandar`'s principal figure exactly** — with "Nimnath, probably Nemi, the twenty-second Jain tirthankara" (p. 354, folio 332), "the naked Jaina Ksapanaka-Yogins were frauds" (p. 352, folio 330), and the index headwords "Tirthankara, 332" (p. 489, folio 465), "Parasnath, 332" (p. 480, folio 456) and "Jaina" (p. 470, folio 446). **In the index "Parasnath" stands in the run of Nath names at the same folio 332, where it reads as a Nath and not as a Tirthankara — two readings, and the index cannot choose between them; see Doubts.**

*Places the book names that take no archive row, and are therefore not converted: **Sind** and **the
Panjab** as provinces (pp. 61, 83, 99, 345, 342; folios 45, 67, 83, 323, 320) — a reversal would sweep
every Sindhi or Punjabi row at once on the strength of one clause; **Kashmir** (pp. 24, 196, 311–314, 411;
folios 10, 178, 289–292, 387), where the only archive row is `sharada-peeth` and **every Kashmir passage
in the book concerns Muslim Kashmir — Srinagar, Shihabuʾd-Din Pura and the Kali Mandir — with Sharda, the
Neelum valley and Saraswati named nowhere**; **Thatta** (p. 197, folio 179); **Hyderabad** as an index
headword whose city the index does not identify (chunk 027); and **Ajmer**, **Delhi**, **Hansi**,
**Bhakkar**, **Qandahar**, **Badaʾun**, **Lakhnauti**, **Kalpi**, **Kanauj**, **Pandua**, **Sunargaon**,
**Gulbarga**, **Thanesar** and the rest of the book's Indian geography, none of which is an archive
location. **Ajodhan** is the book's name for the seat of `shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar` and is treated
throughout as person-level evidence, because the book never equates Ajodhan with Pakpattan and the ruled
join is on `location_short`; nothing is lost, because that row is mapped on the person in every chunk
where Ajodhan occurs.*

### Further mappings identified in the cross-cutting material, not merged into the subsections above

*Recorded by the workers who held the non-archive extracts. Each id below was checked against `shrine_index.tsv`; the material behind it sits in the section the worker names. Left as a list rather than folded in, so that nothing is asserted twice and a human can see which mappings arrived late.*

*Listed for the coordinator to pass on, not mapped here. The first block is persons who are the
principal figure of an archive row and who appear in my extract; under the ruling of 18 September
2026 they take that row's id even where Rizvi names them only as an author, a literary reference or
a passing mention, so they belong in the archive section and not in mine. The second block is
place-names that turned out to be an archive row's location: under ruling (a) of 21 September 2026 a
bare toponym takes the id of every archive row in that place, so these too belong in the archive
section. I have made no mapping and asserted no identification; ids are given only so the
coordinator can find the rows, and each id list is kept on one physical line.*

### Persons

- **Hazrat Ali ibn Usman al-Hujwiri (Data Ganj Bakhsh)** — id: **data-darbar** — named twice in my
  extract, both times as an author or an explainer: "Drawing upon Tirmizi's thesis of sainthood",
  of Hujwiri (p. 115, folio 97), and "As Hujwiri explained at great length, malamat (blame) was the
  distinctive practice of the Qassaris who violated religious laws and behaved in an offensive
  manner so that people would not bother them." (p. 324, folio 302). The Pass 1 worker of chunk 019
  flagged the second explicitly: "Hujwiri is named here only as an explainer of malamat … since he
  is `data-darbar`'s principal figure."
- **Sultan Qutb ud-Din Aibak** — id: **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak** — named twice in my extract, as a
  ruler and not as a saint: Muʿizzuʾd-Din "had conquered Delhi and, departing for Ghazni, left his
  favourite slave, Qutbu'd-Din Aibak" (p. 139, folio 121), and Saiyid Husain Mashhadi was the
  "darogha (military governor)" of Ajmer "appointed by Qutbu'd-Din" (p. 140, folio 122). **Twelve
  other men named Qutbuʾd-Din appear in this book and none of them is him; see Traps.**
- **Shaikhuʾl-Islam Shaikh Ruknuʾd-Din Multani** — id: **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam** — **flagged as
  a query, not asserted.** The chunk 011 worker called this "the chunk's one serious unmade archive
  mapping": "Shaikhu'l-Islam Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din Multani — 'led Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din's funeral prayer'
  (p. 181, folio 163). That is the whole of what the book says about him here: no personal name
  beyond 'Ruknu'd-Din', no dates, no order, no shrine — all (not stated)." The chunk 014 worker
  treats a "Multan Shaikh Ruknuʾd-Din" as already mapped in its own archive section and distinguishes
  him from the Firdawsi Khwaja Ruknuʾd-Din (p. 248, folio 228); the endowment sentence "The Sultan
  awarded Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din one hundred villages for the upkeep of his khangah [OCR?] [khanqah]"
  (p. 233, folio 213) belongs with him. **The coordinator should decide whether the p. 181 (folio
  163) mention joins the rest; I have not joined it, and the collision with the Uch Saiyid Ruknuʾd-Din
  Abuʾl-Fath (p. 304, folio 282) is a separate man and must not be swept in.**
- **Sayyid Jalaluddin Surkh-Posh Bukhari** — id: **shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari** — named in my extract only as a
  father: "Saiyid Ahmad Kabir — son of Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din Surkh, father and pir of both Makhdum-i
  Jahaniyan and Raju Qattal" (pp. 299, 303; folios 277, 281). Thin evidence, but the 18 September
  ruling covers a passing mention.
- **Guru Nanak** — ids: **gurdwara-babay-de-ber** **gurdwara-balila-sahib-bal-lila-sahib** **gurdwara-chakki-sahib** **gurdwara-choa-sahib** **gurdwara-darbar-sahib-kartarpur** **gurdwara-khoohi-bhai-lalo-bhai-lalo-di-khooi** **gurdwara-malji-sahib** **gurdwara-panja-sahib** **gurdwara-patti-sahib** **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur** **gurdwara-rori-sahib** **gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib** **gurdwara-sacha-sauda** **gurdwara-sahib-saidpur-guru-nanak-dev-ji** **gurdwara-sri-kiara-sahib** **gurdwara-sri-tilganji-sahib** **gurdwara-tambo-sahib** **gurudwara-janam-asthan-nankana-sahib** — eighteen rows, under the many-row rule of 22 September 2026. The
  Pass 1 workers held him under Doubts because of the count; the ruling has since landed. Page
  references, merged: "The Janam Sakhis includes a number of slokas which Guru Nanak and his
  successors composed to support, rather than dispute, the ideas contained in Baba Farid's slokas"
  and "Shaikh Ibrahim, a successor of Baba Farid, whom Guru Nanak visited at Ajodhan" (p. 351, folio
  329); "The same is true of the verses written by Guru Nanak's successors" (p. 353, folio 331);
  Kabir "was the movement's earliest known exponent in medieval India which was to reach a climax
  with Guru Nanak" (p. 397, folio 373); "Guru Nanak was born in 1469 in the village of Talwandi later
  known as Nankana Sahib, about forty miles south-west of Lahore, now part of Pakistan" (p. 407,
  folio 383); Shaikh Sajjan and the Panipat discussions, and Jai Ram at Sultanpur (p. 409, folio
  385); Saiyidpur/Amanabad (p. 410, folio 386); "Guru Nanak seems to have visited Kashmir" and the
  Ceylon journey (p. 411, folio 387); Mount Sumeru or Maru (pp. 411–412, folios 387–388); the Baghdad
  journey to "the centre of the Qadiri order of Pir-i Dastgir Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Qadir Jilani" and
  Bahlul Dana (p. 413, folio 389); the appointment of Angad before his death (p. 418, folio 394);
  "Guru Nanak's visit to Ajodhan, Multan and Panipat" (p. 420, folio 396). **Seven bibliography
  entries make him a book's subject** — "Grewal, J.S., … Guru Nanak in history, Chandigarh, 1969."
  (p. 451, folio 427); "Johar, S.S., Guru Nanak, Jullundur, 1969." (p. 452, folio 428); "Kohli,
  S.S., … Philosophy of Guru Nanak, Delhi, 1969." (p. 452, folio 428); "McLeod, W., Guru Nanak and
  the Sikh religion, Oxford, 1968." (p. 453, folio 429); "Narain Singh, Guru Nanak, revised,
  Amritsar, 1965." (p. 454, folio 430); "Talib, G.S., Guru Nanak, Delhi, 1969." (p. 455, folio 431);
  "Trilochan Singh, Guru Nanak's religion, Delhi, 1968." (p. 455, folio 431).
- **Guru Arjan Dev** — ids: **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore** **gurdwara-dera-sahib** **gurdwara-panjvi-chati-patshahi** — "the Guru Garanth [Guru Granth], compiled by the fifth Sikh Guru,
  Arjan, in 1604" (p. 350, folio 328), and the book's own contradictory numbering, "As the First Guru
  he was known as Guru Angad (1539-52)" against "The fifth Guru, Arjan Dev" (p. 419, folio 395) —
  **the contradiction is the book's and both forms stand.**
- **Guru Gorakhnath** — ids: **gorakhnath-goraknath-temple** **tilla-jogian** — two rows, under the
  many-row rule of 22 September 2026. "Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus … identify [sic] sufi beliefs based on
  the Wahdat al-Wujud with the philosophy and practices of Gorakhnath" (p. 358, folio 336);
  "Gahininath, initiated by Gorakhnath" (p. 354, folio 332); "To yogis the Prophet Muhammad was a
  pupil and disciple of Gorakhnath but they concealed this belief for fear of Muslim retribution"
  (p. 376, folio 354); "some verses with slight variations are included in Nath poetry as well as in
  that of Kabir and Gorakhnath" (p. 358, folio 336). **The short form "Gorakh" is used twice more** —
  "Ja'isi says that a yogi becomes a Siddha only after meeting Gorakh, a counterpart of the Mahdi.
  Both Gorakh and Siddhas exhibit the distinctive features of the Mahdi" (p. 393, folio 369), and
  "he believed they had failed to inherit Gorakh's real absorption with the", cut off at the chunk
  boundary (p. 401, folio 377) — **and the bibliography prints the full form, "Raghaw, S.,
  Gorakhnath aur unka yug, Delhi, 1963." and "Banerjea, A.K., Philosophy of Gorakhnath, Gorakhpur,
  n.d." (pp. 449, 450; folios 425, 426), which removes chunk 023's orthographic doubt.**
- **Krishna** — ids: **krishna-mandir-kabari-bazar** **krishna-mandir-ravi-road** — two rows, under
  the many-row rule. "Braj and Gokul (regions of Mathura associated with Krishna's life)", "Jasodha
  (Yashoda, mother of Krishna)" (pp. 383–384, folios 361–362); "Krishna" in Kabir's list of names

### Toponyms that are archive locations under ruling (a)

*Each of these stands in my source as a bare toponym under the superseded interim convention. Under
ruling (a) each takes the id of every archive row in that place, and the join is the coordinator's
to make from `toponym_map.txt`, not mine. I give the merged page references only.*

- **Lahore** — (p. 16, folio 2, the Shirani collection); (p. 23, folio 9, "traded hetween"[OCR?]
  [between] "Kirman in Iran and Lahore" and "business trips to Lahore via Ajodhan"); (p. 28, folio
  14, "thirty miles south-east of Lahore"); (p. 29, folio 15, "Mufti Muhammad Ghulam Sarwar of
  Lahore"); (p. 130, folio 112, ascetic exercises "in Lahore at the Shaikh's tomb", and "the
  annexation of the Panjab by Mahmud of Ghazna … prompted many sufis to settle in the area");
  (p. 139, folio 121, "From there he reached Lahore and stayed near the tombs of Shaikh Husain
  Zanjani and Shaikh 'Ali Hujwiri"); (p. 140, folio 122, "after Mu'izzu'd-Din's death, Lahore had
  become the capital"); (p. 145, folio 127, "His father, Ahmad, migrated from Lahore to Delhi");
  (p. 277, folio 257, the nisba "Shaikh 'Ala'u'l-Haqq bin As'ad Lahori"); (p. 322, folio 300, "A
  frequent visitor to the Mughal court in Lahore"); (p. 339, folio 317); (p. 367, folio 345,
  "Babur's victory over Dipalpur and Lahore in 1523-24"); (p. 407, folio 383, "about forty miles
  south-west of Lahore"); and the index headword "Lahore, 2, 9, 13, 104, 111-13, 117-18, 121-22, 139,
  221-22, 295, 306, 328, 345, 350, 383" (p. 473, folio 449), **a fifteen-item folio run, the longest
  Pakistani toponym entry in the index, cited to the PDF page the index is printed on.** Also in the
  bare-toponym lists of chunks 007, 009, 014 and 018 (pp. 118–133, 148–163, 233–248, 297–314; folios
  100–115, 130–145, 213–228, 275–292). **The chunk 019 worker's caution stands and should travel with
  the mapping: "the archive holds some twenty Lahore rows and nothing in the passage selects among
  them" (p. 322, folio 300).** **Lahore as a publication imprint only — pp. 128, 317, 328, 350, 372,
  449, 452, 455 (folios 110, 295, 306, 328, 350, 425, 428, 431) — is bibliographic data, not a
  toponym.**
- **Multan** — (p. 23, folio 9, "an officer in the Multan mint" and the father-in-law's city);
  (pp. 127–130, folios 109–112, the Ismaʿili dependency of the Fatimids 977–985, Mahmud's invasions
  1004–1011, Muʿizzuʾd-Din's seizure in 1175, and "it was the news of Sultan Mahmud's conquest of
  Multan which prompted sufis to advise their talented and adventurous disciples to settle in that

  154, 155, 158); (pp. 187, 197; folios 169, 179); (pp. 209, 211; folios 191, 193); (pp. 215–232,
  folios 197–212, throughout the ʿIraqi and Amir Husain material); (pp. 233–248, folios 213–228);
  (p. 258, folio 238, "(at Delhi from Uch, near Multan)"); (pp. 285, 287; folios 265, 267, "Shaikh
  Hasan's father, Tahir, migrated from Multan" and "taking the route through Multan and Uch to
  India"); (pp. 297–314, folios 275–292); (pp. 335, 337; folios 313, 315); (p. 366, folio 344,
  ʿAbduʾl-Quddus "visited Ajodhan and Multan"); (p. 420, folio 396); and the index headword "Multan,

- **Uch** — (p. 129, folio 111, **the foundation legend**: "the Shaikh founded a town later called

- **Gorakhattri / Gorkhattri / Gorakhtari (Gor Khatri), Peshawar** — the plate caption, twice: "4.

  sentence is the best thing in the book for it.**
- **Karachi** — index headword "Karachi, 202" (p. 471, folio 447), cited to the PDF page the index is
  printed on. **Everywhere else in the book Karachi is a publication imprint only** (pp. 101, 215,

- **Sialkot** — (p. 409, folio 385, "Babur invaded a third time over the Panjab in 926/1520 reaching

- **Talwandi** — (p. 407, folio 383, as above); index headword "Talwandi, 383" (p. 489, folio 465) —

- **Kartarpur** — index headword "Kartarpur, 394" (p. 471, folio 447), one folio, cited to the PDF

- **Saiyidpur (Amanabad / Eminabad)** — (p. 410, folio 386, "Saiyidpur (Amanabad)"); index headwords

- **Ajodhan** — **flagged as a query.** The archive's row for Baba Farid gives its location as

- **The province-level names — Sind, the Panjab and Kashmir** — **flagged as queries, because ruling

## Province-level material

*Swept from the raw chunks (`chunk_001`–`chunk_028`, `tesseract` route) under ruling (c). Only
statements about a province or region in general are kept. Statements really about one named town,
site or person stay with that toponym or figure and are listed in the sweep notes. Statements about
the Delhi Sultanate as a whole, or about north India as a whole, go nowhere. The book is a history of
the Sultanate and early Mughal period, so almost every "Panjab" and "Sind" below is the historical
region as the book uses the word, never the modern province. The period is given each time in the
book's own terms, and no boundary history is added. Folios follow the book's rule as its takeaways
header states it: no offset is stated anywhere, so every folio below was read off that page's own
running header; where a page carries none, it is "folio not stated". Registers: **book's historical
prose**, **author's argument** (Rizvi in his own polemical voice), and **quoted source** (named).
Every bullet is province-level: weigh accordingly.*

### province: punjab

- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Ghaznawid rule, "Yamain al-Dawla Mahmud (388/998-421/1030)" and "Bahram Shah (1118-52)" as printed; book's historical prose): Mahmud "established Ghaznawid rule over Khurasan, Afghanistan and the Panjab"; after two defeats by 'Ala'u'd-Din of Ghur, "Bahram Shah was forced to flee to the Panjab" (p. 83, folio 67).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Mahmud of Ghazna's annexation and after, with Hujwiri reaching Lahore "in c. 1035, while Sultan Mas'ud I (1031-41) reigned in Ghazna"; book's historical prose): "the annexation of the Panjab by Mahmud of Ghazna and its incorporation into his empire prompted many sufis to settle in the area"; Khattali "ordered his disciple, Shaikh Husain Zinjani to move to the Panjab", and later sent Hujwiri after him (p. 130, folio 112).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "the middle of the twelfth century", after "'Ala'u'd-Din Husain of Ghur in 1150-51, followed by the Ghuzz invasion six years later"; book's historical prose): Baba Farid's ancestors "came from Kabul, but migrated to the Panjab in the middle of the twelfth century" (p. 156, folio 138); the destruction of Ghazna and the Ghuzz invasion "drove a large number of people from that region to the Panjab for safety"; the book names "The Ghaznawid Sultan of the Panjab" as the one who made Farid's father Qazi of Kahtwal (p. 157, folio 139).
- (book's word: "the southern part of the Panjab"; refers to: "1175", as printed; book's historical prose): "A hundred and fifty years later in 1175 Mu'izzu'd-Din Muhammad bin Sam seized Multan, establishing firm control and Sunni rule over the southern part of the Panjab" (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: after Jalalu'd-Din's defeat "in November 1221" and Chingiz Khan's march back west; book's historical prose): Chingiz Khan's "absence enabled Itutmish [OCR?] [Iltutmish] to consolidate power in the Panjab, seven years later routing from Sind his rival, Nasiru'd-Din Qubacha" (p. 152, folio 134).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "previous years" before Muhammad bin Tughluq's scheme "attempted in 1327"; book's historical prose): the Sultan raised "the slogan of jihad against the Mongols, who in previous years had devastated the Panjab and the region around Delhi" (p. 193, folio 175).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "Timur's invasion in 1398-99", as printed; book's historical prose): "Timur's invasion in 1398-99 devastated both the Panjab and Delhi" (p. 290, folio 270).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: the time of Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din Bukhari, Makhdum-i Jahaniyan, born "15 Sha'ban 707/9 February 1308", and of "Sultan Firuz Shah Tughlug" [OCR?] [Tughluq]; book's historical prose): the Suhrawardiyya order "spread from Uch to Gujarat, the Panjab and Kashmir even extending its influence to the Delhi of Sultan Firuz Shah Tughlug [OCR?] [Tughluq]" (p. 299, folio 277).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the time of "Mongol domination of Central Asia and Iran", Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya and Baba Farid; book's historical prose): Qalandariyyas and Jawaliqis passed through Multan, where they "often came into conflict with Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya"; "From the Punjab, the displeased qalandars moved to Delhi creating scenes wherever they saw a khanqah. Some went as far east as Bengal" (p. 325, folio 303).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "the fifteenth century", with "The Mongol invasions between the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries" behind it; book's historical prose): "In the fifteenth century, the Panjab enjoyed a peaceful period due to Afghan rule. The Mongol invasions between the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries had devastated portions of the Panjab and the Afghans had successfully contributed to its re-settlement and urbanization by founding many new towns and promoting trade and commerce" (p. 408, folio 384).
- (book's word: "the Panjab", "west Panjab"; refers to: "About 1520" and Babur's third invasion "in 926/1520", as printed; book's historical prose): Guru Nanak returned from eastern India "only to find the Panjab plunged into a number of significant political crises"; Babur "started unsuccessful negotiations with Ibrahim to annex west Panjab to Kabul. After two minor raids around the region between the Indus and Jhelum, Babur invaded a third time over the Panjab in 926/1520 reaching as far as Sialkot"; a footnote says Daulat Khan "Gradually he also became the governor of the Panjab and in [1523 [OCR?] invited Babur to invade India" (p. 409, folio 385).
- (book's word: "Panjabi", "the Panjab"; refers to: "Guru Nanak's period"; quoted source, W.H. McLeod, *Guru Nanak and the Sikh religion*, quoted so that Rizvi can reject it): "the Panjabi sufism of Guru Nanak's period had evidently departed radically from the classical pattern of Arab and Persian Sufism"; most Muslims Nanak met "would, however, represent in varying degrees the blend of modified orthodoxy and debased Sufism which was dominant … in the Muslim community of the Panjab during this period" (pp. 419–420, folios 395–396).
- (book's word: "Panjabis", "the Panjab"; refers to: "the time of Guru Nanak"; author's argument, against McLeod): "all literate Panjabis, both Hindus and Muslims, at the time of Guru Nanak were conversant with" 'Attar, Rumi and Sa'di; McLeod "seems to be unaware of Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus Gangohi who lived in Shahabad, his disciple, Shaikh Jalal of Thaneswar, and the Qadiris who had made the Panjab the main centre of their activities" (p. 420, folio 396).

### province: sindh

- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the time of the Umayyad Caliph al-Walid I (705-15)", with the conquest "between 711 and 712", as printed; book's historical prose): Rizvi calls it "a myth" that the conquest of Sind began as a punitive expedition against "the ruling Raja Dahir": "In reality, the conquest of Transoxiana and Sind was initiated by Hajjaj, Walid's governor in Iraq, and was one aspect of his expansionist policy. In 710 Muhammad bin Qasim al-Saqafi began his advance through Makran and Baluchistan with the conquest of Sind between 711 and 712" (p. 127, folio 109).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: the early Arab conquest and "the eighth century", as printed; book's historical prose): the spread of sufism seen in Transoxiana and "Khurasen" [OCR?] [Khurasan] is not matched here: "no such developments in Sind are recorded"; "Early Arab conquerors settled their families in large numbers in the various towns of Sind"; Brahmans in high office converted "to retain their positions", and Buddhists "extremely hostile to Brahman domination" converted too; "Hindu and Buddhist scholars from Sind were sent to the 'Abbasid court", and "In the eighth century Abu Ma''shar¢ [OCR?] Najih (d. 170/787) a scholar of Hadis, and the poet Abu'l-'Ata> [OCR?] (died after 158/774) were leading literary figures from Sind" (p. 127, folio 109).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "the tenth century", the Fatimid Caliphate "from 909 to 1171", and "Between 977 and 985"; book's historical prose): "Lack of information about sufis in Sind may be ascribed to two factors": land routes "between Khurasan, Transoxiana and Sind were slow and arduous", and the rise of sufism coincided with the Isma'ili Fatimid Caliphate; "Mansura, the capital of Sind, ruled by the Sumira dynasty had also accepted the over-lordship of the Egyptian Caliphs", so sufism "was naturally cut, from close relations with other areas" (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "the Lower Sind", "the Upper Sind"; refers to: "until the middle of the fourteenth century" and "After the end of the twelfth century", as printed; book's historical prose): "The Sumira dynasty continued to rule the Lower Sind until the middle of the fourteenth century. After the end of the twelfth century, the Upper Sind came under the domination of Sunni rule" (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "Sind"; refers to: "seven years" after "November 1221", and "the annexation of Multan and Sind by Iltutmish in 1228", as printed; book's historical prose): Iltutmish ("Itutmish" [OCR?] at p. 152), "seven years later routing from Sind his rival, Nasiru'd-Din Qubacha" (p. 152, folio 134); Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya "openly sided with Sultan Shamsu'd-Din Iltutmish when he wished to add Multan and Sind to the Delhi Sultanate" (p. 209, folio printed "19]" [OCR?] [191]); "After the annexation of Multan and Sind by Iltutmish in 1228, relations between the Sultan of Delhi and the Shaikh became more intimate" (p. 210, folio 192).
- (book's word: "Sind", "the lower Sind"; refers to: the time of Makhdum-i Jahaniyan and Sultan Firuz, with the Thatta campaign "In 763/1362"; book's historical prose, reporting belief): the Saiyid's "assistance was also sought by the governors of Sind"; "It is believed that the Sumirahs of the lower Sind who were Isma'ilis, adopted Sunnism under the influence of the Saiyid. A Jat chief of Sind, later named 'Abdu'llah, is also said to have embraced Islam through a supernatural feat performed by Makhdum Jahaniyan" (p. 301, folio 279).

### province: balochistan

- (book's word: "Baluchistan"; refers to: "In 710", as printed; book's historical prose): "In 710 Muhammad bin Qasim al-Saqafi began his advance through Makran and Baluchistan with the conquest of Sind between 711 and 712" (p. 127, folio 109).

### not attached

- "Kashmir" / "the valley" / "Kashmiri(s)": the whole "Kashmir" section of Chapter Five (pp. 311–321, folios 289–299 on the legible headers) covers the Shah Mir dynasty "established in Kashmir in 1339", Muhammad bin Qasim's proposed invasion "in 713", Mahmud's failed attempts "Between 1015 and 1021" that "plundered the southern part of the valley", the Suhrawardi and Kubrawiyya migrations, Mir Saiyid 'Ali Hamadani, the Chaks, and Akbar's annexation "in 1586". The same unit appears in Lalla and the Rishi order (pp. 371–373, folios 349–351), Muhammad bin Tughluq's order to a Chishti "to go to Kashmir and preach Islam in the temples there" (p. 196, folio 178), "the Kubrawiyya and of other orders in Kashmir" (p. 423, folio 399), and Guru Nanak's visit to "Kashmir" (p. 411, folio 387). Reason: it is an unspecified Kashmir that the book centres on "the valley" and Srinagar, and the book never names the Pakistani part.
- "Punjabi families" (Babur's "victory over Dipalpur and Lahore in 1523-24", as printed): "A lot of Punjabi families moved to safer areas", and among them was Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus, who went from Shahabad to "Gangoh in the Saharanpur district of U.P." (p. 367, folio 345). Reason: the book frames this as the danger to "regions around Delhi", and its only named emigrant leaves Shahabad for U.P., so the statement is not placed in the Pakistani part.

*Sweep notes:* Hit counts, as matching lines in the 28 `.txt` chunks (a line holding two terms is counted once, under the first): "Panjab" 29, "Punjab" 4, "Panjabi(s)" 7, "Punjabi" 3, "Sind" 32, "Baluchistan" 2, "Frontier" 1 (a title only: A.H. Dani, *Peshawar, historic city of the Frontier*, p. 413), "Kashmir"/"Kashmiri(s)" about 112. There were 0 hits for Sindh, Sindhi, Balochistan, Baluchi, Pakhtunkhwa, NWFP, "Multan province" or *suba*. The book never uses "suba" or any other boundary-marking term for these units, except "Lower Sind"/"Upper Sind", "the southern part of the Panjab" and "west Panjab", which are carried as printed. Many hits came from the bibliography and index (chunks 025–028: *Tarikh-i Sind*, *Punjab Castes*, *Kashmir under the Sultans*, "Panjab University" manuscripts, the index entries "Panjab", "Sind", "Baluchistan, 108") and were skipped, as was the illustration caption "Sind wa Hind (India)" (p. 10). Excluded as town, site or person statements (toponyms, ruling a): Arberry's view that Abu 'Ali Sindi's "Sind was a village in Khurasan and not the province of Sind in modern Pakistan" (p. 61, folio 45), which is about one man's nisba; Rumi's "in the Sindians the idiom of Sind is praiseworthy" (p. 99, folio 83), which is verse about speech; "Multan in the southern Panjab" and "The Raja of Asifan, in the Panjab" (p. 127, folio 109); Kahtwal, "a town in Multan" (p. 157); memorials to Shaikh 'Ala'u'd-Din "in Ajodhan, Dipalpur and in the hills near Kashmir" (p. 167, folio 149); Shaikh 'Arif deputed "to Siwistan (Sehwan) in Sind" (p. 171); Zakariyya's ancestors who "came to Sind with the army of Muhammad bin Qasim", a claim Rizvi footnotes as "not necessarily authentic" (p. 208, folio not stated, chapter opening); "the governor of Sind" accusing Shaikh Hud (p. 234, folio 214); "Jun, a town in Sind" (p. 310); Lal Shahbaz "a native of Sehwan in the Sind" and "his tomb in Sind" (p. 328, folio 306); Baba Ratan "from Tabarhinda or Bhatinda in the Panjab" (p. 342), a person, and a town the book does not place in Pakistan; Hallaj's "visit to Sind" (p. 345); Guru Nanak "back in the Panjab" by 1528 and "remained in the Panjab, occasionally visiting Ajodhan, Multan" (pp. 412–413, folios 388–389), which are itinerary statements about him; Ghulam Sarwar's library "dispensed amongst a large number of Panjabi book lovers" (p. 29, folio 15), which is about one library. Sent nowhere as north-India-wide: "The Naths and the Siddhas spread from the Panjab to Bengal" (p. 376, folio 354) and the bhaktas "in the Hindi and Panjabi speaking regions of northern India" (p. 396). Language-only hits were not used (Gorakhnath's and Charpati's "verses in Panjabi", pp. 353–354). Flags for a human: the folio on p. 209 prints as "19]" and is read [191] from p. 210's "192"; "Itutmish" (p. 152) is read [Iltutmish] from "Iltutmish" on pp. 209–210; "[1523" (p. 409) opens with a bracket the OCR introduced; "Abu Ma''shar¢" and "Abu'l-'Ata>" (p. 127) carry stray marks from footnote callouts; the dynasty is printed "Sumira" (p. 128) and "Sumirahs" (p. 301), both kept as printed. Every bullet above is province-level, weigh accordingly; the join to rows is by the `province` tag.

## Cross-cutting material

*This is the practices, institutions and economy of the book merged from all 28 chunks, followed by
the author's arguments and interpretations merged and deduplicated. Almost none of it is about a
shrine in present-day Pakistan: the book's geography is Khurasan, Iraq, Iran, Delhi, Ajmer, Nagaur,
Bihar, Bengal, Gujarat, Kashmir and the Deccan, and its chronology stops at 1600 with the Qadiri and
Naqshbandi orders held over for volume II (pp. 15–16, folios not stated / 2). It is kept in full
because it is the fullest account in this corpus of what a khanqah was, who paid for it, who
inherited it, how a grave became an institution and what the state did about it — which is the model
against which every row in the archive has to be read. Where a bullet is firmly about one archive
row, that row's id heads it in bold. Place-level and many-row attachments are not made here; they
belong to `## Shrines in the archive this book informs`.*


### A. The institutions named: khanqah, jamaʿat-khana, ribat, takiya, langar

- **The book's own glosses.** *Khanqah* = "monastery", *madrasa* = "seminary": "the principal mission of his later
  years was to reconcile the life of the madrasa, or seminary, to that of the khanqah or monastery" (p. 80, folio 64).
  *Ribat* = "hospices" (p. 54, folio 38; p. 33, folio 17). *Takiya* a straight synonym: "constructed a takiya
  (khangah) [OCR?] [khanqah]" (p. 219, folio 201). *Langar* = "An alms house for the distribution of free food"
  (p. 181, folio 163, footnote 3). *Zanbil* = "Literally a basket made of palm leaves, technically a bag hung across
  the shoulder to collect food obtained by begging locally" (p. 91, folio 75 corrected, footnote).
- **Khanqah and jamaʿat-khana distinguished, order by order.** "Jama'at-khanas were an integral part of Chishti
  khangahs [OCR?] [khanqahs] and were designed to provide hostel accommodation for a large number of dervishes.
  Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din defined a khangah [khanqah] as a place of worship and prayer but in reality the purpose of
  jama'at-khanas was identical. The Chishtis, however, preferred to call their hostels jama'at-khanas, as they were
  generally thatched assembly halls… In Chishti mystic literature both words, khangah [khanqah] and jama'at-khana,
  are interchangeable. **In contrast, the Suhrawardis built impressive khanqahs**" (p. 236, folio 216). Neither the
  thatched halls nor the impressive khanqahs are dated, measured or located — (not stated).
- **The baseline four components of an ordinary khanqah.** "all khanqahs were not built on such a grand scale; a few
  cells and a jama'at-khana, plus a mosque and a lavatory, were generally their main components" (p. 91, folio 75
  corrected).
- **The fullest khanqah plan in the book.** Dust Dada "proceeded to build a khanqah with a big covered platform, a
  jama'at-khana (assembly hall), kitchen and lavatory. Other buildings in the complex were a large arcaded mosque and
  a number of cells"; a second round of caravan money "enabled him to complete the khanqah by adding a harmmmam
  [OCR?] [hammam] (bath), more rooms and assembly halls. **Later Dust Dada erected a bazaar of shops and a
  caravanserai in front of the khanqah's gateway**" (p. 91, folio 75 corrected). Rizvi's claim about the record:
  "This is the first detailed description of a khanqah complex which is available at the present time" (p. 91, folio
  75 corrected) — **a claim about the state of scholarship in 1978, to be attributed to him as such.**
- **The fullest jamaʿat-khana description, with the author's complaint that the sources will not describe it.** "Sufi
  sources frequently mention the Shaikh's jama'at-khana, but fail to give a detailed description… A banyan tree was
  the main feature of the complex. The jama'at-khana itself was a pillared hall, with a few rooms on the first floor,
  and an open space in front. Its court-yard was surrounded by four walls and access was through a vestibule. On both
  sides of the hall there were small rooms which the Shaikh used for his midday rest. A few other small rooms were
  built near the jama'at-khana to store grain for distribution to the people, and there was also a kitchen, where
  food was cooked for the poor and visitors" (p. 181, folio 163); its materials and decay, "The pillars and walls
  were made of mud and the roofs were covered with logs and earth. The building was uncomfortable for' its occupants
  in all seasons… its buildings were expanded in a sprawling, unplanned way" (p. 181, folio 163).
- **A far poorer one, at Ajodhan.** "The jama'at-khana consisted only of a thatched hall, used for communal living and
  a separate cell was for the Shaikh's meditation"; it "contained not a single piece of furniture, every member sat
  and slept on the floor. On special occasions a bed was provided for a new visitor" (p. 159, folio 141).
- **The interior of a deserted ribat — the only such description.** "a ribat-i kuhan, an old deserted ribat" (p. 85,
  folio 69), its parts a gate that shuts, a roof one can go up on, "a chapel which was in the ribat" with a door and
  a "chapel window", "a stick lying on the floor" with "a rope fastened to it", and "a pit that was at the corner of
  the chapel" (pp. 85–86, folios 69–70).
- **Spaces assigned by age, and the facilities a novice cleans.** "In a khangah [khanqah], the young should live
  communally in the jama'at-khana, allowing older sufis to reside privately in cells" (p. 109, folio 91); "he would
  clean their cells, lavatories and privies" (p. 86, folio 70).
- **A khanqah with granaries, restricted access and merchant funding (p. 209, folio 191), and grain and silver moving
  from it to a provincial governor (p. 210, folio 192)** — **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya** material, quoted in full
  in that row's subsection.
- **A khanqah described by its site alone.** "The khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] itself was situated near a cave" (p. 337,
  folio 315). No building description, no endowment, no inmates counted — (not stated).
- **The oldest khanqah foundation in the book, and the stated reason for the building type.** "Before his death at the
  end of the eighth century, a khanqah (sufi monastry) [OCR?] [monastery] was founded in Ramla, near Jerusalem, by a
  Christian dignitary" (p. 47, folio 31); the patron "later learned that there was no resting place for such
  dervishes so he built a khanqah for them" (p. 47, folio 31, footnote 2). No name, cost, endowment or later history
  — (not stated).
- **Khanqahs admitted to be an innovation, by their own theorist.** "Suhrawardi admitted that the establishment of
  khanqahs was an innovation, but saw in them the germs of the suffa life at the time of Muhammad" (p. 109, folio 91);
  "On etymological grounds he rejects the idea that the word was derived from suffa but accepts the fact that the
  life of Muhammad's companions at the Medina mosque, 'the People of the Verandah,' resembled those of later sufis"
  (p. 104, folio 88).
- **The three stated advantages of an established khanqah.** "Firstly, they offered board and lodging to sufis who
  were often without financial resources of any kind. Secondly, the corporate life of a khangah [OCR?] [khanqah]
  provided an opportunity for individual members who shared a close relationship to exchange views and experiences.
  Finally, the propinquity of life in a khangah [khanqah] provided for a healthy rivalry between sufis in the
  perfection of their morals and conduct" (p. 109, folio 91).
- **Three grades of resident, the officers, and the sleeping rank.** Novices "assigned to such duties as waiting on
  others"; a second group who "learnt the social ethics of sufism"; and "aged sufis, generally living in seclusion,
  who were entirely dedicated to prayers and meditation" (p. 109, folio 91). "The head of the khangah [khanqah] was
  the Shaikh, the others were the ikhwan or brethren. Inmates should co-operate and take food communally" (p. 110,
  folio 92); a named office, the **khanqah steward** (p. 109, folio 91); and "Beds would be offered only to
  distinguished guests; permanent members occupied places assigned to them on the floor" (pp. 181–182, folios
  163–164).
- **The hospitality code, with a time of day and a rule against expulsion.** Travellers "should reach the khanqah
  before afternoon prayers, and if late, should spend the night in a mosque. A warm welcome to the visitor should be
  extended by the residents and the best food offered to them by the khangah steward [OCR?] [khanqah steward]. The
  travellers should not be pestered with questions… If a dervish entered who was ignorant of khangah customs and the
  traditions of sufi life, he should not be expelled" (p. 109, folio 91).
- **The written rule of discipline, in ten clauses.** "1. Let them (the inmates) keep their garments clean and
  themselves always pure. 2. Let them not sit in the mosque or in any holy place for the sake of gossiping. [3.] In
  the first instance let them perform their prayers in common. [4.] Let them pray much at night. [5.] At dawn let
  them ask forgiveness of God and call unto Him. [6.] In the morning let them read as much of the Qur'an as they can,
  and let them not talk until the sun has risen. 7. Between evening prayers and bedtime prayers let them occupy
  themselves with repeating some litany (Wirdi-u-zikri) [OCR?]. 8. Let them welcome the poor and needy and all who
  join their company, and let them bear patiently the trouble of (waiting upon) them. 9. Let them not eat anything
  save in participation with one another. 10. Let them not absent themselves without receiving permission from one
  another'" (p. 90, folio 74). **Numerals 3–6 were destroyed by the OCR and survive only as the debris line `DAP w`;
  the Pass 1 worker supplied them in brackets from their position and the brackets are kept. Clauses 8–10 are the
  institutional core: an open door to the poor, obligatory commensality, permission required to leave.**
- **Access control, and a weekly open day.** "Chishti jama'at-khanas were open to all. Yogis, qalandars, and others
  were allowed free access" (p. 236, folio 216), against Bahaʾuʾd-Din Zakariyya's food "offered to only a select few"
  and his intolerance of qalandars on the same page (p. 236, folio 216); and of Shah-i ʿAlam, "Six days of the week
  were spent by the Shah in prayer and meditation and during this period he was inaccessible even to the ruler. On
  Fridays everyone was welcomed including the common people" (p. 305, folio 283).
- **Expulsion as a sanction with material teeth, and the jamaʿat-khana as sanctuary and refuge.** "expelled him from
  the jama'at-khana which meant that none of the Shaikh's disciples were able to offer him lodging… by their
  intercession he was restored to the Shaikh's favour" (p. 201, folio 183); "fugitives who took refuge in his
  jama'at-khana were never extradited to the government" (p. 167, folio 149); "The people of Delhi were reassured
  during political upheavals by the presence of the sufi orders and their khanqahs to which they had constant access"
  (p. 207, folio 189).
- **Khanqahs as instruments of conquest, network and rivalry.** Bakhtiyar Khalji "founded mosques, seminaries and
  khangahs [khanqahs] in order to make the conquered region into a new and great cultural centre in the Muslim east"
  (p. 276, folio 256); "A network of small khangahs [khanqahs] stretching from Bihar to Sunargaon and reaching many
  areas of India disseminated Shaikh Sharafu'd-Din's teachings" (p. 260, folio 240); about twenty Irani sufis
  "established khanqahs and /angars [OCR?] [langars]" across Kashmir (p. 314, folio 292); and "Musa Raina helped the
  Mir to build a khanqah at Jadibal, a suburb of Srinagar **which was intended to rival Mir Saiyid 'Ali's khangah
  [khanqah]**" (p. 320, folio 298).
- **A khanqah run by a deputy, and one lost to fire.** "his Ahangah [khanqah] was administered by his brother"
  (pp. 299–300, folios 277–278) — **the deputy is a brother, not a khalifa**; "his house and thatched jamaʿat-khana
  there were burnt in an accidental fire" (p. 367, folio 345) — **"thatched" is the only construction detail in
  seventeen pages.**
- **The khanqah count for Delhi, with the author's hedge.** "in Delhi there were two thousand large khanqahs. The
  number may have been exaggerated, but it is clear that by that time khangahs [khanqahs] exercised a deep social,
  political, economic and cultural influence in India" (p. 421, folio not stated).
- **The index's institutional runs, cited to the pages they are printed on, never converted to page references.**
  "Khangah, 7, 16, 31, 64, 68, 70-71, 74, 84, 86, 91, 98, 102, 138, 189-93, 201, 204—5, 213, 216, 220, 230-31, 249,
  252, 262, 277, 280, 292, 303-4, 307-8, 397-400, 403, 408-10" (p. 472, folio 448); "Jama‘at-khana, 9-10, 75, 91, 139,
  141-46, 149, 160-63, 176-78, 181, 183-86, 211, 216, 220, 222-23, 345" (p. 470, folio 446); "Khangah-i Muhammadi,
  377" (p. 472, folio 448); "ribat, 16, 68" (p. 482, folio 458); "Madrasa, 64, 84, 115, 191, 205. 257, 261, 276"
  (p. 474, folio 450); "Takiva, 201" [OCR?] [Takiya], "Suffa, 88, 91, 112" (pp. 488, 487; folios 464, 463); "Langar,
  163, 166, 189, 201, 227-28, 291-92, 407" (p. 473, folio 449).

### B. Building a shrine: who pays, who builds, what gets dated

- **The origin story of the shrine as an institution — the single most transferable passage in the book.** "Generally,
  Shaikhs were buried inside their khangahs [OCR?] [khanqahs] or close-by. Eventually rulers or important nobles,
  devoted to a particular Shaikh, erected imposing tombs on the graves of their patron saints. Even the humblest of
  these burial sites became institutions of far-reaching significance. By the eleventh century, khanqahs had become
  centres for the spiritual élite. **However, the tombs were also for the people, both Muslim and non-Muslim, many of
  whom travelled vast distances to reach them**" (p. 121, folio 103).
- **Shrine-building as a dynastic practice, twice, with no particulars.** "He also built a number of tombs at the
  graves of eminent sufis" (p. 194, folio 176) — no site, number or date (not stated); "The Sultans of Malwa showered
  riches on the Chishti tombs at Ajmer and Nagaur and patronized the leading sufis of Nagaur" (p. 295, folio 275) —
  no sums, grants or dates (not stated).
- **A grave in three successive states, only the last with a builder.** "The original grave was of bricks, later a
  stone cenotaph was built over it, leaving the brick construction intact. Khwaja Husain Nagauri built a tomb over the
  grave" (p. 143, folio 125). No date for any of the three — (not stated).
- **A tomb completed on a stated day — the fullest construction datum in the book.** "Sultan Muhammad bin Tughlug
  [OCR?] [Tughluq] erected a tomb over his remains, which was completed on 15 Sah'ban [OCR?] [Sha'ban] 730/3 June
  1330. Other constructions, including a gateway, were built by Shaikh Husain Nagauri" (p. 148, folio 130).
- **A gateway with a year, and a khanqah on a tomb site hedged by the book itself.** "A gateway known as Buland
  Darwaza (High Gateway) seems to have been constructed near the tomb by Mahmud Khaljiin 1455 [OCR?] [Mahmud Khalji
  in 1455]" (p. 144, folio 126); "A khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] on the tomb site was built by the rulers of Mandu,
  probably by Mahmud Khalji (1436-69)" (pp. 143–144, folios 125–126).
- **Mosques accreted to one complex over four reigns.** "The Malwa Sultans also built another mosque in the tomb
  complex, which was later extended by Jahangir and Aurangzeb" (p. 144, folio 126); "Shah-Jahan constructed a mosque
  of white marble in the tomb complex and added a gateway" (p. 145, folio 127); "The great mosque of Ajmer was built
  under his supervision" (p. 144, folio 126).
- **A tomb with a dated Mughal phase and a quarter named after the saint.** "The tomb of Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din Mahmud
  became a beacon for succeeding generations and the area came to be known as the Chiragh-i Dihli. A tomb was built
  by Sultan Firuz on the grave but the existing enclosures and mosque were erected by the Mughal Emperor Muhammad
  Shah (1719-48) between 1142 and 1143/1729 and 1730. Other additions were made by later Mughal princes. The whole
  area is associated with the memories and traditions of a large number of sufis and saints, many of whom lie buried
  there" (p. 205, folio 187).
- **A tomb rebuilt by a named ruler on a dated occasion, with distance and direction.** "His tomb in Gazirgah, about
  three miles north-east of Herat was first rebuilt by the Timurid ruler, Shah Rukh, (807/1405-850/1447) about
  829/1425 and later was further embellished by other Timurid princes" (p. 93, folio 77). Original construction date,
  ʿurs, custodians and endowment all (not stated).
- **A mausoleum begun by one sultan, finished by the next, with a langar attached.** "The building of a beautiful
  mausoleum over the earthly remains of the Shaikh was commenced by Muhammad Shah at Sarkhij and completed in the
  reign of his successor Qutbu'd-Din Ahmad (1451-59). Even after his death huge amounts of money were spent in the
  /angar [OCR?] [langar] of the mausoleum for the provision of food for the poor" (p. 431, folio 407), sourced "AA,
  p. 157" (p. 431, folio 407, footnote 4).
- **A dome built against the saint's wish, and lost.** "Although he had wished to be buried in the open, Muhammad bin
  Tughluq later constructed an imposing dome over the Shaikh's grave", footnoted "SSA [OCR?] [SA], p. 154. However it
  has not survived" (p. 181, folio 163). Building date, motive and date of loss all (not stated).
- **Three further sultan-built tombs, none dated.** "Sultan Mahmud Khalji of Malwa erected a tomb over his grave"
  (p. 167, folio 149); "Muhammad bin Tughluq built a tomb for the Shaikh after his death near his grandfather's
  mausoleum" (p. 167, folio 149); "A structure was erected on it by Sultan Muhammad bin Tughluq" (p. 170, folio 152).
- **Royal cash to a living descendant, converted into shrine fabric at two sites.** "the money he received from Sultan
  Ghiyasu'd-Din Khalji (1469-1501) was spent in constructing the tomb of Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din and the gateway of
  Shaikh Hamudu'd-Din's [OCR?] [Hamidu'd-Din's] tomb" (p. 149, folio 131).
- **A caliph builds and equips a khanqah for a Shaikh "and his family".** "Al-Nasiralso builtanextensive khangah
  [OCR?] [Al-Nasir also built an extensive khanqah] for Shaikh Shihabu'd-Din and his family, which included additions
  such asa bath-house anda garden [OCR?] [such as a bath-house and a garden]" (p. 104, folio 88). No date, cost, site
  or deed — (not stated). **And a ruler relocating beside a khanqah, the mechanism the archive's rows repeat five
  centuries later:** "many requested him to act as a go-between when requesting favours from the Caliph, **who built
  himself a palace near the khanqah complex**" (p. 91, folio 75 corrected).
- **A khanqah built for a Shaikh by a state officer, and the Chishti rule it broke.** Nizamu'd-Din Kharitadar "built a
  khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] for the Shaikh"; Baba Farid wrote that "such misfortunes befell one who violated the
  traditions of his preceptors and that **no other Chishti sufi had lived in a khangah [khanqah] built for his own
  personal use**" (p. 156, folio 138).
- **A private patron builds one and the recipient calls it an idol-temple.** "Maulana Nizam Madani built a small
  khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] for his use while in Munyar. Shaikh Sharafu'd-Din accepted the gift, but referring to the
  khanqah stressed that his friends had forced him to sit in the temple of an idol" (p. 250, folio 230). **The same
  printed page carries both the damaged "khangah" and the correct "khanqah".**
- **Two built for living saints, one as a music venue.** "He had a khangah [khanqah] built for him and then attended
  his conversational and sama' gatherings" (p. 272, folio 252); Mu'inu'd-Din Parwana "built 'Iraqi a khanqah at Tuqat
  which became an important centre for sufi musical gatherings" (p. 225, folio 205).
- **Four khanqahs built on demolished temples, one of them a three-stage sequence on a single plot.** "Sultan Sikandar
  also became his disciple and built an imposing khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] for the young migrant at the site where his
  father had first built the prayer platform" (p. 319, folio 297), the platform's own site being a destroyed temple
  (p. 318, folio 296) — **temple → prayer platform → khanqah**; "There a kafir (either a Hindu or a Buddhist) had
  erected a large temple and a well. The Shaikh demolished the temple and constructed a takiya (khangah) [OCR?]
  [khanqah] and converted a large number of kafirs" (p. 219, folio 201) — **the well is not said to have been
  destroyed** (not stated); "Kali Mandir was demolished and subsequently the Saiyid's khangah [khanqah] was built
  there" (p. 314, folio 292); "There he demolished a temple on a site on the banks of the Jamna where he built a
  khangah [khanqah] and organized sama' gathering." (p. 329, folio 307).
- **A foundation dispute settled by paying off a yogi's prior claim to the ground.** "when the walls of the town were
  being built up to about six feet high they collapsed. The Sultan consulted the Shaikh who summoned a yogi named Bank
  Nath. According to him the site had previously been his property and therefore his name should be mentioned
  somewhere in the town. This was promptly done and the walls were completed" (p. 431, folio 407); the town's own
  foundation is given twice over, "the founding of Ahmadabad, near Asawal, on the banks of the river Sabarmati" and
  "the foundation, in 1411 or 1413, was laid by four different people, all called Ahmad" (pp. 430–431, folios
  406–407).
- **Infrastructure built for pilgrims on a road, for the living rather than the dead.** "From Delhi to Ghiyaspur,
  platforms with thatched roofs were constructed with wells dug for water for the performance of ablutions before
  prayers of the Shaikh's visitors" (p. 178, folio 160).
- **Royal building at Chisht, undated.** "In Chisht itself, Ghiyasu'd-Din Muhammad built a madrasa and a mosque"
  (p. 133, folio 115). A reign of 1163 to 1203 is given; no date for either building — (not stated).

### C. Burial, the grave, and how a site becomes a shrine

- **Burial inside the khanqah, as rule (p. 121, folio 103, above) and by name.** "On 22 Rajab 736/6 March 1336,
  Simnani died and was buried in his khangah [OCR?] [khanqah], Sufiyabad-i Simnan" (p. 270, folio 250); the khanqah
  itself dated and sited, "In 1290 he founded a khangah [khanqah] at Sufiyabad, near Simnan" (p. 269, folio 249).
- **Burial beside the master, three times, and a saint choosing his own place in advance.** Shaikh Badru'd-Din "died
  in about 657/1258-59 and was buried near the tomb of his master, Khwaja Qutbu'd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki" (p. 156, folio
  138); Khwaja Mahmud Muyina-Duz "was buried near the Khwaja's tomb" (p. 217, folio 199); 'Iraqi "was buried near the
  tomb of Ibn al-'Arabi" (p. 225, folio 205); and Qutbu'd-Din "was buried in Mahrauli about eleven miles from Delhi,
  at a place he himself had chosen" (p. 156, folio 138). **And a saint who asked to be buried beside cloth rather
  than a person:** "Before he died he buried some of Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din's clothes which he had carried from Delhi,
  and requested that he be buried next to the clothing" (p. 202, folio 184).
- **A burial inventory: five relics placed on the corpse under a will, and a refusal of succession in the same act.**
  "he considered none worthy of receiving the relics bequeathed to him… In accordance with his will, these were
  buried with his earthly remains. The khirga [OCR?] [khirqa] was placed on his rib bones, the staff was laid beside
  his body, his rosary was wound around his fore-finger, the wooden bowl was placed under his head and the wooden
  sandals on his breast" (p. 205, folio 187). **And letters buried by testamentary direction:** "About two hundred of
  them were said to have been buried in the grave of Shaikh Muzaffar, according to a directive contained in his will.
  A bag containing twenty-eight letters survived, which were later compiled into a book-form and also published in
  1870" (pp. 257–258, folios 237–238).
- **The corpse washed by a disciple who improvised a khirqa at the deathbed.** Gisu Daraz "washed his master's body.
  Then, taking some cords from the Shaikh's matted bed, his disciple wound them around his head, declaring that for
  himself such cords made the best khirqa" (p. 205, folio 187).
- **Two graves side by side become a pilgrimage centre with an income — the key shrine-formation sentence.** "He was
  buried near his father's grave at Pandua, and the two tombs became a significant centre of pilgrimage in Bengal,
  **receiving large endowments from affluent devotees**" (p. 279, folio 259).
- **A site renamed after a saint and drawing pilgrims, and a place-name generated by a tomb.** "the Shaikh's memory
  was treasured by both Hindus and Muslims alike. Devatalla came to be known as Tabrizabad and attracted a large
  number of pilgrims" (p. 220, folio 202) — no numbers, no ʿurs date, no custodian (not stated); "The town of Sawa
  where Haidar lived and was buried gradually became known as the TJurbat-i [Turbat-i] Haidari (the Tomb of Haidar)."
  (p. 329, folio 307).
- **A shrine's whole life-cycle, including its death.** "Bibi Fatima's tomb was in the old Indraprastha; it was a
  rendezvous for holy men until either the fourteenth or fifteenth century, but by the end of the sixteenth century
  it was deserted" (p. 427, folio 403). A pond and a tank are named at the same tomb (p. 426, folio 402).
- **A saint's cell absorbed into a shrine, and a chilla site that is a pilgrimage centre without a grave.** "Gisu
  Daraz ordered him to perform forty days continuous meditation and penance in the Delhi cell of Baba Farid. **The
  cell was later incorporated into the tomb of Shaikh Qutbu'd-Din**" (p. 276, folio 256); "The traditional site of his
  chilla (cell) where he would perform ascetic exercises often for forty days is still a centre of pilgrimage"
  (p. 338, folio 316).
- **An open-air congregation in a grove that already held tombs — the closest thing to a shrine before there is a
  shrine.** "Early in the morning he would leave his dwelling in Awadh and walk to the local mango grove which also
  held some tombs. There he spent the whole day in prayer. A small group of Muslim weavers worked in the grove and
