  with Nasiru'd-Din they would gather in congregation under the trees to pray while he acted as their Imam" (p. 202,
  folio 184).
- **Contested and duplicated graves, four cases.** "After the Shaikh's death on 13 Ramazan 724/3 September 1324 both
  the people of Karnal and Panipat claimed his grave to be in their respective towns." (p. 327, folio 305); "Shah
  Ni'matu'llah's tomb was in Hansi, not in Kirman, as was commonly believed" (p. 328, folio 306); "Abu'l-Fazl refers
  to two different tombs of Kabir; one at Puri in Orissa and the other at Ratanpur in Awadh" (p. 399, folio 375) —
  neither described, dated or attributed to a builder (not stated); and a grave located by inference from a nisba,
  the book saying so: "However the Shaikh is well known as Sharafu'd-Din Panipati and therefore **it is reasonable to
  assume** he was buried in his home town." (p. 327, folio 305).
- **Substitute and symbolic graves as a distributed pilgrimage system.** "A number of symbolic graves scattered from
  western U.P. to eastern Bengal represent events in Salar Mas'ud's life and are worshipped with an equivalent fervour
  to that of the one in Bahraich. **Those not able to travel to the town celebrate festivals at these substitute
  graves**" (p. 336, folio 314); "Symbolic tombs of Pir Badr were built, generally facing river mouths, at Chittagong,
  Arakan and elsewhere in East Bengal" (p. 339, folio 317).
- **The martyr's grave as shared village infrastructure, and its resilience.** "Both Hindus and Muslims in many
  villages in eastern U.P. feature the grave of a martyr, who is worshipped by both groups. **The Muslim and Hindu
  puritanical and revivalist movements have failed to undermine their popularity**" (p. 336, folio 314).
- **Fictitious graves as a class, with a named absurdity.** "Fictitious graves were also constructed and worshipped; a
  curious story is related by J.P. Brown in which an ass's grave was believed to radiate blessings to a group of
  villages in Anatolia" (p. 121, folio 103).
- **Two contested corpses, one Muslim and one shared.** "After his death, Jews, Christians and Muslims all claimed
  Maʿruf as their own, but only Muslims were able to lift his bier from the ground before his burial" (p. 66, folio
  50) — **the book attaches no tomb and no cult to it** (not stated); and, the "shared ground" passage of Chapter Six,
  "After his death Kabir's body was claimed by both Muslims and Hindus, the former wishing to bury it and the latter
  to cremate it. When the door of the room where the dead body was lying was opened it was missing. According to
  tradition only a bunch of flowers was found under the sheet and these were divided amongst the two groups" (p. 399,
  folio 375).
- **A death-place chosen to break a pilgrimage belief.** "Before his death he is said to have migrated from Banaras to
  Maghar… The decision was deliberately taken by Kabir in order to belie the current Hindu belief that one who died
  in Maghar would return in a following life as an ass" (pp. 398–399, folios 374–375).
- **Burial as an organising principle of the sources, and a regional tomb inventory in one letter.** 125 sufis "who
  were all buried in Delhi" (p. 27, folio 13); "ten saints buried at Khuldabad" (p. 29, folio 15); and the tombs "of
  seventy important khalifas of Shaikh Shihabu'd-Din Suhrawardi in Devagaon", other Suhrawardi tombs "in Mahisun (or
  Mahasthan), in the Bogra district", "those of the Jalaliyya order in Devatalla", Narkoti's disciples of Shaikh
  Ahmad of Damascus, and "Sunargoan [OCR?] [Sunargaon] featured the tomb of Shaikh Sharafu'd-Din Abu Tawwama"
  (p. 280, folio 260).
- **Eleven named burials with no tomb, builder, endowment, custodian or date — recorded because the absence is the
  finding.** Makhdum-i Jahaniyan "was buried in Uch" and Raju Qattal died at Uch (p. 303, folio 281); Qutb-i ʿAlam
  "was buried in Batwa near Ahmdabad [Ahmadabad]" (p. 305, folio 283); Shaikh Samaʿuʾd-Din "was buried on the
  embankment of the Hauz-i Shamsi in Delhi" (p. 308, folio 286); Saiyid ʿAli Hamadani's "body was taken to Khuttalan,
  now part of the U.S.S.R., where it was buried" (p. 314, folio 292); Shaikh Mina "died at Lucknow and is buried right
  in the centre of the old part of the town" (p. 293, folio 273); Adhan Jaunpuri "buried in Jaunpur" (p. 283, folio
  263); Shaikh Saidu "buried in Fathpur Hanswa" (p. 285, folio 265); Shaikh Hasan "buried near Vijai Mandal" (p. 286,
  folio 266); Maulana Khwajgi and Maulana Ahmad "Both died at Kalpi where they were buried" and Shaikh Abuʾl-Fath
  ʿAlaʾi Qureshi "buried at Kalpi" (p. 294, folio 274). **In no case does the book say a tomb was built, endowed,
  visited by pilgrims or given a custodian** — (not stated).
- **Mortuary practice: prayers and food for the dead, and a death-anniversary meat custom refused.** "the latter
  appropriately had prayers said and food distributed to the poor for the repose of his mother's soul" (p. 426, folio
  402); against Hamidu'd-Din Nagauri, who "Before his death … requested his descendants not to distribute meat for
  his soul's peace", on the ground that "the butcher would have to kill again in order to replace the meat which was
  sold" (p. 146, folio 128) — **the custom of distributing meat for a dead saint's soul is attested here precisely by
  being rejected.** And a relic-list that ends in the same practice: the household utensils "prompted sufis to be
  hospitable and **in the name of deceased Saints to give food to the poor**" (p. 267, folio 247).
- **Praying at a grave as a saint's own habit, three times.** "the Shaikh quietly prayed at his mother's tomb and
  returned to his jama'at-khana" (p. 179, folio 161); "To the end of his days, the Shaikh would pray at her tomb
  during any crisis or period of anxiety" and "After her death, Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din Auliya' used to go to her tomb to
  offer prayers and obtain spiritual satisfaction" (p. 426, folio 402).

### D. ʿUrs, festival, and langar

- **The ʿurs defined, with its etymology and its theology — the single most citable institutional sentence in the
  book.** "Great celebrations were held to commemorate the chief landmarks in the life of a Shaikh. **Then, as now,
  the most popular date marked the anniversary of the death of great Indian sufis. This was known as the ‘urs or
  wedding, for the death of a mystic denoted the return of his soul to the supernatural source from which it had been
  separated during its earthly existence.** This belief was strengthened by the philosophy of Ibn al-‘Arabi in his
  most significant concept, the Wahdat al-Wujud or the Tawhid-i Wujudi, that is, the Unity of Being" (p. 121, folio
  103). **"Then, as now" asserts continuity to the author's present; no ʿurs of any named shrine, no date, no
  duration and no ritual programme is given there** — (not stated).
- **The ʿurs as a cultural institution, in the Conclusion, again attached to no shrine.** "The 'urs ceremonies and
  other anniversaries celebrated in khanqgahs [OCR?] [khanqahs] developed into significant cultural institutions and
  were eagerly awaited by both the poor and affluent alike" (p. 423, folio 399).
- **An ʿurs with two orders' langars operating at it, in Delhi, for a saint buried elsewhere.** "The Khwaja was very
  fond of sama' and each 'urs, or death anniversary of Shaikh Saifu'd-Din Bakharzi was celebrated with great
  enthusiasm by them both. **Food was freely distributed from Chishtiyya and Firdawsiyya /angars [OCR?] [langars] and
  dervishes assembled in large numbers**" (p. 247, folio 227). "them both" = Khwaja Badruʾd-Din and Shaikh
  Nizamuʾd-Din Auliyaʾ; no place of burial, shrine building or numbers (not stated).
- **An ʿurs with an expenditure attached.** "At Gujarat Shaikh Ahmad lived like a prince. Large amounts were spent on
  such occasions as the 'urs of Baba Ishaq Maghribi, although the money was also spent on charity throughout the
  year" (p. 432, folio 408), sourced "ibid, ff. 33b-34a, 59b-60a" (p. 432, folio 408, footnote 2).
- **A living ʿurs with a gender exclusion — the only access rule in the book in the present tense.** "Every year on
  the occasion of the Shah's 'urs large crowds of Hindus and Muslims assemble, **although women are still not
  permitted near the shrine**" (p. 341, folio 319).
- **Two festival calendars at one tomb, out of step, with a harvest explanation.** "Although his 'urs is celebrated on
  12, 13 and 14 Rajab, the flags are taken to the tomb on the first Sunday of the Hindi month Jaith (May-June).
  **After the completion of the wheat and barley harvest, the leisure of the peasants in June is devoted to
  celebrations marking the Ghazi Miyan festival**" (p. 336, folio 314).
- **An annual visit by non-Muslims which the book does not call an ʿurs.** "Even today, the kafirs of that area are
  devoted to the Khwaja's tomb in the same manner as their ancestors had been. They visit it each year and
  ‘prostrate themselves on the dust of the tomb of that eminent one and the full moon of the heavens of his
  sainthood’" (p. 140, folio 122). No date given — (not stated).
- **A grave-decoration festival, dated in the calendar, recorded as something a saint opposed.** At Shab-i Barat,
  "held in the evening of the middle of Sha'ban, the eighth month in the Muslim Calendar", "fire works were exploded,
  graves were white-washed and lighted lamps were placed on them" (p. 302, folio 280).
- **Tomb visitation for centuries, with a duration and nothing else.** "Although he died there in 342/953-54, for
  centuries his tomb at Marw was visited by devotees" (p. 55, folio 39). No shrine building, ʿurs, custodian,
  offerings or numbers — (not stated).
- **The index's ʿurs and festival headwords, cited to the pages they are printed on and not converted to page
  references.** "‘urs, 102, 227, 314, 399" (p. 489, folio 465); "Holi festival, 284" and "Holi-Nama, 370" (p. 468,
  folio 444). **And the one report of a living shrine festival in some six hundred bibliography entries:** "Ritter,
  H. … Die Mevlanaferier in Konya vom 11-17, December 1960, Oriens, XV, 1962, 249-70," (p. 446, folio 422).
- **The negative, and it is large.** Seventeen chunks state explicitly that no ʿurs and no mela appears in their range
  — pp. 1–29; 50–65; 66–81; 100–117; 148–163; 181–197; 198–214; 215–232; 249–264; 265–280; 299–314; 315–331; 349–365;
  366–382; 383–401; 402–419; 442–462. **The word *mela* appears nowhere in the book's practices material at all.**
- **Langar: the definition, and a deathbed order about its stores.** The Shaikh "ordered them to confirm that there
  was not a morsel of food in the house. They assured him that only a few handfuls of grain remained, and they had
  been kept for the /angar [OCR?] [langar]. The Shaikh was enraged at Iqbal preserving worthless assets and ordered
  it to be immediately given to the poor" (p. 181, folio 163), footnoted "An alms house for the distribution of free
  food" (p. 181, folio 163, footnote 3). **Its confessional openness, flagged as inference by the author's own
  adverb:** "**Almost certainly** Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din's /angar [langar] was open to Hindus and Muslims alike"
  (p. 184, folio 166).
- **Two royal langar foundations in Kashmir, one of them sited and measured.** "Rinchana, who took Sadru'd-Din as his
  Muslim name, gave grants to Bulbul Shah for the establishment of a khangah [khanqah] and a /angar [OCR?] [langar]"
  (p. 312, folio 290); Saiyid Tajuʾd-Din "was given facilities to build a khangah [khanqah] and a /angar [langar] at
  Shihabu'd-Din Pura, **some nine miles north-west of Srinagar**" (p. 313, folio 291).
- **A khanqah founded with a langar and then given land bought by the Shaikh himself.** "He first lived at Lakhnauti,
  constructed a khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] and attached a /angar [OCR?] [langar] to it. **He also bought some gardens
  and land to be attached to the monastery**" (p. 219, folio 201). **And a mausoleum's langar outliving its patron**
  (p. 431, folio 407, quoted above).
- **Langars in the plural, urban and cheap; and one named only as a place beside water.** "There were a large number
  of /angars [OCR?] [langars]; the people organized many public entertainments at little expense" (p. 207, folio
  189) — undated beyond "the reign of Sultan 'Ala'u'd-Din"; and a son of ʿImad "had gone to bathe near the /angar
  [langar] and had started to drown" (p. 248, folio 228).
- **The cauldrons, and the book's only fed-crowd figure.** "On this occasion he presented the Khwaja's khanqah with a
  huge cauldron"; "Jahangir … presented a smaller cauldron than Akbar's, to the shrine. **Food for the poor was
  cooked and 5,000 assembled people were fed from the cauldron**"; "The two survive" (p. 144, folio 126).
- **A khanqah kitchen at scale, with an income the book cannot explain.** Of Sidi Maula: "Although his clothing was
  the simplest and his food humble, his guests were fed lavishly. Travellers from many countries thronged to his
  khangah [khanqah] and they, along with the population of Delhi, benefited from his huge feasts… **The latter's
  financial sources remained a mystery; he accepted neither land grants nor any other type of gift.**" (p. 330, folio
  308); "Whenever he purchased anything or wished to pay someone he would specify a spot and freshly coined money
  would be found. From this grew the story that he was an alchemist." (pp. 330–331, folios 308–309). **Rizvi's own
  explanation: "the funds were provided by the Khan-i Khanan and by merchants from abroad" (p. 331, folio 309).**
- **Feasts as evidence of the scale of offerings.** "The anecdotes of Abu Sa'id's luxurious feasts, in which thousands
  of candles were burnt during the day, give some indication of the amounts both available, and offered, to sufis by
  merchants and other devotees at that time" (p. 88, folio 72); "the Shaikh took care that his neighbours, and often
  people from the town, shared the pleasures of his entertainment" (p. 88, folio 72).
- **The Chishti table rule against the Suhrawardi one, on one page.** "The Chishtis offered food to all guests and, if
  necessary, did not hesitate to sell their few miserable belongings to pay for it. If they had nothing to offer, a
  glass of water would be presented accompanied by their apologies" (p. 236, folio 216), against Bahaʾuʾd-Din
  Zakariyya's food "offered to only a select few" (p. 236, folio 216).
- **Commensality enforced as a fault.** "Let them not eat anything save in participation with one another" (p. 90,
  folio 74, clause 9); and the boiling-milk case, "The Shaikh replied that it was better to waste the milk than not
  to have distributed it equally. He refused to accept their apologies" (p. 217, folio 199).
- **The pooled zanbil collection at Ajodhan — the nearest thing to a langar there, and the book does not call it
  one.** "In the early years of his stay at Ajodhan a zanbil (a basket made of palm leaves hung round the neck) was
  carried by some members of the jama'at-khana **twice a day** to the town and the offerings placed in it were shared
  by everyone. The Baba also preferred to eat bread from the zanbil, perhaps to be more fully integrated into the
  life of all members of his jama'at-khana" (p. 160, folio 142), the footnote cross-referring to "how Baba Sa'id Dust
  Dada, a disciple of Shaikh Abu Sa'id, collected food in a cumbil, p. 74, supra" (p. 160, folio 142); with foraging
  as supply, "Fuel and wild berries, which were then boiled, were collected from the forests" (p. 160, folio 142).
  **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar** material.
- **A langar-like practice attributed to yogis rather than to a Muslim shrine.** The yogis "replied that this was in
  accordance with their custom of sharing all food equally, even with dogs" (p. 290, folio 270).

### E. Offerings: futuh, the unsolicited gift, and the gift economy

- **The two sources of khanqah income, stated as a closed list by the order's chief theorist — the most citable
  institutional-economy sentence in the book.** "According to Shaikh Shihabu'd-Din there were two sources of income
  for a khanqah—endowments and begging" (p. 109, folio 91).
- **Futuh defined by its unsolicited character, with a stated mechanism for its flow.** "merchants were the leading
  financial supporters of the sufi movement and in return the khangahs [khanqahs] offered them both hospitality and
  protection. Gifts from rich men, government officials, princes and rulers were also welcomed, but sufi literature
  indicates that eminent sufis themselves did not crave offerings. **The unsolicited gift (futuh) from devotees was
  their only source of income and a popular belief in the efficacy of sufi prayers for the fulfilment of both worldly
  and non-worldly ambitions provided for the continual flow of gifts**" (pp. 116–117, folios 98–99).
- **Futuh from people outside the faith.** "Even those who refused to accept Islam would send large offerings and
  futuh to what Jamali called Hazrat Ishan (His Holiness Court, here meaning tomb)" (p. 140, folio 122).
- **Futuh as the working income of a household and a jamaʿat-khana, named six times.** Qutbu'd-Din repaid the grocer
  "as soon as some futuh was received" (p. 155, folio 137); "Gradually, however, the Shaikh obtained some futuh and
  was able to afford both paper and wages and the book was transcribed" (p. 169, folio 151); "No regular futuh was
  received, and only a zambil provided the Shaikh and a few disciples with sustenance" (p. 177, folio 159); "He sent
  futwh [OCR?] [futuh] and also made an offer of some villages" (p. 177, folio 159); "as with other futuhs,
  distributed the money amongst both the needy townsfolk and dervishes" (p. 179, folio 161); merchants "were the
  sufis' main source of futuh" (p. 242, folio 222).
- **Futuh split fifty-fifty — the only quantified division of offerings in the book.** Saiyid ʿAli "received regular
  futuh, half of which he needed to support his numerous wives, the rest he gave to the needy. He had no servants and
  he himself waited on other dervishes" (p. 283, folio 263).
- **Futuh at scale, with a named officer and annual sums — the most concrete shrine-economy passage in the book.**
  "Gradually large amounts of futuh were given to him. **The Shaikh was forced to employ a steward (baqqal) to handle
  the large amounts of futuh and its dispersal.** According to the Tuhfatu'l-Majalis, Shaikh Ahmad's malfuzat, **once
  a year, two hundred thousand tankas were spent by the khanqah in gifts. Some nobles gave such large amounts as
  25,000 tankas**" (p. 430, folio 406), sourced "ibid, ff. 19b-20b" (p. 430, folio 406, footnote 5).
- **Futuh declining across a generation, stated as inference.** The Shaikh "admitted it was not a hundredth of what had
  been given in charity by his pir, Shaikh Akhi Siraj, and **it would appear that** the stream of furuh [OCR?] [futuh]
  which had been lavished at that time had gradually dried up" (pp. 277–278, folios 257–258).
- **Futuh refused, with a burial provision and an inheritance inside the story.** "The Shaikh asked the man how could
  he, a lowly-paid labourer, refuse to accept futuh… When she was dying, she pointed to some money hidden in the
  thatched roof. **Some of it was to be used for her burial**, she said, **the rest, twenty dirhams, was the son's
  share.** Like his father he should continue as a vegetable seller and not depend for his living on anything or
  anyone else" (p. 426, folio 402), the offered gift being "a silver tanka", sourced "KM, pp. 277-78" (p. 426, folio
  402, footnote 3).
- **The same-day redistribution rule, in four mouths.** "the Shaikh was opposed to a fixed source of income—nothing was
  accumulated for future use and everything was given away or used the same day it was received" (p. 88, folio 72);
  "Anything received as futuh if not used immediately was kept no longer than a day and was distributed to the needy.
  To a sufi a new day ushered in new hope in God, and a concern for future needs was seen as totally opposed to a
  complete trust in Him" (p. 160, folio 142); the qalandars' "five hundred silver tankas", of which "the Shaikh
  distributed the same day the entire amount among the poor", on the maxim "'if you get money, spend it, for there is
  no dearth of gifts; if it goes away, do not store it for fear of not getting it again,'" (p. 170, folio 152); and
  Shaikh Muzaffar, who "strictly adhered to the sufi rule that whatever was received in charity should be given away
  the same day it had been obtained" (p. 257, folio 237).
- **Endowment by total divestment, with the donor choosing the house; and an initiation that is a divestment.** "Often
  former traders and other wealthy disciples, before becoming ascetics, would give all their possessions to a
  khanqgah [OCR?] [khanqah] of their choice" (p. 88, folio 72); the Jalali followers "offered all their material goods
  to their preceptors, and in return were given a cap and the spiritual tree of the order" (p. 342, folio 320).
- **A named fundraising officer, debts, and caravans as the repayment mechanism — a deliberately unendowed khanqah.**
  "Sometimes Shaikh Abu Sa'id would send his servant, Hasan Mu'addib to raise money for his extravagances from his
  disciples, and on occasions, from his enemies. He contracted huge debts which were invariably paid by visiting
  merchant caravans" (p. 88, folio 72).
- **Money held in trust, and money held in escrow against a change of ruler, with a state clawback.** "he possessed one
  hundred thousand jitals and these he presented to the Shaikh. Jalalu'd-Din asked him to keep the money in trust and
  distribute it through him" (p. 219, folio 201); "Khusraw paid large sums of money to the sufis and dervishes of
  Delhi to pray for his success. **Some of them accepted the payment, keeping it safely so that it could be returned
  to the next ruler if Khusraw lost.** Three eminent dervishes refused financial gifts; Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din, however,
  accepted 500,000 tankas offered him and, as with other futuhs, distributed the money amongst both the needy
  townsfolk and dervishes" (p. 179, folio 161); then Ghiyasu'd-Din Tughluq "ordered the recipients of Khushraw's
  [Khusraw's] money to refund it to the treasury… Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din replied to the Sultan that the money belonged
  to the treasury of the Muslims which he had himself distributed among the poor" (p. 179, folio 161).
- **Cash accepted where land was refused, and the rule drawn from it.** "the Chishtis were unconcerned with the source
  of the gift provided it was paid in cash", justified "that money belonging to the treasury should be spent on the
  Muslim community"; "The gifts were immediately distributed and without them the Chishtis would have not been able
  to assist the poor" (p. 244, folio 224); and the general statement, "**Although Chishti saints were permitted to
  receive cash gifts**, the Shaikh believed that his own path was different" (pp. 145–146, folios 127–128).
- **Royal payment on arrival and departure, redistributed in full.** "During each visit he paid 200,000 tankas at the
  Shaikh's arrival and 500,000 tankas at his departure. The Shaikh always distributed the entire amount among Delhi's
  population" (p. 231, folio 211).
- **Devotees' gifts feeding the establishment rather than the saint, and offerings in the present tense.** "His fellow
  dervishes consumed the gifts given to him by devotees, while the Shah lived only on milk from his cow" (p. 337,
  folio 315); "Offerings of all kinds are even now made to the tomb of Salar Mas'ud" (p. 336, folio 314).
- **Shrine servants paid from the offerings — the one custodianship-and-income sentence in the Ajmer material.** "At
  the same time, large amounts of money are paid to those who tend the shrine" (p. 140, folio 122). **No office named,
  no sajjada nashin, no Auqaf** — (not stated). **And offerings generating a family dispute:** "Wealth from the
  offerings of Akbar and his entourage resulted in a dispute for it amongst the Khwaja's descendants" (p. 144, folio
  126).
- **A coin offering to a river rather than to a shrine.** "'Darya ke panch paise, Badr, Badr' 'Five copper pieces for
  the river, Oh Badr'" (p. 339, folio 317).
- **Two named types of false sufi, one defined by soliciting the state.** "Firstly there was a mugallid [OCR?]
  [muqallid], or sufi without a pir, secondly, was the jarrat, a person who donned an excellent khirga [khirqa] and
  the cap of a sufi and who visiting the sultans and his officials, begged for money. **Such a person was a jarrat
  because he sold religion.**" (p. 206, folio 188).
- **Begging condemned and begging required — both positions, unreconciled.** "The first was begging from door to door,
  the second involved a withdrawal from the world but a continued mental occupation with the attainment of gifts"
  (p. 198, folio 180); against "Begging was also resorted to in the initial stages in the development of a new khangah
  [khanqah], but **affluent khangahs [khanqahs] also insisted that neophyte sufis go to town begging.** Firstly this
  custom was intended to produce humility, and secondly to strengthen the feeling among sufis that they were the
  lowliest of all God's creatures" (p. 117, folio 99). **And begging imposed by a shaikh on a former governor to
  destroy his rank:** "Returning to Baghdad, Junaid again urged Shibli to beg and also to perform menial services for
  his master's companions. All this was done so that not a vestige of the former governor's pomp and pride remained"
  (p. 75, folio 59).
- **Loans: two permitted pretexts, a flat prohibition, and a borrowing actually done.** "the only two pretexts on which
  loans could be accepted were the maintenance of one's family and the entertainment of travellers. He added, however,
  the loan seeking and renayment [OCR?] [repayment] disturbed the spiritual routine of a dervish" (p. 198, folio 180);
  "Other Chishti Shaikhs, including his own teachers, had permitted the borrowing of small amounts of money for
  household needs, but the Baba strictly forbade this practice" (p. 160, folio 142); and Qutbu'd-Din borrowing "up to
  thirty dirhams (copper coins) from a neighbouring Muslim grocer for household expenses" (p. 155, folio 137). **Three
  incompatible Chishti positions on credit, all kept.**
- **Gifts refused and accepted at one audience.** "the Sultan offered each a woollen garment and a purse of tankas.
  Maulana Shamsu'd-Din and Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din took the gifts and departed. Shaikh Qutbu'd-Din Dabir knew Maulana
  Zarradi would not accept the gifts, so he himself took them" (p. 200, folio 182).
- **Merchant withdrawal as proof of the gift economy's load, and a state attempt to cut it off answered by doubling
  expenditure.** "merchants and wealthy supporters appear to have stopped sending gifts to the sufis at the
  jama'at-khana. This action impoverished Baba Farid, his family and other members of his religious community … and
  they all were reduced to near starvation" (p. 163, folio 145); Sultan Qutbuʾd-Din Mubarak Shah Khalji "forbade his
  officials to visit the jama'at-khana, hoping that the Shaikh's charities would cease and members of the
  jama'at-khana would starve to death… the Shaikh learned of the Sultan's action he **ordered the expenditure of the
  jama'at-khana to be doubled**" (p. 242, folio 222); the same move at Sunargaon, "There Shaikh 'Ala'u'l-Haqq
  defiantly ordered his servant to spend double the amount he had at Pandua" (p. 277, folio 257).
- **Gifts of food accepted as doctrine, against a named contrary practice.** "'It was reported that whenever food was
  presented to Maʿruf as a gift he always accepted and ate it. Someone said to him: "Your brother Bishr bin al-Haris
  always refused such food," and Maʿruf replied: "Abstaining causes my brother's hands to be tied, whilst Gnosis
  causes my hands to be stretched forth. I am only a guest in the house of my Lord…"'" (p. 66, folio 50, footnoted to
  "Qut al-Qulub, 1V [OCR?] [IV], p. 61"); and against it Rabiʿa, who "felt ashamed to ask for anything from the
  world's Creator, let alone human beings" (p. 46, folio 30).
- **Aggressive mendicancy as an order's rule.** The Be Qaid wa Be Nawa "made their khirgas [OCR?] [khirqas] from
  shreds which they collected from the streets. Whenever they wanted a gift they reviled the reluctant donor until
  their demands were fulfilled" (p. 341, folio 319).
- **The index's offerings and coin runs, cited to the pages they are printed on.** "Futuh, 98, 122, 142, 151, 159,
  161, 180, 223, 258, 263, 400, 402, 406" (p. 465, folio 441); "Dinar, 200, 282" and "Dirham, 137, 146, 151, 402"
  (p. 464, folio 440); "Jital, 130, 151, 201" (p. 471, folio 447); "Tanka, 136, 154, 166, 187, 277, 402, 406"
  (p. 488, folio 464); "Zakat, 146, 221" (pp. 490–491, folios 466–467); "Zanbil, 75, 142, 159" (p. 491, folio 467).

### F. Endowment, land, revenue and tax

- **The one grant with a stated plot size, and the method of building on it.** Dust Dada "applied to the Caliph,
  al-Qa'im, (422/1031-467/1075), **for land to build a khanqah on the banks of the Tigris, across the river in an
  uninhabited region. After receiving permission, a plot of about 200 square yards was selected** by Dust Dada and he
  began collecting in a small bag, building material from dilapidated buildings nearby" (p. 91, folio 75 corrected);
  and the only income-producing properties attached to a shrine-type institution anywhere in the book, "**Later Dust
  Dada erected a bazaar of shops and a caravanserai in front of the khanqah's gateway**" (p. 91, folio 75 corrected).
- **A grant of one hundred villages for the upkeep of a khanqah — the largest concrete endowment in the book.** "The
  Sultan awarded Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din one hundred villages for the upkeep of his khangah [OCR?] [khanqah]" (p. 233,
  folio 213). **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam** — the book's "Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din Abu'l Fath" of Multan, "The successor
  of Shaikh Sadru'd-Din was his son, Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din Abu'l Fath" (p. 230, folio 210) — **the row treatment belongs
  in that row's subsection.**
- **The state builds a khanqah and assigns it a named revenue district.** "During the early period of his reign,
  Sultan Muhammad bin Tughluq ordered Zainu'd-Din Majdu'l-Mulk, the mugqta' [OCR?] [muqta'] of Bihar, to build an
  imposing kKhangah [OCR?] [khanqah] for the Shaikh and **assign the pargana, Rajgir, to pay the expenses**"
  (p. 250, folio 230), *pargana* glossed "A revenue district comprising several villages" (p. 250, folio 230,
  footnote 1) and, elsewhere, "A grouping of villages for purposes of revenue collection and administration"
  (p. 366, folio 344, footnote 1). **The inauguration had its own ceremony and imperial gift:** "a lavish function
  was organized by the mugta' [OCR?] [muqta'] to inaugurate the khanqah. The Shaikh took his place on a Bulgarian
  prayer mat which the Sultan had sent as a gift" (p. 250, folio 230), accepted under duress — "Shaikh Sharafu'd-Din
  reluctantly accepted the offer for he feared, if he refused, Majdu'l-Mulk might suffer from the Sultan's wrath"
  (p. 250, folio 230).
- **The grant was given back, and the book prints three datings and adopts none.** "In a letter Husain Mu'izz Balkhi
  mentions that Shaikh Sharafu'd-Din returned the villages after a period of fifteen years. Saiyid Hasan 'Askari
  suggests that the farman granting the villages appears to have been returned during Sultan Firuz's visit to the
  Shaikh in 762/1360 … **If the farman was returned, the most appropriate time for this to have taken place seems to
  be the period when the Sultan was alienated from the Shaikh**" (p. 250, folio 230, footnote 3).
- **A khanqah endowed with villages, with a state office in the same sentence.** "Sultan Muhammad bin Tughluq
  appointed him the Shaikhu'l-Islam and made him the head of a khangah [khanqah] in Siwistan (Sehwan) known as the
  Khanqah-i Muhammadi to which several villages were attached" (p. 299, folio 277).
- **Whole villages endowed to a Muslim shrine by a Buddhist and Hindu population — the most citable economic fact in
  Chapter Five.** "A memorial (astana) was built on the top of a hill in Chittagong and according to Talish was cared
  for by attendants who spent most of their time in prayer and fasting. **The Buddhist and Hindu Magh people settled
  some villages as endowment for the shrine and made it a centre of pilgrimage**" (p. 339, folio 317), *astana*
  footnoted "Literally a tomb; Bengalis believed that the saint was buried there" (p. 339, folio 317, footnote 2).
  **A custodial body ("attendants"), an endowment class (villages), a donor community and a purpose; no revenue
  figure, no deed, no date** — (not stated).
- **Land bought rather than granted, and the doctrine that licenses it — the single most citable economics sentence in
  the book.** "Sufis, said Simnani, were incorrect in believing they should live in poverty, as this meant a rejection
  of God's bounty towards His creatures. **He himself bought large tracts of land and revenue from these was used to
  finance his khanqah**" (p. 269, folio 249); with the parallel purchase at Lakhnauti (p. 219, folio 201).
- **Endowments outlasting the town, and royal devotion lapsing while popular endowment continues.** "Gulbarga sank
  into obscurity but the lavish endowments to Gisu Daraz's disciples continued, as did the devotion of the people of
  the Deccan to the memory of the Saiyid" (p. 273, folio 253), against "The Sultan's personal devotion to Saiyid
  Muhammad Gisu Daraz's successors was fleeting" (p. 273, folio 253). **And grants on a new accession:** "Gisu Daraz's
  descendants and his former khanqah were given liberal grants by the new Sultan" (p. 272, folio 252).
- **Royal endowment of a saint's family converted into office — the mechanism behind hereditary custodianship, though
  the book never uses the words.** "Muhammad bin Tughluq liberally bestowed endowments on the Shaikh's family and gave
  Shaikh Faridu'd-Din an honoured place in his court. Other descendants of Shaikh Hamid obtained important
  administrative posts, mainly in the Nagaur region" (p. 148, folio 130).
- **An order that starts poor and turns landholding, with both income streams named.** "A century later many Rishis
  also began to live in khangahs [OCR?] [khanqahs], **accepting land and money from the government and their own
  devotees.** Nevertheless they remained dedicated servants of the people, irrespective of class and religious
  distinctions" (p. 373, folio 351). Not dated beyond "A century later" — (not stated).
- **Land grants refused as Chishti policy, with the rationale.** "Chishtis refused to accept land grants either from
  rulers or their officers as this also tended to compromise them in their attempts to maintain complete independence
  from all powers other than God" (p. 243, folio 223).
- **Five refusals, with their instruments named.** (i) "Ulugh Khan himself visited Shaikh Farid and offered a farman of
  four villages to the Shaikh and a cash payment for dervishes. **The Shaikh returned the farman but accepted the
  money for distribution among the dervishes**" (p. 162, folio 144) — **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar**.
  (ii) Jalalu'd-Din Khalji "sent futwh [futuh] and also made an offer of some villages. Starvation having driven them
  to the point of death, a number of disciples urged the Shaikh to accept the gift… they advised Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din
  to reject the offer" (pp. 177–178, folios 159–160). (iii) A farman of two villages sent "to test his asceticism",
  refused (p. 196, folio 178). (iv) "Once a son-in-law accepted a farman from the mugta' [OCR?] [muqtaʿ] of Rudauli.
  So incensed was the Shaikh when he discovered the gift, **he tore the paper into shreds and ordered that not a
  single piece remain in his khangah [khanqah]**" (p. 291, folio 271). (v) On the same page, "another farman of a
  number of villages from his ruler. Firmly, the Shaikh refused the offer, saying that as God provided food for
  Sultan Ibrahim, his elephants and horses, he felt he could also be trusted to feed himself and his descendants"
  (p. 291, folio 271).
- **A grant torn up on initiation, and a grant accepted at the cost of a year's estrangement.** Kashani "tore up the
  farman of idrar or stipends granted him by the Sultans for his scholarship and exchanged them for the poverty of
  asceticism" (p. 194, folio 176); yet 'Ala'u'd-Din Khalji's farman including "the grant of the post of the Qazi of
  Awadh, which had been held by his ancestors, as well as a number of villages" he accepted (p. 195, folio 177).
- **A cash gift part-refused, with figures.** Firuz "bestowed on him a gift of 100,000 tankas. On being pressed by them
  to accept the gift, the Shaikh consented only to receive 2,000 tankas which he gave in charity", and later "The
  Sultan's gift of an expensive robe was refused" (p. 197, folio 179).
- **A saint refusing land and state money, with his subsistence itemised.** "Hamidu'd-Din had a small ihya' plot in
  the village of Suwali, near Nagaur. He lived from the income it gave and drank milk from his one cow, refusing
  offers from both the mugta' [muqta'] of Nagaur and the Sultan for more land and money. His wife did her own
  spinning and the yarn sufficed for their meagre clothing" (pp. 145–146, folios 127–128). **And a khatib's property
  abandoned on becoming a disciple:** Shaikh Jamalu'd-Din "had been the khatib of Hansi, owning villages and
  extensive property. Discipleship with the Baba meant abandoning material prosperity" (p. 171, folio 153).
- **The book's own definitions of the endowment vocabulary, all from its footnotes.** *Ihyaʾ*: "In Muslim legal
  literature Jhya' [OCR?] [Ihya'] means ‘bringing to life’ and refers to unclaimed land lying fallow in distant and
  unpopulated areas. /Jhya' [Ihya'] becomes the property of anyone who cultivates it. According to Hanafi law, the
  previous authorization from the /mam [Imam] is essential. At the beginning of Turkic rule in India, land was in
  abundance and the cultivators of waste lands were recognized as de facto owners and no advance authorization was
  apparently demanded" (p. 143, folio 125, footnote 1). *Muqtaʿ*: "The chief officer of an /gta‘ [Iqta‘] or revenue
  grant made to military chicfs [chiefs] in order to consolidate the power of the government, collect the revenue and
  maintain an army" (p. 143, folio 125, footnote 2). *Farman*: "A royal command or authorization, an edict" (p. 143,
  folio 125, footnote 3). *Aʾimma*: "Those who had received madad-i ma'ash or stipends from land as their main source
  of income" (p. 302, folio 280). *Darogha*: "A revenue official entrusted to keep accounts in Persian" (p. 301,
  folio 279). *ʿUshr* "One-tenth of the produce"; *wajah-i maʿash* "Rent-free grants to holy men and the ʿulama";
  *aimas* "Holy men"; *muhtasibs* "Officers for the enforcement of Islamic law in urban areas"; *jamaʿ* "Land
  revenue" (p. 367, folio 345, footnotes 3–7). *Diwan* "A chancery, concerning civil administration"; *amirs*
  "Governors"; *ʿamil* "A revenue official"; *Baituʾl-mal* "The treasury of the Muslim state" (p. 368, folio 346,
  footnotes 1–4). *Thakurs* "Village leaders subordinate to revenue officials"; *Patwari* "A village revenue
  official" (p. 406, folio 382, footnotes 3–5); *muqaddams* "Traditional village leaders" (p. 418, folio 394).
- **An *ihyaʾ* village and its farman — an anecdote Rizvi rejects outright.** The Khwaja "had an ihya' village near
  Ajmer. The muqta‘ of Ajmer urged him to obtain a royal farman for the land" (p. 143, folio 125); Rizvi: "Undoubtedly
  this anecdote is apocryphal. Firstly, no muqgta‘ [muqta‘] would dare to harass the Khwaja to gain a farman for
  wasteland" (p. 143, folio 125).
- **Religious grantees dodging land tax, and a governor asking a saint to enforce the state's claim.** "A letter from
  'Ainu'l Mulk Mahru to Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din complained that the Saiyids were avoiding paying land revenue tax
  (kKharaj [OCR?] [kharaj]) and asked him to impress on them'their obligations to the state" (p. 301, folio 279);
  "the taxes remitted by the Sultan were not the main source of income which came from kharaj and jizya" (p. 301,
  folio 279).
- **Twenty-eight taxes abolished to launder royal income for religious men.** "**The abolition of about twenty-eight
  taxes by Sultan Firuz Shah** largely imposed on towns throughout the empire may be ascribed to the influence of the
  Saiyid. The lifting of these taxes was designed to make the sources of income of the Sultan legally acceptable to
  the 'ulama'" (p. 301, folio 279), the Sultan thereby "making his stipends holy for the 'ulama' and sufis" (p. 301,
  folio 279).
- **The legal status of a royal stipend, ruled on by a saint — the single most citable sentence on shrine finance and
  state money.** Asked "whether or not it was permissible to accept stipends offered by the Sultans; his answer was
  that according to the books of Fatwas, **it was makruh (disapproved) however it was not invalidated by the
  Shari'a**" (p. 301, folio 279); with the companion rule, "He advised the aima [aʾimma] not to accept any payment
  which came from illegal sources of revenue" (p. 302, folio 280).
- **The tax fight over holy men's grants, and the bargain offered for exemption.** "Apparently Babur had suggested the
  imposition of ʿushr on the wajah-i maʿash of the ʿulamaʾ and the sufis… the imposition of ʿushr upon the wajh-i
  maʿash of these classes should not be permitted and should be considered a heinous sin; he added it was a
  particularly unwise act to ask for money from dervishes. **The tax should be remitted so that all those people who
  would have fallen into such a category could live peacefully and pray for the prosperity of the Emperor and the
  Muslim community**" (p. 367, folio 345); recorded twice more, "Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus' letter protesting the
  imposition of 'ushr on Muslim rent-free grants" (p. 410, folio 386), and "Babur's extravagance necessitated the
  imposition of new taxes including 'ushr on the madad-i ma'ash. Although this distressed the Muslim community, Guru
  Nanak saw it as the divine will" (p. 412, folio 388).
- **A tax on shrines, in Guru Nanak's own hymn — the most citable economy line in Chapter Six.** "'The Primal Being is
  now called Allah; the turn of the Shaikhs hath come. **There is a tax on the shrines of the gods; such is the
  practice established.** There are ablution-pots, calls to prayer, five daily prayers, prayer-carpets, and God
  appeareth dressed in blue. In every house all say Mian (a Muslim title); your language hath been changed'" (p. 411,
  folio 387), dated only in a footnote, "These hymns seem to have been written sometime after 1526" (p. 411, folio
  387, footnote 1).
- **A temple funded by a town's entire tax yield.** "The town's entire revenue from taxes was spent on this temple" —
  the Hindu temple near Adam's footprints in Ceylon, staffed by "three thousand Brahmans and yogis and five hundred
  Devadasis dancing before an idol", on Ibn Battuta's authority (p. 411, folio 387).
- **A state office controlling all religious land and stipends, and grants revoked as a political act.** Bairam Khan
  repaid Shaikh Gadaʾi "by elevating him to the post of Sadru's-Sudur, **the controller of land and stipends granted
  ior [OCR?] [for] religious purposes**, which was accompanied by unprecedented powers. Apparently the Shaikh revoked
  the grants previously given to a number of the 'ulama' and to sufis who had supported the Afghans" (p. 310, folio
  288) — **the one instance in the book of an endowment being taken away**; in disgrace he "returned to Delhi and was
  content to be supported by a minor madad-i ma'ash" (p. 311, folio 289). The office is glossed elsewhere as "Sadru's
  Sudur (chief controller of religious affairs, particularly charities)" (p. 210, folio 192).
- **The Shaikhuʾl-Islam office as an honour paid in stipends and land.** "Shaikhu'l-Islam was not a permanent position
  like Sadru's Sudur … or Qazi ul-Quzat. The Sultans of Delhi conferred the title on religious dignitaries as an
  honour and **recipients obtained both stipends and land**. Incumbents were not obliged to be in constant attendance
  at court and offered only occasional advice to their rulers" (p. 210, folio 192).
- **Confiscation of a khanqah's entire property by the state, on a charge of misappropriating its income.** "the
  governor of Sind accused Shaikh Hud of misappropriating the income of Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din's khangah [khanqah] for his
  own personal use. **The Sultan ordered the governor to seize the entire property**" (p. 234, folio 214), followed by
  the execution of the incumbent (p. 234, folio 214) and the collapse of the Multan establishment (p. 235, folio 215).
  **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam** material.
- **Taxation advice, price control, and a tax policy measured by cultivator migration.** "Tax collectors they believed
  should show consideration in acquiring revenue and in levying the poll tax" (p. 246, folio 226); "the Sultan had
  introduced price control for the welfare of his subjects" (p. 242, folio 222); Malik Dawaruʾl-Mulk "would accept
  only those taxes under his igta' [OCR?] [iqtaʿ] which were permissible under holy law. **Cultivators loved to
  migrate to his igta' [iqtaʿ]** and even the most desolate area in his care tended to prosper" (p. 305, folio 283).
- **Zakat enforcement with spies, and the term defined in the text.** The ruler's 'alim "should ensure the continued
  payment of zakat by Muslim citizens. Severe penalties should accompany any infringement of holy law, including the
  evasion of the payment of zakat"; he is to be "given spies to watch over his subordinates" (p. 275, folio 255);
  *zakat* is "alms given according to Muslim law, by way of purifying or securing a blessing to all of one's
  possession" (p. 241, folio 221).
- **A saint's diet tied to revenue oppression, and rural debt as Kabir saw it.** "If revenue officials made
  depredations on the ra'iyat (ryot) in order to collect revenue, the Maulana would stop eating meat, fearing it
  might have been obtained from illegally seized animals" (p. 284, folio 264); "Thakurs measured the fields and the
  villagers were never free of debts entered in the Patwari's books. To him the most important accounts were those
  with God" (p. 406, folio 382); and "blood-sucking rajas and dog-like mugaddams [OCR?] [muqaddams]" (p. 418, folio
  394).
- **Conquest as land settlement for a sufi following, with booty as income.** Shaikh Jalal "apportioned the land among
  his followers, thus enabling them to take wives and settle down. … Sylhet was the share of Shaikh Nuru'l-Huda Abu'l
  Karamat Sai'di Husaini" (p. 337, folio 315); "The booty gained from their warring enabled them to live luxuriously"
  (p. 336, folio 314).
- **The index's grant-and-revenue runs, cited to the pages they are printed on.** "Madad-i Ma‘ash, 280, 289, 388"
  (p. 474, folio 450); "idrar, 176" (p. 469, folio 445); "Farman, 125, 144, 170, 176-78, 230-32, 252, 271, 308"
  (p. 465, folio 441); "Igta [OCR?] [Iqta], 125, 283" (p. 469, folio 445); "Mugta’ [OCR?] [Muqta‘], 125, 128, 152,
  230, 244" (p. 477, folio 453); "Jizya, 279, 297-98" (p. 471, folio 447); "Kharaj, 279" (p. 472, folio 448);
  "Nawahun, 279-80" (p. 478, folio 454); "Makruh, 279" (p. 474, folio 450); "Ushr, 345-46, 386, 388" (pp. 490–491,
  folios 466–467); "Zimmi, 123, 295-97" (p. 491, folio 467); "Aima, 280, 345", "Amil, 145, 346", "Amir, 346",
  "Amiru't-Tujjar, 410" (pp. 459, 460; folios 435, 436); "Baitu'l-mal, 346" (p. 461, folio 437); "darogha, 122, 279"
  (p. 464, folio 440); "Muhtasih [OCR?] [Muhtasib], 345" (p. 477, folio 453).
- **The decisive negative for this archive.** **The words *waqf* and *Auqaf* appear nowhere in this book's practices
  material; every chunk that looks for them records their absence.** The instruments the book actually names are the
  *farman*, the *iqtaʿ*, the *pargana*, *madad-i maʿash*, *wajah-i maʿash*, *idrar*, *ihyaʾ* and the outright gift of
  villages. **No waqf deed, no waqf board, no Auqaf department and no revenue figure for any shrine appears anywhere
  in 491 pages** — all (not stated).

### G. Custodianship, succession and the sajjada nashin

- **The succession bequest and the origin of the title — the custodianship passage the archive most needs, and it is
  one paragraph long.** "Before a Shaikh's death, he would nominate his spiritual heir and bequeath to him his prayer
  carpet or skin called a sajjada, his subha, his staff and personal khirga [khirqa]. **The heir's title was
  Sajjada-Nashin, in Arabic, Shaikh as-Sajjada. Succession was not necessarily hereditary or a decision of the
  Shaikh, in some orders after a Shaikh's death a khalifa would be elected**" (p. 120, folio 102). **Four regalia —
  sajjada, subha, staff, khirqa — and three succession modes: nomination, heredity, election. This is the only place
  in 491 pages where *sajjada nashin* is defined, and the index confirms it sits on one folio: "Sajjada, 102" and
  "Sajjada-Nashin, 102" (p. 483, folio 459), "Shaikh as-Sajjada, 102" (p. 485, folio 461).**
- **Hereditary succession stated flatly, in three generations of one Multan line.** "He appointed his own son,
  Sadru'd-Din… his khalifa" (p. 220, folio 202); "The successor of Shaikh Sadru'd-Din was his son, Shaikh
  Ruknu'd-Din Abu'l Fath" (p. 230, folio 210). **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya** and **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**
  material; the row treatments belong in their own subsections.**
- **Hereditary succession named and evaluated, and a three-generation line with no property attached.** "Zahid's
  successors formed a hereditary line of spiritual succession traced from Shaikh Qutb-i 'Alam; none of them, however,
  ever reached the stature of Shaikh Akhi Siraj and his two successors" (p. 280, folio 260); and Shaikh Ahmad
  ʿAbduʾl-Haqq → "Shaikh 'Arif, Shaikh Ahmad's son, was his spiritual successor" → "His son, Shaikh Muhammad, was pir
  to the great sixteenth century sufi, Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus Gangohi" (p. 292, folio 272). **The book uses no
  custodial title for either line and attaches no property, income or shrine** — (not stated).
- **A rule against hereditary sufi property, from a saint whose own line became hereditary.** "the Saiyid asserted it
  was fraudulent to adopt asceticism after bequeathing property to a son for such a way of life involved the total
  distribution of all possession to the poor" (p. 300, folio 278) — **and his line did become hereditary in Uch and
  Gujarat (pp. 303–305, folios 281–283). The book prints both and does not remark on the tension.**
- **A contested succession with three claimants, decided by the throne — a sajjada-nashin dispute in all but name.**
  Nephew Shaikh Ismaʿil (per Jamali) against grandson Shaikh Hud (per Ibn Battuta), "The dispute was laid before
  Muhammad bin Tughluq who gave his verdict in favour of Shaikh Hud" (p. 234, folio 214). **No title for the office,
  and the word *sajjada* is not used** — (not stated).
- **A hereditary claim defeated and administration handed to a state appointee — the clearest analogue to an Auqaf
  takeover.** "The dispute over alleged family successors of the Khwaja between Shaikh Husain, who claimed to be a
  descendant, and his rivals, was placed before the Emperor for judgement. The Shaikh's opponents alleged that the
  Khwaja had left no descendants and that the claims of Shaikh Husain were false. The descendants of Shaikh Salim and
  some qazis supported Shaikh Husain's rivals. **After a laborious investigation, Akbar ruled against the Shaikh, and
  transferred the administration of the shrine to Shaikh Muhammad Bukhari, a trusted officer and a member of a
  distinguished family of holy men**" (p. 144, folio 126), sourced "Bada'uni, Muntakhabu't-Tawarikh, Il [OCR?] [II],
  p. 87. For details of earlier disputes see Chapter Four" (p. 144, folio 126, footnote 3). **The same book records
  that the Khwaja did leave sons (p. 142, folio 124), which the opponents' allegation denies; both statements
  stand.**
- **The same descent question settled twice, in opposite directions, centuries apart.** First for descent: "Shaikh
  Husain Nagauri who had also married his daughter to Shaikh Bayazid confirmed that the latter was a descendant of
  Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din and the Sultan accepted this statement" (p. 296, folio 276) — **the confirming witness is the
  claimant's father-in-law, which the book does not remark on.** Then against it: "the board of enquiry which Akbar,
  the great Mughal Emperor, had appointed did not accept the argument that the Khwaja had issue, and **the Emperor
  assumed control over his khangah [OCR?] [khanqah]**" (p. 296, folio 276), cross-referenced "see Chapter Two, supra,
  pp. 125-26". **The occasion of the first was a madrasa attached to a tomb and a contested right to teach at it:**
  "the other teachers … complained to the Sultan that Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din did not have any offspring and Bayazid was
  therefore not a fit person to work at the Ajmer seminary" (p. 296, folio 276).
- **Shrine management as an administrative task, and standing gifts to shrine personnel.** "It would appear that
  Shaikh Muhammad greatly improved the shrine's management while attempting to provide better conditions for
  pilgrims. Moreover, he looked after the erection of mosques and khangahs [khanqahs] in the territory" (p. 144,
  folio 126); "Both Jahangir and Shah Jahan distributed generous gifts to all connected with the Khwaja's tomb"
  (p. 145, folio 127).
- **A succession claimed without the document.** "Although Gisu Daraz did not receive a khilafat-nama from his pir, to
  all intents and purposes l@ [OCR?] [he] was regarded as the Shaikh's khalifa. Later authorities assert that Shaikh
  Nasiru'd-Din chose Gisu Daraz as his successor, but this view was not supported by any contemporary writer"
  (p. 271, folio 251).
- **The khalifa as the mechanism of continuity, with three interchangeable titles and an accusation attached.** "The
  silsila system guaranteed the transmission of mystical knowledge acquired by founders of the order to further
  generations of sufis through their successors or kKhalifas [OCR?] [khalifas]. **The 'ulama' accused the sufis of
  deifying their spiritual teachers (Shaikhs, pirs or murshids)**" (p. 116, folio 98); the same three titles are given
  as equivalents at "its director (Shaikh, Pir or Murshid)" (p. 77, folio 61). **And the licence to enrol treated as
  a burden:** 'Ala'u'd-Din Nili "was allowed to enrol disciples, but found the associated responsibilities too
  onerous" (p. 201, folio 183), with Amir Khwurd on differentiated commissions, "'To one he ordered to remain silent,
  and behind doors. To another he ordered to enrol a large number of disciples. To a third he would order that he
  live amongst the people, accept the suffering they may cause, and remain courteous to them.'" (p. 203, folio 185).
- **The wilaya as a bounded jurisdiction modelled on secular government, and a boundary named on the ground with a
  distance.** "all sufi Shaikhs believed their own spiritual influence should be confined to a limited territory.
  **This conviction also sprang from the current practice whereby governors of different territories respected the
  independence of other administrations**"; "In his own spiritual territory or wilaya the pir looked after the
  material, as well as the spiritual needs, of his disciples" (p. 116, folio 98); and Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din told Sultan
  Firuz "that his prayers had helped the army to reach Sarsuti, but from there onwards was the spiritual domain of
  Shaikh Qutbu'd-Din Munawwar", who answered "'As my brother, Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din has entrusted the care of the
  people to me, I pray God that Delhi may fall into your hands'" — the boundary being "Sarsuti, **about 300
  kilometres from Delhi**" (p. 197, folio 179).
- **A jurisdictional dispute over khanqah territory settled by royal farman, and wilayat as a commercial boundary.**
  "In Delhi he planned to build a khangah [khanqah] near Shaikh Abu Bakr's but the latter, considering it an
  encroachment of his territory, **insisted that a farman first be brought from the Sultan**." (p. 330, folio 308);
  Shaikh Bakhtiyar "continued to trade, but took great care to stay within the boundaries claimed by Shaikh Ahmad as
  his particular domain of spiritual influence" (p. 292, folio 272).
- **Spheres of influence as an inter-order territorial settlement.** "There was no apparent rivalry between the
  Chishti and Suhrawardi saints. An early sufi tradition based on the example of the Apostles in the Apocryphal New
  Testament, had to wisely divide different regions of the sub-continent into spheres of their respective spiritual
  influence and to refrain from interfering with those of others" (p. 236, folio 216). **And deputisation by
  territory:** "The Khwaja appointed Ziya'u'd-Din his deputy in Balkh and left for Ghazna" (p. 139, folio 121).
- **Named honorific offices, and two refusals.** "Shaikhu'l-Islam (Leader of the Muslims)" and "the Pir, or spiritual
  director, of Herat" (p. 93, folio 77); "the Shaikh al-Shuyukh, or chief sufi, of Baghdad" (p. 91, folio 75
  corrected); "Qaziu'l-Quzat (Chief Qazi)" with "Maliku'l-'Ulama' (Prince of the 'Ulama')" (p. 282, folio 262); "The
  Shaikhu'l-Islam of Mandu, Shaikh Mahmud Dihlawi" (p. 295, folio 275). Refused: "The Sultan offered the post of
  Shaikhu'l-Islam to Khwaja Qutbu'd-Din, but after he refused it the office was conferred on Najmu'd-Din Sughra"
  (p. 154, folio 136); and a sultan's imamate, "the only thing left to him were his own prayers which he did not wish
  to waste by acting as the Sultan's Imam" (p. 177, folio 159). Index: "Shaikhu'l-Islam, 135, 149" and "Shaikh
  al-Shuyukh, 75" (p. 485, folio 461).

### H. Initiation and deputisation: baiʿa, khirqa, khilafat-nama, ijaza

- **Baiʿa, with the physical gesture and nothing else.** "Through baiy‘a [OCR?] [baiʿa] or formal initiation,
  disciples were inextricably spiritually linked to their pirs. In order to solemnize the occasion, the pir would
  place his hand on the disciple's head, or alternately, the ritual involved the grasping of each other's hands"
  (p. 120, folio 102). **No words of the oath, no witnesses** — (not stated). Index: "Baiy'a, 101" (p. 461, folio
  437).
- **Initiation described as having no standard at all.** "Disciple initiation was a complex phenomenon. Some pirs
  carefully examined the talents and potential of those who approached them for initiation; others admitted
  disciples indiscriminately to their silsilas. Each order followed its own special methods of training and formulas
  for zikr and contemplation. **No uniform pattern was evolved.**" (p. 117, folio 99).
- **Initiation at a saint's tomb declared invalid — a dated objection to exactly the practice this archive
  documents.** "he was opposed to the initiation customs involving head shaving rituals at the tombs of sufi saints.
  When one of his own sons performed such a ceremony at the foot of Khwaja Qutbu'd-Din Bakhtiyar's tomb, his father
  remarked that such an initiation was invalid. True initiations involved the disciple holding the Shaikh's hand,
  while it was usual for his pir to offer blessings and prayers for the disciple's welfare" (p. 166, folio 148).
  **Against which the same chunk records Baba Farid shaving heads at initiation twice** — "Nizamu'd-Din was
  immediately initiated and his head shaved" (p. 174, folio 156); "The Shaikh renewed his initiation, 'Arif shaved
  his head and once more firmly accepted the sufi path" (p. 172, folio 154) — **and the doctrinal reason is given
  separately: "hair shaving was performed to cleanse one from pride and was therefore an essential part of sufi
  piety" (p. 161, folio 143). The book holds objection and practice side by side, and so does this file.**
- **The khirqa as admitted accretion and as the instrument of transmitted sainthood.** "Shaikh Shihabu'd-Din admitted
  that the wearing of the khirga [OCR?] [khirqa] or sufi robe was also an accretion, but saw it as a symbol of the
  radical aspect of sufi life… **As sufism was founded on a belief in the transmission of wilaya or saintship, the
  granting of a khirga [khirqa] was significant for it was a sign that the recipient had, according to his pir,
  reached a satisfactory religious standard**" (p. 110, folio 92). Its material: "This was not necessarily made of
  wool but invariably was patched" (p. 120, folio 102); physically, "open in front" and able to hold stones on both
  sides (p. 112, folio 94).
- **Two classes of khirqa grant, one open to non-disciples — a blessing-seeking laity written into the institution.**
  "one awarded in recognition of a disciple's personal spiritual achievement or one given by a Shaikh to a person
  seeking the blessings which surrounded it. **There was no necessity for those in the second group to be sufi
  disciples**, but they should at least observe the Shari'a and keep company with sufis. After the receipt of a
  khirga [khirqa] the blessings accrued from it might lead to a total acceptance of the mystic path" (p. 110, folio
  92).
- **Khirqas received twice from two masters, carried by a third party, and exchanged between equals.** "Abu Sa'id
  obtained a khirga [OCR?] [khirqa] (a cloak which marked sufi initiation) from the celebrated mystic writer,
  al-Sulami. He also received another khirga, this time from an Amul sufi, Abu'l-'Abbas al-Qassab" (p. 85, folio 69);
  "Isfara'ini's khirga [OCR?] [khirqa] was brought to him by the local sufi Sa'du'llah" (p. 269, folio 249) —
  **investiture without the pir present**; and "they exchanged khirgas [khirqas]" (p. 306, folio 284) — **the only
  reciprocal khirqa in the book.**
- **The khilafat-nama defined: a signed, sealed and witnessed instrument.** "Some distinguished disciples who had been
  endowed with khirgas [OCR?] [khirqas] were also sent by their Shaikhs to act as deputies or khalifas at different
  places during their lifetimes. **They were given a licence or diploma called an ijaza or khilafat-nama, authorizing
  recipients to disseminate the principles and practices of their respective orders. Each khilafat-nama was formally
  signed and sealed by the Shaikh and witnessed by one or two of his important disciples**" (p. 120, folio 102).
  Index: "Ijaza, 102" (p. 469, folio 445); "Khilafat- [OCR?] [the headword itself is broken, presumably
  *Khilafat-nama* or *Khilafat*], 102, 148-49, 153-54, 156-57, 163, 176, 178, 251, 297" (p. 472, folio 448).
- **The document as physical, forgeable and tearable, with a counter-signature that was a real veto.** Copies were
  prepared by Maulana Badru'd-Din Ishaq, "who prepared copies of the khilafat-nama was required to sign them, and
  they were later endorsed by the Baba's senior disciple, Shaikh Jamalu'd-Din, who lived at Hansi" (p. 167, folio
  149), the reason being a forgery threat by a rejected disciple (pp. 166–167, folios 148–149); and "Once Shaikh
  Jamal refused to endorse the khilafat-nama which the Baba had granted a disciple, under some pressure. Shaikh Jamal
  was so angry that he tore the paper containing the khilafat-nama to pieces, while informing the disciple he was
  unworthy of such an honour. Baba Farid supported Shaikh Jamalu'd-Din's actions telling the disciple he was unable
  to mend what Jamal had rent apart" (p. 171, folio 153). **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar** material.
- **The content of an actual khilafat-nama, and who its holder had to show it to.** "It recorded that Muhammad, son of
  Ahmad, had studied the Tamhidu'l-Muhtadi of Abu Shakur with great precision under his direction, and was now
  authorized to teach. Moreover, he was permitted to disseminate the Baba's teachings he had acquired and was advised
  to lead an ascetic life" (p. 175, folio 157); he was to show it to "Maulana Jamalu'd-Din at Hansi, and to Qazi
  Muntajabu'd-Din at Delhi" (p. 175, folio 157); "The copy of the khilafat-nama given to him by the Baba is also
  preserved" (p. 166, folio 148).
- **A model khilafat-nama drafted, copied, double-signed, dated — and immediately disputed.** "A model khilafat-nama
  was drafted by Maulana Fakhru'd-Din Zarradi, the copies were produced by Saiyid Husain, the uncle of Amir Khwurd,
  and signed by both the copyist and Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din Auliya'. **They are dated 20 Zu'lhijja 724/8 December 1324,
  but among the Chishtis a controversy developed over their authenticity, and it was alleged that they were not
  signed by the Shaikh while in a state of full consciousness**" (p. 181, folio 163).
- **One written by the Shaikh's own hand, with its text of instruction.** For Qazi Muhi'u'd-Din Kashani: "'Lead the
  life of an ascetic; pay no attention to things of this world and its authorities. **Do not accept gifts from
  rulers.** When you have visitors and have nothing to offer them, consider this a great blessing and favour from
  Allah.'" (pp. 194–195, folios 176–177).
- **Written authority with the document unnamed, and one used as an argument about seniority.** "and was himself given
  written authority to enrol disciples" (p. 249, folio 229) — **the book does not use the word *khilafat-nama* and
  does not describe the document** (not stated); "Although Mir Muhammad was the son of Mir Saiyid 'Ali Hamadani, he
  was Hisari's junior and had even accepted from the veteran sufi, a khilafat-nama." (p. 319, folio 297), sourced
  "Saiyid 'Ali, Turikh-i [OCR?] [Tarikh-i] Kashmir, f. 10b".
- **One khilafat dated, three undated.** "In 804/1401-02, he was appointed a khalifa by Shaikh Nur Qutb-i 'Alam"
  (p. 284, folio 264); against Shah Saidu (p. 285, folio 265), Shaikh Shamsuʾd-Din Turk (p. 290, folio 270), and "The
  Shaikh was highly impressed and gave Mu‘inu'd-Din a khirga [OCR?] [khirqa], appointing him his khalifa" (p. 138,
  folio 120).
- **Succession transmitted by relics and by garments, with one object staying in use.** Qazi Hamidu'd-Din Nagauri
  "gave Baba Farid relics from the Khwaja, including his khirga [OCR?] [khirqa], turban, stick and wooden sandals"
  (p. 158, folio 140); "Khwaja Qutbu'd-Din's stick rested behind his head as a pillow" (p. 159, folio 141);
  Ruknu'd-Din at four donned Baha'u'd-Din's turban and on succeeding "he wore the same turban and Shaikh
  Sadru'd-Din's khirga [khirqa]" (p. 231, folio 211); and turban-tying as a public ceremony, the convert 'Ali "was
  present at the ceremony of the turban-tying of Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din Auliya'" (p. 219, folio 201).
- **A certificate of learning as a parallel document, dated to the day.** "On 22 Rabi' I 679/22 July 1280, the Maulana
  gave the Shaikh a certificate confirming that he had studied under him the Mashariqu'l-Anwar… The certificate also
  referred to Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din as Magqbul al-Masha'ikh al-Kibar [OCR?] [Maqbul al-Masha'ikh al-Kibar] (Approved One
  of the Great Sufis)" (p. 177, folio 159).
- **Indiscriminate initiation as deliberate policy, with two stated reasons, against the careful pirs of p. 117.**
  Imposing "difficult conditions" would deprive disciples "of even that level of piety", and "**The second reason for
  my accepting so many disciples is the fact that Shaikh Faridu'd-Din once gave me a pot of ink and ordered me to
  write amulets for the people**" (pp. 192–193, folios 174–175); with Simnani's version, "sufis should enrol disciples
  in large numbers irrespective of their varying competence and abilities; pirs were like fowlers and could not always
  expect to catch a falcon" (p. 270, folio 250).
- **Probation with stated lengths, and menial service prescribed as a cure for scholarly conceit.** The three-year
  probation and its yearly curriculum, on Hujwiri's authority (p. 117, folio 99) — **data-darbar** material, treated
  in that row's subsection**; "For eight years Shaikh Nuru'l-Haqq brought fuel to the khangah [khanqah], and performed
  such menial services as washing the inmates' clothes" (p. 278, folio 258); "Sharafu'd-Din ordered him to act as a
  menial domestic at the khangah [khanqah], serving other dervishes so that the conceit which usually overpowered an
  'alim … could be replaced by humility" (p. 257, folio 237).
- **Double and multiple initiation, a rule broken, and a stated limit.** "Although Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya had
  insisted during his lifetime that sufis join one order only, from the fourteenth century sufis were often initiated
  into both the Chishtiyya and the Suhrawardiyya… **In such a situation, however, there remained a dominant interest
  and allegiance to one main order**" (pp. 292–293, folios 272–273); the extreme case being Simnani, whose Chishti
  initiation at Pandua "was his last initiation as he had already become a member of every existing sufi order"
  (p. 287, folio 267); and a disciple choosing which to claim, "Shaikh Budhan seems to have been initiated into both
  the Chishtiyya and Suhrawardiyya orders. Qutban preferred to call himself a Suhrawardi" (p. 389, folio 367).
- **Two initiations without a living pir, one given the technical term.** "The Mir claimed that in a vision the Khwaja
  had made him his disciple" (p. 384, folio 362); "Some authors imply that Kabir had no earthly guru and like a Uwaisi
  sufi, was mystically initiated by God" (p. 398, folio 374); the category dated, "From the eleventh century onwards,
  sufis who did not obtain initiation from a particular preceptor called themselves Uwaysis" (p. 41, folio 25).
- **Permission from the pir as a precondition of migration.** "he received his khirga [khirqa] in 588/1192-93 in the
  Ghazna region, and later left for India after having first obtained permission from Shaikh 'Usman" (p. 137, folio
  119, footnote 2).
- **The index's dress-and-succession runs, cited to the pages they are printed on.** "Khirga [OCR?] [Khirqa], 69, 84,
  91-92, 101-2, 120, 140, 187, 188, 205, 223, 248, 263, 265, 283, 319" and "Khirga-poshan-i Kham [OCR?]
  [Khirqa-poshan-i Kham], 309" (p. 472, folio 448); "Muraqga’ (khirga) [OCR?] [Muraqqa‘ (khirqa)], 25, 101" (p. 477,
  folio 453); "Khalifa, 97, 102, 110, 120, 150, 154, 175-88, 190, 202, 205, 224, 246, 251, 257, 265" (p. 472, folio
  448); "Subha, 102" and "Tasbih, 101, 247" (pp. 491, 485; folios 467, 461).

### I. The state: patronage, adjudication, seizure and persecution

- **The book's structural claim about who funds whom.** "The political upheavals from the tenth to the thirteenth
  centuries reinforced sufi beliefs in the transitory nature of the world and the necessity to remain both apart and
  independent from it. By contrast, the 'ulama,' dependent on the state economically, became embroiled in political
  activities. **Sufis were now in a position to remain independent from the government for merchants and craft guilds
  assumed positions as their patrons**" (p. 84, folio 68). **A two-sector model: state-funded ʿulamaʾ against
  merchant-funded sufis, stated as fact with the Abu Saʿid stories as its evidence.**
- **The general rule on sufis and rulers.** "In general, sufis avoided the courts of their rulers and the company of
  the governing classes. However, they did not hesitate to remind the élite, whenever the opportunity arose, that the
  common Muslims had been divinely entrusted to their care" (p. 77, folio 61).
- **The direction of visiting prescribed — and contradicted throughout the book.** Dervishes should "visit rulers,
  government officials and the rich in order to elicit assistance for the deprived"; but "**Dervishes, however,
  should never allow such people to visit them at their Ahangahs [khanqahs]**" (p. 302, folio 280). **Against the
  Caliph at Dust Dada's (p. 91, folio 75 corrected), Sultan Firuz at Sharafu'd-Din's in 762/1360 (p. 250, folio 230),
  Sultan Firuz kept waiting and given "no special treatment" (p. 204, folio 186), Humayun at Gangoh (p. 368, folio
  346) and Ulugh Khan at Ajodhan (p. 162, folio 144).**
- **The shaikh as broker between subject and throne, as a routine of the palanquin, generalised to an order, and
  explained.** "the people of Delhi would place petitions inside the Shaikh's takht-i rawan"; "the Sultan would write
  sympathetic replies"; "the Shaikh would remain at court until all the requests had been granted" (p. 232, folio
  212); "**Through the Suhrawardis petitions from the people were presented to rulers and their periodic visits to
  Delhi were eagerly awaited**" (p. 422, folio 398); and "As there was no official avenue through which grievances of
  the common people could be registered, the only alternative was to seek assistance from members of sufi orders"
  (p. 116, folio 98).
- **Court protocol for receiving a Shaikh, in three stages.** "The court was entered after passing through three
  vestibules; the Shaikh would travel through the first two on his palanquin, and would then be greeted by the Sultan
  in the third. He would be taken inside where the Sultan would squat respectfully at his feet" (p. 232, folio 212).
- **The mahzar as an institution: three convenings, with bench, chairman, procedure and verdicts.** Over samaʿ: "the
  Sultan organized a mahzar. **Over two hundred and fifty scholars** were lined up to oppose the Shaikh"; the
  procedural objection, "the Qazi objected to his argument on the grounds that the Shaikh was not a mujtahid and
  therefore not entitled to quote hadis as an authority. Instead he should support his argument from the statement of
  Imam Abu Hanifa (699-767)"; the outcome, "Sultan Ghiyasu'd-Din Tughlug [Tughluq] refused to give a judgement, and
  the Shaikh and his followers were allowed to depart honourably", and he "later dismissed Qazi Ruknu'd-Din Walwalji"
  (p. 180, folio 162). A second, with a quorum: "The Sultan organized a mahzar to investigate the allegation. **About
  two hundred eminent sufis and 'ulama' were invited**"; "The Sultan said that such respect by a chairman for the
  accused had made the mahzar useless" (p. 218, folio 200). A third, settled by performing the thing at issue: "the
  Shaikh ordered his servants to start playing music. This is reported as having moved everyone at the mahzar to
  ecstasy" (p. 214, folio 196) — **which Rizvi then rejects as anachronistic.**
- **Judicial machinery used against sufis: mahzar, fatwa, farman, execution.** Sultan Firuz "convene[d] a mahzar"; the
  two accused were "condemned in a fatwaissued [fatwa issued] by the 'ulama' and later executed" (p. 251, folio 231);
  against Sharafu'd-Din "A farman was issued authorizing this, but was cancelled" (p. 251, folio 231); Sher Khan "was
  beheaded, on a fatwa from the 'ulama.'" (p. 261, folio 241). **Rizvi's institutional reading: "it was not unusual
  for the Sultan to find himself helpless in religious matters against the power of the 'ulama.'" (pp. 261–262, folios
  241–242).**
- **A Sultan ordering an investigation of a living saint under the Shariʿa, on a written complaint with a named menu
  of offences.** "'A certain man has come hither from Mayhana and pretends to be a sufi… **He holds sumptuous feasts
  and music is played by his orders, whilst the young men dance and eat sweetmeats and roasted fowls and all kinds of
  fruit.**'" (p. 87, folio 71); "The Sultan ordered leading Shafi'i and Hanafi 'ulama' to make a thorough investigation
  of Abu Sa'id and, if guilty, to punish him according to the Shari'a… Feeling trapped, the 'ulama' decided to drop
  their case" (p. 87, folio 71).
- **A state expulsion of dervishes by arrest and boat, with urban unrest as the result.** At Pandua "Ghiyasu'd-Din
  A'zam Shah … had embarked on a scheme to expel all galandars [OCR?] [qalandars] and dervishes from the town. Possibly
  this was part of an attempt to purge non-Bengalis from the administration" (p. 290, folio 270); "Next morning the
  king ordered the Muslim mystics to leave Pandua. All were arrested and escorted to boats which took them to exile.
  **This action led to great unrest in the town**" (pp. 290–291, folios 270–271).
- **A dated state execution of dervishes, and an unlawful one with the ʿulamaʾ objecting on procedure.** Tughril "gave
  the qalandar and his followers a huge quantity of gold, enabling them to wear gold necklaces, bangles and ear-rings
  instead of iron"; **in 1281 the qalandars "were hung from gibbets"** (p. 326, folio 304); "Arkali Khan, one of the
  royal princes, beckoned the elephant drivers who trampled Sidi to death. Such an unlawful execution was deplored by
  the orthodox" (p. 331, folio 309); "The 'ulama' argued that trial by fire was nowhere sanctioned by the Shari'a."
  (p. 331, folio 309). **With its stated consequences:** "The immediate repercussions were an unprecedented dust storm
  which swept through Delhi and a severe drought and famine throughout the empire… Starving people began to commit
  suicide in groups of twenties and thirties by drowning themselves in the Jamna." (p. 331, folio 309).
- **Forced relocation of the religious classes, dated three ways and given a purpose.** "In 1327 Sultan Muhammad bin
  Tughluq … forced the 'ulama' and sufis to migrate to Daulatabad. Amir Khwurd was a reluctant participant in this
  mass exodus of the élite. After a few years, along with others, he was allowed to return" (p. 23, folio 9); "In the
  days when Sultan Muhammad bin Tughluq was sending people from Delhi to Daulatabad (1326-27)" (p. 200, folio 182);
  "he had ordained that all leading sufis and 'ulama' remain in Daulatabad" (p. 201, folio 183); the stated purpose,
  "to transfer most of the 'ulama' and sufis of Delhi to Daulatabad, in order both to undermine their influence in
  Delhi and to populate the Deccan with a Muslim élite", with "**The compulsive nature of the migration of the Muslim
  élite aroused popular feeling against the government**" (p. 193, folio 175).
- **A state attempt to compel congregational attendance, refused.** Mubarak Shah "prohibited his nobles from visiting
  Ghiyaspur" and "also constructed a mosque, the Masjid-i Miri, where all the sufis and 'ulama' were ordered to
  perform their prayers. The Shaikh refused to comply with the Sultan's orders, remonstrating that the mosque in his
  neighbourhood had a greater claim on him" (pp. 178–179, folios 160–161).
- **Patronage as revocable employment — a mosque post and a house, both withdrawn.** "Shaikh Najibu'd-Din accepted the
  post of Imam of a mosque built by a Turkic officer in Delhi who also gave the Shaikh a house to live in. After the
  Turk spent 100,000 jira/s [OCR?] [jitals] at his daughter's wedding, the Shaikh suggested that if this money were
  used on God's work, it would earn a large amount of spiritual reward. **Indignant, the Turk dismissed him from the
  mosque and seized the house**" (p. 169, folio 151).
- **Shaikhs as king-makers, at two strengths.** "Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din, one of the leading king-makers, entreated the new
  Sultan to rule with justice, and in turn received a promise to that effect" (p. 204, folio 186); against "According
  to Mulla 'Abdu'l-Qadir Bada'uni, Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din had installed Firuz on the throne" (p. 204, folio 186),
  **which Rizvi then softens with a conjecture of his own.**
- **A saint's forgiveness overriding the state's criminal jurisdiction, with a payment to the assailant.** "The high
  government officials and Sultan Firuz were unable to punish the assailant due to the insistence of his victim. Turab
  was able to leave Delhi unmolested, even by a mob infuriated by the attack on the city's beloved Shaikh" (p. 205,
