  folio 187); "The Shaikh paid Turab twenty silver tankas and sought his forgiveness lest the latter had suffered some
  injury while wielding his knife" (p. 205, folio 187).
- **Sufis in government office: compelled, defended, and condemned — three positions, unreconciled.** "it would seem
  that Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din and other eminent Chishti sufis were compelled to accept government posts… on another
  occasion he was appointed his jamadar", footnoted "A servant who presents clothes to his master" (p. 203, folio 185);
  "Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din Mahmud advocated that the government service was not necessarily an obstacle to contemplation
  and meditation", quoting "'The essence of sufism is not an external garment. / Gird up your loins to serve the Sultan
  and be a sufi.'" (p. 207, folio 189); and government service "made people authoritarian, reckless, greedy and cruel
  and… involved a great deal of dependence on worldly authorities and was therefore contrary to the sufi trust in God"
  (p. 243, folio 223). **With a resignation rule and a named lowest profession:** "If a master ordered a disciple to
  perform duties which were forbidden by religion, he should resign his post… **Accountants were singled out as being
  members of the lowliest of all professions, for they were constantly involved in the counting of money**" (p. 275,
  folio 255).
- **Royal gifts restoring khanqahs and royal gifts ruining them — two statements the book does not reconcile.** "Sultan
  Firuz did not forget the sufis and showered gifts on their khangahs [OCR?] [khanqahs] **which resulted in the usual
  strains associated with material prosperity**. The jama'at-khana of the Sultanu'l-Masha'ikh remained firmly
  entrenched in its traditions of poverty and austerity" (p. 204, folio 186); and "**Large gifts from Sultan Firuz to
  the Chishti order restored the glory of many khangahs [khanqahs]**" (p. 207, folio 189).
- **A khanqah's expenditure as a political liability, punished by exile.** "Liberal amounts of money were spent by
  Shaikh 'Ala'u'l-Haqq in his khangah [khanqah]. This annoyed the Sultan who imagined that the Shaikh received finances
  from his father who controlled the royal treasury and the Shaikh was duly exiled to Sunargaon, where he remained for
  two years" (p. 277, folio 257).
- **A disciple assigned to a campaign for spiritual protection and left there afterwards.** An officer sent "to conquer
  Chanderi asked the Shaikh to send a disciple to accompany him to battle, to give him spiritual protection. Maulana
  Yusuf was assigned the task and after the war on the Shaikh's orders he remained in Chanderi" (p. 195, folio 177).
- **Samaʿ commissioned by the state as a rain ritual, with a division of labour stated by the throne.** "The Sultan …
  sent a message to the dervishes that **his business was war and theirs was to pray for the welfare of the people**,
  therefore they should pray for rain. Qazi Hamidu'd-Din advised the Sultan to arrange an entertainment for the
  dervishes in order that they might perform sama'. The Sultan was pleased, and after the sama' rain fell heavily"
  (p. 216, folio 198). **And a Sultan settling a ritual dispute in the sufis' favour while using them politically:**
  "Unwavering in their opposition to sama', the 'ulama' were over-ruled by Iltutmish who supported the sufis, using
  them as a counter to the former" (pp. 153–154, folios 135–136).
- **A ruler's shrine visit made the hinge of a fiscal policy by a contemporary historian.** After Salar Masʿud appeared
  to Firuz Shah in a dream at Bahraich, "Next morning, in the manner of a sufi novice the Sultan shaved his head and a
  large number of Khans and Maliks followed suit. **'Afif traces the beginning of the orthodoxy of Sultan Firuz Shah
  and the abolition of non-Islamic taxes to this period**" (p. 334, folio 312).
- **A state ban on a pilgrimage procession, and its failure.** "Although Sikandar Lodi banned the processions of
  pilgrims carrying spears on their annual visit to the tomb of Salar Mas'ud, the festival was only stopped
  temporarily" (p. 334, folio 312), sourced "Tahagat-i Akbari [OCR?] [Tabaqat-i Akbari], p. 336; Tarikh-i Dawudi,
  p. 38".
- **A puritan emperor visiting a shrine anyway, dated to the day.** "On 14 Rabi' II, 1069/9 January 1659 while marching
  against his rival Prince Shuja', the puritanically orthodox Aurangzeb yet saw fit to visit the tomb" of Shah Madar at
  Makanpur (p. 341, folio 319).
- **Imperial pilgrimage: four journeys, three dated to the day, one made on foot as a thanksgiving for a birth.**
  Akbar's devotion began "after hearing the songs of some minstrels at Midhakur, near Agra, glorifying the Khwaja";
  "He commenced his journey on 14 January 1562"; "After his Chitor conquest, Akbar made a pilgrimage to Ajmer on 6
  March 1568"; "In February 1570, Akbar reached Ajmer by foot from Agra to offer thanks for the birth of Prince Salim
  on 30 August 1569"; "Early in 1614, Akbar's successor, Jahangir, stayed in Ajmer" (p. 144, folio 126).
- **Two dynasties giving to one tomb, with no sums.** "After his visit Muhammad bin Tughlug gave liberally to Salar
  Mas'ud's tomb, but the reforming Sultan Firuz Shah Tughluq was reported to have gone to extreme limits in exhibiting
  his devotion to the tomb. In 776/1374—75 he visited Bahraich and stayed there for some time" (p. 334, folio 312).
  **No sum, no land grant and no named office** — (not stated).
- **A state programme written by a Suhrawardi Shaikhuʾl-Islam, printed without editorial comment.** Rulers "should
  promote Islamic customs, promulgate the commands of the Shari'a … and uproot kufr (infidelity), shirk (polytheism)
  and idolatry", failing which "they should make every effort to disgrace and humiliate Hindus, mushriks (polytheists)
  and idolaters", "should not tolerate the sight of Hindus, and in particular they should exterminate the Brahmans";
  prostitutes "should be compelled to practise their trade secretly"; "Philosophers should be banished"; "Shi'is,
  should be mercilessly disgraced and should not receive government posts"; and "Justice should be strictly dispensed,
  but it is only possible if the dread and fear of the king uproots tyranny and tyrants." (pp. 212–213, folios
  194–195). **Rizvi's own judgement on the attribution of these four principles is recorded under "The two orders contrasted" below, and must travel with any citation of them.**
- **Two more fiscal-communal programmes, from Chishtis.** "No kafir should be appointed to any post in the diwan of a
  Muslim capital or should hold offices such as amirs and ʿamil. They should receive no financial assistance from the
  government, and should live in a miserable condition… **their worship should be in secret** and they should not
  openly indulge in heretical practices… Equal treatment with Muslims was not to be given in the interests of Islam"
  (p. 368, folio 346); and "In another letter the Shaikh advised the Sultan that high government posts should not be
  given to Hindus", arguing "An infidel may be entrusted with some work but he should not be made Wali (chief
  supervisor or Governor) so that he may have control over and impose his authority on Muslims" (p. 259, folio 239).
- **The *zimmi* category defined, with Hindus and Buddhists expressly at issue — directly relevant to how the archive's
  Hindu, Sikh and Jain sites stood in Muslim law.** "Ahl al-Zimma, a non-Muslim subject of a Muslim state… Idolaters,
  however, were not regarded as zimmis. On the analogy that the idol worshippers of Arabia were not given the status of
  zimmis, many jurists also refused to regard Hindus or Buddhists as zimmis. **From the time of the earliest conquest
  of the Arabs by Muhammad bin Qasim in 711-13, Hindus and Buddhists, however, have been recognized as zimmis**"
  (p. 141, folio 123, footnote 1).
- **Six clauses of the alleged "covenant of ʿUmar" that bear on shrines, temples and burial, printed in full by the
  book** (pp. 317–318, folios 295–296): "1. They (the Hindus) will not build new idol temples."; "2. They will not
  rebuild any existing temple which may have fallen into disrepair."; "3. Muslim travellers will not be prevented from
  staying in temples."; "4. Muslim travellers will be provided hospitality by zimmis in their own houses for three
  days."; "18. They will not carry or bury their dead near Muslim graveyards."; "19. They will not mourn their dead
  loudly." **Clauses 18 and 19 are the book's only material on non-Muslim burial custom and mourning, and they are
  prohibitions.**
- **Fiscal and legal measures against Hindus in Kashmir, and their reversal.** "Ancient temples in Pompur, Vijabror,
  Martand, Anantnag, Sopur and Baramula were desecrated and demolished; many puritanical and discriminatory laws were
  introduced and **the jizya or poll tax was imposed on Hindus for the first time in Kashmir**." (p. 319, folio 297);
  then the Sultan "abolished turushkadanda (jizya)" (p. 319, folio 297); under Zainuʾl-ʿAbidin the policy "included
  restoration of devastated temples" (p. 320, folio 298); under Fath Shah "Temples were demolished, land confiscated
  and non-Islamic Kashmiris were forcibly converted to Shi'ism" (p. 321, folio 299).
- **Persecution of sufis and book-burning.** "the persecution of sufis who publicly advocated the Wujudi doctrines"
  under Firuz Shah (p. 268, folio 248); "In Bukhara, the works of Ibn al-'Arabi were burned" (p. 270, folio 250); "the
  study of Muhasibi's writings was banned" (p. 68, folio 52).
- **The earliest layer's record of church–state friction, four named instances and the state's instruments.** Tirmizi
  "retired to Nishapur" after conflict "with the authorities of his own town" (p. 58, folio 42); "persecution by the
  orthodox forced Sahl to take refuge in Basra" (p. 62, folio 46); Zuʾn-Nun, "In 214/829 he was accused of heresy,
  arrested and sent to Baghdad. After examination the Caliph had him released" (p. 63, folio 47); Bayazid "was forced
  to hide because of the enmity of orthodox elements in Bastam" (p. 59, folio 43). Hallaj was imprisoned "in 301/913"
  for "nine years" and "hung on a gibbet after various revolting and merciless tortures" on "29 Zu'l-Qa'da 309/1 April
  922" (pp. 73–74, folios 57–58); "For some time Shibli was committed to a mental asylum because of his ideas" (p. 75,
  folio 59).
- **The doctrinal weapon named.** "The movement's esoteric and ascetic practices and its members' indulgence in music
  and dancing also provided the theologians with opportunities to crush sufi activities" (p. 78, folio 62); and *hulul*
  "was to become the most convenient and dangerous weapon in the ʿulamaʾ armoury with which to denounce and suppress
  sufism" (p. 78, folio 62).
- **Quietism prescribed, and contradicted in the same book.** "'violent actions of tyrants were a punishment sent by God
  which could not be opposed by the sword but must be endured with patience'" (p. 43, folio 27); against the ʿulamaʾ
  who "managed to overthrow the Sultan himself in 1242" (p. 30, folio 16); and "Sufis kept themselves aloof from both
  the administrative machinery and imperialist wars of the government" (p. 117, folio 99), **which the book contradicts
  elsewhere, and both stand.**
- **A ruler's public grievance proclamation and his own judicial procedure.** Firuz "had it proclaimed throughout the
  city of Delhi that whosoever might have any grievance or claims against Firoz, the slave of God, might come and put
  forward his charges and claims which he would satisfy" (p. 258, folio 238); "he enquired about the opinion and the
  'Fatwas' of the Qazis, and the Muftis had to cite precedents after which he gave his award" (p. 258, folio 238).

### J. Merchants, guilds, caravans, and 'spiritual insurance'

- **'Spiritual insurance' — the book's most quotable economic institution, and it is Rizvi's own term.** "Some khangahs
  [khanqahs] operated a type of **'spiritual insurance' scheme in which financial pledges were made by merchants in
  return for sufi prayers for protection during a journey, instalments being collected en route.** The chain of
  khangahs of Shaikh Abu Ishaq from Kazirun to China is one case in point. Naturally the system was one-sided and
  hardly compares with a modern insurance scheme. **If the prayers of a great sufi saint failed, the unfortunate
  merchants and travellers were killed, and there was no means by which the money advanced could be restored**"
  (p. 423, folio 399).
- **The vow-economy behind it, in Ibn Battuta's words, and it is a footnote.** Travellers on the Sea of China make vows
  and "each one of them sets down in writing the obligation he has undertaken in his vow. Then when they come safely to
  land, the servitors of the hospice go on board the ship, take the inventory, and exact (the amount of) his vow from
  each person who has pledged himself. **There is not a ship that comes from China or from India but has thousands of
  dinars in it (vowed to the saint), and the agents on behalf of the intendant of the hospice come to take delivery of
  that sum.**" Poor brethren "receive a written order for some amount, sealed with the die into red wax", the order
  "being for a thousand or a hundred dirhams, or some intermediate or smaller sum, according to the standing of the poor
  brother concerned"; the mendicant "takes from him and writes for him a receipt for the amount on the back of the
  order"; and "**The king of India (probably Muhammad bin Tughluq) once vowed ten thousand dinars to the Shaikh Abu
  Ishaq**" (p. 129, folio 111, footnote 1). **A written vow, an inventory, collecting agents, an intendant, a sealed
  red-wax draft, an endorsement receipt and a royal donation of stated size — with "(probably Muhammad bin Tughluq)"
  marked by the notes as Rizvi's or the translator's conjecture.**
- **The collection network named and located, with the author's own admission of its gaps.** "Many sufis of the Kaziruni
  orders began to travel from India to China to collect money promised by sailors and merchants to the order" (p. 433,
  folio not stated); "money pledged to Shaikh Abu Ishaq Kaziruni was paid to the khangah in Calicut. Its Shaikh was
  Shihabu'd-Din Kaziruni, and all gifts promised to his order's founder were presented to him" (p. 434, folio 410);
  "khanqahs established by the disciples of Shaikh Abu Ishaq Kaziruni were not only centres for the collection of money
  and for hostel accommodation to travellers, but also gave the local people in these areas a greater exposure to the
  ideas of Islam" (p. 434, folio 410); "There may well have been other minor Kaziruni khanqahs in the islands which Ibn
  Battuta fails to mention" (p. 434, folio 410).
- **Merchants as the financial base, stated four times.** (pp. 116–117, folios 98–99; p. 84, folio 68; p. 242, folio 222;
  p. 331, folio 309 — "the funds were provided by the Khan-i Khanan and by merchants from abroad").
- **Khanqahs as nodes of craft-guild organisation.** "The Irani khangahs [khanqahs] also became important links between
  the Kashmiri and Irani artisan and craft guilds (asnaf)." (p. 319, folio 297); behind it, "the organizations of
  Anatolian and Iranian dervishes who themselves were either members of, or associated with, merchant and artisan
  guilds" (p. 316, folio 294). Index: "Isnaf (the artisan guilds), 16, 297" (p. 470, folio 446) — **one of the few index
  entries that supplies its own gloss.**
- **The Akhi/Futuwwa hospice economy, quoted at length — the fullest description of a sufi institution's daily finances
  in the book.** "The Akhi builds a hospice and furnishes it with rugs, lamps, and what other equipment it required. His
  associates work during the day to gain their livelihood, and after the afternoon prayer they bring him their
  collective earnings; with this they buy fruit, food and the other things needed for consumption in the hospice. If,
  during that day. a traveller alights at the town, they give him lodging with them… If no newcomer arrives, they
  assemble themselves to partake of the food, and after eating they sing and dance" (p. 303, folio 281, note 2); "The
  members are called fityan. and their leader, as we have said, is the Akhi." **Rizvi's own limit on it: the Akhis "were
  also warriors of the faith" and "the Akhi system never really penetrated into India" (p. 303, folio 281).** The
  Futuwwa's convergence with Sufism is dated: "From the time of the eighth century, both movements, the Futuwwa and the
  sufi, drew closer together in mutual assistance and respect" (p. 45, folio 29). Index: "Futuwwa, 16, 29, 130, 281,
  293-95", "Risala-i Futuwwa, 293-94" (p. 465, folio 441); "Akhi, 16, 281, 293, 295" (p. 459, folio 435).
- **Rizvi's own framing of the Dust Dada story.** "The story of Dust Dada is significant, however, as it serves to
  illustrate the interrelation between khanqahs, caravans, merchants and sufis" (p. 91, folio 75 corrected).
- **Why merchants carried preachers and mystics — three stated reasons, an account of what a travelling sufi was for.**
  "Firstly, as experts in religious law and the practical side of theology they acted as Imams in congregational
  prayers, as peace-makers and as judges in solving disputes involving the Shari'a. Secondly, they offered spiritual
  comfort to boat passengers undertaking voyages which were often hair-raising and dangerous. Thirdly, they were useful
  as intermediaries between merchants and local authorities whenever the former were involved in political or economic
  crises" (p. 433, folio not stated).
- **Reputation explained by trade routes, four times.** Herat, "Strategically located on the trade routes between the
  Mediterranean Sea and India and China… proved a natural source from which the fame of its leading sufi was to spread
  widely" (p. 93, folio 77); "Tirmiz was the greatest emporium of goods coming from the north to Khurasan" (p. 56, folio
  40); "Nishapur was the leading trade centre of Khurasan and featured a daily traffic of caravans" (p. 58, folio 42);
  "Baghdad … was a junction of caravan routes" (p. 65, folio 49).
- **Trade endorsed with an ethics and a pricing rule, and sufis in ordinary trades.** "The Chishtis encouraged people to
  indulge in trade and commerce for their livelihood. They also approved of agriculture and the pursuit of crafts.
  However, these occupations were to be followed honestly and were not permitted to interfere with spiritual exercises"
  (p. 241, folio 221); a merchant "should never lie about the price paid by him for any article, but should specify the
  correct price and be satisfied with a small profit which, in the long run, would make him rich" (p. 241, folio 221);
  "Many eminent sufis worked as petty traders, artisans or cultivators, to enable them to remain part of the community,
  rather than becoming inhabitants of ivory towers" (p. 117, folio 99).
- **Caravan protection as a purchased service, and its pious substitute.** "A pious merchant was travelling in Marw
  region accompanied, not in the traditional manner by a hired, protective escort, but by a professional reader of the
  Qurʾan" (p. 53, folio 37). **And a three-part annual cycle of pilgrimage, war and trade:** "In alternate years he
  would perform a hajj, and go to war, and during the third year, he would engage in commerce, distributing his profits
  to the poor" (p. 54, folio 38).
- **Sufism as an agent of urbanisation, and one named example is in Pakistan.** "In some cases, the deep impact of sufism
  and its popularity among the masses transformed rural regions, such as **Uch**, Nagaur and Sylhet, into flourishing
  urban centres" (p. 421, folio not stated). **The Conclusion's only economic claim about a place in present-day
  Pakistan; it names no shrine, saint or custodian there** — (not stated).
- **How a settlement grew around a saint, in three stages.** Ghiyaspur is "a small village near Kilukhari, then virtually
  unknown to the citizens of Delhi", then "a much busier suburb of Delhi" once Kaiqubad "made Kilukhari, about two
  kilometres from Ghiyaspur, his new capital" (p. 177, folio 159), then "a prosperous suburb of Delhi" under
  'Ala'u'd-Din Khalji (p. 178, folio 160).
- **The urban economy the sources actually document.** "It shows that the lives of Muslims in that affluent eastern
  capital, continually replenished by the proceeds from booty and plunder, were burdened by serious problems.
  **Accommodation was one of the gravest difficulties encountered by the average citizen in Delhi and employment in the
  metropolis was exceedingly hard to find.**" (p. 24, folio 10), the groups documented being "members of the court, the
  princes, the governing classes, the 'ulama', merchants, agriculturalists, artisans and the common Muslims" (p. 24,
  folio 10).
- **Manual labour compared with monasteries, and found wanting.** "Most sufi khangahs [khanqahs] urged their disciples
  to pursue hard manual labour in order to crush the lower self, but **unlike medieval Christian monasteries they did
  not invent labour saving devices, for example in a field like agriculture**" (p. 423, folio 399).
- **The trades and origins of the early sufis, given systematically, and in India.** Sari "had been a merchant of spices
  and seasonings" (p. 66, folio 50); "Kharraz was a cobbler by trade" (p. 67, folio 51); "Junaid's father was a glass
  merchant" (p. 70, folio 54); Hallaj's "father was a wool carder, who later settled in the textile centre of Wasit"
  (p. 73, folio 57); Shibli was "The son of a high-ranking court official" and "had served as governor of Damawand"
  (p. 75, folio 59); and, of one Qazi's four named disciples, "a weaver, a butcher, a hair-rope maker and a fur tailor"
  (pp. 216–217, folios 198–199). **Long-distance trade and a mint office:** the Kirman–Lahore merchant who called at
  Ajodhan, and "Saiyid Ahmad Kirmani, an uncle of Saiyid Muhammad bin Mahmud, an officer in the Multan mint" (p. 23,
  folio 9).
- **Slaves bought, resold and freed, at named prices.** "While in Devagiri, Hasan's servant bought a slave girl for five
  tankas… the distraught parents begged to re-buy their daughter for twice the amount… Amir Hasan himself paid the sum
  and returned the girl to her family" (p. 184, folio 166); "1,500 dinars" for a Turkic slave boy, "500 dinars" for a
  dancing girl of which "250 dinars in cash" with the rest "deposited with a Muslim baggal [OCR?] [baqqal] (grain
  merchant)" (p. 218, folio 200); and Rabiʿa "seized by a man in the street and sold for six dirhams", footnoted "A coin
  worth less than a penny" (p. 46, folio 30).
- **A Shaikh's estate as inheritable property, with a figure and two irreconcilable stories.** "property worth 700,000
  tankas", one of seven sons inheriting it, given away at once, against the robbers' ransom version in which the sons'
  shares are extracted one after another (p. 220, folio 202). **Rizvi refuses to choose: "Regardless of which story is
  the correct one, it seems clear that wealth accumulated by Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din did not long remain with his sons"**
  (p. 220, folio 202).

### K. Pilgrimage, intercession, relics and amulets

- **The economy of disappointment — a stated mechanism for why a shrine's reputation is never falsified by failure.**
  "Pilgrims sought the intercession of the Shaikh's spirit for the fulfilment of their own ambitions, both religious
  and mundane. **Naturally, the most enthusiastic disseminators of miraculous powers attached to the tombs were those
  whose wishes were attained, but the empty-handed cursed their own lot, rather than questioning the genuine sanctity
  of the saints.** Such activity at the tombs led to an identification between the common man and the silsila, and
  this was often associated with a genuine feeling of respect for the Shaikh" (p. 121, folio 103).
- **Intercession with a stated radius — blessing made a function of physical proximity, claimed for the saint alive.**
  "'Whoever has seen me and has done good work for my family and disciples will be under the shadow of my intercession
  hereafter… **My neighbours are Balkh and Marw and Nishapur and Herat.** I am not speaking of those who live here
  (Mayhana)…If any one has mounted an ass and passed by the end of this street, or has passed my house or will pass
  it, **or if the light of my candle falls on him**, the least thing that God will do with him is that He will have
  mercy upon him'" (p. 90, folio 74).
- **The doctrinal charter for healing and petition at a saint's hands.** God to the saints: "He gave them knowledge of
  the places where the means of healing is to be found. . .and to them He gave assurance of an answer to their
  prayers, and He said: 'Oh My saints, if there come to you one sick through separation from Me, heal him, or a
  fugitive from Me, seek him out...or afraid of Me, then reassure him'" (p. 64, folio 48); with devotion to saints as
  divinely implanted, "He put love towards them into the hearts of His creatures" (p. 64, folio 48).
- **Tomb visitation as an ascetic discipline, with distance, season and posture.** "In the winter months Shaikh Mina
  would pray at the tomb of the adopted father, keeping himself awake by wearing wet clothes. **Shaikh Sarang's tomb
  filled him with such awe that, barefooted, he would walk forty miles in order to reach it**" (p. 293, folio 273).
  **And ziyarat named as an intention and overridden:** "Although the Shaikh wished to visit the tombs of the Delhi
  saints, he left for Agra where the court had been located since 1502" (p. 286, folio 266).
- **Thread-tying for the granting of wishes — the clearest lay shrine practice in the book, and it is at a sultan's
  tomb.** "They would visit the tomb of Sultan 'Ala'u'd-Din Khalji, and tie threads and pieces of cloth around it so
  that their prayers and wishes might be granted" (p. 207, folio 189).
- **Flags and spears as contested shrine paraphernalia, with a dissenting authority.** "people from distant places
  carrying multi-coloured flags and numerous presents made pilgrimages to the Salar's tomb at Bahraich"; "**According
  to Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Haqq the carrying of flags was a later innovation**"; and the banned "processions of pilgrims
  carrying spears" (p. 334, folio 312). The Madaris "wore black turbans and carried black flags" (p. 341, folio 319).
- **Dust as relic, three times.** "Both Hindus and Muslims were attracted to the Shaikh's tomb and considered its dust
  a sacred relic" (p. 181, folio 163); "the dust on the road where he had passed was collected as a relic" (p. 154,
  folio 136); "The dust from a saint's foot had more value than a rosary or any other such objects" (p. 404, folio
  380).
- **Touching and kissing a living saint, and the crowd management it forced.** "On a day of 'Id, when the Shaikh was
  returning from prayers, a large crowd followed, kissing his hands and touching his garment" (p. 170, folio 152); "A
  sleeve from his shirt was hung from the roof of his dwelling facing the street. Crowds of people came to kiss the
  sleeve and in the process it was torn to shreds"; "on his instructions his disciples formed a circle around him, so
  his devotees could not come too close. A farrash (menial servant) broke the circle of protectors and falling at the
  Shaikh's feet, kissed them" (pp. 162–163, folios 144–145). **And crowds round a devotee, to the pir's annoyance:**
  Malik Muhammad "chose the lowly service of drawing the Shaikh's water from the River Sabarmati, unflinchingly
  walking through the bazaar with the pot on his head. His humility drew crowds which tended to annoy his pir"
  (p. 306, folio 284).
- **Prostration before the pir: practice, objection, defence, verdict and compromise, all five printed.** "the Chishti
  practice of kissing the feet of, and prostration before pirs… shocked their orthodox opponents. Sajda or
  prostration is an act of worship in which a man's forehead touches the ground" (p. 238, folio 218); it "was not
  invented by the Indian Chishtis. It was even sanctioned by Shaikh Abu Sa'id bin Abi'l Khair who argued that
  prostration was designed to show humility before the Shaikh and that this invariably raised the spiritual status of
  the disciple" (p. 238, folio 218); the defence, "Although sajda is not obligatory it is not illegal. There can be no
  question of prohibiting what is legally permitted" (p. 239, folio 219); **Rizvi's verdict, "The argument was
  unconvincing and the Shaikh's disciples continued to prostrate themselves" (p. 239, folio 219)**; the alternative of
  "placing of the head on the ground and in this instance the word sajda was not used", rejected by the orthodox, and
  Nasiruʾd-Din's compromise of kissing the ground with the lips (p. 240, folio 220). **And a disagreement about
  interrupting prayer for it:** Amir Hasan held "the disciple should abandon them to kiss the pir's feet",
  Nizamuʾd-Din "disagreed and believed that prayers should be finished first" (p. 240, folio 220). Index: "Sajda,
  218-20" (p. 483, folio 459).
- **A collective vocal practice criticised as pir worship, defended from Ibn al-ʿArabi.** "the key word before and
  after prayer was Haqq. Whether they were talking or buying and selling in the bazaar, the Shaikh's disciples would
  cry 'Haqq.' When the Shaikh visited the mosque his disciples walked in front of him crying *'Haqq'. **Many people
  were critical of such a custom, calling it pir worship**, but the Shaikh justified it by quoting from the Futuhat
  al-Makkiyya of Ibn al-'Arabi" (p. 292, folio 272).
- **Devotion to the pir ranked above devotion to God — and two flat contradictions of it.** "A disciple worshipping his
  pir was better than the worshipper of the Lord, argued the Shaikh, for the latter was busy with the contemplation of
  his own self and therefore neglected God; one who adored his pir, however, worshipped God through the contemplation
  of His creature" (p. 370, folio 348); against Masʿud-i Bak, who "rejected the growing belief in the superiority of
  the saints over the prophets … saints themselves not possessing intrinsic spiritual eminence" (p. 263, folio 243),
  and Gisu Daraz, for whom "the prophets were God's messengers and therefore their superiority to the saints was
  unquestionable" (p. 273, folio 253). **Three positions; the book reconciles none.**
- **Sainthood as an open, continuing office — the theological ground on which later shrine cults stand.** "After
  Muhammad, prophethood, or the process of law-giving, ceased, but **the state of Wali never ceased to exist**"
  (p. 126, folio 108); "Who can prevent the mercy of God from prevailing over people even in these modern times? ...
  Do they think that there is no siddig [OCR?] [siddiq], no mugarrab [OCR?] [muqarrab], no mujtaba, no mustafa
  nowadays?" (pp. 57–58, folios 41–42); **with a ceiling on it:** "Tirmizi clearly acknowledges the superiority of the
  prophets over the saints", though "He sees no reason preventing saints from equalling, and even surpassing the early
  Caliphs, Abu Bakr and ʿUmar" (p. 57, folio 41).
- **A hierarchy of saints with fixed numbers.** The rungs are "al-budala, al-siddigin [OCR?] [al-siddiqin] (or
  al-umana), al-muhaddasun, and Khatm al-Awliyaʾ, in order of increasing precedence" (p. 57, folio 41); the budala
  "are forty in number and surround the divine throne" (footnote 2); the umana "also number forty" (footnote 3); the
  muhaddasun "are the sadat al-awliyvaʾ [OCR?] [sadat al-awliyaʾ], 'masters of the saints'" (footnote 4) (p. 57, folio
  41). Index: "Abdal, 41, 250, 356", "Abdali, see Abdal" (p. 457, folio not stated); "Awtad, 97", "Akhyar, 97",
  "al-Budala, 4}" [OCR?] (pp. 460, 462; folios 436, 438) — **the last with a damaged locator, not repaired.**
- **Blessings of a hajj obtained by visiting a living saint on the day of the hajj; company as transferable blessing;
  and two named institutions of company equated.** "It was the tenth Zu'lhijja, the day when pilgrimages to Mecca were
  performed… Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din decided instead to visit the Shaikh in order to gain from him the blessings of a hajj"
  (p. 232, folio 212); "Only true saints should be sought as companions and those who even spoke to them received
  blessings transferred by them" (p. 404, folio 380); "The suhbat (company of the pious) was as important a spiritual
  institution to sufis as the satsang (society of holy men) was to the bhaktas" (p. 413, folio 389), with "Satsang,
  389" and "Suhbat, 389" at the same single folio in the index (pp. 484, 487; folios 460, 463).
- **Pilgrimage devalued, in seven places and seven mouths.** "To him pilgrimage was the jihad of women" (p. 55, folio
  39), with the instruction to give the money "to an orphan or to a poor man" instead; "'Fasting only means the saving
  of bread, / Formal prayer is the business / Of old men and women, / **Pilgrimage is a pleasure of the world**'"
  (p. 94, folio 78); "The hajjis walked around the Ka‘ba, but the ‘arifs (spiritualists) circumambulated the heart.
  **The most superior kind of worship was to assist the helpless and to feed the hungry**" (p. 142, folio 124);
  "Brahmanical sacrifices, pilgrimages and penances were of no avail" (p. 352, folio 330); "You have been to Gaya,
  Benares (Kasi) and Vrndavana,—and have travelled through many rivers and forests and other places of pilgrimage;
  but say,—have you seen in all these anything of Him of Whom you have heard?" (p. 380, folio 358); "The profit which
  is obtained from pilgrimages, repeating the Smritis, and bestowing alms all day long, Is, O Nanak, obtained in one
  ghari by remembering the Name" (p. 411, folio 387), with "'Pilgrimages, Austerities, Mercy, Charity, Bring but
  honour small and paltry'" (p. 415, folio 391); and "To Kabir, the prayers, pilgrimages and fasting of the Muslims
  were equally abominable" (p. 402, folio 378). **And the built place of worship ranked below the interior one:** "'The
  material temple, / And the temple of the heart. / Make your best endeavour / To worship at the temple of the
  heart'" (p. 94, folio 78) — **three pages from the Timurid rebuilding of the same man's own tomb (p. 93, folio 77),
  and the book does not remark on it.**
- **Temples and idols rejected on the Hindu side, and idolatry reframed on the Muslim side.** "What are thine idols
  but lumps of stone? / What but stone the temples that are thine? / Venerable Brahman, who alone / **Offerings** to
  these to make Divine?" (p. 372, folio 350) — **"Offerings" is the one word touching shrine economy on the Hindu
  side, and it appears inside a refusal**; "Idol worshippers offered food to their gods, which in reality was eaten by
  Brahmans, said Kabir" (p. 402, folio 378); against "As long as an idolater was aware he was worshipping God,
  idolatry could be tolerated" (p. 125, folio 107), "he who saw God and not a stone idol was in fact His worshipper"
  (p. 371, folio 349), and the verse reading Hindu worship as unwitting zikr, "'Hindu, who always worships idols, /
  Every morning makes invocations. / On his tongue there is nothing but Thy zikr…'" (p. 230, folio 210), **printed
  without comment eleven pages after a temple demolition also printed without comment (p. 219, folio 201).**
- **A working liturgy at a river, a joint Hindu–Muslim ritual with a named apparatus, and occupational patronage.**
  "'Amara achchi polapan / Gazi achche nigahman, / Shire Ganga dariya Panch Pir, / Badr, Badr, Badr'", glossed "'We
  are children and the Ghazi is our protector, the river Ganges is over us. Oh Panch Pir (Oh, the five saints), Oh
  Badr, Badr'" (p. 338, folio 316); Pir Badr and Khwaja Khizr "Both were worshipped in a joint ceremony held on a
  village pond or nearby river in which a little grass raft carrying a lighted lamp was floated" (p. 339, folio 317);
  "Shah Madar is the patron saint of both Hindu and Muslim jugglers, acrobats and those who earn their livelihood by
  exhibiting tricks with monkeys and bears" (p. 342, folio 320); Pir Badr is "the patron saint of sailors, both Muslim
  and Hindu" (pp. 338, 339; folios 316, 317).
- **A yogi pilgrimage centre on a Buddhist site, dated by reign — the one institutional statement in its chunk about a
  site now in Pakistan.** "Gorakhtari, Like other Buddhist sites occupied by yogis, it became a centre for their
  activities. In the reign of Akbar and Jahangir it was an important yogi centre of pilgrimage" (p. 413, folio 389).
  **The book's other spelling is in the illustrations list — "4. Babur visiting Gorakhattri. XVI century, from Babur
  Nama in British Museum, London" (p. 10, folio not stated). Whether "Gorakhtari"/"Gorakhattri" is the Gor Khatri of
  Peshawar is not stated anywhere in this book, and no archive id is asserted for it here** — (not stated).
- **The index's one named pilgrimage site.** "Adam's foot-print, 387" (p. 458, folio 434); with "Anasagar lake, 117"
  (p. 460, folio 436) as the only other water-site headword.
- **Amulets as a mass service, with a supply problem, a doctrinal licence and a founding instruction.** "A large number
  requested ta'wiz (amulets) and the demand for amulets became so great that it was physically impossible for the Baba
  to fulfil all requests, so often they would be written instead by Maulana Ishaq" (p. 160, folio 142); "the fulfilment
  of desires belonged to no-one; the amulets contained God's name and His words, and could be given to the people"
  (pp. 154–155, folios 136–137); "Khwaja Qutbu'd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki advised his disciples to write the different names
  of God or Qur'anic verses on amulets and give them to those who requested them" and "the Chishti jama'at-khanas were
  filled with people wanting amulets" (p. 240, folio 220); and "Shaikh Faridu'd-Din once gave me a pot of ink and
  ordered me to write amulets for the people" (pp. 192–193, folios 174–175). Index: "Ta‘wiz, 142, 220-21" (p. 488,
  folio 464). **A relic-amulet made from a saint's body:** the relic-amulet of Baba Farid's beard hair (p. 240, folio
  220) — **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar** material.
- **The relics a Shaikh bequeaths, itemised and glossed, ending in feeding the poor in dead saints' names.** "To Shaikh
  Qasim the prayer carpet indicated firmness in prayer, the tashih [OCR?] [tasbih] or 'rosary' was the recollection of
  scattered thoughts; the comb was a symbol of virtue, the staff represented the idea that the One Real God should be
  relied on; the pair of scissors symbolized the severance of relations from everything other than God; the needle was
  a reminder that the exoteric and esoteric should be intertwined; slippers were a symbol of spiritual firmness; the
  ewer, cup and other such household utensils prompted sufis to be hospitable and **in the name of deceased Saints to
  give food to the poor**" (p. 267, folio 247).
- **A relic transmitted hand to hand, and one received with royal ceremonial on a purpose-built platform.** Baba Ratan
  "handed him the comb which the Prophet had given him in order to pass on to Shaikh Raziu'd-Din 'Ali Lala" (p. 342,
  folio 320); "Sultan Ahmad sent emissaries to Saiyid Ni'matu'llah Husaini of Kirman and obtained for himself the
  Shaikh's robe and cap. **These gifts were received with great ceremony at a specially constructed platform and the
  occasion was reminiscent of royal ceremonies at which farmans from paramount powers were received**" (pp. 272–273,
  folios 252–253).
- **A priced relic transaction, told as a joke about credulity.** "The Sultan bought four hoofs believed to have
  belonged to the ass of Christ. **For each he paid fifty thousand tankas.** Later someone else brought him another
  onc [OCR?] [one] claiming that it also was a genuine hoof from the ass of Christ. The Sultan bought that too"
  (p. 299, folio 277, note 1). **And objects made relics by a miracle, with no stated repository:** "Miraculously the
  object turned into a mixture of each of the three materials, and later became celebrated relics" (pp. 304–305,
  folios 282–283) — (not stated).
- **A shirt as a cure and a shirt as a fire-proof marvel.** "The holy shirt cured the Sultan" (p. 340, folio 318); the
  Haidari chief's shirt "showing not a single trace of burning on it" (p. 332, folio 310).
- **Relic veneration judged, in a quotation that names a Lahore building.** Goldziher: "the mischief done with relics
  (had) blossomed especially in India … Buddhist relics could simply be changed into those of 'Ali. In India
  veneration of relics of all sorts, as for example of the footprints of the Prophet, etc., found its way into the
  public cult of the mosque. **One of the richest treasuries of such precious objects is the Padishahi mosque at
  Lahore**" (p. 339, folio 317, footnote 3). **Rizvi introduces it as "On the basis of his modern informants" and
  calls it "interesting"; he does not endorse or rebut it, and no archive id is asserted for the building here.**
  **And relic veneration of a founder on the Hindu side, recorded as a relapse:** "They not only worshipped Vishnu,
  Krishna and Radha, but also relics associated with their founder" (p. 379, folio 357).
- **Books as inheritable khanqah property.** Akhi Siraj left Delhi "taking with him some books from the great Shaikh's
  library" (p. 202, folio 184); the Shaikh's "only possession, a bundle of books", carried by the Kirmanis when he was
  evicted (p. 176, folio 158).
- **The negative the archive should know.** The book nowhere records a visitor count at a shrine, an offering box, a
  pilgrim register, a shrine fee or a pilgrim tax collected at a tomb. **The only "tax on the shrines" is Guru Nanak's
  hymn (p. 411, folio 387) and the only counted crowd is the 5,000 fed from a cauldron (p. 144, folio 126); every
  other visitor and revenue figure for a shrine is (not stated).**

### L. Samaʿ, qawwals, music and dance

- **Samaʿ as the Chishti mark, defined against salat and zikr, with the one place-specific practice rule in the
  book.** "Sama' was an indispensable part of Chishti rituals. It sharpened mystical sensitivity, and the trance-like
  state or ecstasy known as hal offered the spirits and hearts of its participant's mystical experiences which could
  never be gained through salat or zikr. **The Suhrawardis discouraged sama' but were unable to reject it totally.
  Many eminent Suhrawardis indulged in it enthusiastically**" (p. 237, folio 217); "**it would appear that the use of
  sama' by the Suhrawardis was generally not permitted in Multan**" (p. 238, folio 218).
- **Three positions in one chunk, from permissive-with-rules to prohibitive.** Suhrawardi "supported the practice, but
  prescribed its performance under strict rules, to prevent a degeneration into the use of music and dancing to
  promote licentiousness" (p. 110, folio 92); Ghujduwani, "Mystics should not necessarily avoid sama' but any
  over-indulgence should be abandoned" (p. 113, folio 95); the Naqshbandis, who "should not practise spoken zikr,
  seclusion and sama'" (p. 115, folio 97).
- **Hujwiri's defence, quoted against the charge of dancing.** "all foot-play (pay-bazi) is bad in law and reason, by
  whomsoever it is practised", but genuine ecstatic "agitation (iztirab) is neither dancing nor foot-play nor bodily
  indulgence, but a dissolution of the scul [OCR?] [soul]. Those who call it 'dancing' are utterly wrong" (pp. 110–111,
  folios 92–93). **data-darbar** material; the row treatment belongs in that row's subsection. **And the comparative
  defence, argued from travel:** "Everywhere he had been, he argued, sufis indulged in sama'; there was no objection in
  those areas to its use by those who knew its merits" (p. 180, folio 162).
- **A samaʿ procession under a state ban, with a count and a distance.** "At the same time as Sultan Ghiyasu'd-Din
  Tughluq issued strict orders prohibiting musicians from singing at sama' gatherings or elsewhere"; "When the khangah
  [khanqah] doors were unlocked **about 200 gawwals [OCR?] [qawwals]**, and a large number of sufis, were standing
  outside. They set off to Tughluqabad **about three miles away**, singing and dancing" (p. 235, folio 215).
- **Qawwals as carriers of a saint's reputation between cities, and as a khanqah's regular company.** "the report …
  came through a qawwal (a musician) who had been to both Multan and Ajodhan"; afterwards "Nizamu'd-Din was so excited
  that after each prayer he began to repeat the name of Baba Farid" (p. 173, folio 155); "Under Chishti influence he
  became a lover of sama' and gawwals [OCR?] [qawwals] thronged to his khangah [khanqah]" (p. 305, folio 283); a
  quatrain "sung by a gawwal [OCR?] [qawwal] or musician" at a domestic samaʿ (p. 84, folio 68); and "He himself
  composed Hindi dohas, which gawwals [OCR?] [qawwals] would recite at his sama' gatherings" (p. 432, folio 408).
  Index: "Qawwal, 68, 154, 215, 283, 404" and "Qawil [OCR?] [Qawwali? Qaul?], 172" (p. 480, folio 456).
- **Music at a shrine, a customary payment, a refusal of it as protest, and the state backing down.** "Because the
  Emperor's officers had stopped the musicians from playing, they refused to accept the customary gift. On a subsequent
  visit, the Emperor prevented his officers from interfering with the playing of music performed in memory of the
  Khwaja. The anecdote relates that the Emperor was deeply moved by it and **paid the musicians double the normal
  gift**" (p. 145, folio 127). **Attributed to Aurangzeb and marked by the book as an "anecdote".**
- **Tomb-side music as a described norm rather than a controversy.** "Rituals involving religious music and dancing
  were often held around the tombs and gardens of the Shaikhs, and the use of sama' was at its peak" (p. 207, folio
  189).
- **Samaʿ in private houses, in khanqahs, without a musician, and decided by a property rule.** "many sufis in Mayhana,
  Abu Sa'id's father included, preferred to live, and hold sama' rituals, in their own houses" (p. 84, folio 68);
  Qutbu'd-Din "took part in a sama' ritual in the khangah [khanqah] of Shaikh 'Ali Sijzi" (p. 155, folio 137); "Once
  the Baba wished to partake of sama' although no musician was present" and was brought the Qazi's letters instead
  (p. 216, folio 198); and the opposing party at a samaʿ "were then faced with the dilemma of whether or not to enter
  the house without the owner being present. However, they decided to withdraw, after which sama' was continued"
  (p. 215, folio 197).
- **Samaʿ as a cause of death, twice; ecstatic dancing in three registers; and a dance form named after its
  inventor.** Shaikh Qutbuʾd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki died "a few days later" after "a Persian verse produced such powerful
  ecstasy" in him (p. 348, folio 326); "In 834/1430-31, Shaikh Yusuf died while engrossed in a performance of sama'"
  (p. 295, folio 275); Adhan Jaunpuri "was so enthusiastic that ten men were not strong enough to quell his inspired
  dancing" (p. 283, folio 263); Shaikh Badru'd-Din "at a ripe old age … still danced like a boy of ten" (p. 156, folio
  138); Burhanu'd-Din Gharib "invented a peculiar style of dancing which was performed during the ritual of sama'.
  This came to be known as the Burhani" (p. 201, folio 183).
- **A dancing order, two instruments, a dated eyewitness visit and a name change.** "During his lifetime, Rumi invented
  the famous whirling dance through which dervishes could achieve ecstasy. **This was accompanied by lamenting reed
  pipes and beating drums.** The order he founded was known as the Mevlevi or the Mawlawiyya… **in 1332 when Ibn
  Battuta visited Quniya he found it flourishing under the name, Jalaliyya.**" (p. 98, folio 82). **Rizvi's "invented"
  carries no source.**
- **A performer's freedom of repertoire, and a composed repertoire attributed to a named poet.** Shaikh ʿAli "greatly
  approved of sama' and believed that the musicians should recite ghazals or melodies of their own choice" (p. 283,
  folio 263); "It is believed that Khusraw invented the musical instrument, the sitar, and several melodies for it by
  a mixture of Persian and Indian tunes. **Popular Indian melodies such as Qawi/ [OCR?] [Qawl] are undoubtedly the
  invention of Amir Khusraw.** They were designed to produce novelty in sufi sama' rituals, in which he himself
  participated" (p. 190, folio 172). **Rizvi hedges the first claim and does not hedge the second; neither carries a
  source, and this note must travel with any use of the passage for qawwali at shrines.**
- **Indigenous-language music entering samaʿ, dated, with its performers identified and its loss recorded.** "**From
  the thirteenth century onwards Hindu mystical songs were recited at sama' gatherings and many of the most talented
  musicians were newly converted Muslims**"; "The recitation of Hindawi music at sama' was popular at all sufi centres,
  particularly those some distance from Delhi" (p. 348, folio 326), *Hindawi* footnoted "a generic term referring to
  indigenous dialects in northern India"; and "Most of the Hindawi songs recited at sama' gatherings held during this
  period have been lost" (p. 349, folio 327).
- **Vaishnavite song inside a Muslim ritual, with a named apologetic and a working glossary.** "The popularity of
  Vaishnavite themes used in sufi samaʿ rituals of Hindi speaking regions is a most remarkable development. The sufis
  regarded them as welcome additions to their devotional poetry to induce ecstasy"; "**The Mir justifies the popularity
  of the names of kafirs used in sufi samaʿ on the grounds that the Qurʾan itself uses the names of both kafirs and
  enemies**" (p. 381, folio 359); the glossary reading "Krishna: Sometimes Krishna and his other names in Hindawi
  (Hindi) indicate the Prophet Muhammad and sometimes the (Perfect) Man … Sometimes it represents Iblis. Often it
  stands for idols, Christians or the sons of fire-worshippers", "Gopi and Gujari (milkmaid): Sometimes these words
  represent angels", "Uddhava … Sometimes it indicates Gabriel" (p. 382, folio 360), with a stated limit, "If wise men
  perceive into them a different meaning, the source of difference is the intellect itself. The symbols themselves do
  not warrant any difference" (p. 382, folio 360).
- **Vernacular poetry recited from the pulpit for ecstatic effect, defended on Qurʾanic grounds.** Bada'uni: "Even
  Maulana Shaikh Tagqiu'd-Din [OCR?] [Taqiu'd-Din], a godly preacher (wa'iz-i rabbani) used to recite its verses from
  the pulpit. It had an indescribable ecstatic effect upon the audience… **Even now sweet-singers of India captivate
  the heart by reciting it.**" (p. 386, folio 364). **"Even now" is Badaʾuni's present, not Rizvi's.**
- **Samaʿ as the property of holy men, and the charlatan's inversion of it.** Amir Husaini "considered [sama'] to be the
  exclusive practice of holy men"; "Charlatans considered that indulgence in lust was Tariga [OCR?] [Tariqa], to them
  good food and merrymaking were asceticism" (p. 229, folio 209).
- **Samaʿ attested visually, and named as a source of the book's own knowledge.** The plate is captioned "Sufi Sama"
  from a manuscript "dated 959/1552" (p. 108, folio not stated); the frontispiece is "Sufi Sama' from the Majalisu'l
  'Ushshaq by Sultan Husain written in 908/1502-3. Miniature from Bodleian Ms., dated 959/1552, Ouseley Add. 24, f. 119
  R. coloured, on dust-jacket and facing p. 91" (p. 10, folio not stated); "The author's knowledge of the music used in
  sufi sama' rituals enabled him to give useful details about different aspects of the development of music in northern
  India" (p. 28, folio 14).
- **Group singing with instruments on the Hindu side, and the fire ritual as an order's regular custom.** Chaitanya's
  "favourite form of worship as a bhakta was in kirtan or samkirtan (group singing) which was accompanied by drums,
  cymbals or a one-stringed fiddle and during which the words Hari and Krishna were constantly chanted" (p. 379, folio
  357); and of the Rifaʿiyyas at Wasit, "**This is their regular custom and it is the peculiar characteristic of this
  corporation of Ahmadi brethren**" (p. 332, folio 310), the sequence running afternoon prayers, "drums and
  kettle-drums were beaten and the poor brethren began to dance", sunset prayer, "the repast, consisting of rice-bread,
  fish, milk and dates", first night prayer, zikr "with the shaikh Ahmad sitting on the prayer-carpet of his ancestor",
  then "the musical recital" and the fire — **with its Indian counterpart in the present tense, "The Madari dervishes,
  crying 'Dam Madar', walk on burning coals while their feet remain unscathed" (pp. 341–342, folios 319–320).**
- **The index's samaʿ run, quoted across its own line break exactly as printed.** "Sama’, 15, 68, 76, 92, 95, 135-36,
  138, 161-62, 172, 183, 189, 193, 196" / "98, 215-16, 227, 252, 263, 283, 288, 307, 326-27, 359, 408" (p. 484, folio
  460). **`196` / `98` is `196-98` broken by the OCR; it is flagged, not repaired.**

### M. Zikr, breath, chilla and ascetic technique

- **The two modes of zikr, assigned to named orders — the first attribution of a practice to the Chishtiyya in the
  book.** "Zikr was performed both communally and in seclusion. The former enabled senior disciples to supervise the
  progress of their juniors. **The zikr-i khafi, recollection performed either mentally or in a low voice, was
  recommended by the Naqshbandis; the Qadiriyya and the Chishtiyya generally performed zikr-i jali, which was recited
  aloud.** Both forms of zikr involved control of the breath and over inhalation and exhalation. The formulas for zikr
  differed from one order to another, but generally they involved the recitation of different syllables of the
  ka/ima [OCR?] [kalima] or some of the many names of God" (p. 119, folio 101).
- **The Chishti zikr with its yogic component named, and the preliminary act of visualising the Shaikh.** "contacts
  with yogis added a new dimension to zikr and involved the strenuous co-ordination of limb movements and postures
  associated with alternate exhalation and inhalation. During zikr, there was a concentration on the utterance of the
  first part of al-kalimat at-tayyiba (the blessed phrase) or the phrase of shahada (testimony), /a Ilaha Illa'llah
  [OCR?] [la Ilaha Illa'llah]" (p. 237, folio 217); the preliminary being "to imagine that his Shaikh was personally
  present before him, directing his contemplation. **The practice amounted to a belief that the Shaikh's spirit was
  divine both in its emanation and power**" (p. 238, folio 218).
- **The Khwajgan zikr given as a physical procedure — the densest practice passage in the book.** "The sufi controls
  his breath from below the navel, shuts his lips tightly and fastens his tongue to his palate to prevent suffocation.
  He then diverts the spiritual heart into a union with the physical heart which is pineal in shape, and zikr is
  begun… The uttering of /a [OCR?] [la] (no) involves a process whereby the word is lifted from the navel to the
  brain; //aha [OCR?] [illaha] (God) is expressed, at the same time as the right shoulder is jerked sharply and
  i/'Allah [OCR?] [illa Allah] (but Allah) is uttered as if the heart of flesh has been soundly struck. This process
  produces a spiritual heart which ontologically circulates throughout the body" (p. 114, folio 96).
- **The eight principles of Ghujduwani, with the bazaar clause.** "1. Hosh dar dam (awareness while breathing)";
  "2. Nazar bar gadam [OCR?] [Nazar bar qadam] (watching the steps)"; "3. Safar dar watan (journey to one's
  homeland)" — "a journey from human to angelic attributes"; "4. Khalwat dar anjuman (solitude in an assembly)" —
  "**A sufi may wander into a bazaar but so engrossed in zikr should he be that not a single voice is heard by him**";
  "5. Yad-kard (remembrance) is related to both oral and mental zikr"; "6. Baz-gasht (restraint)" — after "al-kalimat
  al-tayyiba" (footnoted "The Islamic confession of faith") the reciter "should add: 'Oh God! Thou art my Goal and I
  seek Thy satisfaction'"; "7. Nigah-dasht (watchfulness)"; "8. Yad-dasht (recollection)" (pp. 113–114, folios 95–96).
- **The three Naqshbandi additions, one a counted quota.** "1. Wuquf-i Zamani (temporal pause) is the constant
  self-examination by a sufi on the way his time is spent"; "2. Wuquf-i 'Adadi (numerated pause) is the prevention of
  thought-anarchy through a process of counting the number of times zikr in the heart is repeated. **The mystic
  completes one round of zikr between three and twenty-one recitations in the one breath; however, if there is no
  spiritual change after a second round of twenty-one zikrs this implies there has been no real impact**"; "3. Wuquf-i
  Qalbi (heart pause)" — "According to Khwaja Baha'u'd-Din, this was the essence of sufi discipline" (p. 115, folio
  97).
- **Breath control as the essence of discipline, with a technical term and a yogic comparison — and the two
  traditions' terms equated outright.** "The essence of sufi discipline was control of the breath… A sufi was one
  whose breaths were counted and the perfect sufi was a **sahib-i anfas (the master of articulated breath). The
  breaths of faultless yogis, known as Siddhas were also measured.** A dervish related he had learnt concentration
  from a cat by watching it sit before a rat hole in such a way that it had full control over its breathing so that
  not a single whisker moved" (pp. 206–207, folios 188–189); "The contemplative life of the Rishis was founded on the
  **pas-i anfas or pranayama**" (p. 373, folio 351). Index: "pas-i anfas, 272, 341, 351, 353" and "Pranayama, 332,
  337, 341, 351" (p. 480, folio 456); "Hatha-Yoga, 143, 199, 332, 335, 351, 353" (p. 467, folio 443).
- **The counted breath-practice with a Hindu syllable inside a sufi discipline.** "Onkar is the basis of pranayama. In
  the initial stages, breath is drawn up through the left nostril, the ira [OCR?] [ida], while the sacred Hindu
  syllable 'Om' is repeated slowly **sixteen times**. The breath is then suspended in the upper part of the nose where
  the breath nostrils meet. The junction of the nostrils is called the sukhmana. Just as the breath has been drawn up
  by the left nostril, so it is forced down through the right nostril to pingala, while the syllable is again repeated
  **sixteen times**… Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus expects sufis to absorb themselves in Onkar through zikr" (p. 359, folio
  337).
- **The chilla, the inverted prayer, and a lineage of three practitioners.** Forty days of "spiritual exercises and
  fasting", and the inverted form requiring suspension in a well "head down, for forty days and nights, while both
  fasting and praying" (p. 158, folio 140); *salat-i maʿkus* footnoted "Namaz performed hanging upside down with the
  legs suspended from a roof or a branch of a tree" (p. 358, folio 336, footnote 6); and in practice, "For years after
  the evening namaz he would perform the namaz-i ma'kus. This was carried out by hanging, probably head downwards, and
  was generally continued the whole night. **Although Chishtis believed this type of namaz to be a legacy from the
  Prophet … Shaikh Abu Sa'id bin Abi'l Khair was the first known sufi to have practised it; the first Indian sufi to
  perform it was Baba Farid.** Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus considered it to be the counterpart of the u/ti sadhna [OCR?]
  [ulti sadhna]" (p. 364, folio 342). **The Baba Farid statement is** **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar** material.
  Index: "Chilla, 140, 316", "Chilla-i ma‘kus, 140" (p. 463, folio 439); "Namaz-i ma‘kus, 342" (p. 478, folio 454).
- **A daily prayer regime with a count and its physical evidence, and a smaller counted quota.** "Besides obligatory
  prayers the Shaikh would perform **four hundred rak 'ats of namaz during the day and four hundred rak 'ats at
  night**. The clothes covering his knees would be threadbare from kneeling… After performing the evening namaz he
  would begin the zikr-i jahr. Those who joined him would tire" (p. 364, folio 342); "From then until morning he would
  incessantly pray and recite the Ya Sin chapter of the Qur'an **forty-one times**" (p. 283, folio 263).
- **A named terminal state produced by the practice.** "Continual performance of namaz-i ma'kus produced in the Shaikh
  a condition he called sultan-i zikr in which one experienced strange changes in the physical and spiritual condition
  including a deprivation of the senses and a lack of feeling of consciousness. Repeated appearances of the sultan-i
  zikr led to the state of fana' al-fana'" (p. 364, folio 342), described further over pp. 364–365 (folios 342–343).
  Index: "Sultan-i zikr, 342-43" and "Ulti Sadhna, 342" (pp. 487, 489; folios 463, 465); "Zikr, 44, 61, 70, 76, 84,
  95-96, 98-101, 155, 193, 203-4, 210, 217-18, 240, 245, 249, 337, 342, 352", "Zikr-i jahr, 342", "Zikr-i khafi, 101"
  (p. 491, folio 467); "Wuquf-i Qalbi, 96", "Wuquf-i Zamani, 96", "Wudquf-i ‘Adadi, 96" [OCR?] [Wuquf-i ‘Adadi],
  "Yad-dasht, 96", "Yad-kard, 95" (p. 490, folio 466).
- **A zikr technique with a described visual effect.** The exercises "included a special type of zikr in which the head
  moved rapidly in all directions, resulting in strong flashes of light, believed to be mystical" (p. 269, folio 249).
- **Six conditions for concentration on God, prescribed as a regime — the fifth places the pir inside the practice.**
  "One should retire to a lonely cell and leave it for neither company nor change"; "One should always exist in a
  state of performing wuzu (ablutions)…"; "Perpetual fasting should be observed"; "With the exception of zikr,
  constant silence should be practised"; "**Zikr should be continually recited at the same time as a recollection in
  the heart of the presence of one's pir**"; "One should expel every thought except that of God" (p. 199, folio 181).
  **And the pir placed spatially beside God and the Prophet:** "he should think that God was in front of him, the
  Prophet on his right and his pir on the left" (p. 195, folio 177); with "Generally dervishes meditated on some
  particular verse of the Qur'an, and at the same time an image of the pir was recalled to mind" (p. 120, folio 102).
- **Mujahida under a pir, and the rule that experience is reported only to him.** "Mujahida, or self-mortification
  performed under the guidance of a pir, strictly adhered to the Shari‘a and was designed to achieve purification of
  the soul" (p. 118, folio 100); mystical experience "could be revealed only to one's pir", with "some exceptions to
  this rule among sufis who claimed they were disciples of Khazir (or Khizr)" (p. 118, folio 100).
- **Retreat graded by how often the recluse eats, and the Chishti fasting rule.** "the weakest, who broke their fasts
  every evening; the above average sufi who took nourishment on alternate nights and the strongest, who ate only on
  the third" (p. 110, folio 92); "In order to avoid making fasts a regular habit, the Chishtis often fasted on
  alternate days" (p. 237, folio 217), against the Suhrawardis, to whom "fasting in the month of Ramazan was
  sufficient" (p. 237, folio 217).
- **An eighteen-clause ascetic regime in the Shaikh's own voice, several clauses economic — and a contradiction on one
  page.** "I fasted continually; 1 [OCR?] [I] abstained from unlawful food; I practised recollection (zikr)
  uninterruptedly; I kept awake at night; I never reclined on the ground; I never slept but in a sitting posture; I
  sat facing the Ka'ba… **I did not beg**; I was content and resigned to God's will; **I always sat in the mosque and
  did not go into the market, because the Prophet said that the market is the filthiest of places and the mosque the
  cleanest**… Every four-and-twenty hours I completed a recitation of the Qur'an… For a whole year I conversed with no
  one" (p. 86, folio 70). **"eighteen things" is the book's own count and the printed list does not obviously run to
  eighteen items; and the same page has the same man begging for his fellow sufis (p. 86, folio 70). Both stand.**
- **The nine conditions of the dervish life, dictated and written down — the one written rule of conduct for
  individuals in the book.** The Khwaja "asked his disciple, Hamidu'd-Din Nagauri, to both explain and write them down
  so that such rules could be made known to a wider section of Muslims" (p. 141, folio 123). As printed (pp. 141–142,
  folios 123–124): "One should not earn money"; "One should not borrow money from anyone"; "One should not reveal to
  anyone nor seek help from anyone if one has eaten nothing, even for seven days"; "4. If one gains plenty of food,
  money, grain or clothing, one should not keep anything until the following day"; "5. One should not curse anyone…";
  "6. If one performs a virtuous deed, one should consider that the source of the virtue is due either to one's pir's
  kindness, to the intercession of the Prophet Muhammad on one's behalf, or to divine mercy"; "7. If one performs an
  evil deed one should consider one's evil self responsible"; "8. Having fulfilled all the above conditions, one
  should regularly fast during the day and spend the night in prayer"; "9. One should remain quiet, and speak only
  when it is imperative to do so." **The numerals of items 1–3 are destroyed in the OCR and are not supplied here.
  Items 1–4 are a prohibition on income, credit, begging and accumulation.**
- **The ten stages of the mystic path, with the book's only gendered practice rule; and the seven stages of the
  soul.** "tawakkul (trust)"; "gana'at [OCR?] [qana'at] or contentment"; "'uz/at [OCR?] ['uzlat] (self-seclusion)",
  subdivided into "Uziat-i Zanana [OCR?] ['Uzlat-i Zanana] (female self-seclusion)" and "'uz/at-i Mardana ['uzlat-i
  Mardana] (male self-seclusion)"; "zikr (recollection)", its negative "(/a-Ilaha) [la-Ilaha]" and positive
  "(il'allah)"; "tawajjuh"; "sabr (patience)"; "muraqgaba [OCR?] [muraqaba] (contemplation)"; and "Riza
  (satisfaction)… the tenth and final stage", "a gift from God" (p. 265, folio 245). The soul's stages:
  "(al-ammara)", "(lawwama)", "(al-mulhama)", "(rmutma‘inna) [OCR?] [mutmaʾinna]", a contented soul, "marziyya, the
  approved one", and "a faultless soul (a/-kamila) [OCR?] [al-kamila] … achieved by only the most perfect of sufis"
  (pp. 118–119, folios 100–101).
- **The Path as a supervised itinerary with named stations.** "The Tariga [OCR?] [Tariqa] was an arduous journey
  involving numerous risks and impediments. **It could be traversed only under the strict supervision of Shaikhs or
  pirs who had themselves traversed all the hills and dales of the Path**"; the stations "included repentance (tawba),
  conversion (inabat), renunciation (zuhd) and trust in God (tawakkul)"; the conditions "such as gabz [OCR?] [qabz]
  (contraction), bast (dilation) and illumination, descended into the heart from God" (p. 117, folio 99). **The
  sentence is cut off at the chunk boundary.**
- **The pir as indispensable, the category of the pirless, and one man doing both at once.** "The absence of a pir
  turned seekers of the mystic path into philosophers, materialists, heretics, mu'tazila, ibahatis and the like"
  (p. 255, folio 235); "A sufi disciple should not be attached to any particular sect … but adhere to the faith of his
  pir" (p. 263, folio 243); "if a blind man led another blind man, both were bound to fall into a well. A ceaseless
  effort was needed to find the perfect guru whom the Shaikh likened to a diamond mine" (pp. 363–364, folios 341–342)
  — **set against the same Shaikh taking initiation from a living pir while claiming inspiration from a dead one, in
  one sentence (p. 365, folio 343).**
- **A parable of obedience that is also a theory of transmitted grace — directly relevant to how shrine lineages
  justify themselves.** "A truly obedient disciple was like a white patch on a garment of the same colour. When the
  garment was washed, the patch also became white and this symbolized the fact that **whatever divine grace was
  received by the pir, the disciple also shared.** A disciple who did not obey his pir received no blessings, was a
  disciple in name only and could be likened to a black patch on a white garment" (p. 284, folio 264).
- **The rosary, with bead counts and a transmission claim.** "Zikr popularized the use of the fasbih [OCR?] [tasbih] or
  rosary, consisting of 99 or 100 beads. Some orders used rosaries of 301 beads, but ones of 1000 beads were not
  unknown" (p. 119, folio 101), footnoted "It seems probable that the Muslims borrowed the rosary from the Buddhists,
  and that the Crusaders copied their Muslim opponents and introduced it into Christendom" (pp. 119–120, folios
  101–102, quoting "A Dictionary of Islam, p. 546").
- **The Kanphata initiation rite in full, and the book says it is still performed.** "At the time of initiation, the
  ear cartilages of a novice were **as is still customary today** split, and two enormous ear-rings were inserted in
  the holes. Ear splitting was believed necessary to open a mystical channel in order to assist the development of
  yogic powers. During the ceremony a knife was driven into the ground and vows recited over it by the initiate; these
  included a vow to protect the ears which, according to Hindu mysticism, were believed to contain a network of
  invisible nadis (ganglia) connecting them with the inner organs of perception. The rites also included the symbolic
  slaying of the neophyte, the washing of his entrails, and the hanging of his body on a tree" (p. 354, folio 332).
- **Qalandar and Haidari bodily insignia, itemised, with their stated origins.** A "namd or garment of coarse cloth"
  (p. 323, folio 301); the shaved beard and eyebrows, "as did their habit of never being without a razor" (p. 324,
  folio 302); "Indian loin cloths round their private parts, pieces of blanket on their shoulders and iron pieces,
  probably necklaces, bangles or chains" (p. 325, folio 303); "iron rings on their necks and arms" (p. 331, folio
  309); "iron rings through their ears, hands and other parts of their bodies" and "the Haidaris passed round iron
  rods through their male organs and because both ends were sealed called them **sikh-i muhr (rods of the seal)**… it
  indicated their determination to remain celibate. The Haidaris like other qalandars adopted the practice of ear
  piercing from the Nath-yogis or Kanphata yogis (Split Ear Yogis)." (p. 329, folio 307). **Rizvi's three claims of
  Hindu origin for these are unevidenced and are recorded below.** **And intoxicants in two orders:** "a type of
  grass, probably Indian hemp, and some other drugs" (p. 325, folio 303); the Madaris "ate a large amount of hemp
  leaves, proudly relating stories of their excessive consumption of this intoxicating drug" (p. 341, folio 319).
- **Hatha-Yoga's named operations, taken up by the sufis, and the translated Hindu manual inside the sufi
  curriculum.** "The processes of asana (posture), dhauti (washing), bandha (different kinds of motionlessness), mudra
  (gesture), pranayama (breathing) and other techniques of Hatha-Yoga are prescribed so as to achieve the
  transformation of the body… This entire philosophy is called Hatha-Yoga, the union of the moon (tha) and the sun
  (ha)" (p. 357, folio 335), with "nine cakras or centres of psycho-vital forces", the serpent power in "the lowest
  muladhara cakra", rising "to the highest cakra (sahasrara)", and "The region below the navel … the region of Shakti,
  while that above it is that of Shiva" (p. 356, folio 334); and the *Amrita-Kunda*, "translated by Qazi Ruknu'd-Din
  Samarqandi … who visited Lakhnauti between 1209-10 and 1216-17 and was initiated into Hatha-Yogic principles by a
  Siddha, called Bhojar Brahman. The work was later translated into Persian. A further Arabic version was again
  prepared by a Brahman from Kamrup, apparently in collaboration with a Muslim scholar. This version was re-translated
  into Persian by Shaikh Muhammad Ghaus Shattari (906/1500-01-970/1562-63)" (p. 357, folio 335), its titles
  "Hauzu'l-Hayat… Another title of the book is Bahru'l-Hayat (Ocean of Life or Immortality)" (p. 357, folio 335,
  footnote 2), its content prescribing "methods for the continued suppression of respiration … and fixed the eye on
  the tip of the nose", with "control of the semen" as "A prerequisite for yogic discipline" (p. 358, folio 336).

### N. Women, and the structural exclusion from the khanqah system

- **The load-bearing institutional claim, the only one of its kind in the book.** "**Moreover, female mystics were
  never incorporated into khanqahs and orders as spiritual succession could not be traced through them. Often they
  became hermits or lone dervishes and more often than not, lived without the comforts, both spiritual and material,
  provided by pirs and khangahs [khanqahs]**" (p. 427, folio 403). **This bears on every archive row with a woman as
  principal figure, although Appendix A names none of them.**
- **Rizvi's own summary judgement, with his evidentiary caveat before it.** "**Therefore, it would not be unfair to say
  that, Muslim women who became deeply committed to mysticism and a life of asceticism did so in spite of a lack of
  encouragement and assistance from their male counterparts and from Islam in general**" (p. 427, folio 403); and "We
  have mentioned here only the most outstanding female sufis amongst those whose lives have been documented. **The
  biographies of a large number of saintly women remain unknown**" (p. 427, folio 403).
- **A live exclusion at a functioning shrine, in the present tense.** "although women are still not permitted near the
  shrine" (p. 341, folio 319). **And the one gendered rule in the technical vocabulary:** "Uziat-i Zanana [OCR?]
  ['Uzlat-i Zanana] (female self-seclusion)" against "'uz/at-i Mardana ['uzlat-i Mardana] (male self-seclusion)"
  (p. 265, folio 245).
- **Two women's ascetic regimes, itemised.** Of the second Bibi Fatima, "two cakes of barley each day… along with a
  glass of water", and "For forty days and nights she remained without sleep or food, and the bread was given to
  dervishes" (p. 427, folio 403); of Rabiʿa, her entire property, "A broken pitcher, out of which she drank and made
  ritual ablutions, an old reed mat, and a brick which she occasionally used as a pillow, were her only belongings"
  (p. 46, folio 30). **And Rabiʿa as a doctrinal authority rather than an institutional member:** "Neither the desire
  for paradise nor the fear of hell should be the incentive for prayers or love of God" (p. 46, folio 30), her
  disinterested love being "an important milestone in the development of sufism" (p. 47, folio 31).
- **Celibacy and marriage argued on eight sides and never settled.** The *Masalikuʾl-ʿArifin* rule, "Peace in the heart
  and a control of the eyesight would help in the pursuit of celibacy. Married life involved the sufi in every-day
  problems and exposed him to the threat of loss of faith" (p. 113, folio 95); against ʿAbduʾl-Qadir Jilani marrying
  at fifty-one (p. 100, folio 84), Kubra marrying his preceptor's daughter (p. 111, folio 93) and a khanqah built for
  a Shaikh "and his family" (p. 104, folio 88); Khwaja Muʿinuʾd-Din "who until then had been celibate, took two wives.
  According to tradition he decided to marry in order to imitate all the Prophet's practices", the wedding "seems to
  have taken place in 606/1209-10" and the marriage lasting "twenty-seven years" (p. 142, folio 124); celibacy dated
  as a Chishti preference, "From the time of Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din Auliya', celibacy became a desirable aspect of
  Chishti discipline… He believed that marriage was permissible for a sufi, but that celibacy called for a degree of
  determination and was therefore preferable" (p. 238, folio 218); family life defended, "Family responsibilities
  which produced a necessary involvement in worldly affairs were worthy and acceptable to the Shaikh, who fully
  realized that few could become totally dedicated ascetics" (p. 185, folio 167); against "The first shackle was the
  family, and the second, sensuality" (p. 206, folio 188); Ibrahim bin Adham "was married and left a child in Balkh,
  but advocated celibacy for mystics" (p. 50, folio 34); and Bishr, who "did not himself marry" but "never preached
  against family life" (p. 55, folio 39).
- **Baraka priced as a marriage good — sanctity as a transactable asset.** "ʿAbduʾllah's fame as a mystic made him
  exceedingly eligible as a marriage prospect to the daughters of kings and nobles. **Marriage to him was considered a
  great prize because of the blessing accruing from it.** ʿAbduʾllah did not choose one wife but many, and is said to
  have contracted four hundred marriages. These were not consummated and were generally later annulled" (printed
  "laterannulled") (p. 61, folio 45).
- **A marriage alliance between the Sultanate and a saint's lineage, proposed by the Sultan in writing.** "Sometime
  after 1327-28 the Sultan married his daughter, Bibi Rasti, to Shaikh Faridu'd-Din's grandson, Shaikh Fathu'llah bin
  Shaikh Auhadu'd-Din, after he himself had suggested it in a letter to Shaikh Farid" (p. 148, folio 130).
- **Sati three times, twice as narrative and once as a sufi exemplar.** Mrigawati and Rukmani "became saris [OCR?]
  [satis] by throwing themselves on their husband's funeral pyre" (p. 388, folio 366); "the two widows, Padumavati and
  Nagmati, immolate themselves on their husband's pyre" (p. 390, folio 368); and Amir Khusraw's couplet held up to
  sufis, "Khusraw, in love rival the Hindu wife, / For the dead's sake she burns herself in life." (p. 385, folio 363).
- **Zikr in a household, by slave girls.** In Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya's khanqah "even his slave girls performed constant
  zikr" (p. 173, folio 155). **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya** material.

### O. Hindu, Sikh, Nath and Jain material, and the shared-ground question

- **The chapter names its own problem, and its periodisation, in its first paragraph.** "**HE study of the interaction
  between medieval Hindu mystic traditions and sufism has been marred by deep prejudices and studded with conflicting
  hypotheses.** The subject can best be studied by dividing it into two epochs: firstly, the pre-eleventh century and
  secondly, the eleventh to the sixteenth centuries" (p. 344, folio 322). **The dropped T is the chapter's drop-cap,
  swallowed by the OCR.**
- **The limit on syncretism — the sentence any "shared ground" argument in this archive has to answer.** "These
  spiritual trends and movements which we have discussed drew from each other both in a conscious and unconscious
  way; **in general, however, they remained within a framework of their respective religious traditions.** For example
  Muslims never subscribed to the Hindu belief in transmigration" (p. 396, folio 372); and the Conclusion's careful
  restatement, "**They were unconcerned with the idea of achieving any form of union between the two religions and
  instead tended to work within their respective religious communities for an understanding of the spiritual and
  social values of each other**" (p. 423, folio 399).
- **Shared verse as jointly owned property — the most quotable line of Chapter Six for this archive.** "In fact some
  verses with slight variations are included in Nath poetry as well as in that of Kabir and Gorakhnath. **Such verses
  were regarded as the common property of both Muslim and Hindu mystics**" (p. 358, folio 336).
- **The book's own negative finding about shared *sites*, and it is decisive.** Across pp. 349–365 (folios 327–343)
