  the Pass 1 notes record that there is **no temple taken over, no tomb and temple at one place and no contested site
  anywhere**, the chapter's shared ground in that range being "**entirely textual and doctrinal**"; and across
  pp. 366–382 (folios 344–360) that "**not one physical site is said to be held, visited or contested by two
  communities**". **What the book does supply on sites is one-directional and is recorded above: four khanqahs built
  on demolished temples (pp. 219, 314, 319, 329; folios 201, 292, 297, 307), the Kashmir demolition programme
  (pp. 314, 319, 321; folios 292, 297, 299), and the covenant's ban on new or repaired temples (pp. 317–318, folios
  295–296).**
- **Cross-communal veneration at Muslim graves, recorded five times as fact — and its exact counter-instance.** "the
  tombs were also for the people, both Muslim and non-Muslim" (p. 121, folio 103); "Both Hindus and Muslims were
  attracted to the Shaikh's tomb and considered its dust a sacred relic" (p. 181, folio 163); "the Shaikh's memory was
  treasured by both Hindus and Muslims alike" (p. 220, folio 202); "Both Hindus and Muslims in many villages in
  eastern U.P. feature the grave of a martyr, who is worshipped by both groups" (p. 336, folio 314); and "large crowds
  of Hindus and Muslims assemble" at an ʿurs (p. 341, folio 319). **Against:** "**The Kayasthas, who by this time had
  adopted Muslim dress, were amongst those who would humbly kiss the threshold of the Shaikh's khangah.** However
  Shaikh Ahmad was unimpressed, and would emerge only to curse the Hindus and bless the Muslims who had gathered to
  show their respects" (p. 432, folio 408), footnoted "Musalmanan ra nikbakht farmudand wa kafiran ra halak-Allah
  Gufta, f. 65a", with Rizvi's verdict, "**Shaikh Ahmad, however, showed a high degree of antagonism towards
  Hindus**" (p. 432, folio 408).
- **Non-Muslim endowment of a Muslim shrine (p. 339, folio 317) and non-Muslim futuh at a Muslim tomb (p. 140, folio
  122), both quoted above; and conversion located at the shrine rather than in the saint's lifetime, as Rizvi's own
  reading.** "it does not refer to conversions made by the Khwaja himself, but to the process of conversions beside
  the Khwaja's shrine which was visited by the Hindus of the region" (p. 140, folio 122, footnote 1).
- **The Naths: caste policy stated twice as a boundary of the order, with one recorded renunciation of wealth.** "The
  Naths initiated members of all castes, including those outside the Hindu caste system, such as Chandalas and
  sweepers, into their non-hierarchical order. Generally it would seem the Brahman castes were not attracted to the
  Naths" (p. 354, folio 332); "All Naths however were hostile to Hindu caste distinctions, particularly those
  practised by Brahmans and respected even the pariah and the untouchable" (p. 355, folio 333); "Manikchandra, a bania
  by caste, who left his wealth to join the Naths" (p. 354, folio 332).
- **Where the Naths lived, and the Shaivite ascetics distinguished from them by where they eat.** "The Naths and the
  Siddhas spread from the Panjab to Bengal lived in forests, wandered in towns and also established **permanent
  monasteries**" (p. 376, folio 354) — **none named or located** (not stated); "The Shaivite ascetics who haunted
  cemeteries, ate from skulls and even consumed corpses at the burning ghats, known as Aghoris or Aghora-panthis and
  the ascetic order of Kapalikas (wearers of skulls) are also known as yogis but are basically different to the Naths"
  (p. 354, folio 332), with the geographical consequence, "Aghoris and Kapalikas were uninterested in Middle Eastern
  regions where there were no burning ghats" (p. 355, folio 333) — **cremation ground as an institutional
  prerequisite.** **A numbered pantheon:** "The traditions venerate nine Naths and eighty-four Siddhas" (p. 354, folio
  332). **Muslims taking up yogic livelihoods:** "By the fifteenth century many groups of Muslims also became yogis,
  though not necessarily Naths or Siddhas. Some became professional beggars and acrobats" (p. 376, folio 354). **And
  a named base:** "from their centre at Peshawar moved to all parts of Central Asia and Iran, at the same time
  influencing both qalandars and sufis. … Some of the yogis who thronged the court of the Mongols were Buddhist
  Tantrics, but they were hardly distinguishable from the yogis" (p. 355, folio 333) — **the last clause is
  self-contradictory as printed and is not repaired here.**
- **The Sikh material, and it is thin.** "the Guru Garanth [Guru Granth], compiled by the fifth Sikh Guru, Arjan, in
  1604" (p. 350, folio 328) — **the only Sikh institutional date in the book**; "The Japji, the opening chapter of the
  Adi Granth which all Sikhs are required to repeat in the morning" (p. 414, folio 390); "**The idea of the
  transmission of light helped to form the disciples of Guru Nanak and his successors into a panth or order**"
  (p. 419, folio 395); and "After the death of the Fifth Guru, Arjan, at the hands of the Mughal Jahangir, the Sikhs
  dropped their pacifist policy and adopted their famous martial spirit. This led to a series of protracted conflicts
  between the Sikhs and the Mughals which soon took the form of a religious war" (p. 419, folio 395). **Guru Nanak is
  the principal figure of many archive rows and Guru Arjan Dev of several; those are many-row attachments and are made
  in `## Shrines in the archive this book informs`, not here. The substantive Guru Nanak material — the tax-on-shrines
  hymn, the devaluation of pilgrimage, honest work against religious professionals, the Guru "dressed like a malamati"
  at Delhi (p. 409, folio 385) and the confession-and-restitution advice "In keeping with recognized sufi practices"
  (p. 409, folio 385) — is recorded above.**
- **Religious professionals living on charity, condemned from the Sikh side, with Rizvi's own comparison.** "The great
  Guru emphasized honest work and deprecated mullas, pirs and yogis who lived on charity" (pp. 417–418, folios
  393–394); "'He alone, O Nanak, Knoweth the Way, Who earneth with the sweat of his brow, and then shareth it with the
  others'" (p. 417, folio 393), on which Rizvi remarks "**The teachings of the Chishtis on this subject were
  identical**" (p. 417, folio 393).
- **Outward marks of religion rejected from three sides at once.** Against yogis, "wearing rings in their ears and
  rosaries of Eleocarpus ganitrus around their necks, or the recitation of sakhi and sabad (Nath poetry), fails to
  make one a yogi" (p. 360, folio 338); against qazis, in the *Gorakhbani*, for "mechanically crying the name
  'Muhammad'" and reciting "the Kal/ima [OCR?] [Kalima] without gaining its real meaning" (p. 361, folio 339); and in
  Baba Farid's own doha, against the cap of initiation and the shaven head (p. 350, folio 328) —
  **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar** material. **And ritual attacked from inside the bhakta tradition:** "Fearlessly
  and cuttingly Kabir criticized ritualism and priest-craft, refusing to spare even monasticism in his scathing
  attacks… Devotion, penance, austerity, fasting and ablutions were meaningless without knowing the way to love and
  serve God" (p. 401, folio 377).
- **The Jain material, and it is one clause.** The only mention of the Jains in the practices and arguments extracts is
  inside a Sahajiya polemic: "the naked Jaina Ksapanaka-Yogins were frauds" (p. 352, folio 330). **There is no Jain
  temple, no Jain site and no Jain endowment anywhere in this book** — (not stated).
- **A definitional footnote on Tantricism that bears on the shared-ground question.** "Tantricism is the cult followed
  by certain left-handed sects of Hindus and Buddhists. Although not hostile to the Vedas, Tantricists do not approve
  of the conventional schools of Hindu philosophy. **Tantric doctrines are opposed to caste and class distinctions**"
  (p. 352, folio 330, footnote 1).
- **Caste and access, on both sides.** Ramanuja "taught that all classes should be given access to the sacred Vedas …
  permitted idol worship and accepted the Hindu division of society into castes, but he admitted Sudras and outcastes
  to his order" (p. 377, folio 355); the Alvars were "Rebels against the superior caste claims of the Brahmans"
  (p. 377, folio 355); Lalla "was suitably married to a member of another Brahman caste" "As was the custom of her
  caste" (p. 371, folio 349); "Guru Nanak accepted the caste structure in a spirit of resignation to the divine will
  but condemned the social prejudices surrounding the concepts of high and low castes" (p. 418, folio 394); Kabir
  "rebuked Brahmans who found defilement in almost everything" and held that "A man was courageous who ignored the
  rituals of his own caste" (p. 405, folio 381); and "the bhaktas were hostile to all idolatrous practices and caste
  distinctions and **with equal vehemence ridiculed Muslim forms of worship**" (p. 396, folio 372).
- **An order's habit invented for it, with its colours, and what that order did instead of building.** "Shaikh
  Zainuʾd-Din invented a distinctive dress for Rishis which consisted of a variegated woollen cloak with a black and
  white pattern running through it" (p. 373, folio 351); "Both mention them planting fruit trees for the benefit of
  the people" (p. 373, folio 351). **And a temple used as a residence and a wedding venue, from the *Mrigawati* and
  not a report of a real site:** "his father constructed a temple near the lake in which the prince could live"; "The
  two were married in the temple" (p. 388, folio 366).

### P. The book's own apparatus as a finding aid — and the rule that governs its use

*Chunks 026–028 are the Bibliography and Index. **Nothing in them is a statement about anything**: an index headword
and its folio list say only that the book indexes that term at those folios. Everything below is cited to the PDF page
on which the entry is printed, and **no index or endnote locator has been turned into a `(p. N)` anywhere in this
file**; the book's own folio numbers inside an entry are reproduced as part of the entry's text.*

- **The manuscript the book leans on that is held in Pakistan.** "'Abdu'llah Khweshgi Qasuri, Ma‘ariju'l-Wilayat, MS,
  **Punjab University, Lahore**." (p. 437, folio not stated) — a Persian manuscript by an author whose nisba is Qasuri
  (Kasur), and the source Rizvi leans on throughout Appendix B, cited there as "Ma‘ariju'l-Wilavat, f. S38b" [OCR?]
  [f. 538b], "ff. 530a-b, S3la" [OCR?] [531a] and "f. 538a" (pp. 428, 429, 431; folios not stated / 405 / 407).
- **The one manuscript in the whole bibliography held by a living sufi institution.** "Farid bin Salar, Rafigu'l-'Arifin
  [OCR?] [Rafiqu'l-'Arifin], **Mujibiyya Khangah [OCR?] [Khanqah], Phulwari, Patna.**" (p. 441, folio 417). **The
  repository is a khanqah, not a library; it is in Patna, not Pakistan.**
- **The one repository named as an *astana*, and the limits of what can be said about it.** "Muhammad Sadiq-Kashmiri
  Hamadani, Kalimatu's-Sadigin [OCR?] [Kalimatu's-Sadiqin], **Mashhad, Astana-i 'Aliyya**." (p. 445, folio 421) —
  **the only repository in the bibliography named as an *astana* rather than a library, museum, university, office,
  collection or private holder, and the book supplies no gloss for the word there.** The index carries "Astana, 317"
  as a headword of its own (p. 460, folio 436) and "Bari Dargah, 317" beside it (p. 462, folio 438), **so the book
  uses *astana* as a term of its own, in the same breath as *dargah*. That is an inference from the apparatus, it is
  marked as one, and no archive id is asserted for it.** Elsewhere the book does gloss the word: *astana* is
  "Literally a tomb; Bengalis believed that the saint was buried there" (p. 339, folio 317, footnote 2).
- **Custody of the Indian Sufi archive as the bibliography records it — roughly half institutional, half in named
  families and princely libraries.** Five entries lodge manuscripts with "Shah Taqi Hasan Balkhi" at Patna and
  Alamganj (pp. 442, 444, 448; folios 418, 420, 424) and one with "Shah Taqi Husain, *Alamganj, Patna" (p. 446, folio
  422) — **the holder's name differs between the two and the discrepancy is not resolved here**; three with Sir Salar
  Jang, Hyderabad (pp. 444, 445, 446; folios 420, 421, 422); one with the Asafiya, Hyderabad (p. 444, folio 420); two
  with Rampur (pp. 443, 444; folios 419, 420); one with the Habibganj Collection, Aligarh (p. 443, folio 419); one
  with "(Personal collection)" whose holder is not named (p. 437, folio not stated); and "Gisudaraz … Tarjuma-i
  'Awarif al-Ma'arif, Gulbarga MS" (p. 441, folio 417) — **the saint's own shrine town holds his manuscript, though
  the book does not say the shrine holds it** (not stated). **Six Kashmiri manuscripts at Srinagar are the largest
  single cluster in the Arabic–Persian run** (pp. 437, 438, 440; folios not stated / 414 / 416).
- **Three Lahore imprints, one Amritsar imprint, and the one imprint naming Pakistan.** "'Ali Asghar, Jawahir-i
  Faridi, Lahore, 1884." (p. 438, folio 414) — **a Lahore imprint whose title names Farid; the book says nothing
  further about it** (not stated); "'Abdu'l-Ha'i, Nuzhatu'l-Khawatir, Urdu tr., Lahore, 1965." (p. 437, folio not
  stated); "Baba Dawud Khaki … Wirdu'l-Muridin, Lahore, 1894." (p. 440, folio 416); "'Ali Hamadani, Mir Saiyid …
  Zakhiratu'l-Muluk, Amritsar, 1903-4, MS, Tashkent and Leningrad." (p. 439, folio 415); and "'Ali Sher Qani, Mir,
  Tuhfatu'l-Kiram, **Hyderabad, Pakistan**, 1971." (p. 439, folio 415).
- **The tazkira Rizvi demolishes, entered plainly in his own bibliography.** "Ghulam Sarwar, Khazinatu'l-Asfiya',
  Lucknow, 1873." (p. 441, folio 417). No author nisba, scope note or place of compilation — (not stated).
- **The five shrine-practice and endowment studies in the secondary list, and there are no others of their kind.**
  "Subhan, J.A., Sufism, its saints and shrines, reprint, Lucknow, 1960." (p. 455, folio 431); "Ja'far Sharif, Islam
  in India (Qanun-i Islam), tr. G.A. Herklots, Oxford, 1921, reprinted, New Delhi, 1972." (p. 452, folio 428); "Rose,
  H.A., The Darvishes, new edition, London, 1927." (p. 455, folio 431); "Goswamy, B.N. and Grewal, J.S., The Mughal
  and Sikh rulers and the Vaishaavas [OCR?] [Vaishnavas] of Pindori, Simla, 1969."; and "Grewal, J.S., The Mughals and
  the Jogis of Jakhbar, Simla, 1967." (p. 451, folio 427).
- **Four arguments the bibliography makes by its arrangement.** (i) **Urdu is classified with Sanskrit and Hindi, not
  with Persian, and that group alone is both primary and secondary**: "PRIMARY AND SECONDARY SOURCES / Sanskrit, Hindi
  and Urdu" (p. 448, folio 424) against "SECONDARY SOURCES / English and European Languages" (p. 450, folio 426).
  (ii) **A stated exclusion printed as a sentence that does not finish**: "The articles published in journals are not
  listed. The titles and the authors of the articles in the following work:" (p. 456, folio 432) — **and the list
  carries at least a dozen journal articles in full, among them Ritter in *Oriens* three times (p. 446, folio 422),
  Macdonald in *JRAS*, Makdisi in *BSOAS*, Margoliouth in *Islamic Culture*, Meier in *Der Islam* and
  *Eranos-Jahrbuch* (p. 453, folio 429), Molé twice (pp. 453–454, folios 429–430), Browne twice in *JRAS* (p. 451,
  folio 427), Nazim in *JRAS* (p. 454, folio 430) and Yusuf Husain in "F. Asiat" [OCR?] [J. Asiat.] (p. 456, folio
  432).** (iii) **The author cites himself nine times** — seven Hindi and Urdu titles (p. 449, folio 425) and
  "Muslim revivalist movements in northern India in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries, Agra, 1965." and
  "Religious and intellectual history of the Muslims in Akbar's Reign, Delhi, 1975." (p. 454, folio 430), **the first
  being the unattributed footnote source cited in the body at p. 394 (folio 370).** (iv) **The Kabir verses of Chapter
  Six come from a work on the Sikh religion**: the footnotes cite "Macauliffe, VI, p. 249" and eight further
  volume-and-page pairs with no title, and the bibliography supplies it — "Macauliffe, M.A., The Sikh religion,
  Oxford, 1909." (p. 453, folio 429).
- **The catalogue sigla, three of four decoded inside the book, and one work cited four times that is absent from
  it.** "Rieu, C., Catalogue of the Persian Manuscripts in the British Museum, reprint, London, 1966." (p. 454, folio
  430); "Ivanow, W., … Concise descriptive catalogue of the Persian Manuscripts in the collection of the Asiatic
  Society of Bengal, Calcutta, 1924." and "… in the Curzon collection, Asiatic Society of Bengal, Calcutta, 1926."
  (p. 452, folio 428); "'Abdu'l-Mugtadir [OCR?] [Abdu'l-Muqtadir], Catalogue of the Arabic and Persian manuscripts in
  the Oriental Public Library at Bankipore, Patna, 1908-30." (p. 450, folio 426); "Sachau and Ethé, Catalogue of the
  Persian Manuscripts in the Bodleian Library, Oxford, 1889." (p. 455, folio 431). **"Ethé" used alone, for the India
  Office, is the one siglum the bibliography does not decode, and "Descriptive catalogue of Bengali manuscripts",
  cited four times in the body, is not in the bibliography at all — checked against the B, C and D runs of all three
  sections by the Pass 1 worker and absent.**
- **Where the book's own index says its shrine-economy material sits.** Four fiscal headwords point at folios 345–346
  — "Aima, 280, 345", "Amil, 145, 346", "Amir, 346" (pp. 459, 460; folios 435, 436) and "Baitu'l-mal, 346" (p. 461,
  folio 437) — and "Ajodhan" also lists folio 345 (p. 459, folio 435). **That the grant-and-revenue material clusters
  there is an inference from the numbers, it is marked as one, and those folios have not been read.**

### Q. Rizvi's source criticism: what he says can and cannot be trusted

- **His two-class test for malfuzat, and his verdicts on the best sources.** The dependable class is "the second
  category which consisted of discourses recorded soon after they were delivered by a pir, and which were also dated"
  (p. 17, folio 3), against compilations "collected by a descendant or disciple of the sufi long after his death",
  whose "flavour … differed little from that of Christian hagiological works" (p. 17, folio 3); "The Fawaidu'l-Fu'ad
  and the Khairu'l-Majalis are the best examples of the art of malfuzat writing." (p. 21, folio 7); "The most
  dependable and scholarly collection of biographies of Indian sufis is the Akhbaru'l-Akhyar fi Asraru'l-Abrar" and
  "our best source of information for biographies still remains the Akhbaru'l-Akhyar" (pp. 25–26, folios 11–12).
- **What the genre does to its readers, and what it is nonetheless worth.** "Never in debate form, the discourses
  failed to include an analysis of opposing views and were believed to bear the stamp of infallibility. **This tended
  to produce a rigidity in the thinking of the devotees of the great Shaikh and to prevent the emergence of a detached
  rationality.**" (p. 20, folio 6); yet they "take us down to the lower echelons of Muslim society, and show that the
  passivity and inertia of its members was matched only by their firm faith in Allah." (p. 21, folio 7).
- **Pious forgery as a market response, with the demand named and generalised.** Seven spurious Chishti malfuzat are
  listed as "alleged discourses" (p. 18, folio 4); they "catered to the insatiable popular taste for details of the
  miracles of mystics, as well as those of yogis and dalandars"[OCR?] [qalandars]; "They also provided for the
  proselytizing militancy of many Muslims whose concerns were not really with sufism, but in the assertion of their
  own superiority in the field of religion." (p. 19, folio 5); "This type of pious forgery was not peculiar to sufis,
  the Qussas or story tellers during the first and second centuries of Islam and the forgers of the Hadis of the
  Prophet Muhammad were many." (p. 19, folio 5).
- **The nineteenth-century tazkira most shrine records rest on, declared unreliable and given a motive.** "it refers at
  length to many earlier sources, and its author believed these should all be considered of equal importance, even
  though many confused fact with fiction. **Using unreliable sufi family trees, Ghulam Sarwar accepted every date
  given in these texts and composed verses of chronograms.** The publication of this work synchronized with the impact
  of British liberalism in India, giving scholars an opportunity to glorify the mission of sufis and the impact of
  their miracles in the Islamization of the sub-continent." (p. 29, folio 15).
- **Hagiography discounted as a class, six times.** "Mystic literature gives an exaggerated account of the interest of
  Iltutmish in sufism" (p. 153, folio 135); "Later Chishti records give an exaggerated account of the intimate
  relationship between Baba Farid and Sultan Balban" (p. 162, folio 144); the Khizr verdict, "included to add a pious
  flavour" (p. 152, folio 134); "The story of his life in the biographical accounts, such as the Miratu'l-Asrar, are
  legendary" and "More reliable are the Saiyid's own letters, although they fail to provide biographical evidence"
  (p. 286, folio 266); "The anecdotes relating Jamali's witty replies to Jami's questions … are apocryphal" (p. 309,
  folio 287) — **while the fullest such anecdote is reproduced in the footnote anyway**; and of the Janam-Sakhis,
  "**hagiographic accounts … Inevitably supernatural and miraculous material predominates, in order to inspire
  devotion to the Guru**" (p. 407, folio 383).
- **A hagiography read selectively — rejected on facts, accepted on doctrine.** The *Mahbubiya* "should not be accepted
  literally" (p. 300, folio 278), yet "The Mahbubiyya, however, gives a legitimate insight into the teachings of the
  Saiyid" (p. 300, folio 278). **The method matters for any entry built on this book: Rizvi takes teachings from a
  text whose narratives he discards.** **And hagiography read as a later generation's self-image:** "**They also give
  some indication of the type of image Suhrawardi saints later sought to present of their spiritual ancestors**"
  (p. 304, folio 282).
- **Legends treated as instruments with a purpose, not as errors.** "However incredible these legends may appear, they
  formed an integral part of popular sufi beliefs, not only in India but throughout the Islamic world. **They were
  mainly designed to glorify Islam and to strengthen beliefs in the supernatural and in the performance of
  miracles**" (p. 343, folio 321); and a story kept for what it reveals, "noteworthy, not for its novelty, but for the
  sufi response to the Mongol invasion of Iran… it reveals the superstitious beliefs associated with the power of
  dervishes" (p. 329, folio 307).
- **Separating the cult from its occupant — the most transferable methodological act in the book for a shrine
  archive.** "There is no mention of Salar Mas'ud in the history of the I]bari [OCR?] [Ilbari] Turks"; "**Probably it
  was one of Prince Nasiru'd-Din's fallen generals who was the real occupant of the grave** which was to become the
  centre of the Salar Mas'ud's legend. By the reign of Sultan Muhammad bin Tughlug the legend had been greatly
  expanded" (p. 333, folio 311); **with the corollary, his most quotable and most contestable sentence:** "To the
  common Muslims who remembered Salar Mas'ud Ghazi with pet names … no historical perspective was needed. **To the
  Hindus who considered him a saint of miraculous power, the number of their brethren he killed or Islamized was then,
  as it is now, meaningless**" (p. 336, folio 314). **And a source dated as a forgery:** the *Tarikh-i Mulla Muhammad
  Ghaznawi* "is not referred to by any Ghaznawid authority and seems to have been written to satisfy popular curiosity
  about Salar Mas'ud at a later date" (p. 334, folio 312).
- **Chronology used to destroy a foundation legend, and the evidential position stated.** "Both latter accounts make
  the Khwaja's encounter with Prithviraj an impossibility" (p. 135, folio 117); "The earliest works which relate
  anecdotes of the early life of Khwaja Mu‘inu'd-Din Chishti and his encounters with the court of Prithviraj at Ajmer
  are the apocryphal malfuzats" (p. 134, folio 116); "**no reliable information regarding his early life, before he
  settled in Ajmer, remains. The only information recorded is the name of the area where he was born, the name of his
  teacher, and the fact that he had travelled widely**" (p. 134, folio 116), reinforced by an argument from silence,
  "Strangely enough, the voluminous book, Khairu'l-Majalis, does not mention Khwaja Mu‘inu'd-Din in any connection"
  (p. 134, folio 116).
- **The same technique applied seven more times.** "This story must be apocryphal for ʿAli al-Riza, who was born in
  Medina in 148/765, was summoned to Marw by Caliph al-Maʾmun (813-33) in 201/816 and was killed at Tus, two years
  later in 818…" (p. 65, folio 49); "as he died in 697/1298, the meeting would have been impossible" (p. 138, folio
  120, footnote 4); "The Sururu's-Sudur states he was then ninety, but this would appear to be incorrect" (p. 142,
  folio 124); "Such a story, however, is anachronistic for it was not possible for both the Qazi and the Sultan to
  have been together in Baghdad" (p. 214, folio 196); "According to Firishta, the Saivid [Saiyid] was initiated by
  Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din into the Chishtiyya order in 772 1370-71, but as the Shaikh died in 1356, this must be
  incorrect" (p. 299, folio 277, note 2); "Shaikh Taqi of Kara Manikpur… should not be identified as Kabir's guru for
  he was a disciple of Shaikh Salim Chishti (1479-1572)" (p. 398, folio 374); and, using a dated work to overturn a
  dated death, "the date of his death, 16 Shawwal 718/11 December 1318, given by Jami is incorrect, for another of his
  works, entitled the Zadu'l- Musafirin… was composed in 720/1320" (p. 226, folio 206). **Amir Husain is nevertheless
  said to have attended a mahzar "in the reign of Iltutmish… with his spiritual director" (p. 226, folio 206), which
  Rizvi's own chronology makes impossible.**
- **A modern scholar's chronology demolished in a footnote, with the corrected dates supplied.** "Relying uncritically
  on the Sivaru'l-Auliya' [OCR?] [Siyaru'l-Auliya'], Nizami says that the visit took place in Jumada I, 664/February,
  1266 and the khi/ajat-nama [khilafat-nama] is dated 13 Ramazan 664/18 June 1266… The main difficulty in following
  this chronology is that the Baba is believed to have died on 5 Muharram 664/17 October 1265. AA, p. 54. Nizami also
  accepts this date (see p. 56.) but fails to reconcile the two dates which imply that the Baba would have been
  visited by the Shaikh five months after his death and the khilafat-nama granted nine months after his death. It
  would therefor [therefore] seem that 664 must be a printing error. **Nizami's conversion of Hijra dates is also
  wrong**" (p. 174, folio 156, footnote 9); **Rizvi's corrected dates in the text are Jumada I 663/February 1265 and
  13 Ramazan 663/29 June 1265** (pp. 174–175, folios 156–157). **shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar** material.
- **Two incompatible death dates printed side by side, the later preferred but not asserted.** "As indicated by his
  letters to Sultan Ibrahim Shah Shargi [Sharqi] on Ganesa's uzurpation [OCR?] [usurpation] of power, Saiyid Muhammad
  Ashraf was still alive in 1415. This would tend to negate the date of his death as given in the Khazinatu'l Asfiya'
  as 27 Muharram 808/25 July 1405, although it has been accepted uncritically by many modern scholars. Basing his
  assumption on the Tabagqat-i Shahjahani [OCR?] [Tabaqat-i Shahjahani], Rieu believes the Saiyid died after
  840/1436—37. Although this would make the Shaikh's life rather long, this date is more acceptable than the
  anachronistic date specified in the Khazinatu'l-Asfiya'" (p. 288, folio 268).
- **Three dates decided on the author's judgement alone, no criterion stated.** "Nothing can be said about the
  authenticity of Shaikh Taqi's date of death but that for Kabir is undoubtedly incorrect" (p. 398, folio 374); "Of
  various dates for his birth 1425 is the most acceptable" (p. 398, folio 374); "Of the many dates given for Kabir's
  death 1505 is the most reliable" (p. 399, folio 375).
- **An initiation left as an unresolved source problem in a footnote while the main text asserts one answer.** "In an
  earlier statement Jamali says that Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din initiated Khwaja Bakhtiyar into his discipleship at Ush
  p. 18… According to the Siyaru'l-Auliya', Khwaja Qutbu'd-Din met Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din in the mosque of Imam
  Abu'l-Lais Samarqandi, see p. 48. Abu'l-Fazl relates that Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din initiated Khwaja Qutbu'd-Din into his
  discipleship at the age of eighteen… According to Firishta, Qutbu'd-Din became Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din's disciple in
  Isfahan" (p. 152, folio 134, footnote 2); **the main text asserts Baghdad** (p. 152, folio 134).
- **Two accounts declared irreconcilable, and two stories left unresolved with the outcome taken instead.** "The
  inconsistencies between the Chishti legend and Jamali's study are irreconcilable, but the latter's account is
  supported to a greater degree by political events and the Khwaja's own philosophy of what entailed a saintly life"
  (p. 140, folio 122); "**Regardless of which story is the correct one**, it seems clear that wealth accumulated by
  Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din did not long remain with his sons" (p. 220, folio 202); "**Whether the story is true or not**,
  there remains little doubt that the Shaikh was a man of independent views" (p. 223, folio 203); and a source
  conflict left open, "is said to have abdicated in favour of his son. Another version of the story is that he was in
  fact imprisoned by his son" (p. 430, folio 406).
- **Sorting myth from probability inside a hagiography.** "**The first part of this story is probably mythical but it
  is quite likely that Shaikh Ahmad was presented to Baba Ishaq by some merchants when he was about four years old**"
  (p. 429, folio 405). **And two anecdotes rejected on plausibility:** "Undoubtedly this anecdote is apocryphal.
  Firstly, no muqgta‘ [muqta‘] would dare to harass the Khwaja to gain a farman for wasteland" (p. 143, folio 125);
  and "The accusations are typical of the kind of resentment shown by the orthodox against the Wahdat al-Wujud and did
  not necessarily have to be proved" (p. 251, folio 231, footnote 3).
- **A miracle traced to a prototype, and transmission by memory warned against.** The vanished-corpse story
  "undoubtedly owes much to the tale mentioned in Chapter One of the bier of Ma'ruf Karkhi which was fought over by
  Muslims and non-Muslims alike, and may therefore be of sufi origin" (p. 399, folio 375); "The most important of his
  verses were generally memorized by his disciples after they had been uttered, and then written down immediately or
  soon afterwards. **This process gave rise to considerable interpolation and naturally many unauthentic verses are
  included**" (p. 399, folio 375).
- **A silsila printed and disowned in the same breath, and one overruled outright.** The Chishti chain from Hasan Basri
  to Abu Ishaq Shami is printed in full with the footnote "**Many scholars dispute the authenticity of this
  genealogical tree**" (p. 132, folio 114), and "Some Indian Chishti genealogical trees do not mention Khwaja Ahmad or
  Ruknu'd-Din" (p. 133, folio 115, footnote 7); "Hasan's discipleship with ‘Ali and his conversion by him, would
  appear to be pious myths composed by later writers. Both medieval and modern scholars have challenged their
  authenticity" (p. 43, folio 27) — **with the claim itself stated on the same page.** **And a founder absent from the
  early record:** "Early sources do not refer to Shaikh 'Ala'u'd-Din 'Ali bin Ahmad Sabir, the founder of the Sabiri
  branch of the Chishti order"; the branch "rose to prominence in the sufi movement mainly because of the popularity
  of Shaikh Ahmad 'Abdu'l-Haqq" (p. 172, folio 154).
- **A source-criticism warning that should govern every qalandar fact in the book.** "**Although the Chishtiyya records
  are our only source of information about the Qalandariyyas and the Jawaliqis, and represent their behaviour as
  shocking and sacrilegious, the authors admit that among these radicals were genuine spiritualists.**" (p. 325, folio
  303). **And a source-criticism of the Chishti tradition itself:** "Chishti sources and other authorities, impressed
  with the accounts of both sufis and members of the 'ulama' group, have condemned the Sultan as irreligious and
  heretical" (p. 194, folio 176), with the instruction to read the khalifas' careers "against such a background,
  rather than in the light of assumptions made by a number of medieval and modern scholars" (p. 194, folio 176).
- **A warning about the Afghan-period chronicles that governs any date taken from them.** "The board of scholars
  appointed by Akbar … admitted that they had no written record of the Afghan days and compiled an account of that
  period from Mughal sources and oral traditions… **This material cannot be safely relied on to dispute the account
  written by Bhai Gurdas Bhalla**" (p. 408, folio 384).
- **A silent correction of the book's own narrative in its own footnote, and a general caution about numbers.** Having
  written that the Shaikh "quoted a Hadis from the Prophet" (p. 180, folio 162), the footnote says "The Shaikh
  himself, however, did not quote a Hadis but an opinion expressed by Ghazali" (p. 180, folio 162, footnote 4); and
  "**although, of course, numbers used in mystic literature are not necessarily accurate**" (p. 213, folio 195), with
  "The number may not be correct as it is used for long ascetic exercises" (p. 430, folio 406, footnote 1). **That
  caution governs every count quoted anywhere in this file.**
- **Where he says the evidence runs out — eight admissions, the honest limits of this book.** "The date of Shaikh
  Jalalu'd-Din Tabrizi's death is unknown, and no reliable information of his Bengali khalifas is available" (p. 220,
  folio 202); "No existing sources explain such changes." (p. 319, folio 297); "The information available is, however,
  insufficient to ascribe, with much certainty, the above theories to Saiyid Nuru'd-Din" (p. 213, folio 195); "There
  are no objective data available to determine the impact of Hallaj's ideas of the soul after his visit to Sind"
  (p. 345, folio 323); "**Unfortunately existing literature throws little light on yogis, who are constantly referred
  to as 'jogis'**" (p. 345, folio 323); "**No authentic record of this process exists**" (p. 433, folio not stated);
  "no Bengali work in prose or poetry on sufism written between the thirteenth and fifteenth centuries has yet come to
  light" (pp. 373–374, folios 351–352); and "There may well have been other minor Kaziruni khanqahs in the islands
  which Ibn Battuta fails to mention" (p. 434, folio 410).
- **Where a conjecture fills a gap and is marked as one.** "Hagiologists fail to mention the name of the Sultan to whom
  they refer in this anecdote. The conflict may have occurred during the reign of any of three Sultans…" (p. 278,
  folio 258); "**The demolition of the temple contravened the covenant; probably the Brahmans had not allowed Saiyid
  'Ali's followers to stay in the temple and the infringement was used as a pretext**" (p. 318, folio 296) — **his own
  "probably", no source cited**; "**Although available records give no evidence of a connection between the Saiyid
  followers of Mir 'Ali Hamadani and guilds it may not be wrong to presume** that before joining Mir Saiyid 'Ali some
  of them were connected with all the three organizations." (p. 316, folio 294); "**It is possible the orthodox may
  have kept the Haidaris from Firuz's court, as there is no mention of their presence there**" (p. 332, folio 310) —
  **explicitly an inference from silence, and he says so.**
- **A late source accepted on plausibility alone.** Of Sher Khan's beheading: "Contemporary sources fail to reveal this
  hostility and Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Huqq [OCR?] ['Abdu'l-Haqq] is also silent on the matter; however a later authority
  writes that Sher Khan was beheaded … **There is no reason to doubt the validity of this statement**" (p. 261, folio
  241). **The only evidence is a late authority and the author's own sense of what was usual.**
- **Sources preferred and corrected.** "The story of the Managqibu'l-Asfiya' [Manaqibu'l-Asfiya'] seems more authentic
  as it incorporates family traditions" (p. 249, folio 229, footnote 2); "According to the Akhbaru'l-Akhyar, Shaikh
  Ahmad … practised arduous ascetic exercises in the forests near Agra. **It would appear, however, that these were
  performed in Rajgir, in Bihar**" (p. 249, folio 229); "Firishta and Taba-Taba, who had access to sources in the
  Deccan unavailable to north Indian scholars, say that it was about 815/1412-13…" (p. 272, folio 252). **And four
  named confusions of one person for another, corrected:** "Ibn Battuta confused him with Shaikh Faridu'd-Din"
  (p. 167, folio 149, footnote 7); "In the Rihla of Ibn Battuta, Shah Jalal of Sylhet is confused with Shaikh Jalal
  al-Tabrizi whom he had visited. Halayodha Misra, the author of Shek Subhodya, also made the same mistake" (p. 220,
  folio 202); "According to Jamali, the Sultan was in Thatta, although it was more likely he was in Bengal." (p. 330,
  folio 308); "Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din … himself made this statement about his ancestry to Ibn Battuta, however, it is not
  necessarily authentic" (p. 208, folio 190).
- **The sober compiler's technique, held up as a model, and political chronicles characterised as sources.** Shaikh
  'Abdu'l-Haqq's three-tabaqa scheme "helped Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Haqq to place the biographies of sufis whose dates were
  questionable into a reasonable historical sequence, **thus avoiding the acceptance of dates based on unauthentic
  traditions**" (p. 26, folio 12); "Political chronicles refer to sufis only in connection with their political role in
  history or in short biographical notes either at the end of each reign or in the conclusion of the works themselves."
  (p. 29, folio 15); Barani's work "is an interesting psychological study in the analysis of the background to
  conflicts between different groups at all levels of society" (p. 30, folio 16). **And a private library dispersed
  into a regional book market:** Ghulam Sarwar's books "were dispensed amongst a large number of Panjabi book lovers
  and scholars" at the end of the nineteenth and in the early twentieth century (p. 29, folio 15).

### R. Rizvi against named scholars

- **Against K.A. Nizami, six times, and the sixth turns personal.** (i) "It is obvious that in his story of Hindu
  mistrust of Islam, Nizami borrows from al-Biruni, who relates only the situation created by the plundering raids of
  Mahmud of Ghazna"; "he did not hesitate to claim that Prithviraj and the high caste Hindus disliked the Khwaja
  nevertheless the common people flocked to him. **This only indicates the thinking of modern Muslims who seek the
  support of a theory that the caste system was responsible for the Hindu defeat in India and that by it Islam
  rescued the low caste Hindus from degredation**" (p. 136, folio 118). (ii) "Nizami's attempt to prove that
  Iltutmish's rise to the throne was due to the influence of Baghdad mystics precludes him from re-examining other
  historical possibilities" (p. 153, folio 135). (iii) **The most consequential for the archive:** "**The Baba did
  not, therefore, select Ajodhan in order to reform or convert 'backward Hindu tribes', as Nizami seeks to show**"
  (p. 159, folio 141). (iv) The chronology footnote quoted above (p. 174, folio 156, footnote 9). (v) "Although nafs
  means lower self, nafas means breath. The Shaikh himself translated nafas as dam but **Nizami, reluctant to give any
  credit to Siddhas, deliberately reads na/s [OCR?] [nafs] in the sense of the lower self**" (p. 206, folio 188,
  footnote 8). (vi) "A modern Muslim scholar who appears not to be conversant with the different forms of Bhakti
  argues that Chaitanya was influenced by Shaikh Nur Qutb-i ʿAlam" (p. 379, folio 357), with Majumdar quoted against
  him and Rizvi's barb appended: "**Majumdar's only mistake is in referring to Nizami as 'Dr.'**" (pp. 379–380,
  folios 357–358). **Note also the unnamed targets, "A modern author states rather naively" (p. 135, folio 117) and
  "A more quaint appraisal of the Khwaja's work by another modern Muslim scholar" (p. 136, folio 118).**
- **Against W.H. McLeod, six times, on sufi influence on Guru Nanak.** "**What is disconcerting is the general
  dependance, notably by McLeod, on historically unreliable material and an interpretation founded on nineteenth and
  twentieth century conditions**" (p. 407, folio 383); "McLeod's arguments are founded on an ignorance of contemporary
  sufi accounts… **to Muslims travelling to Mecca, Guru Nanak would not have been considered a Hindu, but a muwahhid
  dressed as a malamati**" (p. 412, folio 388); on divine will, his claim "fails to show an understanding of either
  the theological Riza or the mystical Riza which is equivalent to Hukam" (p. 417, folio 393), with "McLeod owes his
  definition of the divine will to Canon Kenneth Gragg [Cragg?] but fails to acknowledge any reference to any of the
  latter's works" (p. 417, folio 393, footnote 2); on "debased Sufism", "**These remarks introduce a number of ideas
  which are founded on a total ignorance of both sunni orthodoxy and sufism in India. Readers of the previous pages
  need not be reminded that there was no classical pattern of Arab and Persian sufism**" (p. 420, folio 396), with "A
  large number of sufi centres had emerged all over the Islamic world which had established their own particular
  features and **the developments in India were significant in their own right**" (p. 420, folio 396); "McLeod's
  remark … **tends to indicate that in his mind Sunni orthodoxy and sufism were identical**" (p. 420, folio 396); and
  the one silsila claim about Pakistan in the Conclusion, "He seems to be unaware of Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus Gangohi who
  lived in Shahabad, his disciple, Shaikh Jalal of Thaneswar, **and the Qadiris who had made the Panjab the main
  centre of their activities**" (p. 420, folio 396) — **no Qadiri shrine, saint or town in the Panjab is named** (not
  stated).
- **Against the epigraphers, on Baghdad and Ceylon — directly relevant to how shrine claims get "proved".** "Some Sikh
  scholars have sought archaeological confirmation of Guru Nanak's journey to Baghdad and their attempt shows more
  enthusiasm than judgement… **The Arabic inscription at Baghdad which is alleged by some to refer to the Guru in fact
  does not and is in sixteenth century Turkish**" (p. 413, folio 389); "A similar effort is made by other scholars to
  authenticate Guru Nanak's visit to Ceylon on the basis of epigraphical evidence **but such pursuits have no real
  relevance to the great Guru's life**" (p. 413, folio 389). **And against Trumpp:** "His remark that the Sikh authors
  were generally unaware of the fact that the popular religion in Ceylon was Buddhism proves his ignorance of the
  Ceylonese religious and racial composition" (p. 411, folio 387).
- **Against Nicholson, using modern Buddhology.** "**This theory which has great appeal to many Muslim scholars is
  questionable in the light of modern research. Nirvana is not a purely negative concept but a positive one.** The
  state of arhantship in which nirvana has been achieved, cannot be described as either existence or non-existence
  and can be likened to the goals not only of Abu Yazid, but even of Muhasibi and Junaid" (pp. 344–345, folios
  322–323).
- **Against R.C. Temple and Muhibbuʾl Hasan on Lalla, with the direction of influence reversed.** "modern scholars such
  as R.C. Temple and Muhibbuʾl Hasan have mistakenly sought to prove a sufi influence on Lalla's verses… These,
  however, so strongly express the teachings of the Kashmiri Shaivites that such a theory seems implausible" (p. 371,
  folio 349); then "The cross fertilization of sufi beliefs with those expressed by Lalla throughout her verses led to
  the establishment of the Rishi order of sufis in Kashmir" (p. 371, folio 349), and "It would seem, however, that the
  Shaikh obtained inspiration from Lalla" (p. 372, folio 350).
- **Against M.A. Rahman on who converted in Bengal.** "There is no evidence that they were 'down-trodden and persecuted
  Buddhists and Hindus,' as a modern scholar writes" — footnoted "M.A. Rahman, Social and cultural history of Bengal,
  1, Karachi, 1963, p. 99" (p. 220, folio 202).
- **Against Molé, Mahdi Husain, I.H. Qureshi, Minhaj Siraj, Firishta, Jami and Badaʾuni.** "Mole's conclusion that
  Shaikh Najmu'd-Din tended to create a type of Sunnite-Shi'ism has no real basis" (p. 113, folio 95); "Modern
  authorities, such as Mahdi Husain and Nizami, believe that the Sultan's hostility to the mystics was influenced by
  the teachings of Maulana 'Abdu'l-'Aziz Ardbili… **This theory is without foundation**" (p. 194, folio 176); "Ibaha
  … **is not a Hindu sect as I.H. Qureshi believes**" (p. 255, folio 235, footnote 3); "Minhaj's account of the
  conquest of Nodia (Nadia)… reads like a historical novel rather than a detached history" (p. 276, folio 256); "The
  statement by Minhaj Siraj … that Berke was brought up from infancy as a Muslim, is hardly feasible" (p. 247, folio
  227); "Bada'uni's remarks that Jamali had sought corrections of his verses from Jami are also not authentic"
  (p. 309, folio 287).
- **Against a whole consensus, on the ground that nobody checked.** "To nineteenth and twentieth century scholars from
  H. Blochman and A.L. Sriwastava to 'Aziz Ahmad, Shaikh Gada'i has been depicted as a Shi'i, for it seemed to them
  natural that Bairam Khan, who was believed to be a Shi'i, would never have elevated a Sunni to such a status.
  **None of these writers have considered the unlikelihood of a son of Shaikh Jamali becoming a Shi'i or, if such was
  the case, to try to establish when and how the change occurred**" (p. 311, folio 289).
- **Against unnamed opponents, three times.** "A modern scholar's view that philosophy was a problem which had been
  highlighted during the Tughluq period is historically inaccurate" (p. 213, folio 195), cited only as "Some aspects
  of religion and politics, p. 161" (p. 213, folio 195, footnote 2) — **no author** (not stated); "Their writings were
  not designed to fulfil a missionary aim, as some admirers have suggested" (p. 386, folio 364) — **no opponent named
  and no evidence given** (not stated); "The view that Sultan Muhammad bin Tughluq broke the central Chishti
  organization is incompatible with the spirit of the si/sila [silsila] which had been set up by Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din
  and Baba Farid" (p. 207, folio 189) — **no holder of the view named** (not stated).
- **Against Majumdar, whose thesis he quotes in full and then declines to refute on its central point.** "'… **the two
  orthodox religions showed no visible signs of being seriously affected by this sudden intrusion of radical elements.
  They pursued their even tenor, resembling the two banks of a river, separated by the stream that flows between them.
  Attempts were made to build a bridge connecting the two, but ended in failure. Even if there were any temporary
  bridge, it collapsed in no time**'" (pp. 421–422, folio not stated / 398). **Rizvi's two-sentence reply does not
  defend the bridge:** "Firstly, the influence of sufism was not short-lived; secondly, **members of the orthodox
  sections of both Hinduism and Islam moved in different spheres, while both sufis and Hindu saints (bhaktas) remained
  unconcerned with the activities pursued by the orthodox**" (p. 422, folio 398), followed by "This is not the place to
  discuss the circumstances which may have made, as Majumdar suggests, the so-called temporary bridge collapse."
- **Where he defends a source, and where he defends the accused against the medieval verdict.** Of Jamali: "the
  anecdotes which his hosts in Irani khangahs [khanqahs] related are not necessarily correct, but they are set in the
  perspective of contemporary mystical traditions, and Jamali cannot be censured for accepting them" (p. 136, folio
  118), and "**Jamali's research on the Khwaja's career appears, to a large degree, to be factual**" (p. 140, folio
  122). Of Hallaj: "Hallaj's concept of Anaʾl-Haqq does not imply that human nature (nasut) is identical or
  interchangeable with the Divine (Lahut); to take a less elaborate simile—water does not become wine, when they are
  mixed"; "Actually Hallaj meant that his 'I' was 'acted upon' by divine grace" (p. 74, folio 58). Of Sidi Maula: "The
  available evidence tends to indicate that it was the Khan-i Khanan, Arkali Khan's rival who was the real conspirator
  and Sidi Maula a mere tool. The belief that the latter's finances came from alchemy or a divinely inspired source
  were myths" (p. 331, folio 309), with "Sidi Maula's life was to become the great example which justified the Chishti
  principle of unreserved non-involvement in politics and all power struggles." (p. 330, folio 308).
- **Two judgements of his own delivered without a source.** "Hallaj's concept is **identical** with that of the leading
  Christian mystic, St. John of the Cross" (p. 74, folio 58); and of Barani, "A bonvivant who lived extravagantly, he
  was endowed with a naive sense of his own intellectual and religious superiority" (p. 192, folio 174).

### S. Rizvi's own unevidenced claims, flagged

*Recorded together because nothing may enter an entry from general knowledge and these are the places where the book
supplies an assertion without an argument. Anyone citing them must carry the flag.*

- **"obviously" doing the work of an argument, three times.** The zanbil "custom is **obviously** of Buddhist origin"
  (p. 91, folio 75 corrected, footnote) — no source, argument or intermediary; "'Iraqi sent a copy of the Lama'at to
  Shaikh Sadru'd-Din 'Arif, **obviously** in an attempt to popularize Ibn al-'Arabi in India" (p. 226, folio 206); and
  "**This story was an obvious invention** and was an attempt to associate Kabir's parentage in some way with
  Hinduism", whose substitute rests on "**That his parents' ancestors were yogis is not impossible**" (p. 398, folio
  374). **And a thesis of untraceable borrowing that by its own terms cannot be evidenced:** "the monastic tradition of
  Buddhism and Christianity, and Vedantist and Yogic philosophy were all Islamized by members of silsilas in such a
  way as to make them virtually unidentifiable" (p. 99, folio 83).
- **A yogic source for the Naqshbandi disciplines, with no yogic text named, and the same move repeated.** "his eight
  principles of sufi life and the rituals he advocated were largely based on yogic practices, current in the Bukhara
  region" (p. 113, folio 95); "The division is influenced by the Hath- Yogic ideas" (p. 217, folio 199, footnote 2).
  **And a Buddhist source for a sufi mode of life, as Rizvi's inference and not his source's statement:** "His
  description implies that that particular mode of life was based on the life-pattern of a section of Buddhist monks"
  (p. 104, folio 88). **And an Indian, Chinese and Tibetan genealogy for a sect in one sentence:** "A sect of sufis
  which was imbued with Hindu, Chinese and Tibetan beliefs of the eternity of the spirit shocked the majority of sufis
  … The former were known as Hululis" (p. 77, folio 61) — **the supporting footnote is devoted to the Qurʾanic proof,
  not to evidence of the borrowing.**
- **A diffusionist substitute offered in place of a controversy he declares unimportant — directly relevant to any
  Sindhi row that would like fanaʾ to have come from Sind.** "Some hagiologists suggest that Bayazid learnt the
  doctrine of fanaʾ from his teacher Abu ʿAli Sindi. Among modern scholars Nicholson and R.C. Zaehner support this
  theory, while Arberry rejects it. The latter provides evidence to prove that Sind was a village in Khurasan and not
  the province of Sind in modern Pakistan. **The controversy is insignificant because ancient Indian thought and ideas
  on mysticism had continually aroused interest in the Khurasanian region and these naturally fused with Bayazid's
  expression of his own mystical experiences**" (p. 61, folio 45). **Rizvi neither adopts nor refutes Arberry; he
  declares the question unimportant and substitutes a claim for which no evidence is cited.**
- **Yogic influence on the qalandars and the Rifaʿiyya, and three claims of Hindu origin for qalandar bodily practice,
  none evidenced.** "The various branches of qalandars and sufis of the Rifa'iyya order, confined mainly to Turkey,
  Syria and Egypt, **were significantly influenced by wandering yogis**" (p. 345, folio 323); the *sikh-i muhr* is "A
  custom acquired from the Hindu Naga sanyasis" and ear piercing came "from the Nath-yogis or Kanphata yogis (Split Ear
  Yogis)" (p. 329, folio 307); "The contact of qalandars with Nath-yogis, also wandering throughout that part of the
  world, influenced them to wear ear-rings." (p. 325, folio 303).
- **Sufi influence inferred from two loan-words, with no doubt marked.** On Namdeva's "Come God, the Qalandar / Wearing
  the dress of an Abdali": "Abdal, mystical officers of the Divine court; see p. 40. supra. **The use of these Sufi
  terms shows a deep sufi influence on Namdeva**" (p. 378, folio 356, footnote 1).
- **Three assertions of doctrinal identity, of which only one is demonstrated.** "The Nath theory of the Lord existing
  alone in a void is no different from Jili's theory of a/-'Ama [al-ʿAma] (the dark mist and blindness)" (p. 363, folio
  341); "To Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus the Nath theory of creation was a replica of Ibn al-'Arabi's theory" (p. 363, folio
  341); "This philosophy of Gorakhnath and the Siddhas called the Dwaita-dwaita-vilakshana-vada or
  Pakshapata-binirmukta-vada is nearest to the Wahdat al-Wujud" (p. 362, folio 340). **Only the water-and-bubbles
  analogy is set out from the texts:** "Akulam kulam adhatte kulam cakulam icchati jala-budbuda-bat nyayat ckakarah
  [OCR?] [ekakarah] Parah Sivah", translated "Akula embraces Kula (the phenomenal self-expression of Reality) and Kula
  yearns for Akula (the noumenal essence of Reality). The relation is analogous to that between water and water
  bubbles. In reality Para-Siva (Supreme Spirit) is absolutely one" (p. 356, folio 334), with the sufi restatement at
  p. 362 (folio 340). **Further equations of technique are asserted the same way:** "the union of Shakti, the sun, and
  Shiva, the moon, according to the Shaikh, is the salat-i-ma'kus of the sufis. The yogi equivalent is the ulti sadhna
  (regressive process)" (pp. 358–359, folios 336–337); "sultan-i zikr was comparable to the Nath Siddha's nad, and that
  fana' al-fana' was a state experienced by the jivan-mukta" (p. 364, folio 342); "The sunya, or sahaja of the Shaikh is
  also identical with the sunya and the sahaja of the Naths" (p. 360, folio 338).
- **A musical invention asserted at two strengths in two consecutive sentences, neither sourced, and a second invention
  stated flatly.** "**It is believed that** Khusraw invented the musical instrument, the sitar" is hedged; "Popular
  Indian melodies such as Qawi/ [Qawl] are **undoubtedly** the invention of Amir Khusraw" is not (p. 190, folio 172);
  "During his lifetime, Rumi invented the famous whirling dance" (p. 98, folio 82). **This note must travel with any use
  of the first passage for qawwali at shrines.**
- **Forcible conversion asserted in his own voice, unhedged and unsourced, inside a favourable characterisation.** The
  Khwaja's ascetic life "was an inspiration to both the Turkic ghazis, who swelled the Islamic forces through a lust for
  plunder, and to the Hindus who were forcibly converted to Islam" (p. 141, folio 123). **And a characterisation offered
  without a source:** the Chishti mission was to save converts "from emulating the example of the **racist and élitist**
  Turkic governing classes" (p. 246, folio 226).
- **An evaluative paragraph whose whole weight rests on "might have".** "**Many Naths might have indulged in
  homosexuality, orgiastic practices, necrophilia, scatology, bestiality and other sexual perversions**, but there were
  many groups of sober Naths who disseminated the real spiritual tenets of their founders" (p. 355, folio 333). **No
  source, no case and no text is cited for any of it.**
- **A refutation resting on an admitted unknown, and the counter-thesis whose evidence is not named.** "The date of
  Ardbili's arrival at Muhammad bin Tughluq's court is unknown, but he was certainly absent from it during these early
  years, and **probably** reached India from Syria sometime after 1330" (p. 194, folio 176); "**All independent sources
  affirm** that the Sultan was a most religious man… The Sultan was not hostile to the sufi movement per se, as has been
  represented, but was unprepared to tolerate opposition towards the implementation of his political policies" (p. 194,
  folio 176) — **no independent source is named** (not stated).
- **Corrections and conjectures offered without evidence.** "All medieval, and some modern, historians interpret the
  battle between Ghazi Malik and Khusraw as a Hindu-Islamic conflict. In fact, the struggle was political, each party
  raising emotive, religious slogans to rally support" (p. 179, folio 161); of Badaʾuni's king-making claim, "rumours of
  a rebellion in the capital **must have been widespread** at the time" (p. 204, folio 186); and a defence of a poet's
  dedication offered without evidence from the poet, "the author was not hoping for temporal rewards from his ruler…
  Sana'i was therefore not in a position to ignore his earthly ruler" (p. 96, folio 80).
- **A borrowing argued from a shared word and then qualified out of existence.** "Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din in fact borrowed
  the ideal both from the Akhi system… **However, the Akhi system never really penetrated into India.** It is
  nevertheless quite reasonable to attribute Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din Bukhari's puritanism and religious militancy to
  inspiration from the Akhi and Futuwwa organizations" (p. 303, folio 281). **And a cause that precedes its effect by
  almost a century, which the book does not address:** "during the early sixteenth century the conversion of a number of
  important officials seems to have been prompted by the approaching millennium of Islam which was completed in 1591-92"
  (p. 306, folio 284).
- **Hedges on his own central claims.** "the later use of Hindawi in further explanations of this concept was **most
  probably** a significant factor in the arousal of Hindu interest in sufism" (p. 349, folio 327); "However, he **does
  not seem to have** accepted either financial assistance or land grants. His family then faced a severe economic crisis
  often starving for days" (p. 366, folio 344). **And a geographical fact the book cannot fix:** "Some authors suggest
  that Maghar was close to Banaras, others believe it was in the district of Basti, near Gorakhpur in U.P." (p. 399,
  folio 375).

### T. Conversion, and why the orders went where they went

- **The Conclusion's refusal of the standard explanation — the most consequential argument in the book for any account
  of how the archive's shrine populations became Muslim.** "What is noteworthy is that **both Chishtis and Suhrawardis
  only managed to convert high caste Hindus. The theory that the influence of sufism and of Islamic 'egalitarianism'
  were significant factors which led members of the Hindu lower classes to embrace Islam is unfounded.** The Muslim
  conquests did not unleash forces of liberation or change the position of the exploited castes of Hindus and of the
  untouchables. The social and economic position of the masses of Muslim converts who accepted Islam under a variety of
  pressures, all which have been analysed by Ja'far-i Makki, was in fact no better than that of Hindus" (p. 422, folio
  398).
- **Conversion by example, not preaching, and the inter-order contrast drawn from it.** "the Shaikh was overcome with
  sadness, and told his disciple that people remained untouched by preaching as only pious example would result in
  conversion"; "some Hindus had been convinced of the truth of Islam nevertheless they had refused to be converted"
  (p. 184, folio 166); "**The Suhrawardis, as depicted in the legend surrounding Shaikh Jalalu'd-Din's activities in
  Bengal, were unhesitating in their enforced conversion of Hindus to Islam. By contrast, the Chishtis believed that
  only the company of pious and ascetic Muslims prompted others to accept Islam, and neither the sword nor preaching
  served any purpose.** The Chishtis were interested in various yoga practices, particularly those connected with
  breathing, and refused to force Hindus to renounce their faith" (p. 246, folio 226). **And mass conversion declared
  contrary to the Chishti mission:** the legends of mass conversion of Hindus are "quite contrary to the Chishti
  mission" (p. 27, folio 13) — **the author's judgement, not the source's.**
- **The comparative claim about temple destruction, setting two orders against each other on the question the archive's
  Hindu and Jain rows turn on.** "**Unlike the Chishtis, Mir Saiyid 'Ali Hamadani was imbued with the searing missionary
  zeal of 'Ala'u'd-Dawla Simnani. This enthusiasm and that of his followers took the form of temple demolition and the
  enforced conversion of many Kashmiris**" (p. 314, folio 292); with the commercial motive in Rizvi's own words, "In
  Kashmir they seem to have found new avenues to promote commercial interests and exhibit a Robin Hood-type spirit,
  **ransacking Hindu temples and enriching themselves and their local followers**" (pp. 316–317, folios 294–295); and
  the missionary instruction, "In India Simnani's missionaries exhorted sufis to convert the Brahmans and become active
  proselytisers of Islam" (p. 270, folio 250). **And Rizvi's separation of the two roles:** "**In emphasizing such a
  covenant, Saiyid 'Ali was acting as an 'alim and not as a sufi.**" (p. 318, folio 296), the covenant itself being "in
  reality a convenient justification for the intolerant practices of later ages" whose clauses "were, to say the least,
  detested Hindus" (p. 317, folio 295).
- **Five conversion motives enumerated by a sufi and dismissed by him, and public debate with stakes.** "conversion
  occurred in the following five circumstances: 1. fear of death; 2. fear of the enslavement uf [OCR?] [of] one's
  family; 3. the preaching of Muslims; 4. the desire for stipends and booty; and 5. the bigotry of ancestral religions.
  **Such conversions, therefore, were superficial**" (p. 266, folio 246); "Like Simnani he indulged in debates with
  Brahmans on the condition that whoever was beaten should embrace the religion of the victor. Gisu Daraz claimed to
  have defeated many Brahman scholars. However, he regretted that they always failed to fulfil their promise on one
  pretext or another" (p. 274, folio 254).
- **Conversion as a by-product of charisma, twice, and reported as a genre.** "**The magic surrounding his name prompted
  many to embrace Islam**" (p. 337, folio 315); Pir Badr's "stay in this wilderness greatly impressed the predominantly
  Buddhist and animistic Hindus of Chittagong and Arakan" (p. 338, folio 316); "The stories of their conversions are
  like many others concerned with mystic conversions" (p. 373, folio 351).
- **A royal conversion explained politically, and a Muslim ruler's Hindu practice defended against the charge of
  syncretism.** "Failing to assert his authority over the Brahmans, at the suggestion of Shah Mir, Rinchana embraced
  Islam"; "The rapid Islamization of the Mongols in Central Asia and Iran was a further incentive" (p. 312, folio 290);
  "He visited Hindu shrines, dressed like a Hindu and celebrated Hindu festivals; on one occasion he even performed a
  yajna in order to avert a famine. **This does not imply that Qutbu'd-Din was Hinduised**… Rather he followed the
  traditions of his predecessor, Shah Mir, in maintaining friendly relations with Hindu subjects and the Brahman élite"
  (p. 314, folio 292).
- **Why sufis went to the frontier — an economic-demographic explanation, not a missionary one, and a hedged date for
  Multan.** "it was the news of Sultan Mahmud's conquest of Multan which prompted sufis to advise their talented and
  adventurous disciples to settle in that region. **A new, reasonably unpopulated area offered tranquillity to a
  contemplative**" (p. 129, folio 111); "It would seem, however, that by the middle of the eleventh century, sufism had
  penetrated into the areas surrounding Multan" (p. 128, folio 110).
- **Why there are no early Sind sufis to write about — a two-factor explanation of an archival silence, and it bears on
  every Sindhi row.** "Lack of information about sufis in Sind may be ascribed to two factors. Firstly, land
  communications between Khurasan, Transoxiana and Sind were slow and arduous. Secondly, the blossoming of sufism during
  the tenth century had synchronized with the rise of the Isma‘ili Fatimid Caliphate"; "Therefore sufism, which
  subscribed to Sunni Islam, was naturally cut, from close relations with other areas of Central Asia, also within the
  Sunni fold" (p. 128, folio 110).
- **A foundation myth of Muslim India rejected in as many words, and where Islam in the subcontinent began.** "The belief
  that the conquest of Sind at the time of the Umayyad Caliph al-Walid I (705-15) originated as a punitive expedition
  against the ruling Raja Dahir, after his pirates had seized some Muslims on a hajj to Mecca, **is a myth**. In reality,
  the conquest of Transoxiana and Sind was initiated by Hajjaj, Walid's governor in Iraq, and was one aspect of his
  expansionist policy" (p. 127, folio 109); "The Arab sea traders operating between the Malabar coast and Ceylon first
  introduced Islam to that region of the sub-continent" (p. 127, folio 109).
- **Why Delhi became the centre — a refugee thesis, with the author's own word for the invaders.** "at the beginning of
  the thirteenth century, due to Mongol invasions in Central Asia and Iran, Delhi became the heart of the sufi movement"
  (p. 151, folio 133); "The exodus of scholars and holy men from Central Asia and Iran after the fall of the Muslim
  powers to the Mongol **barbarians**, made Delhi the strongest eastern Islamic capital" (p. 152, folio 134). **And the
  contest for respectability there:** "For Qutbu'd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki, the leader of the Chishti sufis in Delhi, it was
  a Herculean task to firmly establish the order there" (p. 152, folio 134).
- **Why the Chishtis chose peripheries — a structural claim that contradicts a passage Rizvi himself quotes, and both
  stand.** "he left both Lahore and Delhi and ultimately chose Ajmer, on the borders of the Delhi Sultanate, for this
  purpose. By so doing, **he followed the tradition of the founders of the Chishti silsila, who had chosen for their
  activities remote Chisht, rather than other great centres of political power**" (pp. 140–141, folios 122–123),
  **against the Nizami passage Rizvi quotes and rejects, which made Ajmer a deliberately chosen centre of "political and
  religious significance" (p. 136, folio 118).** **Generalised for every provincial shrine in the archive:** "His
  successors and some other disciples of Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din Auliya' **wisely decided to carve Chishti centres in the
  provinces of the empire, rather than remain in the capital**"; "In reality **the sufi spirit worked more successfully
  remote from centres of political power**" (p. 207, folio 189). **And Ajmer's two-stage value to the empire:** "Akbar's
  earlier visit to Ajmer had been as a devotee of the Khwaja. Later the location of Ajmer in the heart of the Rajput
  states and on the route to Gujarat, which Akbar was to later conquer, gave Ajmer a new political significance"
  (p. 144, folio 126).
- **Why centres multiplied, and why one order moved its centre.** "long before Timur's inyasion [OCR?] [invasion] in
  1398-99 which accelerated the establishment of new Chishti centres throughout India, the Wahdat al-Wujud theory had
  become firmly rooted in the mystic soil of Delhi" (p. 261, folio 241); "The order spread from Uch to Gujarat, the
  Panjab and Kashmir even extending its influence to the Delhi of Sultan Firuz Shah Tughlug [Tughluq]… **after the death
  of Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din the order declined in Multan only to be rejuvenated in Uch**" (p. 299, folio 277) —
  **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam** material; and "the continuous history of the silsila can be traced only through the
  khalifas of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya" (p. 220, folio 202) — **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya** material, and an
  explicit exclusion of the Bengal and Delhi Suhrawardis from the order's history.**
- **Why the Suhrawardiyya succeeded in Kashmir, and why towns gained and lost their sufis.** "their impact in Kashmir was
  striking. The main reason was the support they acquired from the migrant Kubrawiyyas, although their mutual rivalries
  in turn retarded progress" (p. 311, folio 289); "The early mystics of Jaunpur took considerable interest in Figh
  [Fiqh] but this trend did not long survive the demise of Sharqi rule. During that period sufis who wished to remain
  aloof from the traditions of Qazi 'Abdu'l-Mugtadir [Muqtadir] and Qazi Shihabu'd-Din moved to other towns" (p. 283,
  folio 263); "with the establishment of Sharqi rule in Jaunpur, outstanding sufis from other parts of India and even
  abroad, preferred to settle there, rather than in Bengal" (p. 280, folio 260); "The deaths of Khwaja Maudud's disciples
  marked the end of the great spiritual peak in sufism which had occurred in Chisht… The region became a fierce battle
  ground between rival Turkic tribes" (p. 133, folio 115).
- **Migration explained by expediency, not piety, and by a communal cause the author distrusts.** "It would appear,
  however, that the move was precipitated more by expedient than pious motives" (p. 366, folio 344); against "The
  reasons for the Shaikh's migration, as given in the Lata'if-i Quddusi, **are emotional**. The work states that Rudauli
  had succumbed to the infiltration of kafirs, Islamic practices disappeared and pork was openly sold in the bazaar"
  (p. 365, folio 343). **Rizvi flags the source's register and does not endorse it.**
- **Exile and attack restated as envy rather than doctrine.** "A more probable explanation is that he aroused the envy of
  the local 'ulama'; he was a newcomer, who no doubt had succeeded in stealing their religious limelight" (p. 249, folio
  229); "The 'ulama' and some sufis were envious of Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din's personal popularity among the élite and the
  masses… **They re-opened the age-old question of sama' as a weapon by which to attack him**" (p. 179, folio 161).
- **Politics, not cruelty, made the cause of the Chishti–Tughluq rupture; and conditions, not doctrine, the cause of
  Chishti aloofness.** "The final conflict was precipitated not by the cruelty of Sultan Muhammad bin Tughlug [Tughluq],
  for he was extremely generous to many sufis, but by the activities of the Delhi Chishtis who during his reign appeared
  to be associated with members of the 'ulama', hostile to government policies" (p. 245, folio 225); "Political
  conditions in India during the 13th and 14th centuries contributed a great deal towards reinforcing the Chishti policy
  of aloofness from rulers and their officials" (pp. 243–244, folios 223–224).
- **The Chishtiyya claimed as Indian, and conflict made the engine of Indian Sufism on one piece of evidence.** "T#e
  [OCR?] [The] Chishti order of sufis is essentially an Indian one. Other branches emanating from the town of Chisht in
  modern Afghanistan did not survive for long in the Perso-Islamic world" (p. 132, folio 114); "It would appear that
  from its very inception, sufism in India developed conflicting trends mainly due to challenges from movements amongst
  local mystics. Sufi history in India is the story both of various challenges and responses, and also of the
  cross-fertilization of new ideas" (p. 131, folio 113) — **the only evidence offered is the hostility Hujwiri met in
  Lahore**, which is **data-darbar** material and belongs in that row's subsection.
- **The Punjab's fifteenth century, and who joined the orders and why.** "In the fifteenth century, the Panjab enjoyed a
  peaceful period due to Afghan rule. The Mongol invasions between the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries had devastated
  portions of the Panjab and the Afghans had successfully contributed to its re-settlement and urbanization by founding
  many new towns and promoting trade and commerce" (p. 408, folio 384); "The reliance of sufis on God, particularly in
  the material sense, attracted both people from economically deprived classes and members of the oppressed élite into
  orders. **Khangahs [khanqahs] gave to most people a feeling of hope and a vision of a bright future, both in this
  world and the one to come**" (p. 421, folio not stated); with the stated social cross-sections, "scholars, merchants,
  government servants, artisans, sufis and qalandars, in short, men from all classes and sections of Indian society"
  (p. 160, folio 142) and "the powerful and the common, the rich, poor, learned and illiterate, townsfolk, villagers,
  soldiers, warriors, freemen and slaves" (p. 178, folio 160).
- **What the orders were for, and what the Suhrawardis were.** "Assistance from the withdrawn and ascetic Chishtis …
  was also sought to avert such calamities as drought and panic"; "the Chishti khangahs [khanqahs] did offer consolation,
  peace and nourishment to the thousands of Muslims who crowded the towns" (p. 422, folio 398); "Sufis in this period
  also sheltered both the politically and socially persecuted at the risk of their own popularity or reprisals from the
  government" (p. 424, folio 400); against "**They also amassed large fortunes and tried to pressurize the government
  into taking a very narrow view of the world**" (p. 422, folio 398) — **the silsila of eleven archive rows.**

### U. The two orders contrasted, and the doctrine the shrines rest on

- **Where the difference lies, on Rizvi's account.** "Both orders trained their followers to pursue the conventional
  mystic path, which began with tauba (repentance), under the guidance of a pir (preceptor)… **The differences between
  the two orders lay in their distinct rituals and ceremonies and, more significantly, in their attitudes to society and
  politics**" (p. 237, folio 217).
- **The Suhrawardi economy stated as policy, against the Chishti one, and restated as temperament.** "**They accepted
  state patronage, received huge gifts from merchants and artisan guilds, and treasured riches as future assets**"
  (p. 236, folio 216); a shaikh's reluctance over a state grant "was not founded on any ideological tradition of his
  si/sila [silsila], but sprang from his own ascetic temperament. **In this respect he was more of a Chishti than a
  Suhrawardi**" (p. 250, folio 230) — **a direct statement that Suhrawardi practice accepted state endowment and
  Chishti practice did not**; and "With the Suhrawardi order there are few stories revealing that its members lived in
  extreme poverty, unlike members of the Chishti si/sila [silsila]" (p. 210, folio 192).
- **Their differing state theories, with the Suhrawardi one condemned in Rizvi's own voice.** "The Suhrawardi view of
  the function of the state, envisaged by Nuru'd-Din Mubarak Ghaznawi, encompassed the prosperity of the Sunni élite
  alone; the non-orthodox Sunnis, Shi'is and Hindus were permitted to survive, provided they did so in a deprived
  economic condition. **This concept was an unimaginative replica of the ideas of Nizamu'l-Mulk Tusi, and ignored the
  situation of the Muslims at that time in India**" (p. 245, folio 225); against "The Chishtis believed that governments
  should strive for the prosperity of the whole empire" and "to the Chishtis the state was morally bound to care for the
  well-being and prosperity of all its people" (pp. 245–246, folios 225–226); with Chishti political theory assimilated
  to Ghazali, "who advised Muslims to obey unquestionably whoever seized power as this was indispensable for the
  preservation of government, law and order", so that "the Chishtis believed the Sultanate was not necessarily evil"
  (p. 244, folio 224). **And Rizvi's judgement on the attribution of the four Suhrawardi principles quoted above:**
  "The information available is, however, insufficient to ascribe, with much certainty, the above theories to Saiyid
  Nuru'd-Din. However, he may also have forwarded identical, or similar, theories currently accepted in that period"
  (p. 213, folio 195). **Anyone citing those principles must carry this.**
- **One shared textbook for the whole field, and Rizvi's judgement on it.** "The early Chishti preceptors were scholars,
  but they did not commit their ideas to writing and **the Suhrawardi manual, the 'Awarifu'l-Ma'arif, was the only text
  book used by all sufis**" (p. 236, folio 216), the mould of mystic ideas "Until the mid-fourteenth century" (p. 237,
  folio 217); "**This work marks a watershed in the reconciliation of sufism with orthodoxy.** To later generations of
  sufis it became the most closely studied text on sufism" (p. 104, folio 88). **And a shared goal:** "Both the
  Suhrawardis and the Chishtis sought to achieve the sufi goal of jana' [OCR?] [fana'] and baga' [OCR?] [baqa'] or fana
  'an al-fana' (annihilation beyond annihilation)" (p. 236, folio 216).
- **The Firdawsiyya placed on the Suhrawardi tree, and the Suhrawardiyya's Indian foundation attributed to migration.**
  "The Firdawsiyya order was part of the collateral line of the Suhrawardiyya… The Firdawsis, however, were unable to
  make a mark in Delhi. Late in the fourteenth century they established themselves in Bihar, a move which was to prove
  most successful" (p. 246, folio 226); "HE Indian soil was enriched by the migration from Iran and Transoxiana of a
  number of disciples of Shaikh Shihabu'd-Din Suhrawardi" (p. 208, folio 190) — **the dropped T is the chapter's
  drop-cap, not damage.** Index: "Suhrawardiyya, 1, 11, 87-88, 190-240, 272, 277-300, 315, 367, 398-400" and, as a
  separate headword sharing part of that span, "Suhrawardiyya Centres (new), 277-300" (p. 487, folio 463); "Silsila,
  83-84, 97, 131, 190", "Silsila-i Khwajgan, 94, 96-98, 102, 314", "Shattariyya, 1, 12" (p. 486, folio 462).
- **The silsila as an organisational device and as a defence.** "The twelfth century saw the crystallization of the new
  silsilas, or orders, as chains of lineages through which different sufis could be traced. **The establishment of
  silsilas placed sufism on a firm and organized basis**… It connected them with a spiritual hierarchy, thus giving
  sufis greater respectability and a stronger base of defence against the onslaught of the orthodox"; "**Each silsila
  devised its own method or school of guidance as well as its own rituals and ceremonies. Later generations were to
  follow these rules rigidly**"; "The founders of the silsilas in the twelfth century linked themselves with Muhammad
