  through either 'Ali or Abu Bakr, but generally through the former" (p. 99, folio 83). **No individual order is named
  before p. 99, so the Chishti, Suhrawardi, Qadiri and Naqshbandi lines of the archive rows lie beyond it** — (not
  stated).
- **Why the Qadiriyya spread — legend, not organisation.** "The expansion of the Qadiriyya order was very slow; in many
  parts of the Islamic world, **the legends associated with Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Qadir played an important role in its
  development**" (p. 101, folio 85). **This is the book's whole treatment of the order to which many archive rows
  belong; the rest is held over for volume II (pp. 15–16, folios not stated / 2).**
- **Qalandar hostility to endowed shrine life, stated as doctrine and then reversed.** "Both groups are depicted as
  being strongly hostile to the settled khangah [khanqah] life of the Suhrawardiyya and the Chishtiyya, considering it
  unholy and binding in its duty to assist homeless dervishes."; the Punjab qalandars "moved to Delhi creating scenes
  wherever they saw a khanqah" (p. 325, folio 303). **Then:** "In the thirteenth century, however, many qalandars began
  to adopt the settled life of the khangah [khanqah]. The earliest moves towards a sedentary existence can be seen
  amongst members of the Qalandariyya-Chishtiyya" (p. 326, folio 304); "gradually many itinerant qalandars became
  absorbed into the Chishti order" (p. 327, folio 305); al-Jawaliqi "founded a monastery of qalandars near Cairo"
  (p. 325, folio 303); and "Qalandars were frequent visitors at the court of the Lodis and the doors of a large number
  of his nobles were also open to them" (pp. 332–333, folios 310–311).
- **The ʿurs theology's doctrinal source, its own history, and the dispute left open.** The ʿurs belief "was
  strengthened by the philosophy of Ibn al-‘Arabi in his most significant concept, the Wahdat al-Wujud" (p. 121, folio
  103); "The twelfth century was a watershed in the history of sufism. This was brought about by the introduction and
  widespread acceptance of the theory of Wahdat al-Wujud. **Ibn al-‘Arabi did not, in fact, devise the concept himself
  but he managed to reconcile varying sufi views on Reality**" (p. 121, folio 103); its Indian arrival dated, "The Wahdat
  al-Wujud of Ibn al-'Arabi was introduced to India through the Suhrawardi, 'Iraqi, however, by this time had not yet
  penetrated deeply into Chishti or Suhrawardi ideology" (p. 237, folio 217); and the pantheism dispute with four
  scholars and no verdict from Rizvi — Massignon's "existential monism", Affifi's "Islamic pantheism", Burckhardt's
  refusal of the word, and "Henry Corbin takes even stronger exception to a monistic interpretation" (pp. 123, 125;
  folios 105, 107).
- **The tolerance clause, the doctrinal ground for any shared-shrine argument, in four voices.** Ibn al-ʿArabi
  "advocated that the Divine existed and was worshipped in all religions, and that God was worshipped in Love, His
  highest manifestation" (p. 126, folio 108); Masʿud-i Bak, "that in all religions only One Road was concealed. If the
  subtle truth of this path, gleaned from different forms, was to be understood, all the different religions would
  appear identical" (p. 263, folio 243); Shabistari, sufis "are also ready to appreciate the positive and true aspects
  contained in other religions such as Christianity, Magianism and even in idolatry" (pp. 228–229, folios 208–209); and
  the Conclusion's last word, "The spiritual life of India discussed in this chapter bears out the Rig Vedic assertion:
  **'The One Reality, the learned speak of in many ways.'**" (p. 420, folio 396).
- **Law and sainthood as shell and pearl, with the guard-rail stated twice.** "The law can be compared to a shell and
  the state of sainthood to the pearl within it… **it is the disposition of saintliness which sanctifies the efforts of
  devotees**" (p. 228, folio 208); "Every rite not informed by the spirit of Reality is valueless, and every spirit of
  Reality not restrained by the Law is incomplete… Know that the Law is the Reality because God ordained it, and the
  Reality is also the Law" (p. 79, folio 63); "Inasmuch as God has joined the Law to the Truth, it is impossible that
  His saints should separate them... Rejection of the Law is heresy, and rejection of the Truth is infidelity and
  polytheism" (p. 63, folio 47).
- **Charity ranked above ritual in four mouths — the doctrine that licenses a langar — and renunciation redefined as
  distribution.** "it was better than a hundred genuflexions during prayers; if he gave him a mouthful of food, it was
  more praiseworthy than an entire night spent praying" (p. 89, folio 73); "The most superior kind of worship was to
  assist the helpless and to feed the hungry" (p. 142, folio 124); "altruistic service to others is more meritorious
  than the performance of obligatory prayers" (p. 183, folio 165); "hundreds of prayers and days of fasting equalled a
  single act of kindness or assistance given to a Muslim" (p. 252, folio 232); renunciation is "the distribution to the
  poor of anything surplus" (p. 183, folio 165). **Against indiscriminate giving:** "However, indiscriminate charity was
  extravagant, only charity to please God had spiritual merit" (p. 185, folio 167). **And generosity taught as
  indifference to the recipient's use of the gift:** "Baba Ishaq replied that every one would have to answer for his own
  actions so whatever the dervish did was of no concern to them" (p. 431, folio 407).
- **Poverty and wealth made a matter of indifference — directly relevant to how endowed shrines justify wealth.** "the
  mystic Tariga [Tariqa] was not identical to either fagr [OCR?] [faqr] (poverty) or zuhd (asceticism)… **To him an
  obsessive opposition to wealth was a sign of weakness**, amounting to a dependence on causation and an attachment to
  the anticipation of reward. A true sufi did not differentiate between poverty and wealth" (p. 105, folio 89).
  **Against the whole weight of the ascetic material above.** **And a three-class taxonomy of the poor with begging as
  the discriminating variable:** "1. those who neither begged nor accepted anything, yet received everything they asked
  for from God; / 2. those who did not beg but accepted what they were given; and / 3. those who held out for as long as
  they could, but then begged" (p. 55, folio 39).
- **Service of the poor named as an institution, and named in order to say a great saint omitted it.** Abu Yazid "did
  not exercise, as they did, a social activity (khidmat al-fugaraʾ [OCR?] [khidmat al-fuqaraʾ])" (p. 60, folio 44).
- **Karamat devalued, and miracles ordered concealed.** For Bayazid, "miracles (kKaramat [OCR?] [karamat]), zikr, even
  the mystic stages (magamat [OCR?] [maqamat]) are for him no more than so many barriers holding him from God" (p. 60,
  folio 44); "**One's spiritual achievements and miraculous powers were also to be preserved from the gaze of the
  ordinary man**", with "sufis who sought fame in their own lifetime were soon forgotten. Those, however, who withdrew
  from worldly adulation achieved a long-lasting fame in death" (p. 146, folio 128); and the Chishti discipline list
  built on the same principle, "avoiding the company of the wealthy, maintaining a veil of secrecy around mystical
  accomplishments, and the necessity for a disciple to avoid trying to acquire supernatural power and to consider one's
  pir as the greatest spiritual figure of his age" (p. 275, folio 255). **The Khwaja twice leaves a city because of
  crowds** — Herat (p. 138, folio 120) and Delhi (p. 140, folio 122).
- **The sober and intoxicated schools, named and assigned, with shatahat quarantined.** "the states of sukr and sahw
  were recognized as two different schools of sufism; the former represented by Bayazid and Hallaj and the latter by
  Muhasibi and Junaid" (p. 76, folio 60); "These expressions were not, however, a normal aspect of sufi life; they
  emanated from what was called sukr" (p. 76, folio 60); and the formula both schools share, "as a rain drop was not
  annihilated in the ocean, although it ceased to exist individually, similarly the sufi soul in the unitive state was
  indistinguishable from the Universal Divine" (p. 76, folio 60).
- **Antinomianism disowned and erotic language conceded, in the author's own voice; and earthly love made a bridge.**
  "All true sufis denounced antinomianism and libertinism. Nevertheless, their language describing divine love
  unavoidably involved the use of erotic symbolism which was tinged with sensuality" (p. 76, folio 60); "Amir Khusraw
  believed that earthly love, expressed in allegorical and historical stories, was a bridge to spiritual love" (p. 190,
  folio 172), on the maxim "a/-majazu guntartu'l Hagiga [OCR?] [al-majazu qantaratu'l Haqiqa] (the phantasmal is the
  bridge to the Real)" (p. 385, folio 363).
- **The costs of sufism, listed by Rizvi himself — the passage any celebratory shrine entry has to answer.** "**The
  Ghazalian tradition in sufism in India did inculcate hostility towards philosophy. The sufi movement tended to promote
  gullibility and credulity and discourage self-reliance**" (p. 423, folio 399); "**The most serious threats to the
  survival of sufism were the presumptuous and preposterous claims of sufi charlatans and impostors.** The latter
  exploited the influence of sufism, and the popular passion for the occult and thaumaturgy, to their own advantage.
  Their poetry and music promoted immoral practices, the use of drugs and the practice of homosexuality" (p. 424, folio
  400); "this form of poetry gradually tended to degenerate as it became conventionalized and developed grotesque
  language, erotic obsessions and imitative and repetitive thinking" (p. 423, folio 399). **And the countervailing
  claim:** sufi poetry was "an avenue for emotions and feelings which would otherwise have never been expressed due to
  the fury of the orthodox, social inhibitions and political repression" (p. 423, folio 399).
- **Deification of pirs, against the pirs' own wishes.** "**Sufi disciples tended to deify their pirs, even though the
  latter were totally disinterested in turning themselves into Godheads**" (p. 421, folio not stated). **Set against
  the ʿulamaʾ's accusation of the same thing (p. 116, folio 98) and against the archive's own practice.**
- **"Heterodox" refused as a category for sufism anywhere.** "Some Muslim Bengali scholars characterize the mystic
  themes in Bengali works as heterodox but **the term is hardly applicable to the sufi movement in any region. Sufism
  had had a long tradition of borrowing both ideas and practices from other non-Islamic mystical systems**" (pp. 375–376,
  folios 353–354).
- **The decline thesis restated, with the counter-claim in the same paragraph and a decline verdict in a saint's own
  mouth.** "The history of the silsilas is thus marked by the perennial rise and fall of true spirituality" (p. 100,
  folio 84); against "From the twelfth to the end of the fourteenth centuries, the sufi silsilas served in Iran as a
  bulwark against the pessimism aroused by the onslaught of the Ghuzz and the Mongols. They re-orientated religious life
  away from strict and often meaningless formalities. **However their passivity and serenity did not become inertia**"
  (p. 100, folio 84); and "Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din commented that the art of sufi teaching had degenerated into child's
  play, that is, something not to be taken seriously" (p. 207, folio 189).
- **Sufi asceticism did not withdraw the sufis from society, and animals as a practice with a theological edge.** "The
  asceticism and renunciation of the mystics did not cut them off from Muslim society anywhere in the Islamic world.
  Their example radiated to all sections of the community" (p. 77, folio 61); "**Their humane treatment of animals and
  birds, especially to dogs, regarded as unclean by orthodox Muslims, was noteworthy and it tended to affect their
  attitude towards all human beings, including non-Muslims** and members of the so-called heterodox sects" (p. 77, folio
  61).

### V. Contradictions in this material, kept side by side

*Nothing here is resolved. Each pair is in the book, and the book does not reconcile it.*

1. **Royal gifts restore khanqahs / royal gifts ruin them** — p. 207 (folio 189) against p. 204 (folio 186).
2. **Futuh is the sufis' only income / endowments and begging are the two sources** — pp. 116–117 (folios 98–99) against
   p. 109 (folio 91).
3. **Begging is unworthy of an ascetic / affluent khanqahs require neophytes to beg** — p. 198 (folio 180) against
   p. 117 (folio 99); and, in one man on one page, "I did not beg" against his begging for his fellow sufis (p. 86,
   folio 70).
4. **Borrowing forbidden / permitted on two pretexts / actually done** — p. 160 (folio 142), p. 198 (folio 180), p. 155
   (folio 137).
5. **Dervishes must never let rulers visit their khanqahs / rulers visit khanqahs throughout the book** — p. 302 (folio
   280) against pp. 91, 204, 250, 368 (folios 75 corrected, 186, 230, 346).
6. **Sufis keep aloof from the administrative machinery / eminent Chishtis were compelled into government posts and one
   defends the practice in verse** — p. 117 (folio 99) against pp. 203, 207 (folios 185, 189).
7. **Initiation at a saint's tomb is invalid / the same Shaikh initiates by head-shaving** — p. 166 (folio 148) against
   pp. 172, 174 (folios 154, 156).
8. **Celibacy preferable / marriage permissible / family life worthy / the family is the first fetter** — pp. 238, 113,
   185, 206 (folios 218, 95, 167, 188), with married Shaikhs and a khanqah built for a Shaikh "and his family" (p. 104,
   folio 88).
9. **Renunciation requires distributing everything, and bequeathing to a son is fraudulent / the same saint's line
   becomes hereditary** — p. 300 (folio 278) against pp. 303–305 (folios 281–283).
10. **A saint left no descendants (the Akbar board) / the book records that he left sons** — p. 144 (folio 126) against
    p. 142 (folio 124); **and the same descent question decided in opposite directions centuries apart** — p. 296
    (folio 276), twice.
11. **Ajmer chosen as a remote periphery on the Chisht model / Ajmer chosen as a centre of political and religious
    significance** — pp. 140–141 (folios 122–123) against the Nizami passage quoted at p. 136 (folio 118).
12. **Saints may equal and surpass the early Caliphs / prophets are unquestionably superior / saints have no intrinsic
    eminence** — p. 57 (folio 41), p. 273 (folio 253), p. 263 (folio 243).
13. **Worship of the pir is better than worship of the Lord / disciples deify their pirs against the pirs' wishes / the
    ʿulamaʾ accuse the sufis of exactly that** — p. 370 (folio 348), p. 421 (folio not stated), p. 116 (folio 98).
14. **An obsessive opposition to wealth is a sign of weakness / poverty and austerity are the order's own tradition** —
    p. 105 (folio 89) against pp. 204, 236 (folios 186, 216).
15. **A grant returned after fifteen years / in 762/1360 / when the Sultan was alienated** — three datings on one page,
    p. 250 (folio 230, footnote 3).
16. **Tomb veneration degenerated into superstition / pilgrimage and intercession are normal and "often associated with
    a genuine feeling of respect for the Shaikh"** — both on p. 121 (folio 103), the register shifting inside one page.
17. **"Then, as now" the ʿurs is the most popular commemoration / the ʿurs headword points at only four folios in the
    whole book** — p. 121 (folio 103) against the index at p. 489 (folio 465).
18. **The Chishtis refused land grants as policy / a Chishti accepts a qaziship and villages at the cost of a year's
    estrangement** — p. 243 (folio 223) against p. 195 (folio 177).
19. **A temple demolition printed without comment / a verse reading Hindu idol-worship as unwitting zikr printed
    without comment, eleven pages later** — p. 219 (folio 201) against p. 230 (folio 210).
20. **Sufis avoid holy war and imperial politics / a Suhrawardi Shaikhuʾl-Islam writes a state programme demanding the
    extermination of Brahmans** — p. 117 (folio 99) against pp. 212–213 (folios 194–195).

### Legends and miracles not attached to an archive row

*The karamat extract for this book runs to 529 lines across all twenty-eight chunks, and the great
majority of it concerns figures with no archive row — Khwaja Muʿinuʾd-Din Chishti, Khwaja Qutbuʾd-Din
Bakhtiyar Kaki, Shaikh Nizamuʾd-Din Auliyaʾ, Shaikh Nasiruʾd-Din Chiragh-i Dihli, Abu Saʿid bin Abiʾl
Khair, Hallaj, Shibli, Bayazid, Gisu Daraz, Ashraf Jahangir Simnani, Shah Madar, Salar Masʿud, Baba Ratan
and the Bengali and Kashmiri saints. **The legends that do belong to an archive row have been folded into
that row's Legends & Miracles run above and are not repeated here.** Everything below is attributed
exactly as the book attributes it, with the book's own hedges kept; where Rizvi disowns a story, the
disowning is part of the entry. The coordinator folds this list into Cross-cutting material.*

### The author's method, stated in his own voice

- **Hagiography read against the grain is Rizvi's stated method** — of the *Asraruʾt-Tawhid*, "designed to glorify the mystical achievements of Shaikh Abu Sa'id and presents him as a supernatural being. Nevertheless, glimpses of the economic and social life of the Shaikh which tend to emerge accidentally are both informative and entertaining" (p. 18, folio 4).
- **Miracles as by then inseparable from sufi belief** — of the *Mirʾatuʾl-Asrar*: "The Shaikh could not resist the temptation to record many miracles and legends, which by that time were an integral part of the beliefs associated with sufis" (p. 28, folio 14).
- **One compiler who excluded them** — Shaikh ʿAbduʾl-Haqq "scrupulously excluded all references to supernatural and miraculous elements, highlighting instead the mystical and spiritual ideas, ethical behaviour and psychological perceptions of the great sufis" (p. 26, folio 12).
- **A whole branch's miracle literature dismissed** — the *Saiyaru'l-Aqtab*[OCR?] is "legendary and fantastic in the recounting of Chishti miracles, particularly of the Sabiriyya branch" (p. 27, folio 13).
- **Martyr-sainthood as an unreliable genre** — "Many early sufis were honoured as martyr saints and their exploits became legendary, at the expense of historical authenticity" (p. 280, folio 260).
- **Miracles as evidential proof of sainthood, Tirmizi's doctrine quoted** — "It is possible for saints to work miracles… The miracle is both the proof of this genuineness and its result, for it is the saint's genuineness that enables him to work miracles" (p. 57, folio 41), with "Saints understand both the future and what is hidden from ordinary men" (p. 57, folio 41). **Set against Bayazid, for whom "miracles (karamat), zikr, even the mystic stages (maqamat) are for him no more than so many barriers holding him from God" (p. 60, folio 44) — Rizvi prints both without comment.**
- **Miracles refused as a category by Nizamuʾd-Din Auliyaʾ** — "'A miracle means that one remains steadfast at God's door. If you remain firm on your path, why do you seek miracles?'" (p. 198, folio 180).
- **Miracles to be hidden, in a deliberately shocking image** — "Miracles were to be compared, said the Shaikh, with what he called the ‘menstrual discharge of men’. As women maintained a veil of secrecy around their own menstrual flow, so should sufis hide from the public view their own supernatural powers" (p. 146, folio 128).
- **Two flat refusals of miracle in near-identical words, five pages apart, and the book does not note the echo** — Abu Saʿid "compared those who claimed to walk on water with frogs and waterfowls, and those who claimed to fly through the air with flies and insects" (p. 89, folio 73); Ansari's Munajat, "'If thou canst walk on water / Thou art no better than a straw. / If thou canst fly in air / Thou art no better than a fly. / Conquer thy heart / That thou mayest become somebody'" (p. 94, folio 78).

### Chapter One: the early Islamic and Khurasanian material

- **The Prophet's forecast of a visit to a man he never met** — ʿUmar and ʿAli at Qaran, conveying "the Prophet's greetings" (p. 41, folio 25).
- **Wild animals gathering round Rabiʿa and fleeing from others** — "In the mountains, deer, mountain goats, ibexes and asses would surround her, and then flee at the sight of others, including sufis", as "Many hagiologies say" (p. 46, folio 30).
- **A bird's suicide at a grave and a voice from the tomb — the only tomb miracle in Chapter One.** Sufyan's freed bird "suicided by dashing itself on the ground near its liberator's grave. A voice from the tomb was heard to say that God the Most High had forgiven the sufi because of his compassion for the creatures which were His" (p. 48, folio 32).
- **The dog preferred to wife and child** — "If I give bread to the dog ... he keeps watch over me all through the night so that I can pray" (p. 48, folio 32).
- **The thrice-repeated voice at the hunt, the third time from the saddle** — Ibrahim bin Adham, "'Not for this was thou created…'", from al-Kalabazi (p. 49, folio 33), with Rizvi's note that "When translated from Arabic to Persian and from Persian to Malay and Javanese, the legend of Ibrahim became increasingly fanciful" (p. 49, folio 33).
- **The city of the blind and the elephant, as a sufi parable about God** (p. 34, folio not stated), quoted from Sanaʾi and ending "Naught of Almighty God can creatures learn, / Nor e'en the wise such mysteries discern" (p. 35, folio 19).
- **A dream that converts, reported without hedge** — Bishr and the inscribed paper, "The same night God visited him in a dream, extending His approval" (p. 55, folio 39).
- **A conversion at an ambush the author calls perhaps false** — Fuzayl, "The story of his conversion depicts it as sudden, dramatic and is perhaps apocryphal" (p. 53, folio 37, citing "Qurʾan, LVI, 16" [OCR?] — **the most dangerous locator in that chunk; see Doubts**).
- **Wild beasts fed at Sahl's house "according to tradition"** (p. 63, folio 47).
- **Ascetic knots grown in the body, told by an eyewitness wife** — "From his breast to his navel there were fifteen knots (ʿagd [OCR?] [ʿaqd])…" (p. 62, folio 46).
- **A night mistaken for an evening** — ʿAbduʾllah ibn al-Mubarak at his beloved's wall (p. 54, folio 38).
- **Bayazid's shatahat and his miʿraj, through Ritter** — "Subhani! Ma aʿzama shaʾni"; "I saw the Kaʿba walking round me" (p. 60, folio 44).
- **A count of teachers dismissed as legend** — "one hundred and thirteen spiritual teachers during his thirty years of roaming… would, however, seem to be based on legend" (p. 59, folio 43).
- **A conversion story rejected outright** — Maʿruf al-Karkhi through ʿAli al-Riza, "This story must be apocryphal" (p. 65, folio 49).
- **The bier only Muslims could lift** — "After his death, Jews, Christians and Muslims all claimed Maʿruf as their own, but only Muslims were able to lift his bier from the ground before his burial" (p. 66, folio 50), **with no source named.**
- **Sari's dream of the winnowing of mankind**, told by Junaid — "'O Sari, I created mankind, and all of them claimed to love Me…'" (p. 67, folio 51).
- **Shibli stoning his visitors to prove them false friends** (p. 75, folio 59) and **running to burn the Kaʿba, and Hell and Paradise** (pp. 75–76, folios 59–60), both from ʿAttar via Arberry, presented as his "own rationalization of his behaviour, which others interpreted as madness".
- **The bridge of Sirat**, footnoted "thinner than a hair and sharper than a sword's edge" (p. 70, folio 54).
- **The revelation denying the eternity of the spirit, with its occasion** (p. 77, folio 61, footnote 2) and **the pre-existential covenant (misaq)** quoted in full from "Qurʿan, VII, 172" (p. 71, folio 55).
- **Visions on the mystic path, as Ghazali describes them** — "they behold angels and the spirits of the prophets" (p. 81, folio 65).
- **The Mahdi and the Antichrist, in a long footnote from Ibn Khaldun** (pp. 57–58, folios 41–42, footnote 8).

### Abu Saʿid bin Abiʾl Khair, Ansari and the Khurasanian khanqah

- **Telepathy as a defence against a state inquiry, reported without hedge** — "Through his telepathic powers, he was aware of the proposed inquiry" (p. 87, folio 71); and "The Shaikh's biographers however, mention a number of stories in which the two sufis were reconciled through Abu Sa'id's use of telepathy" (p. 87, folio 71).
- **A prophecy of high office** — Nizamuʾl-Mulk "would become a great vizier" (p. 88, folio 72).
- **The dead Sultan's dream, told against royal power** — Mahmud of Ghazna: "'There is no place for any sultan here. I am nothing here… I am a prisoner here and have to account for every minor thing that happened during my life. The treasury was enjoyed by someone else, grief and lamentation have befallen me'" (p. 88, folio 72, from "Asraru't-Tawhid, p. 268").
- **Intercession promised for anyone who passes the house or is touched by the candlelight** (p. 90, folio 74).
- **A voice from the Kaʿba guaranteeing a school of law to a dynasty** — Abu Hanifa, "the founder of the Hanafi school of Figh [OCR?] [Fiqh]", promised "that as long as the sword remained in the hands of the Turks, the Hanafis would not perish" (p. 84, folio 68).
- **A Hadis of the Ascension set as a memory exercise** — "through Me he hears, and through Me he sees, and through Me he takes" (p. 85, folio 69).
- **A Hallaj-like utterance with its stage business** — "'there is none other than God in this robe'", with the forefinger pushed through the cloak (p. 88, folio 72).
- **A Tradition licensing the appearance of madness** — "a man's faith is not made perfect until he is supposed to be mad" (p. 86, folio 70).
- **God's rebuke to Moses over the shepherd's prayer, from the Masnawi** — "'I look not at the tongue and the speech; I look at the inward (spirit) and the state (of feeling)'" (pp. 98–99, folios 82–83).
- **The birds who find they are the Simurgh** — ʿAttar's allegory of annihilation in Browne's translation (pp. 96–97, folios 80–81), presented as literary allegory of "the sufi conception of annihilation in God (fana' fi'llah)", not as an event.
- **Abu Saʿid's descending kisses, marked as late** — knee, foot, hoof and the ground, "Although the source of this story is a late one, it is typical of Shaikh Abu Sa'id's teachings" (p. 238, folio 218).

### ʿAbduʾl-Qadir Jilani, Kubra and the twelfth-century orders

- **ʿAbduʾl-Qadir's miracles, reported as allegation** — "He is alleged to have crushed mountains, dried up oceans and raised the dead to life. It is claimed that a large number of Jews and Christians embraced Islam through the influence of his spiritual prowess"; "Whatever his alleged supramundane powers, Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Qadir died an earthly death in 561/1166" (pp. 101–102, folios 85–86).
- **His claimed primacy** — "'My foot is on the neck of every saint of God.'" (p. 101, folio 85, footnote 3).
- **A living miracle tradition with drums, in the present tense** — the Javanese Qadiris who "even today, perform supernatural feats accompanied by beating drums" (p. 101, folio 85).
- **Kubra's martyrdom in Jami's narration** — the prophecy "a Fire is kindled from the East"; the refusal to pray it away; and the pigtail in the dead hand "which after his death could not be removed from his hand, until at last they were obliged to cut it off" (p. 112, folio 94). A saying of his: "'The ways of God are as numerous as the number of breaths of his creatures.'" (p. 112, folio 94).
- **Posthumous, spirit-to-disciple training** — "With Saiyid Kulal, Baha'u'd-Din is said to have obtained training from the spirit of Khwaja 'Abdu'l-Khaliq Ghujduwani" (p. 115, folio 97); and **Khizr as the source of the eight principles, believed by the disciples against the author's own explanation** (p. 113, folio 95).
- **The light of Muhammad predating his birth** — "illuminated by the light of Muhammad which had existed even before his birth and was the sole cause of creation" (p. 116, folio 98).
- **The defence of Iblis as monotheist, twice** — "Like the martyr Hallaj, Shaikh Ahmad, defended the monotheism of Iblis" (p. 102, folio 86); and the *Tamhidat*, "That mad lover whom you call Iblis in this world… This mad one loved God" (p. 103, folio 87). Later: "La (No) was the station of /b/is [OCR?] [Iblis], and Illa (Except), the stage of the Prophet Muhammad. /b/is [Iblis] was the sentry at the door of the Divine" (p. 262, folio 242).
- **Khizr's immortality and his rescues** — "In legends Khizr saved men in desperate situations"; "Some sufis were said to be his constant companions" (p. 118, folio 100); and Khizr "who also helped travellers on both the sea and on rivers" (p. 434, folio 410).
- **The cloak given to ʿAli after the miʿraj** (p. 120, folio 102).
- **The ass's grave in Anatolia**, believed "to radiate blessings to a group of villages", from J.P. Brown (p. 121, folio 103).
- **Ibn al-ʿArabi's childhood vision and his dreamt book** — "Ibn al-‘Arabi claimed that Fusus had been revealed to him in a single dream" (pp. 121–122, folios 103–104).
- **A prophesied birth in the Chishti chain** — Abu Ishaq Shami "is said to have prophesied the birth of Khwaja Abu Ahmad Abdal which occurred during the same year" (p. 133, folio 115).
- **The pilot's vow miracle, implied rather than narrated** — ships spared pirates and storms after vows to Abu Ishaq Kaziruni, "recognized as the protector of travellers" (p. 129, folio 111, footnote 1).
- **The flying contest with the yogi — the book's first sufi/yogi miracle contest**, at Uch (pp. 129–130, folios 111–112); **folded above into the Uch place-level bullet, and recorded here because the distinction drawn — technique versus grace — is put in the yogi's own mouth.**

### Khwaja Muʿinuʾd-Din Chishti and Ajmer

- **The prophecy against Prithviraj, and its fulfilment** — "'We have seized Pithaura alive and handed him over to the army of Islam.'" (p. 134, folio 116), from "Amir Khwurd, Siyaru’l-Auliya’, Delhi, 1885, pp. 45-7"; "The Akhbaru’l-Akhyar also contains the same account" (p. 135, folio 117).
- **The long *Jawahir-i Faridi* cycle, given entire in one footnote** (p. 135, folio 117, attributed to "‘Ali Asghar Chishti, Jawahir-i Faridi, Lahore, 1884, pp. 155-60"): the twelve-year prophecy by Pithaura's mother, "an expert in astronomy and magic"; the circulated portrait, so "Every foreigner’s face was compared with that picture"; "seven hundred people, besides Hamidu’d-Din Dihlawi, embraced Islam"; the Prophet's warning at Samana; the camel keepers at Ajmer; the cow killed for kebabs; "There were one thousand temples on the two lakes"; the ewer that dried "all the lakes, tanks and wells around"; the idol "Sawi Deva" made to "recite kalima" and converted "into a human being, naming it Sa‘di"; Jaipal's "700 magical dragons, 1,500 magical discs and 700 disciples" killed inside a protective circle; the restored water; the slippers ordered "to bring Jaipal back to earth, which they did"; and the joint ascent "to the highest heaven".
- **The sesame seeds and the awakening** — "The majzub took out some sesame seeds, chewed them, and put them in Mu‘inu’d-Din’s mouth" (p. 137, folio 119).
- **ʿUsman Harwani and the Zoroastrian fire temple** — the Shaikh and the boy emerging unharmed "After some hours", "The head priest embraced Islam and the fire temple was demolished" (p. 137, folio 119, footnote 2), with the book's own variant: "In the KAairu'l-Majalis [Khairu'l-Majalis] the Zoroastrian priests are replaced by Hindus and the conversation is reported in the Hindawi."
- **The crane at Balkh** — "As soon as the Maulana had eaten the leg he underwent a deep spiritual experience, after which all philosophical learnings were obliterated from his mind" (p. 139, folio 121), told with "so the story goes".
- **The terror of the Khwaja's presence at Sabzwar**, which Rizvi calls "a stock-in-trade sufi story" (p. 139, folio 121, footnote 7).
- **Ram Deo's instant conversion, quoted from a modern author** — "Legend relates that in approaching the Khwaja, Ram Deo was so much impressed by his personality that he became, from that moment, a faithful disciple" (p. 136, folio 118).
- **Conversion by the blessing of relics, at the tomb** — "Many prominent kafirs of the region accepted Islam because of the barakat (blessings) of the asar (relics, monuments or traditions) of that (embodiment of) sufism" (p. 140, folio 122). **The gloss of *asar* as "relics, monuments or traditions" is the book's own, and the ambiguity is the book's.**
- **Firishta's and Abuʾl-Fazl's milder versions set against each other** — "According to Abu'l-Fazl the Khwaja: ‘lit a large number of (spiritual) lamps and crowds of people benefitted from his intuitive soul.’ Abu'l-Fazl's statement does not necessarily imply conversion" (p. 140, folio 122, footnote 1).
- **The granting of a wish, and the disciple who asked for nothing**, producing the title "Su/tanu’t-Tarikin [Sultanu’t-Tarikin] (King of Hermits)" (p. 145, folio 127).
- **A royal birth attributed to a saint** — "the birth of the crown prince, Salim, which was attributed to the spiritual intervention of the famous Chishti saint, Shaikh Salim of Sikri" (p. 144, folio 126); and, in Chapter One, "Shaikh Salim Chishti whose prayers were said to have been responsible for the birth of Akbar's son" (p. 27, folio 13).
- **The process itself, summarised by the book** — the early anecdotes "tend to indicate that within about a hundred and fifty years of his death, the Khwaja had become a legend in India" (p. 134, folio 116).

### Khwaja Qutbuʾd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki and Delhi

- **Khwaja Khizr delivers the child to his Qurʾan teacher — two incompatible versions and a flat verdict on the genre.** Version one: the mother "gave some sweets to her slave girl and asked her to take the child to the Hafiz… Aba Hafs [OCR?] [Abu Hafs] then told Qutbu'd-Din that the old man was Khwaja Khizr." Version two, Jamali's: "when Qutbu'd-Din was five, his mother asked one of her neighbours… Khwaja Khizr met the neighbour on the way to school and offered to take the boy himself." Rizvi: "Some type of intervention by Khwaja Khizr in sufi stories is quite common and is included to add a pious flavour" (p. 152, folio 134, footnote 1).
- **The blind arrow that turns back the Mongols, with the rationalist counter-explanation in the same sentence** — "Giving him an arrow, the Khwaja suggested he shoot it blindly into the enemy's camp. Qubacha obeyed, and the following day the Mongols retreated. Although, in reality, the Mongols left the Indus region for political reasons, this further increased Qubacha's devotion to the Khwaja" (p. 153, folio 135).
- **The Prophet appears to a Sultan and a saint to site a public water tank** — Iltutmish's tank, "the Prophet Muhammad appeared to both the Sultan and the Khwaja, indicating a particular spot" (p. 154, folio 136).
- **A poet's success at court obtained by mental invocation** (p. 154, folio 136).
- **The miraculous bread that gives Kaki his name — two versions differing in mechanism.** Amir Khwurd's: "a piece of bread would miraculously appear under his prayer carpet… The wife revealed the secret of the bread's appearance and it never appeared again"; Jamali's: "He pointed to a niche in his cell and told his wife to go there and recite B'ismillah and she would get as much bread as she needed." The name is glossed "the word Kaki or Man of Bread" (p. 155, folio 137). **The etymology rests on both and on neither.**
- **A saint so absorbed he does not know his own son has died** (p. 155, folio 137).
- **Death by sustained ecstasy over five nights, triggered by a sung verse** — "'The martyrs of the dagger of taslim (surrender) / Each moment get a new life from the Unseen World.'"; "the Khwaja ordered the verse to be repeated each time he regained consciousness… On the fifth night … he died" (pp. 155–156, folios 137–138); and, in Chapter Six, "Shaikh Qutbu'd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki", the Chishti "who died of samaʿ ecstasy" (p. 348, folio 326).
- **A dervish's prophecy of kingship, reported and then rejected** — "Both stories are myths because the better slave market was at Ghazna not Baghdad" (p. 153, folio 135, footnote 2); the same prophecy stands in Chapter One as "it was the blessings of a dervish which enabled Sultan Shamsu'd-Din Iltutmish to succeed to the throne of Delhi", with "'Isami gave a different colour to this particular story" (p. 29, folio 15).
- **A verse read as a political omen** — "'The glorious Faridun was not an angel… If you are munificent and just, you are Faridun.'" (p. 162, folio 144).
- **A dervish's fatalism against a king's request for intercession** — the king of Nishapur and the Mongol invasion, "it was already too late and only a surrender to the divine will was advisable at that stage" (p. 150, folio 132), "generally ascribed to Shaikh Faridu'd-Din 'Attar".
- **A revenge plot with elephants, truncated at the chunk boundary** — the dervish ʿAyyub's "attempts to have the Qazi trampled to death by elephants seem to have been thwarted by the" (p. 29, folio 15).

### Shaikh Nizamuʾd-Din Auliyaʾ, Amir Khusraw and the Delhi khalifas

- **The flying camel** — Amir Khwurd "could not restrain himself from recording that each night during the Shaikh's lifetime a flying camel had stopped at the window to take Nizamu'd-Din Auliya' to the Ka'ba and bring him back in time for early breakfast" (p. 24, folio 10). **The author frames it as a lapse.**
- **A saint reveals angels physically moving the heavens, to silence a philosopher** (p. 185, folio 167).
- **Involuntary prostration before the Shaikh** — two scholars who "immediately found themselves involuntarily prostrated on the ground before him" (p. 195, folio 177).
- **Inspired knowledge as a diagnostic gift** — "his inspired knowledge ('ilm-i ladunni) is recorded as greatly helping those who called on him" (p. 182, folio 164).
- **The dust of the grave as relic** (p. 181, folio 163).
- **A verse at the grave read as an unwitting prophecy of the reciter's own death** — Khusraw's "'…Oh Khusraw! Return to your own home for the entire world is now covered by night'", glossed "Khusraw was in fact predicting his own death" (p. 190, folio 172); and grief so total it could not be wept (p. 190, folio 172).
- **A child's terror dissolved by a sentence, and a vision of nobles as lambs** — "'Greatness and power belong to God alone.'… he saw the assembly of great nobles as a collection of mere lambs" (p. 197, folio 179).
- **Prayers credited with moving an army 300 kilometres** — "his prayers had helped the army to reach Sarsuti" (p. 197, folio 179), restated as "Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din claimed that the imperial army reached Sarsuti safely because of his own prayers" (p. 204, folio 186); and the answering prayer "'I pray God that Delhi may fall into your hands'" (p. 197, folio 179).
- **A handshake that converts a hostile Sultan** (p. 197, folio 179).
- **A charitable reputation saves a captive from Chingiz Khan** — the father "had been a constant magnanimous distributor of food to the poor… he had given to all, regardless of their origin. Chingiz Khan was so impressed that he released the sufi" (pp. 183–184, folios 165–166).
- **Water-carrying rated above prayer, in a majzub's rebuke** (p. 183, folio 165).
- **The turban prophecy of ʿAli Maula, with its two stated reasons** — prostration before elders, and a turban "completely made of cotton, without a single silken thread" (p. 173, folio 155).
- **The water of blessing — poverty turned into a karama by the guests themselves** (p. 170, folio 152).
- **The melon-and-turban vow that freed a prisoner** (p. 171, folio 153).
- **The prophecy of victory at Warangal, confirmed the same day** (p. 178, folio 160).
- **The month that never came** — "he was assassinated on ihe [OCR?] [the] first night of the Jumada II, 720/8 July 1320 by his favourite and protégée, Khusraw Khan Barwar" (p. 179, folio 161).
- **A prophecy of calamity that Rizvi both reports and corrects** — "he prophesied that some calamity was likely to befall the capital" (p. 180, folio 162), footnoted "Similar prophesies are aiso [also] ascribed to other saints. The Shaikh himself, however, did not quote a Hadis but an opinion expressed by Ghazali".
- **"Delhi is still far away", which Rizvi calls a myth** — "This myth became popular mainly because of Sultan Ghiyasu'd-Din Tughluq's sudden death caused by the collapse of the Afghanpur pavilion hurriedly built for a grand reception" (p. 180, folio 162).
- **A teaching miracle** — Shadi Muqri's teaching "was considered to be miraculous and enabled his students to master the Qur'an perfectly" (p. 172, folio 154).
- **The crescent answer, on appointing a child khalifa** — "the crescent of Islam was also small" (p. 171, folio 153). **The boy is named Burhanuʾd-Din two sentences earlier and Badruʾd-Din twice after; see Doubts.**
- **The miracles of Sabir, and the rediscovery of his tomb, both dismissed** — "The story of the rediscovery of the Shaikh's tomb in Shah-Jahan's reign, and the account of the miracles attributed to the Shaikh described by Ilahdiya, are legendary" (p. 172, folio 154).
- **A foreseen execution that did not happen** — Zarradi "could see his head rolling before the court" (p. 200, folio 182); he was not in fact killed at that audience (pp. 200–201, folios 182–183).
- **Silence under eleven knife wounds** — "A galandar [OCR?] [qalandar], named Turab, entered the room and inflicted eleven knife wounds on the Shaikh's body. The bleeding was so profuse that it flowed into a drain in his cell. While being stabbed, the Shaikh did not utter a sound" (pp. 204–205, folios 186–187).
- **Green wood and dry wood — the justification of a seventeen-day initiation** (p. 208, folio 190); **the rose on the milk**, telling the scholars of Multan by a gesture that there was room for one more (pp. 208–209, folios 190–191).
- **A knife mended miraculously by an ironsmith sufi, told by a sceptic against sufism** — "As no other mystic was like the ironsmith, the Maulana advocated that it was unnecessary to believe in the sufi movement itself" (p. 209, folio 191). **A karamat used as an argument against sufism.**
- **A career owed to an infant blessing** (p. 212, folio 194); **wishes granted at a tomb by tying threads**, reported as ordinary urban practice with no miracle language (p. 207, folio 189); **twelve hajjs immediately discounted** — "numbers used in mystic literature are not necessarily accurate" (p. 213, folio 195).
- **A throne won by the prayers of Baghdad sufis, and a tribunal moved to ecstasy by music — and Rizvi rejects it** — "Such a story, however, is anachronistic for it was not possible for both the Qazi and the Sultan to have been together in Baghdad." (p. 214, folio 196).
- **A body divided between intercessors, footnoted as yogic** — "The division is influenced by the Hath-Yogic ideas" (p. 217, folio 199 and footnote 2).
- **A summons by the Prophet in a vision, as a refusal to be healed** (p. 232, folio 212).
- **Two merchant miracles** — a sunken bale recovered because "he had paid zakat… on the goods so he would not lose anything", and a stolen purse restored by prophecy (p. 241, folio 221).
- **A cultivator with power over rain, with Ghazali as his visitor** — "If he wished, it rained, if he did not, it was dry" (p. 241, folio 221).
- **A conviction that a Shaikh's money was not of this world** — "Many gullible Muslims, however, continued to believe that the Shaikh's funds came from other than earthly sources" (p. 242, folio 222). **"gullible" is Rizvi's word.**
- **A prophecy of a city's destruction by Hindu merchants**, the *Fawaʾiduʾl-Fuʾad* going "to the extent of ascribing the destruction of Lahore, by the Mongols in 1241, to the profiteering of its merchants" (pp. 241–242, folios 221–222).
- **Two drownings read as a saint's retaliation, and the reading repudiated** (p. 248, folio 228).
- **A conversion by dream-vision** — Berke "embraced Islam after having had a vision in which Shaikh Bakharzi appeared to him" (p. 247, folio 227); and **a snake spared because it came to listen** (p. 247, folio 227), footnoted as also told of Abu Saʿid.
- **Rain after samaʿ** — after a long drought and a samaʿ, "rain fell heavily" (p. 216, folio 198).

### Shaikh Jalaluʾd-Din Tabrizi, the Suhrawardi line and Bengal

- **Evil spirits vacating a house before a saint enters it** (p. 218, folio 200); **clairvoyance with the author's naturalistic alternative attached** — "although it is possible the slave may have informed him" (p. 218, folio 200).
- **A dream of God's throne that converts a Qazi** (p. 219, folio 201); **instantaneous conversion by sight**, the Katihar robber "renamed 'Ali" (p. 219, folio 201).
- **A demon killed with a staff and an idol vanquished — disowned while printed in full** — "reads like other stock-in-trade stories and fairytales… It was related by such an authority as Gisu Daraz" (p. 219, folio 201, footnote 4).
- **A prince killed by a saint's miracle** — "Multan was invaded by the Mongols and the Prince was killed, according to sufi tradition, due toa [OCR?] [due to a] miracle of Shaikh Sadru'd-Din" (p. 223, folio 203).
- **Enlightenment transmitted through a shared drink** — Ahmad-i Maʿshuq and the sherbet (p. 230, folio 210).
- **A whole conversion cycle dismissed** — "The stories of Amir Husain's conversion to sufism are obviously mythical" (p. 226, folio 206).
- **A hagiographical number debunked** — "The figure of twelve years for the length of his stay in these forests is mythical, as this was the standard period used to describe prolonged mortifications performed in lonely forest areas" (p. 249, folio 229).
- **Forty disciples said to have achieved union with God**, out of "more than 100,000" (p. 257, folio 237).
- **Supernatural powers ranked against Hindu yogis and Christian monks** — "like the Hindu yogis who walked on water and flew like birds… these exercises made Christian monks superior even to yogis… To him, however, these achievements were not the essence of sufism" (p. 253, folio 233).
- **Direct divine answers as the sufi's superiority over the ʿulamaʾ** — "sufis consulted the Lauhu'l Mahfuz (Preserved Tablet), and if the answer was not there they questioned God and received a reply directly from him" (pp. 254–255, folios 234–235).
- **A retributive reading of imperial collapse** — the *Manaqibuʾl-Asfiyaʾ* ascribing "the disintegration of Sultan Firuz's administration, fall of the Tughluq empire and the catastrophe that befell Delhi during Timur's invasion, specifically to the execution of the two sufis" (p. 251, folio 231).
- **An accusation of incarnation against a living saint** — "Ahmad's disciples preached that God had appeared in Delhi in the form of Ahmad" (p. 251, folio 231, footnote 3).
- **Two moral anecdotes the book calls charming** — the broken fast, "there was no atonement for the sin of breaking someone's heart"; and the drunken imam, of whom the Shaikh said "that no doubt he did not drink during the fasting month of Ramazan" (p. 252, folio 232).
- **The idle devil** — "the worldly 'ulama' had taken his place and there was no work left for him to do" (p. 255, folio 235).
- **A claim of having met 380 saints** (p. 264, folio 244); **an extreme act of service** — cleaning "the istinja clay of his preceptor" with his beard (p. 264, folio 244).

### Gisu Daraz, Simnani and the fifteenth-century centres

- **A birth attributed to a pir's blessing** (p. 267, folio 247); **light produced by a bodily zikr technique, "believed to be mystical"** (p. 269, folio 249); **a vision as the trigger of a career**, "In 1284 a vision aroused in him a desire for further mystical experiences" (p. 268, folio 248).
- **The long-hair legend and the prophecy couplet, then the author's demolition** — "'Whoever is admitted to the discipleship of Gisu Daraz / By God! it is true that he is engrossed in (divine) love.'"; "This story, however, appears to be a later legend as an earlier authority says that 'Gisu Daraz' was not an Indian title, and that in countries outside India, Saiyids were known as Daraz Gisu (Long Locked Ones)" (p. 271, folio 251).
- **A refused blessing and a fulfilled succession prophecy — the legend that underwrites a shrine's royal patronage** — the Saiyid "saying that Ahmad, the Sultan's brother, was destined for the throne and that there should be no interference in the divine decree"; later "Ahmad managed to escape this fate and took refuge in Gisu Daraz's house; the Saiyid blessed him and prophesied he would succeed to the throne", which came to pass on "5 Shawwal 825/22 September 1422" (p. 272, folio 252).
- **A devotee ranks his pir above the Prophet, reported by Firishta without comment** — "'Although Muhammad was the prophet of God; God be praised, Saiyid Muhammad Gisu Daraz was something else'" (p. 273, folio 253).
- **Death by ecstasy at a wedding, and two bodies in one palanquin** — "When it was opened two bodies were revealed instead of one. Both bodies were buried side by side." **Rizvi calls it "a romanticized account"** (p. 276, folio 256).
- **The Shaikh Piyara story, introduced as "Equally fanciful"** — he "had worn a Brahmanical thread and had begun living in the temple where she worshipped. Gisu Daraz was delighted with Piyara's bold enterprise" (p. 276, folio 256). **A Muslim disciple living in a Hindu temple, told approvingly.**
- **Rain compelled by a king's self-abasement** — "if a country was seized with drought the king should don tattered clothes, start digging with a spade, then sow some seeds and pray for rain" (p. 275, folio 255).
- **A saint's son tortured to death over a hidden treasury** — "Raja Ganesa is said to have banished him to Sunargaon and then had tortured him to death in order to discover where his ancestral treasury was hidden" (p. 280, folio 260). **Note the unexplained assumption that a saintly family had "an ancestral treasury".**
- **Two supernatural circumnavigations of the world, which the book separates from the real one** — "Simnani travelled round the world only once; and then performed the same journey on two occasions in a supernatural way; hagiologists including the author of the Lata'if-i Ashrafi, however, have failed to discriminate between the two" (p. 286, folio 266); **a miraculous flight with its technical term footnoted**, "in the state of Tair… A sufi term which indicates miraculous flying from one place to another" (p. 287, folio 267).
- **An arrival timed to a death, which Rizvi reads as evidence of standing, not as a miracle** — Simnani reached Munyar "on 2 January 1381. At the time of his arrival, the body of Shaikh Sharafu'd-Din Yahya Munyari was awaiting burial. The Saiyid led the pre-burial prayers" (p. 287, folio 267).
- **Thought-reading, twice** (pp. 284, 286; folios 264, 266); **a cure by forced emesis that converts an enemy of sufism** (p. 294, folio 274); **extreme longevity given as plain numbers** — "The latter lived for 150 years and Shah Miyanji survived for 120" (p. 296, folio 276), and Adhan Jaunpuri "At his death he was over a hundred years old" (p. 283, folio 263).
- **A teaching miracle by demonstration — pebbles, mud and water** — "He was expected to lose himself in the Divine… like the mud in the water" (p. 291, folio 271).
- **A feast for dogs, a rebuke disguised as an insult** — "as the feast had been given only for dogs, that is the worldly, how could humans have been invited 76 [OCR?] [invited?]" (p. 292, folio 272).
- **A saint's insulated presence at court** — "The Shaikh declared that the king did not expel dervishes and galandars [qalandars], only ignorant mystics" (pp. 290–291, folios 270–271).
- **Leaves as a head-covering, in the poverty story** (p. 285, folio 265).
- **Mahdi claimants and their fate** — "a holyman named Rukn claimed to be the Mahdi… Sultan Firuz Shah, who duly had Rukn beheaded" (p. 289, folio 269); Simnani "met another Mahdi pretender in Turkey and advised him to refrain from such adventurous and dangerous ambitions" (p. 290, folio 270).
- **The relic miracle in the tank** — "Miraculously the object turned into a mixture of each of the three materials, and later became celebrated relics" (pp. 304–305, folios 282–283).
- **A generosity story the book says is told of many saints** — the robbers, the unlocked house and the forgotten bag of ten thousand dinars (p. 304, folio 282).
- **A Holi trance lasting three days and nights** — "on the day of the Hindu festival of Holi a group of Hindu boys passed his house singing and dancing. Their music so moved him that he fell into a trance, and followed the revellers around the streets of Kanauj for three days and nights. So infectious was the group that many of the townsfolk joined them" (pp. 306–307, folios 284–285). **A Suhrawardi saint in ecstasy at a Hindu festival, with townspeople following.**
- **A coronation blessing obtained by a ruse** — "Although the story may appear fanciful to modern readers, medieval historians ascribed its possible authenticity to the Sultan's wisdom in attaining a blessing through indirect means" (p. 307, folio 285).
- **A tomb that splits to admit a second body — a literary, not a hagiographic, miracle** — in Jamali's *Mihr wa Mah*, "The Prince's tomb, already sealed, breaks in half and the two are buried side by side" (pp. 309–310, folios 287–288).

### Kashmir, the Qalandars, the martyr saints and Chapter Five

- **Fairy-tale conversion legends around Bulbul Shah, dismissed** — "The story of his conversion as outlined by Kashmiri historians is a fable and Abu'l-Fazl's account is more historical" (p. 312, folio 290, note 3).
- **The conversion of a temple priest, dismissed by name** — "The miracles alleged to have been performed in order to convert the Brahman priest of Kali Mandir in Srinagar are reminiscent of those attributed to Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din Chishti and to a large number of other sufis. Yogis at the court of Sultan Muhammad bin Tughlugq [Tughluq] had been seen by Ibn Battuta to fly through the air. Such stock-in-trade legends merit no attention" (p. 314, folio 292). **See Doubts: the Kali Mandir here is in Kashmir and takes none of the archive's Kali rows.**
- **The legend of three visits to Kashmir, and how it grew** — "The stories that the Saiyid visited Kashmir three times in 774/1372, 781-1399 [OCR?] [781/1379?] and 785/1383… are mythical"; "From the Saiyid's alleged statement that he had travelled three times the rub 'i-maskun… grew the legend of his three visits to Kashmir" (pp. 313–314, folios 291–292). **A number deflated: "they would not have numbered 700 as is popularly believed" (p. 314, folio 292).**
- **The qalandar who turned Khwaja ʿAbduʾllah Ansari back from wandering** (p. 323, folio 301).
- **The shaving of Shaikh Jamaluʾd-Din of Sawa in Ibn Battuta's version**, and **the poisoned water and the restored beard** — "he bent his head, and as he raised it exposed a chin covered with a long white beard", with the book's note that "An identical story is related by Ibn Battuta, but in his version the wise man is replaced by Qazi Ibn-i 'Imad" (pp. 324–325, folios 302–303).
- **Wall-smashing with a single blow of the hand** (p. 325, folio 303).
- **The 500-year life of Shaikh ʿAbduʾl-ʿAziz Makki** — approval by the Prophet for shaving; the mountain cave; reappearance under Caliph ʿAli; reappearance "in the third century Hijri" as Bayazid's disciple; "emerging after two hundred years to initiate Khizr Rumi"; then the disappearance into a grotto (sardabad) on the outskirts of **Ajodhan** and the prophecy of return with the last Imam Mahdi (p. 326, folio 304). **The grotto is at Ajodhan but carries no shrine, tomb or ʿurs, and must NOT be cited as evidence for `shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar`.**
- **Initiation by a dead saint's spirit** — "'Abdu'r Rahman Chishti compromised by asserting that Shaikh Abu 'Ali Qalandar had been initiated into the Chishtiyya order by the spirit of Shaikh Qutbu'd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki." (p. 327, folio 305).
- **Shah Niʿmatuʾllah's apocalyptic verses** on "the 'Mischief of the Last Day' and the advent of the Mahdi" (p. 328, folio 306).
- **Haidar's fire and snow, and the iron jewellery** — "in summer he would walk through fire, and in winter he would stand in the snow… he would hold rods of red hot iron in his hands and, as if they were wax, fashion necklaces and bangles, which he could then wear." (p. 329, folio 307); and **the Mongol dervish who outmatched Haidar**, told by Nizamuʾd-Din Auliyaʾ (p. 329, folio 307).
- **Coin from nowhere** — "Whenever he purchased anything or wished to pay someone he would specify a spot and freshly coined money would be found. From this grew the story that he was an alchemist." (pp. 330–331, folios 308–309).
- **Divine retribution for killing a holy man** — the dust storm, drought, famine and floods; "To Barani the moral was clear—no king could afford to kill a dervish" (p. 331, folio 309).
- **Miracles as a literary genre inside a silsila** — Baba Dawud's *Dasturuʾs-Salikin* "enthusiastically describes the miracles of Shaikh Hamza" (p. 321, folio 299); Shah Khizr Rumi "Even then he performed exciting miracles" (p. 326, folio 304).
- **The fire-walk and the unburnt shirt, in Ibn Battuta's first person** — the Haidaris "kindled it, and at length, when it was a mass of glowing coals, they began their musical recital and went into that fire, still dancing and rolling about init [OCR?] [in it]"; the chief "put it on dnd [OCR?] [and] began to roll about in the fire with it on… He then brought me the shirt showing not a single trace of burning on it, at which I was greatly astonished." (p. 332, folio 310).
- **The Rifaʿiyya fire and snake feats** — "some of them rolled in the fire, and others ate it in their mouths… Some of them will take a large snake and bite its head with their teeth until they bite it clean through." (p. 332, folio 310).
- **A saint appears to a Sultan in a dream and reforms him** — Salar Masʿud (p. 334, folio 312).
- **A saint's spirit dictates his own hagiography, twice** — "the spirit of the Prince of Martyrs had appeared to him in a vision and approved of his planned work"; and for the *Mirʾat-i Madari*, "the author asserted he was helped in its compilation by the holy spirit of the great saint" (pp. 335, 340; folios 313, 318).
- **A saint who "controlled everything that happened in the world"** — reported to Ibn Battuta by "Shaikh Burhanu'd-Din Sagharji of China"; "it is believed he miraculously performed his morning prayers in Mecca whither he also made an annual pilgrimage" (p. 337, folio 315).
- **Extreme asceticism as marvel** — Shah Jalal "would fast for ten days at a time and was as thin as a stick" (p. 337, folio 315); Shah Madar's disciples "reported that he took no meals for twelve years" (p. 341, folio 319); **the veiled face that fells the onlooker** — "Anyone who glanced at him would instantly fall to the ground" (p. 341, folio 319).
- **Travel by fish and by rock** — "The legend that Pir Badr arrived in Chittagong on a floating piece of rock or that he travelled to Arakan on the back of a fish symbolize his powers over rivers." (p. 339, folio 317).
- **Training received directly from the Prophet, with a retro-fitted chain** — Shah Madar's followers, "others added five or six names in an ascending order of spiritual ancestors, in order to connect him with the Prophet" (p. 341, folio 319); **the Dam Madar origin myth set in the Miʿraj** — the gates "narrower than the eye of a needle" and the cry "Dam Madar (Madar is Life)" (p. 341, folio 319); **the Madari theology of the body** — "God was Spirit, Muhammad His body, the Four Caliphs His two arms and feet" (p. 341, folio 319); **walking on coals unscathed, in the present tense** — "The Madari dervishes, crying 'Dam Madar', walk on burning coals while their feet remain unscathed" (pp. 341–342, folios 319–320).
- **Companionship of the Prophet across seven centuries** — Baba Ratan "Born before the birth of Muhammad", made "a companion of the Prophet", died "in 700/1300"; and ʿAbduʾllah Chingal, who "observed Muhammad's miracle of the splitting of the moon" and "was reported to have survived for several centuries before he died in Gujarat" (pp. 342–343, folios 320–321). **On Baba Ratan and `dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee` see Doubts: the book writes "Baba Ratan", never "Nath", and it has not been mapped.**
- **A sufi who dies on command**, al-Biruni's story — "'Oh master, do you know here a place fit for us to die on?'… I rose, went to him and shook him, but lo! he was already cold.'" (p. 347, folio 325).
- **A thread from a mother's garment brings rain to Delhi** — "Taking a thread from a garment worn by his mother he held it in his hand and started praying. Instantly it began to pour" (p. 425, folio not stated), sourced "AA, p. 294". **The extract does not name whose mother, so the story is left unattached — see Doubts.**
- **A woman who fasts to death on the fortieth day** — Bibi Fatima, and the moral that "a real sufi was one who was an ibnu'l-waqt" (p. 427, folio 403).
- **A dust storm that abducts a child, in two variants** — Ahmad blown "to a place far from his home" into the hands of Baba Ishaq Maghribi (p. 428, folio not stated), and, in the *Maʿarijuʾl-Wilayat*, carried into "a caravan of cotton merchants" (p. 429, folio 405).
- **A childless Brahman given five sons, and the promised eldest hidden away** — the bargain, the deception "under the guise of his eldest child", and the shaikh's knowledge of the hiding place; the boy "died at the age of twenty-five, and although the Shaikh was heart-broken it is recorded that he was offered in a divine message an even worthier child" (pp. 428–429, folio not stated / 405).
- **A leafless mulberry tree turns green** — "this miracle was formed the basis [OCR?] [this miracle formed the basis] for his immediate fame" (p. 429, folio 405).
- **Bread from heaven for forty prisoners, and Timur's conversion into a devotee** — "Each day the spiritual powers of the Shaikh provided each prisoner with a ration of hot cake allegedly sent from heaven… Timur later became his devotee" (p. 430, folio 406), with Rizvi's footnote, "The source of the bread remains unknown and the miracle is legendary".
- **A prophecy of Timur's invasion, fifteen days out** (p. 430, folio 406); **a yogi's prior claim on the ground of Ahmadabad, and collapsing walls** (p. 431, folio 407); **knowledge at a distance between Delhi and Bengal** (p. 431, folio 407).
- **A dream in Medina in which the Prophet offers the world as a girl** — "The girl was the world personified", and "The Shaikh ascribed his affluence in Gujarat-to [OCR?] [Gujarat to] the blessings of the Prophet Muhammad" (p. 432, folio 408).
- **A motionless yogi between two idols who reappears in China two centuries on** — "his gestures convinced the Moorish traveller that the yogi was in fact really a Muslim"; at Canton "an old cave dweller who was reported to have been two hundred years old… told Ibn Battuta he was the same yogi he had met in the island temple" (pp. 434–435, folios 410–411), with Rizvi's framing, "Such legendary personalities seem to have greatly influenced the local islanders who, prior to the impact of Islam, were imbued with animistic beliefs". **This is the closing sentence of Appendix C and of the book's main text.**

### Nath, Bhakti and Hindi-romance material with no archive row

- **Matsyendra overhears the doctrine while in fish form** — "while swimming like a fish he overheard the esoteric doctrines of the Nath which the Lord Shiva was imparting to his spouse, Parvati. Matsyendra is therefore known by such names as Minanath or Lui-pa, meaning fish Lord in Tibetan" (p. 353, folio 331).
- **A queendom ruled by female succession** — "Lakshinkara, a legendary princess of the mythical kingdom of Indrabhuti which was successively ruled by female issue. Lakshinkara has been compared to Eleanor of Aquitain" (p. 353, folio 331).
- **Puranbhagat and Rasula** — "who, with his half-brother Rasula, has inspired hundreds of miraculous tales" (p. 354, folio 332); **Guga (or Guganath) "whose power over serpents was phenomenal"** (p. 354, folio 332). **Neither tale is told.**
- **Posthumous initiation from a dead shaikh's spirit, as the man's own claim** — ʿAbduʾl-Quddus "also claimed to have directly obtained inspiration from the spirit of Shaikh Ahmad 'Abdu'l-Haqq" (p. 365, folio 343); **and, in the same sentence, a living pir is declared necessary** (p. 365, folio 343).
- **An ecstatic state compared to prophetic revelation** — sultan-i zikr against wahi, "the meditator felt as if he were listening to the humming of a bell whose sound then gradually became thunderous" (p. 365, folio 343).
- **The hadith of the Hidden Treasure, attributed to a revelation to David** — "'I was a Hidden Treasure and I wished to be known, so I created creation that I might be known.'" (pp. 362–363, folios 340–341), with Rizvi's note, "This desire is identical with the concept of divine will as held by the Naths."
- **A prayer that changes a courtier's fortune** (p. 366, folio 344); **a prediction of a battle's outcome made to the loser** (p. 367, folio 345); **initiation from the Prophet's spirit without a living master** — "Some early Rishi sources state that the Shaikh was an Uwaisi" (p. 372, folio 350); **a chronicler's superlative** — "According to Jonaraja, the Shaikh was the greatest sage of his time" (p. 372, folio 350); **a cave shared with insects as fellow worshippers** (p. 373, folio 351); **a legendary meeting Rizvi will not allow to become influence** (p. 371, folio 349).
- **God takes a devotee's cymbals** — Namdeva's trance, "God finally appeared and took the instruments from him" (p. 378, folio 356); **ecstasy with a clinical description attached** — "Chaitanya's emotional attachment to Krishna led to prolonged spells of ecstasy and epileptic fits" (p. 379, folio 357); **a faith transmitted across the religious line by a woman** — "Bhirbhadra, a son of Nityananda (1473-1544)… received the Baul faith from a Muslim woman called Madhava Bibi" (p. 380, folio 358).
- **Prophets for every nation, including India — the scriptural warrant for Chapter Six** — "Each country and nation received its own prophet", quoting "Qurʾan, XXXV, 24", "XVII, 15", "XIV, 4" and "X, 48" (p. 369, folio 347).
- **A dead poet initiates a living one in a vision** — "The Mir claimed that in a vision the Khwaja had made him his disciple" (p. 384, folio 362).
- **A beauty whose passage absolves sin** (pp. 386–387, folios 364–365); **a son granted for generosity** (p. 388, folio 366); **fairies at a lake, and a doe who is a woman** (p. 388, folio 366); **a parrot whose praise induces love at a distance, with nine Naths and eighty-four Siddhas as helpers** (p. 390, folio 368).
- **The marks of a Siddha, as bodily prodigies** — "The Siddha is one on whose limbs flies do not settle… He whom the Lord has made a Siddha in this world, none can recognise him whether he be revealed or disguised." (p. 393, folio 369); **a stake on the path of love reserved for a thief or a Hallaj** (p. 393, folio 369).
- **A virgin birth by a saint's blessing, compared by the book to Christ** — "he was conceived by a widow because of Ramananda's blessings, and that, like Christ, this occurred without a natural father"; the abandonment "is reminiscent of the adoption of Moses by the Pharoah's daughter" (p. 398, folio 374); **the vanished corpse and the flowers** — "only a bunch of flowers was found under the sheet and these were divided amongst the two groups" (p. 399, folio 375); **Kabir saved from prison by miracle**, "Although such a story would seem mythical…" (p. 406, folio 382); **Kabir's feigned drunkenness with a courtesan, as malamati practice** (p. 403, folio 379); **a saint's non-death** — "To Kabir saints didn't really die they just returned home" (p. 404, folio 380).
- **A prayer answered with a refusal** — ʿAbduʾl-Quddus "was told by Allah that even Prophets and great sages had had to suffer similar hardship and the Shaikh himself was not their equal" (p. 410, folio 386, footnote 1).
- **A yogi who reached the Kaʿba** — "the Siraju'l-Hidaya mentions a yogi accompanying a Muslim saint to the Ka'ba" (p. 412, folio 388). **Cited by Rizvi as hard evidence, not as legend, and it is the book's one attested Hindu presence at a Muslim sanctuary.**
- **The parallel adduced from Ibn al-ʿArabi, in Affifi's words** — "'He tells us the dates when and the places where he had the visions, in which he saw prophets and saints and discoursed with them…'" (pp. 413–414, folios 389–390).

## Figures and sites not in the archive

*This is a finding aid, not a selection. Every distinct figure and place named in the twenty-eight
chunks' "other saints, sites and events" material is entered once, with the page references from
every chunk that names it merged onto the single entry. The form is deliberately compact — name,
one line of what the book says, page references — and is fuller only in section A, where the entries
are being proposed as future archive rows. **Every name below was checked against
`shrine_index.tsv`.** Names that turned out to be an archive row's principal figure, and
place-names that turned out to be an archive row's location under ruling (a), have been taken OUT of
this section and listed at the end of this file under `## Misfiled — belongs in the archive
section`; they are not mapped here. Page references are reproduced exactly as the chunk notes give
them, damage flags and all. **No index or endnote locator has been converted into a `(p. N)`**:
where an entry rests on the book's index, the page cited is the PDF page the index entry is printed
on and the entry says so.*

### A. Sites with a named tomb, shrine, cult or observance — candidates for future archive rows

- **Tomb of Sipah Salar Masʿud Ghazi ("Ghazi Miyan"), Bahraich, eastern U.P.** — **STRONG CANDIDATE
  FOR A NEW ARCHIVE ROW: the only site in the book carrying a dated ʿurs, a second dated
  flag-ceremony on a Hindu calendar, an endowment of villages and a continuous pilgrimage history.
  Do not fold it into any existing row — no archive row has Bahraich as its location and none has
  Salar Masʿud as its principal figure; it is not Sakhi Sarwar, not Shah Madar, not any Ghazi row.**
  Tomb "alleged to be in Bahraich in eastern U.P.", "now, as it has been since the fourteenth
  century, a centre of popular pilgrimage" (p. 333, folio 311). "Although his 'urs is celebrated on
  12, 13 and 14 Rajab, the flags are taken to the tomb on the first Sunday of the Hindi month Jaith
  (May-June)" (p. 336, folio 314). "The Buddhist and Hindu Magh people settled some villages as
  endowment for the shrine and made it a centre of pilgrimage" (p. 339, folio 317). "To Muslims and
  Hindus alike, Salar Mas'ud was the great local saint of eastern India" (p. 336, folio 314); names
  "Bale Miyan (Revered Boy), Bale Pir (Boy Saint) and Hateli Pir (Obstinate Saint)" (p. 336, folio
  314) and "the Prince of Martyrs" (p. 335, folio 313). Legendary dates as printed: born "On 21
  Sha'ban 405/14 February 1015"; at "his Somnath expedition in 1025-26"; "Seizing Multan and Delhi he
  crossed the Ganges and camped at Satrikh in the Barabanki district, near Lucknow"; his father "On
  25 Shawwal 423/4 October 1032, Salar Shahu died from natural causes"; "On 14 Rajab 424/15 June
  1033, he was struck at Bahraich by an arrow from a Hindu chief and died aged nineteen" (p. 335,
  folio 313). **Rizvi does not believe the occupant is Masʿud: "Probably it was one of Prince
  Nasiru'd-Din's fallen generals who was the real occupant of the grave"** (p. 333, folio 311).
  Competing descriptions at pp. 333–334 (folios 311–312): Barani's "outstanding warrior in the army
  of Mahmud of Ghazna", Abuʾl-Fazl's "one of the martyrs of the Ghaznawid armies", Shaikhuʾl-Hidaya's
  "an Afghan who had met a martyr's death", Amir Khusraw's "a well-known saint"; ʿAbduʾl-Haqq
  "contradicts the popular belief that Salar Mas'ud was a disciple of Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din Chishti"
  (p. 334, folio 312), and "Shah 'Alam Bukhari is said to have observed that Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din
  Chishti would transfer requests made to him to Salar Mas'ud" (p. 334, folio 312, footnote 7). Index
  headwords "Salar Mas‘ud Ghazi, 311-17" (p. 483, folio 459), "Ghazi Miyan, see Salar Mas‘ud Ghazi,
  311" (p. 466, folio 442), "Hateli Pir, 314" (p. 467, folio 443), each cited to the PDF page the
  index is printed on.
- **Tomb ("Choti Dargah") of Pir Badr-i ʿAlam in Bihar, with his chilla at Chittagong** — **STRONG
  CANDIDATE: a death date to the day, a named mausoleum with a named counterpart, a surviving chilla
  that is itself a pilgrimage site, a stated patronage and a described joint observance. Do not fold
  into any existing row; no archive row carries Chittagong or Bihar.** "He died on 27 Rajab 844/22
  December 1440"; his Bihar tomb "is known as the Choti Dargah (Smaller Mausoleum)", set against
  "the Buri Dargah (Bigger Mausoleum) of Shaikh Sharafu'd-Din Yahya Munyari" (p. 339, folio 317).
  "The traditional site of his chilla (cell) where he would perform ascetic exercises often for
  forty days is still a centre of pilgrimage"; "His lasting impact has been as the patron saint of
  sailors, both Muslim and Hindu" (p. 338, folio 316). Ancestors at Meerut; grandfather "Shaikh
  Shihabu'd-Din Haqq Go! [OCR?] [Haqq Go] (Speaker of the Truth) was executed by Sultan Muhammad bin
  Tughlaq"; initiated "by his father and by Shaikh Jalalu'd-Din Bukhari"; "He married into a Bihari
  Hindu family"; Chittagong "believed to be haunted by evil spirits" (p. 338, folio 316). The
  observance with Khwaja Khizr: "a joint ceremony held on a village pond or nearby river in which a
  little grass raft carrying a lighted lamp was floated" (p. 339, folio 317). Index "Pir Badr-i
  ‘Alam (d. 1440), 316-17" (p. 480, folio 456) and "Choti Dargah, 317" (p. 463, folio 439), cited to
  the PDF pages the index is printed on.
- **The "Buri Dargah" of Shaikh Sharafuʾd-Din Ahmad Yahyaʾ Munyari, Munyar (Maner), Bihar** —
  **STRONG CANDIDATE: a named mausoleum, an exact death date, a khanqah with a named builder and a
  named revenue assignment. Do not fold into any existing row; this man is not Baba Farid and the
  archive has no Bihar row.** He "died from natural causes on 5 Shawwal 782/2 January 1381" (p. 252,
  folio 232); mausoleum named at p. 339 (folio 317). Born "at the end of Sha'ban 661 /early July
  1263"; "Sharafu'd-Din (Glory of the Faith)" a title (p. 248, folio 228); the town footnote "This
  town is now known as Maner or Muner but the Sharaf Nama-i Ahmad Munyari rhymes only when the last
  word is read as Munyari or Manyari" (p. 248, folio 228). First khanqah built by Maulana Nizam
  Madani (pp. 249–250, folios 229–230); second ordered by Muhammad bin Tughluq, who "ordered
  Zainu'd-Din Majdu'l-Mulk, the mugqta' [muqta'] of Bihar, to build an imposing kKhangah [khanqah]
  for the Shaikh and assign the pargana, Rajgir, to pay the expenses" (p. 250, folio 230). His
  letters: a hundred in 747/1346-47 for Qazi Shamsuʾd-Din of Chausa, and 151 compiled "In
  769/1367-68" by Shaikh Zain Badr ʿArabi (p. 260, folio 240); his *Maʿdanuʾl-Maʿani* covering "15
  Sha'ban 749/8 November 1348" to "the end of Shawwal 751/December 1350", the *Malfuz-i Safar* of
  762/1360-61, a further collection covering "21 Sha'ban 774/15 February 1373" to "1 Muharram 775/23
  June 1373", and the *Ganj-i La Yafna* (p. 22, folio 8); sons "Qazi Husamu'd-Din, Qutbu'd-Din,
  'Abdu'l Malik and Fakhru'd-Din" (p. 260, folio 240); educated under Abu Tawwama at Sunargaon, the
  marriage question left open (p. 249, folio 229); ascetic exercises at Rajgir, "not in the forests
  near Agra as the Akhbaru'l-Akhyar has it" (p. 249, folio 229); rejected by Nizamuʾd-Din Auliyaʾ and
  by Abu ʿAli Qalandar before taking Najibuʾd-Din Firdawsi as pir (p. 249, folio 229).
- **Tomb of Khwaja Muʿinuʾd-Din Chishti, Ajmer** — **STRONG CANDIDATE: the book's densest account of
  a shrine's construction, funding, succession dispute and change of custodianship, and the archive
  holds no Ajmer row at all (chunk 012, p. 200, folio 182). Do not fold into any Pakistani Chishti
  row.** "Khwaja Husain Nagauri built a tomb over the grave" (p. 143, folio 125), **no date (not
  stated)**; "the money he received from Sultan Ghiyasu'd-Din Khalji (1469-1501) was spent in
  constructing the tomb of Khwaja Mu'inu'd-Din" (p. 149, folio 131); "The Sultans of Malwa showered
  riches on the Chishti tombs at Ajmer and Nagaur" (p. 295, folio 275); patrons named "the rulers of
  Mandu", "Mahmud Khalji (1436-69)", "Akbar", "Jahangir", "Shah-Jahan"/"Shah Jahan", "Aurangzeb",
  "Raja Bihara Mal", "the crown prince, Salim" (pp. 143–145, folios 125–127). The custodianship case:
  "Akbar ruled against the Shaikh, and transferred the administration of the shrine to Shaikh
  Muhammad Bukhari, a trusted officer", who "greatly improved the shrine's management while
  attempting to provide better conditions for pilgrims" (p. 144, folio 126); "the board of enquiry
  which Akbar … had appointed did not accept the argument that the Khwaja had issue, and the Emperor
  assumed control over his khangah [OCR?] [khanqah]" (p. 296, folio 276); "The descendants of Shaikh
  Salim and some qazis supported Shaikh Husain's rivals" (p. 144, folio 126). Later the shrine's
  administration is offered as a rank: Chisht Khan "accepted the title but not the position at
  Ajmer" in 1455 (p. 295, folio 275).
- **Tomb of Khwaja Qutbuʾd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki, Mahrauli near Delhi** — **STRONG CANDIDATE: a burial
  site the saint chose himself, a dated death, a measured distance and a documented cluster of later
  graves. Do not fold into `tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak`: different men, and the book distinguishes them
  throughout.** He died "On the fifth night, 14 Rabi' I 633/27 November 1235 … and was buried in
  Mahrauli about eleven miles from Delhi, at a place he himself had chosen" (p. 156, folio 138). The
  cluster: Shaikh Badruʾd-Din of Ghazna "died in about 657/1258-59 and was buried near the tomb of
  his master" (p. 156, folio 138); Khwaja Mahmud Muyina-Duz "was buried near the Khwaja's tomb"
  (p. 217, folio 199); Ziyaʾuʾd-Din Rumi "was buried on the road to the tomb" (pp. 213–214, folios
  195–196); "Najibu'd-Din's grave lay on the path to the tomb of Khwaja Qutbu'd-Din" and "A
  structure was erected on it by Sultan Muhammad bin Tughluq" (p. 170, folio 152); a head-shaving
  initiation at the tomb was held invalid (p. 166, folio 148). Life, compressed: born "at Ush, in
  the province of the Jaxartes", father "Kamalu'd-Din Ahmad Musa" dead when he was eighteen months
  old (p. 152, folio 134); met Muʿinuʾd-Din "in a mosque" at Baghdad (p. 152, folio 134); lived at
  Kilukhari before Delhi, "Twice weekly he was visited by the eminent people of Delhi" (p. 153,
  folio 135); refused the post of Shaikhuʾl-Islam (p. 154, folio 136); "Two sons were born", one
  surviving (p. 155, folio 137); "trained nine disciples, only two of which are well-known" (p. 156,
  folio 138); took his death-ecstasy at a samaʿ in Shaikh ʿAli Sijzi's khanqah (p. 155, folio 137;
  p. 348, folio 326). Firishta dissents: discipleship "in Isfahan" (p. 152, folio 134). Index
  headword "Qutbu’d-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki, Khwaja (d. 1235), 4, 125, 133-40, 152, 170, 193, 196-97,
  199, 219-20, 226, 304-5, 326, 394, 401" (p. 481, folio 457), cited to the PDF page it is printed
  on.
- **Tomb of Shaikh Hamiduʾd-Din Suwali Nagauri, Nagaur** — **STRONG CANDIDATE: a death date to the
