  day, a named burial town and named later building work including a gateway. Do not fold into any
  archive row; "Hamidu'd-Din" is not an archive principal figure and he is NOT Baba Farid — the book
  keeps "Shaikh Farid of Nagaur" and Baba Farid apart on the same line (p. 349, folio 327).** He
  "died on 29 Rabi' II 673/1 November 1274 and was buried at Nagaur"; Khwaja Husain Nagauri added
  "Other constructions, including a gateway" (p. 148, folio 130). Titled "Su/tanu’t-Tarikin [OCR?]
  [Sultanu’t-Tarikin] (King of Hermits)"; "His real name was Muhammad. His father, Ahmad, migrated
  from Lahore to Delhi where the Shaikh was born after its conquest in 1192"; teachers "Maulana
  Shamsu'd-Din Halwa'i and Shaikh Muhammad Juwaiyni"; "a good command of the Hindawi dialect of
  Rajasthan" (p. 145, folio 127). Teaching: "Heaven and hell are nothing but one's own actions", and
  *futuwwa* printed "JSutuwwa [OCR?] [futuwwa] (the qualities of the chivalrous)", "like a tree
  growing in a garden of friendship", against "muruwwa" (p. 148, folio 130); on music, "Hindawi music
  … was also subtle and elegant, penetrating deeply into the heart and arousing humility and
  gentleness" (p. 349, folio 327); he "made a free Hindi translation of one of Nizami's ghazals"
  (p. 350, folio 328) and wrote the *ʿIshqiyya* (p. 237, folio 217). Also "Shaikh Hamid" (p. 148,
  folio 130) and, damaged, "Shaikh Hamudu'd-Din [OCR?] [Hamidu'd-Din]" (p. 149, folio 131). Index
  headword "Hamidu'd-Din Suwali Nagauri, Shaikh (d. 1274), 6, 123, 127-30, 176, 202, 217, 327,
  328-29, 333" (p. 467, folio 443), cited to the PDF page it is printed on.
- **Tomb of Bibi Fatima Sam, old Indraprastha (Delhi)** — **STRONG CANDIDATE: the book's only full
  account of a woman's shrine and its only account of how a shrine is lost. Do not fold into
  `bibi-pak-daman`, whose principal figure is "The six Bibis" at Lahore; nothing here names Lahore or
  six Bibis.** "Bibi Fatima's tomb was in the old Indraprastha; it was a rendezvous for holy men
  until either the fourteenth or fifteenth century, but by the end of the sixteenth century it was
  deserted. The memory of Bibi Fatima fell into obscurity and she became known to the local people
  only as Bibi Sa'ima or Bibi Sham" (p. 427, folio 403); her teaching, "feeding the hungry and giving
  water to the thirsty was more meritorious than hundreds and thousands of namazes" (p. 426, folio
  402). **No dates, no order, no custodian — (not stated).**
- **Tomb of Khwaja ʿAbduʾllah Ansari at Gazirgah, near Herat** — **STRONG CANDIDATE: the one tomb in
  the book with a named royal rebuilder, a construction date and a named later phase of patronage.
  Do not fold into any archive row.** "His tomb in Gazirgah, about three miles north-east of Herat
  was first rebuilt by the Timurid ruler, Shah Rukh, (807/1405-850/1447) about 829/1425 and later
  was further embellished by other Timurid princes" (p. 93, folio 77); "born in the Herat citadel on
  2 Sha'ban 396/4 May 1006 and died in the same city on 22 Zu'l-hijja 481/8 March 1089"; "he became
  knownas Shaikhu'l-Islam (Leader of the Muslims). He was also known simply as the Pir, or spiritual
  director, of Herat"; after conversion "disputes with the 'ulama' resulted in several attempts on
  his life" (p. 93, folio 77); the tomb is a ziyarat stop (p. 138, folio 120); works the *Manazil
  al-Saʾirin*, the *Tabagat al-Sufiyya* [OCR?] [*Tabaqat*], the *Munajat* (p. 93, folio 77), a
  Persian *Tabaqat* from his lectures (pp. 78–79, folios 62–63) and a *Qalandar Nama* (p. 323, folio
  301). **The book shifts from "Ansari" to "Harawi" for the same book on one page (p. 93, folio 77).**
- **Khanqah and tomb of Shaikh ʿAlaʾuʾd-Dawla Simnani at Sufiyabad, near Simnan** — **STRONG
  CANDIDATE: a foundation year, a death date to the day and a burial inside the founder's own
  khanqah — the cleanest khanqah-to-shrine sequence in the book.** "In 1290 he founded a khangah
  [OCR?] [khanqah] at Sufiyabad, near Simnan" (p. 269, folio 249); "On 22 Rajab 736/6 March 1336,
  Simnani died and was buried in his khangah [khanqah], Sufiyabad-i Simnan" (p. 270, folio 250).
  "Born in Simnan in Zu'l-Hijja 659/November 1261"; father "the governor of Baghdad and Iraq under
  the Ilkhanid Arghun (1284-91)"; in Ilkhanid service at fifteen; "In 1284 a vision aroused in him a
  desire for further mystical experiences"; resigned after October 1286; Baghdad February–March
  1287, intercepted at Hamadan, taken to Sultaniyya "which was then being constructed", debated the
  bakhshis "and claimed himself to be the victor"; Baghdad again September 1289 (pp. 268–269, folios
  248–249); "a diehard opponent of Ibn al-'Arabi's doctrines" (p. 268, folio 248), arguing "that Ibn
  al-'Arabi identified Being with God" (p. 269, folio 249).
- **Tomb of Shaikh Saifuʾd-Din Bakharzi, Bukhara** — **STRONG CANDIDATE: it carries a named, repeated
  ʿurs, one of only two described ʿurs observances in the whole book.** "each 'urs, or death
  anniversary of Shaikh Saifu'd-Din Bakharzi was celebrated with great enthusiasm by them both"
  (p. 247, folio 227); "Shaikh Saifu'd-Din Bakharzi died in 658/1260" (p. 247, folio 227); disciple
  of Najmuʾd-Din Kubra, converted at a lecture (pp. 246–247, folios 226–227); he converted "Berke
  (1257-67), the Mongol Khan of the Golden Horde" at Bukhara, whom Nasiruʾd-Din Mahmud names "by both
  his pre-Islamic name, Khar Banda, and his Islamic one, Khuda-banda" (p. 247, folio 227); place
  footnote "In the tenth century, Bakharz also known as Guwakharz was a famous town south of Jam on
  the river Hari" (p. 246, folio 226).
- **The Kali Mandir at Srinagar, its demolition, and the khanqah built on its site** — **CANDIDATE:
  the only site in the book where one tradition visibly replaces another on a fixed spot. Do not
  fold into any of the archive's three Kali rows — none of them is at Srinagar and the book names
  none of them.** "the Brahman priest of Kali Mandir in Srinagar"; "However, Kali Mandir was
  demolished and subsequently the Saiyid's khangah [khanqah] was built there" (p. 314, folio 292);
  the Sultan "attended congregational prayers led by the Saiyid on a platform built at the site of
  the Kali Mandir, which he himself had helped to destroy" (p. 318, folio 296); Sultan Sikandar later
  "built an imposing khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] for the young migrant at the site where his father had
  first built the prayer platform" (p. 319, folio 297). Index headword "Kali Mandir, 292, 296"
  (p. 471, folio 447), **which does not say where the temple is**, cited to the PDF page it is
  printed on.
- **Shah Madar's shrine at Makanpur** — **CANDIDATE: a shrine that generated its own hagiography on
  site, plus a named order with a described living practice.** The *Mirʾat-i Madari* was "Written in
  1064/1654" near the shrine (p. 340, folio 318), its author claiming it "dictated in part by the
  saints' spirits" (pp. 335, 340; folios 313, 318). "Shah Madar's name was Shaikh Badi'u'd-Din";
  father "Abu Ishaq Shami"; the family "were Jews from Aleppo, the Shah's birthplace in 715/1315";
  he met Ashraf Simnani in Mecca and "At Medina, Shah Madarembraced [Shah Madar embraced] Islam";
  via Hurmuz, shipwreck and Gujarat to Ajmer, then Kalpi, Jaunpur and Makanpur (pp. 340–341, folios
  318–319); "He is said to have remarked that he spent thirty-five years of his life in Syria, forty
  in Mecca, Medina and Najaf, and fifty in India" (p. 341, folio 319). **No death date — (not
  stated).** The Madaris' practice is under section F. Index "Madari, 319-20", "Makanpur, 318-20"
  (p. 474, folio 450), cited to the PDF page they are printed on.
- **Tomb of Shaikh ʿAlaʾuʾd-Din ʿAli Ahmad Sabir, Kaliyar near Roorkee** — **CANDIDATE: a named,
  located tomb with a stated devotee following and a founder's role in the Chishti branch the
  archive's Sabiri rows descend from.** "the tomb of Shaikh Sabir in Kaliyar, near Roorkee in the
  Saharanpur district, of what is now western Uttar Pradesh, was quite famous, and he had many
  devotees"; "the founder of the Sabiri branch of the Chishti order"; "Early sources do not refer
  to" him (p. 172, folio 154). Index "Sabiriyya-Chishtiyyas, 13, 154", "Chishti-Sabiri order, 154,
  343" (pp. 483, 463; folios 459, 439), cited to the PDF pages they are printed on.
- **Tabrizabad (Devatalla / Deva Mahal) near Pandua, north Bengal** — **CANDIDATE: a place renamed
  after a saint that then "attracted a large number of pilgrims", with a demolished temple and a
  converted population.** "He moved to Devatalla (Deva Mahal) near Pandua in northern Bengal";
  "Devatalla came to be known as Tabrizabad and attracted a large number of pilgrims" (pp. 219–220,
  folios 201–202); "The Shaikh demolished the temple and constructed a takiya (khangah) [khanqah]
  and converted a large number of kafirs" (p. 219, folio 201); Jalaliyya tombs "in Devatalla"
  (p. 280, folio 260).
- **The two tombs at Pandua, Bengal** — **CANDIDATE: a named endowment flow and a stated pilgrimage
  status, though the occupants are not named in the sentence.** "the two tombs became a significant
  centre of pilgrimage in Bengal, receiving large endowments from affluent devotees" (p. 279, folio
  259); Ashraf Simnani's letter "refers to Bengal as the chief sufi centre in the Islamic world" and
  lists the Bengal tombs (p. 280, folio 260); Abu Tawwama's tomb is at "Sunargoan [Sunargaon]"
  (p. 280, folio 260).
- **Panch Pir collective mausoleums, the most famous at Sunargaon** — **CANDIDATE as a shrine type:
  a named category of collective shrine with a named exemplar and a five-member cult shared by
  Hindus and Muslims.** "Some Bengali traditions assign Pir Badr the fifth position in the group of
  legendary Panch Pir or five pirs. The four others were Ghazi Miyan (Salar Mas'ud), Zinda Ghazi,
  Shaikh Farid, and Khwaja Khizr, and all were worshipped by both Hindus and Muslims. There are
  several collective mausoleums of the Panch Pir, one of the most famous being in Sunargoan"
  (pp. 339–340, folios 317–318). **"Zinda Ghazi" and "Shaikh Farid" are bare names with no dates,
  seats or identifications — (not stated); on "Shaikh Farid" see the Misfiled section.** Index
  "Panch Pir, 317-18", "Panch Pandits, 318", "Panch Tathagatas, 318" (p. 480, folio 456), cited to
  the PDF page they are printed on.
- **Turbat-i Haidari, Sawa in Kuhistan** — **CANDIDATE: a town that took its name from a tomb, the
  book's only example of a toponym generated by a grave.** "a Turkic saint called Haidar. He lived
  in Sawa in Kuhistan (Mountain Land), a province about seventy miles south of Nishapur"; "In
  617/1220, the Mongols invaded Sawa"; "The town of Sawa where Haidar lived and was buried gradually
  became known as the TJurbat-i [OCR?] [Turbat-i] Haidari (the Tomb of Haidar)" (p. 329, folio 307).
  **No death date — (not stated).**
- **Tomb of Shaikh Ahmad-i Khattu Maghribi, Sarkhij, Gujarat** — **CANDIDATE: birth year, death date
  to the day, a documented khilafat with relics and a named mausoleum site.** "born in 738/1337-38"
  (p. 428, folio not stated); khalifa at twenty-five, given "the relics of his pirs" (p. 429, folio
  405); "After twelve years of hard ascetic exercises the Shaikh went on a hajj, returning to Delhi
  prior to the invasion of Timur" (p. 430, folio 406); he "died at a ripe old age during the latter's
  reign on 14 Shawwal 849/13 January 1446" and his mausoleum is at Sarkhij (p. 431, folio 407). He
  eclipsed Gisu Daraz at the Gujarat court (pp. 271–272, folios 251–252). A yogi's claim on the
  ground of Ahmadabad attaches to his circle: "a yogi named Bank Nath. According to him the site had
  previously been his property and therefore his name should be mentioned somewhere in the town"
  (p. 431, folio 407; index "Bank Nath Yogi, 407", p. 462, folio 438, cited to the PDF page it is
  printed on).
- **Tomb of Baba Ishaq Maghribi "Lal-Posh", Khattu near Nagaur** — **CANDIDATE: a death date, a fixed
  seat and a red-clothing epithet the archive will want beside Madho Lal Hussain's. Do not fold into
  `madho-lal-hussain` or any Lahore row; the book never brings this man to the Panjab.** "a
  spiritual descendant of Abu Madyan Shu'aib bin al-Husain (1126-98) and a friend of Shaikh Ahmad
  ar-Rifa'i (d. 1182). Abu Ishaq Maghribi was so enamoured with India that he decided to settle
  permanently at Khattu, near Nagaur, from where he occasionally visited Delhi, Meerut and even
  Khurasan. Due'to [OCR?] [Due to] his habit of wearing red clothes he became known by the name,
  'Lal- Posh' (wearer of red clothes)" (p. 428, folio not stated); "After Baba Ishaq's death in
  776/1374-75 at Khattu" (p. 429, folio 405).
- **The khanqah chain of Shaikh Abu Ishaq Kaziruni, from Kazirun to China** — **CANDIDATE as a
  network rather than a single site, and the book's single best documented shrine economy.** "The
  chain of khangahs [OCR?] [khanqahs] of Shaikh Abu Ishaq from Kazirun to China is one case in
  point" (p. 423, folio 399); "The veneration accorded to Abu Ishaq Kaziruni by mariners and sea
  travellers on routes between the Persian Gulf and India and China, gave rise to the establishment
  of khangahs [khanqahs] for Kaziruni sufis at several places along the way" (p. 433, folio not
  stated); he is "recognized as the protector of travellers and both merchants and rulers gave huge
  gifts to his Khangah [OCR?] [Khanqah]", and "There is not a ship that comes from China or from
  India but has thousands of dinars in it (vowed to the saint)" (p. 129, folio 111, footnote 1); he
  died "426/1035" (p. 129, folio 111). Named stations and shaikhs: **Shihabuʾd-Din Kaziruni** at
  Calicut, his son **Shaikh Fakhruʾd-Din** at Quilon (Kawlam), **Burhanuʾd-Din Kaziruni** at Zaytun
  (Ts'wan-chow-fu) in China (p. 434, folio 410). **His khalifa Safiuʾd-Din is the founder of Uch —
  see the Misfiled section.**
- **The Hindu temple near Adam's Peak, Ceylon** — **the best temple-economy passage in the book and a
  comparator the archive's Hindu-temple rows will want; Ceylon, so not a row.** "Ibn Battuta reports
  of a famous Hindu temple in a town nearby where he found three thousand Brahmans and yogis and
  five hundred Devadasis dancing before an idol. The town's entire revenue from taxes was spent on
  this temple" (p. 411, folio 387), footnoted "Banat al-Hinaud [OCR?] [Banat al-Hunud?], girls who
  dedicated themselves to singing and dancing before temple gods"; beside it, "There is a tax on the
  shrines of the gods; such is the practice established." (p. 411, folio 387). **The footprints of
  Adam** on the same page: "In the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries a number of sufis are known to
  have visited Ceylon to pay homage to the legendary foot-prints of Adam. According to Ibn Battuta,
  Adam was known in Ceylon as Baba (Father) and Eve as Mama (Mother)"; "Near the footprints were
  several caves associated with legendary Muslim holy men" (p. 411, folio 387; also p. 308, folio
  286).
- **Contested graves, all recorded and none resolved** — **Shaikh Sharafuʾd-Din Abu ʿAli Qalandar**,
  "After the Shaikh's death on 13 Ramazan 724/3 September 1324 both the people of Karnal and Panipat
  claimed his grave to be in their respective towns" (p. 327, folio 305); **Saiyid Shah
  Niʿmatuʾllah Shah of Kirman**, "the Saiyid died on 22 Rajab 834/5 April 1431 in Mahan near Kirman",
  but his qalandars "believed that their founder … was killed at Hansi. According to these
  qalandars, Shah Ni'matu'llah's tomb was in Hansi, not in Kirman, as was commonly believed."
  (p. 328, folio 306); **Kabir**, "After his death Kabir's body was claimed by both Muslims and
  Hindus, the former wishing to bury it and the latter to cremate it" and "Abu'l-Fazl refers to two
  different tombs of Kabir; one at Puri in Orissa and the other at Ratanpur in Awadh" (p. 399, folio
  375). **Do not fold Abu ʿAli Qalandar into `shamsabad`, `lal-shahbaz-qalandar` or
  `shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya`.**
- **Other named, located graves with a date** — Shaikh Samaʿuʾd-Din, "On 17 Jumada I 901/2 February
  1496 … buried on the embankment of the Hauz-i Shamsi in Delhi" (p. 308, folio 286); Saiyid
  Nuruʾd-Din Mubarak Ghaznawi, "died in 632/1234-35 and was buried near Shamsi Hauz" (p. 213, folio
  195); Shaikh Hasan Tahir, "On 24 Rabi' I 909/16 September 1503, the Shaikh died and was buried near
  Vijai Mandal", "one of the bastions built by Sultan Muhammad bin Tughlug [OCR?] [Tughluq], where
  many sufis had also lived" (pp. 285–286, folios 265–266); Shaikh Adhan Jaunpuri, "died in
  976/1568-69 and was buried in Jaunpur" (p. 283, folio 263); Khwaja Ikhtiyaruʾd-Din ʿUmar, "died at
  Iraj on 14 Muharram 809/1 July 1406" (pp. 294–295, folios 274–275); Burhanuʾd-Din Qutb-i ʿAlam,
  "died on 8 Zu'lhijja 857/10 December 1453 and was buried in Batwa near Ahmdabad [OCR?]
  [Ahmadabad]" (p. 305, folio 283); ʿIraqi, "died on 8 Zu'lqa'da 688/23 November 1289 and was buried
  near the tomb of Ibn al-'Arabi" at Damascus (p. 225, folio 205); Shaikh Muzaffar "at Aden in
  803/1400-01" (pp. 258–259, folios 238–239), with the mass grave "About two hundred of them were
  said to have been buried in the grave of Shaikh Muzaffar, according to a directive contained in
  his will" (p. 257, folio 237); Shaikh Hamza Makhdum, "dying in Srinagar on 24 Safar 984/23 May
  1576" (p. 321, folio 299); Shaikh Nuruʾd-Din Rishi, "died on 26 Ramazan 842/12 March 14339 [OCR?]
  [1439]", **the CE year damaged and not repaired**, with his cave "in the village of Kaimuh near
  Srinagar" (p. 372, folio 350).
- **Named graves without a date** — Shaikh Mina, "He died at Lucknow and is buried right in the
  centre of the old part of the town" (p. 293, folio 273); Shah Saidu, "buried in Fathpur Hanswa, a
  town between Kanpur and Allahabad" (p. 285, folio 265); Maulana Khwajgi, Maulana Ahmad Thaneswari
  and Shaikh Abuʾl-Fath ʿAlaʾi Qureshi, all three "buried at Kalpi" (pp. 293–294, folios 273–274);
  Shaikh Kamal alias Shaikh Kalu, who "died at Kara, about forty-one miles west of Allahabad"
  (p. 284, folio 264); Saiyid Husain Mashhadi, "killed by tribesmen and buried in Tulambli" (p. 140,
  folio 122) — **a place-name that occurs once in the book and that I have not identified**; Shaikh
  Najibuʾd-Din Mutwakkil, "buried outside the town near the Manda gateway" with "A structure … 
  erected on it by Sultan Muhammad bin Tughluq" (p. 170, folio 152); Shah Turkman Sahib, whose "tomb
  was located on the road to Firuzabad" and of whom ʿAbduʾl-Haqq "mentions only his name" (p. 214,
  folio 196); Shaikh Kamaluʾd-Din at Dhar, over whose grave "Sultan Mahmud Khalji of Malwa erected a
  tomb" (p. 167, folio 149); Shaikh ʿAlaʾuʾd-Din, grandson of Baba Farid, for whom "At several places
  in Ajodhan, Dipalpur and in the hills near Kashmir, people had erected memorials and mausoleums"
  and for whom "Muhammad bin Tughluq built a tomb … near his grandfather's mausoleum" (p. 167, folio
  149); Baba Ratan, who "returned to the Punjab and died in 700/1300 where he was buried" (p. 342,
  folio 320); Abuʾl-ʿAbbas al-Sayyari, "he died there in 342/953-54, for centuries his tomb at Marw
  was visited by devotees" (p. 55, folio 39); Abu Saʿid bin Abiʾl Khair at Mayhana and Abuʾl-Hasan
  Kharqani at Kharqan, both visited as tombs (p. 138, folio 120); Shaikh Nasiruʾd-Din Astarabadi at
  Astarabad (p. 138, folio 120); the Delhi lodging of Khwaja Muʿinuʾd-Din, "now marked by the grave
  of Shaikh Rashid Makki. Traces still remain of the arch of the Khwaja's mosque" (p. 140, folio
  122); and Shaikh Ahmad ʿAbduʾl-Haqq of Rudauli, who "died on 15 Jumada II 837/27 January 1434"
  with **no tomb named** and a three-generation succession at one seat — "Shaikh 'Arif, Shaikh
  Ahmad's son, was his spiritual successor … His son, Shaikh Muhammad, was pir to the great sixteenth
  century sufi, Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus Gangohi" (p. 292, folio 272), **the clearest sajjada-style
  descent in the book, though the book does not use that word.**
- **Named khanqahs and their economies** — the khanqah of Baba Saʿd Dust Dada at Baghdad, with "Later
  Dust Dada erected a bazaar of shops and a caravanserai in front of the khanqah's gateway" (p. 91,
  folio 75 corrected) on land granted by "The Caliph al-Qa'im (422/1031-467/1075)", later his own
  disciple (p. 91, folio 75 corrected), and whose "influence … was also fleeting" (p. 91, folio 75
  corrected) — **no death date, no tomb, no successor by name (not stated)**; the khanqah of Shaikh
  Shihabuʾd-Din Suhrawardi, "Al-Nasiralso builtanextensive khangah [OCR?] [Al-Nasir also built an
  extensive khanqah] for Shaikh Shihabu'd-Din and his family, which included additions such asa
  bath-house anda garden [such as a bath-house and a garden]" (p. 104, folio 88), whose rule is
  "According to Shaikh Shihabu'd-Din there were two sources of income for a khanqah—endowments and
  begging" (p. 109, folio 91); Abuʾn-Najib's, "At a ruined site on the bank of the Tigris,
  Abu'n-Najib built a khangah [OCR?] [khanqah]" (p. 102, folio 86); Najmuʾd-Din Kubra's at Khwarazm
  (pp. 111–112, folios 93–94); Ghazali's "own khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] at Tus" (p. 80, folio 64);
  Muʿinuʾd-Din Parwana's for ʿIraqi, "built 'Iraqi a khanqah at Tuqat which became an important
  centre for sufi musical gatherings" (p. 225, folio 205); Hasan al-Jawaliqi's "monastery of
  qalandars near Cairo" under "al-Malik al-'Adil Ketbogha (1294-96)" (p. 325, folio 303); Bulbul
  Shah's in Kashmir, where "Rinchana, who took Sadru'd-Din as his Muslim name, gave grants to Bulbul
  Shah for the establishment of a khangah [khanqah] and a /angar [OCR?] [langar]" (p. 312, folio
  290); Mir Shamsuʾd-Din ʿIraqi's "khanqah at Jadibal, a suburb of Srinagar" (p. 320, folio 298);
  the two ribats of al-Hanzali, "Al-Hanzali built two hospices (ribats) at Marw, one for
  traditionists and the other for jurisprudents" (p. 54, folio 38); and **the earliest dated khanqah
  foundation in the book, whose founder is a Christian** — "Before his death at the end of the
  eighth century, a khanqah (sufi monastry) was founded in Ramla, near Jerusalem, by a Christian
  dignitary" (p. 47, folio 31), because "there was no resting place for such dervishes so he built a
  khanqah for them" (p. 47, folio 31, footnote 2). **The Khanqah-i Muhammadi at Siwistan (Sehwan) is
  an archive location and is carried to the Misfiled section.**
- **Other named religious sites** — the **mosque of the Prophet Khizr at Quqa (Gogo), Kathiawar**,
  where "Ibn Battuta found a party of Haidari qalandars accompanied by their Shaikhs" under "a Hindu
  Raja called Dunqul" (p. 434, folio 410); the **Nawbahar at Balkh**, "the city's pride was the
  Buddhist cloister, the Nawbahar. This was raided by Arabs in 653 and conquered in 663-64" (p. 49,
  folio 33); **Rajgir (ancient Rajagriha)**, "the early home of the Buddha and the capital of ancient
  Magadha … The town abandoned by the Muslims was allowed to become a dense jungle" (p. 249, folio
  229, footnote 5), later assigned as a pargana to fund a khanqah (p. 250, folio 230), and a second
  Rajgir, "a lonely village on the Ganges" (p. 306, folio 284) — **the book does not say whether the
  two are the same place**; the **Anasagar Lake temple, Ajmer**, and its idol "Sawi Deva" (p. 135,
  folio 117); **Chisht**, "written as Khisht in the Persian geographical work, the Hudud al-'Alam,
  which was compiled in 372/982, is now a small village known as Khwaja Chisht on the river Hari Rud,
  some hundred kilometres east of Herat", where "Previously, the Buddhists had been displaced by
  Jews, the Jews by Manichaeans, and they in turn by Muslims" (p. 132, folio 114); **the minaret at
  Jam**, "Sultan Ghiyasu'd-Din Muhammad built his famous minaret, the proto-type of the famous Qutb
  Minar in Delhi" (p. 155, folio 137), and **the Qutb Minar** itself as a preaching site, "the
  Minar-i Shamsi (Qutb Minar) near the Jami' mosque, (the Qubbatu'l-Islam or Quwatu'l-Islam)"
  (p. 267, folio 247) — **a monument, not a shrine, and not `tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak`**; the
  **Hauz-i Shamsi**, "excavated, and the area became significant, not only as a source of water, but
  more importantly, as a cultural and religious centre, where the spiritual and intellectual élite of
  Delhi would gather" (p. 154, folio 136); **Khuldabad**, "ten saints buried at Khuldabad" (p. 29,
  folio 15; index "Khuldabad, 14", p. 472, folio 448, cited to the PDF page it is printed on);
  **Kichaucha**, Ashraf Simnani's seat "in the modern Faizabad district of U.P." (p. 287, folio 267;
  index "Kichaucha, 7, 267, 270", p. 473, folio 449) — **no tomb, no ʿurs (not stated)**; and
  **Burhanpur and Zainabad**, **two towns founded and named for a shaikh and his khalifa**, "Sultan
  Nasir Khan Faruqi (1399-1437), the ruler of the Faruqi dynasty of Khandesh (1382-1601) built a town
  which he called Burhanpur … and also built another town, Zainabad, named after Shaikh Zainu'd-Din"
  (pp. 201–202, folios 183–184), with **Sarangpur in Malwa**, which Shaikh Sarang "is said to have
  founded … naming it after himself", and whose own tomb drew a barefoot forty-mile walk (p. 293,
  folio 273).

### B. Saints and Sufis of the Indian subcontinent named without a site of their own

*None is the principal figure of an archive row; each was checked. Compact form: name — what the
book says — page references. Where a name looks like an archive figure and is not, the entry says so
and the case is repeated under Traps.*

- **Shaikh Nizamuʾd-Din Auliyaʾ of Delhi** — the most frequently named figure in the book. Born
  Bada'un, "Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din was born in 636/1238", real name Muhammad, father Ahmad dead when he
  was five, grandfather "Saiyid 'Ali … migrated from Bukhara to Bada'un" "Some time between 1202 and
  1209"; teachers Shadi Muqri, "Maulana 'Ala'u'd-Din Usuli", Shamsuʾl-Malik, "Maulana Kamalu'd-Din
  Zahid" (pp. 172, 173, 177; folios 154, 155, 159). **Death: the two chunks that carry it are both
  cut at a chunk boundary and neither completes the other** — "They both died in 1325, the Sultan in
  Sha'ban 725/" (p. 180, folio 162) and "July 1325 and the Shaikh on 18 Rabi' II 725/3 April, of the
  same year" (p. 181, folio 163), **the subject of "July 1325" unrecoverable**; four later chunks
  state that no death date, tomb or burial place appears in their range — (not stated) (pp. 198–214,
  215–232, 233–248, 420–427; folios 180–196, 197–212, 213–228, 396–403). He denied authorship of any
  book on "15 Muharram 710/20 September 1301"[OCR?] — **the Pass 1 worker records that this date
  pair cannot stand as printed** (p. 18, folio 4); the *Fawaʾiduʾl-Fuʾad* began "Early in 1308",
  prompted again "On 8 Shawwal 708/18 March 1309", covering "the fifteen years up to 722/1322-23"
  (p. 19, folio 5). "Before Zu'lhijja 724/November-December 1324, the Shaikh had appointed only
  three or four khalifas. In that month he decided to appoint more" (p. 181, folio 163). Character
  and teaching: "No one in the world is as sad and unhappy as I am" (p. 182, folio 164); "Although a
  celibate, Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din Auliya' loved children" (p. 186, folio 168); the first lesson,
  "whatever you do not like to be done to yourself, do not wish it to happen to others" (p. 183,
  folio 165); "the master of the sufi method of instruction by the use of anecdotes" with "inspired
  knowledge ('ilm-i ladunni)" (p. 182, folio 164); six conditions of concentration (p. 199, folio
  181); two pilgrimages better than one combined journey (p. 198, folio 180); the sajda quarrel and
  his defence of it (p. 239, folio 219); on women, "When the lion emerges from a forest, nobody asks
  about its sex" (p. 425, folio not stated); on money, "money belonging to the treasury should be
  spent on the Muslim community" (p. 244, folio 224), the doubled jamaʿat-khana expenditure (p. 242,
  folio 222) and the offer to leave Delhi (p. 245, folio 225); "Until the death of Shaikh
  Nizamu'd-Din Auliya', the Chishtis had also refused to play any rdle [OCR?] [rôle] in the
  conversion of Hindus to Islam" (p. 422, folio 398). Further mentions pp. 129, 134, 161–163,
  184–186, 191, 198–205, 217, 219, 223, 231–232, 234–245, 249, 261, 276, 329 (folios 111, 116,
  143–145, 166–168, 173, 180–187, 199, 201, 203, 211–212, 214–225, 229, 241, 256, 307). Damaged
  spellings on one page: "Shaikh Nizammu'd-Din Auliya' [OCR?]" and "Shaikh Nazamu'd-Din [OCR?]"
  (pp. 161–162, folios 143–144). Index headword "Nizamu'd-Din Auliya’(b. #238 [OCR?] [1238?], d.
  1325), 4-7, 9, 14, 110, 117, 143, 148-50. 152-72, 189, …, 385, 398, 402" (p. 479, folio 455),
  cited to the PDF page it is printed on.
- **Khwaja Muʿinuʾd-Din Chishti (Muʿinuʾd-Din Sijzi)** — founder of the Chishtiyya in India; shrine
  material in section A. **Three birth/death statements, all kept:** "As he died on 6 Rajab 633
  Hijri at the age of ninety-seven, he must have been born in 536/1141-42. Abu'l Fazl says he was
  born in 537/1142-43" (p. 137, folio 119, footnote 1); "On 6 Rajab 633/16 March 1236, Khwaja
  Mu'inu'd-Din died in Ajmer at the old age of ninety-seven" (p. 143, folio 125); **and a third year
  quoted from a modern author and not corrected, "After the death of the Khwaja in 1234" (p. 136,
  folio 118).** Born "in Sijistan (Sistan) and brought up in Khurasan"; father "Khwaja Ghiyasu'd-Din
  Hasan … a most pious and God-fearing rian [OCR?] [man]", dead when he was fifteen; he lived "on the
  earnings from a garden and a water-mill"; Samarqand and Bukhara, then Harwan under ʿUsman Harwani
  "for about two and a half years" (p. 137, folio 119). Two wives, "a daughter of a local Hindu
  chieftain who had been seized in war", three sons "Shaikh Abu Sa'id, Shaikh Fakhru'd-Din and
  Shaikh Husamu'd-Din", the maternity of the last two disputed (p. 142, folio 124). Also pp. 18, 26,
  27, 130, 133, 149, 152–158, 200, 207, 237, 295, 334, 336, 340 (folios 4, 12, 13, 112, 115, 131,
  134–140, 182, 189, 217, 275, 312, 314, 318). Index headword at p. 477 (folio 453), cited to the
  PDF page it is printed on.
- **Shaikh Nasiruʾd-Din Mahmud Chiragh-i Dihli** — tomb and life in section A. Also: "(d. 1356)", the
  *Khairuʾl-Majalis* of Hamid Qalandar begun after 754/1353-54, "consisting of one hundred chapters
  and an appendix", an earlier draft by the Shaikh's nephew rejected (p. 20, folio 6); he
  "categorically refuted that any of his predecessors had written sufi texts" (p. 18, folio 4);
  heads the third tabaqa (p. 26, folio 12); "played a prominent role in the accession of Firuz
  Shah" and inspired "the army which marched from Thatta to Delhi" (p. 197, folio 179); defined "a
  khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] as a place of worship and prayer" (p. 236, folio 216); on prostration,
  "placing the forehead on the ground before created beings was not permissible, but one could kiss
  the ground with the lips" (p. 240, folio 220); "rulers were appointed to people as they themselves
  deserved" (p. 244, folio 224); "Amir Khusraw and Amir Hasan tried their best to imitate Sa'di's
  style, but did not succeed" (p. 191, folio 173); on Jamaluʾd-Din of Sawa, "a mu/fti [OCR?] [mufti]
  and a travelling library" (pp. 324–325, folios 302–303). **Three forms of the name in one chunk
  (pp. 234, 238, 247; folios 214, 218, 227), and a bare "Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din" at pp. 216, 223
  (folios 198, 203) whose identity with him the book does not state.** Index headword at p. 478
  (folio 454), cited to the PDF page it is printed on.
- **Shaikh Fariduʾd-Din Mahmud of Nagaur and his line** — grandson and successor of Hamiduʾd-Din
  Nagauri, "as the Shaikh's own son, 'Azizu'd-Din, had died during his father's lifetime"; Muhammad
  bin Tughluq "gave Shaikh Faridu'd-Din an honoured place in his court" (p. 148, folio 130); the
  *Sururuʾs-Sudur* is his (p. 20, folio 6). **The book calls him "Shaikh Farid" — a direct collision
  with Baba Farid; see Traps.** "Sometime after 1327-28 the Sultan married his daughter, Bibi Rasti,
  to Shaikh Faridu'd-Din's grandson, Shaikh Fathu'llah bin Shaikh Auhadu'd-Din", who settled "at
  Deh, not far from Nagaur" (pp. 148–149, folios 130–131).
- **Khwaja Husain Nagauri** — "a descendant of Shaikh Hamid, and a disciple of one of Shaikh Farid's
  sons, Shaikh Kabir"; credited with "the re-establishment of Nagaur as a strong centre of sufism";
  "lived like the local cultivators, driving a bullock cart"; "He lived both at Ajmer and Nagaur";
  wrote the *Nuruʾn-Nabi* and a biography of Ahmad al-Ghazali (p. 149, folio 131); built Muʿinuʾd-Din's
  tomb (p. 143, folio 125) and a gateway at Hamiduʾd-Din's (p. 148, folio 130); patronised by Malwa
  though he refused to move to Mandu, and he "confirmed that the latter was a descendant of Khwaja
  Mu'inu'd-Din" (pp. 295–296, folios 275–276). **No dates, no tomb — (not stated).**
- **Khwaja Ziyaʾ Nakhshabi** — "Among the disciples of Shaikh Farid, the most notable"; family "from
  Nakhshab in the Sughd province"; "A born raconteur" (p. 149, folio 131); "Nakhshabi died in
  751/1350-51" (p. 151, folio 133). Works: *Silkuʾs-Suluk* in "151 short chapters"; "*Ashra-i
  Mubasshshara [OCR?] ['Ashra-i Mubashshara] and Kulliyyat-wa Juziyyat"; the *Tuti Nama*; "the
  Lazzatu'n-Nisa' (Sex Enjoyments)" (pp. 149–151, folios 131–133); his Persian *Tuti Nama* a model
  for later Hindi masnawis (p. 385, folio 363).
- **Qazi Hamiduʾd-Din Nagauri (Shaikh Muhammad ʿAta), the Suhrawardi** — "Probably the most learned
  khalifa of Shaikh Shihabu'd-Din Suhrawardi … He is not to be confused, however, with the Chishti
  Hamidu'd-Din Suwali"; father "'Ata'u'llah Mahmud"; the family "migrated from Bukhara to Delhi
  sometime before 1200"; Qazi of Nagaur three years; Baghdad, Medina "for just over a year", Mecca
  "for three years", Delhi (p. 214, folio 196); "although a Suhrawardi [he] was deeply interested in
  the practice of sama'" (p. 153, folio 135); he executed Qutbuʾd-Din's will and handed the relics to
  Baba Farid (p. 158, folio 140); his works are the lost **Lawaʾih**, the **'Jshgiyya [OCR?]
  ['Ishqiyya]**, **Tawaliʿ al-Shumus** and **Risala Min Kalam**, with "Love is the source of
  everything that exists" and "The greatest name of God is Huwa (He)" (p. 215, folio 197); "Qazi
  Hamidu'd-Din Nagauri did not generally enrol disciples, and only four were named" (p. 216, folio
  198); his son **Maulana Nasihuʾd-Din** "lived in Delhi and also enrolled disciples" (p. 217, folio
  199); his works "aroused the interest of all Chishti pirs" (p. 236, folio 216). **No death date,
  no tomb — (not stated). Distinct from "Qazi Hamidu'd-Din Maliku't-Tujjar (head of the merchant
  guilds)" (p. 242, folio 222).** His four artisan disciples: **Shaikh Ahmad Naharwani**, "Onewasa
  weaver [OCR?] [One was a weaver]", later "an eminent sufi with a large number of followers";
  **Shaikh ʿAinuʾd-Din**, "was a butcher" (p. 216, folio 198); **Shaikh Shahi Muy-tab (Hair-Rope
  Maker)** of Badaʾun, who refused at first to pray for Nizamuʾd-Din, "He excused himself by saying
  he was merely an artisan" (pp. 216–217, folios 198–199); **Khwaja Mahmud Muyina-Duz (Tailor of
  Fur)**, buried near Qutbuʾd-Din's tomb (p. 217, folio 199).
- **Shaikh Jalaluʾd-Din Tabrizi** — the book's most tangled identity, and on the settled reading the
  principal figure of no archive row. "The second most outstanding disciple of Shaikh Shihabu'd-Din
  Suhrawardi, who became famous in Bengal"; studied "at several places, including Bukhara"; served
  his pir seven years with a portable stove (p. 217, folio 199); "did not remain long in Multan and
  travelled to Delhi, via Ajodhan" (p. 218, folio 200); the haunted house **Baituʾl-Jinn**, the
  slave-boy episode and the dancing girl **Gawhar**, hired "for a sum of 500 dinars", whose false
  charge collapsed (pp. 218–219, folios 200–201); Badaʾun, the Qazi Kamaluʾd-Din dream, the
  conversion of the Katihar curd seller **ʿAli Maula**, who "possessed one hundred thousand jitals"
  (p. 219, folio 201); Lakhnauti and Devatalla (p. 219, folio 201). **"The date of Shaikh
  Jalalu'd-Din Tabrizi's death is unknown, and no reliable information of his Bengali khalifas is
  available" (p. 220, folio 202).** Also pp. 146, 157, 208, 210, 237, 246, 277, 280, 337 (folios 128,
  139, 190, 192, 217, 226, 257, 260, 315). **Two authors are said to have confused him with Shah
  Jalal of Sylhet (pp. 220, 337; folios 202, 315). Do not map him to either Uch row: nisba Tabrizi,
  region Bengal, no dates, no tomb.**
- **Shah Jalal of Sylhet ("Shah Jalal Mujarrad", the Bachelor)** — "a Turkistani by birth and a
  khalifa of Saiyid Ahmad Yasawi of the Silsila-i Khwajgan"; "seven hundred (a mythical figure) most
  eminent disciples"; at "Sirhat (Sylhet)" 313 followers against "Raja Gaur Govind, heading one
  hundred thousand footmen"; he "apportioned the land among his followers, thus enabling them to
  take wives and settle down" but did not marry (pp. 336–337, folios 314–315); Rizvi's
  reconstruction has him at Multan and Uch, "At Uch it is possible that he was formally initiated
  into the Suhrawardiyya order as is suggested by local legends"; "The Shaikh was reported to have
  died in 748/1347, therefore he would have been born in 598/1201" (p. 337, folio 315).
- **The Bengal Chishti line** — **Shaikh Akhi Sirajuʾd-Din ʿUsman**, "a semi-literate khalifa" who
  "laid a firm foundation of the Chishti order in Bengal"; "Lakhnauti was his home town"; "Some
  sheets of paper and a few elementary books were the only possessions of the young Akhi"; "In 1327,
  Shaikh Akhi left Delhi for Lakhnauti, taking with him some books from the great Shaikh's library";
  he buried some of Nizamuʾd-Din's clothes and "requested that he be buried next to the clothing"
  (pp. 202, 277; folios 184, 257) — **no death date of his own (not stated)**. **Shaikh ʿAlaʾuʾl-Haqq
  bin Asʿad Lahori**, "an eminent scholar" whose relations "were prominent in the government of
  Ilyas Shah", who "would carry a cauldron of hot food on his head even though it would burn his
  hair", and who "died on 1 Rajab 800/20 March 1398" (pp. 277–278, folios 257–258) — **the nisba
  "Lahori" is carried to the Misfiled section.** **Shaikh Nuruʾl-Haqq, "Shaikh Nur Qutb-i ʿAlam"**,
  who "For eight years … brought fuel to the khangah [khanqah]", "A staunch believer in the Wahdat
  al-Wujud", who urged Ibrahim Shah Sharqi to invade Bengal (pp. 278–279, folios 258–259) — **his
  death is given twice and incompatibly and both stand: "According to the Akhbaru'l-Akhyar, Nur
  Qutb-i 'Alam died in 813/1410-11, but the Mir'atu'l-Asrar states that the Shaikh died on 10
  Zu'lqada 818/11 January 1416" (p. 279, folio 259)**; also pp. 431, 379–380 (folios 407, 357–358).
  **Shaikh Anwar**, banished to Sunargaon and "tortured … to death in order to discover where his
  ancestral treasury was hidden"; **Rafgatu'd-Din [OCR?] [Rafqatu'd-Din]** and his son **Zahid**,
  recalled to Pandua (p. 280, folio 260). **Shaikh Husain Dhukarposh (Dust-Ridden) of Purnea**, the
  most prominent of those who "established khangahs [khanqahs] throughout Bengal" (p. 280, folio
  260). **Shaikh Sharafuʾd-Din Abu Tawwama**, from Bukhara, who left Delhi "because of Sultan
  Balban's jealousy" (Rizvi prefers envy of "the local 'ulama'"), made Sunargaon "a centre of Islamic
  learning in Bengal", and whose tomb is at Sunargaon (pp. 248–249, 280; folios 228–229, 260).
- **The Manikpur line** — **Maulana Jalaluʾd-Din of Manikpur**, who rose when "the other khangah
  [OCR?] [khanqah] inmates retired", recited "the Ya Sin chapter of the Qur'an forty-one times" and
  "survived by transcribing the Qur'an and the completed copies were sold in Delhi", refusing for
  forty years to eat in a Qazi's house (pp. 283–284, folios 263–264) — **no death date (not
  stated)**; his son **Maulana Khwaja**, who "would fast for three days" (p. 284, folio 264); his
  grandson **Shaikh Husamuʾd-Din Manikpuri**, trained at Pandua carrying "from the forest wood for
  fuel", "In 804/1401-02 … appointed a khalifa by Shaikh Nur Qutb-i 'Alam", his malfuzat the
  *Rafiquʾl-ʿArifin* compiled by Farid bin Salar, "The Shaikh died in 853/1449-S0 [OCR?] [1449-50]"
  (p. 284, folio 264; also p. 24, folio 10) — **the CE half of the death date is damaged and is
  flagged, not repaired**; and **Raji Hamid Shah**, whose "ancestors were Saiyids from Gardiz" and
  who "retired to Manikpur where he died. The date of his death is not authoritatively known"
  (p. 285, folio 265), with his son **Raji Saiyid Nur**, who "combined a profession in the army with
  mysticism" (p. 285, folio 265).
- **Mir Saiyid Ashraf Jahangir Simnani** — "born at the end of the thirteenth century and left Simnan
  at about the age of twenty-three"; studied at Kashan under "Shaikh 'Abdu'r-Razzaq Kamalu'd-Din bin
  'Abdu'l-Ghana'im al-Kashani", leaving "some time before Shaikh 'Abdu'r-Razzaq Kashani's death in
  730/1329"; travelled with Mir Saiyid ʿAli Hamadani, spent time "with Khwaja Baha'u'd-Din
  Naqshband", passed through Multan and Uch, was rebuffed at Delhi by Gisu Daraz, reached "Munyar on
  2 January 1381" and "led the pre-burial prayers of Shaikh Sharafu'd-Din Yahya Munyari", was
  "initiated as a Chishti by Shaikh 'Ala'u'l-Haqq" at Pandua, "his last initiation as he had already
  become a member of every existing sufi order", and settled at Kichaucha (pp. 286–287, folios
  266–267); books for novices and specialists listed at p. 288 (folio 268); his malfuzat, the
  *Lataʾif-i Ashrafi*, by "Nizamu'd-Din Yamani", "an introduction and sixty chapters, called Latifas
  (Elegant Sayings)", plus "Malfuzat-i Ashrafi, by Nizam Haijji"[OCR?] (p. 21, folio 7); also
  pp. 279, 280, 340 (folios 259, 260, 318). **A different man from ʿAlaʾuʾd-Dawla Simnani, and the
  book says so (p. 279, folio 259).**
- **Shaikh ʿAbduʾl-Quddus Gangohi** — the dominant figure of chunks 021–022. "came from Rudauli and
  was born about ad 1456. His father Shaikh Isma'il was an 'a/im [OCR?] ['alim]"; Chishti-Sabiri,
  initiated from Shaikh Muhammad and "directly … from the spirit of Shaikh Ahmad 'Abdu'l-Haqq"; "used
  Alakh as his Hindi nom de plume"; wrote the *Anwaruʾl-ʿUyun* and the *Rushd-Nama* at Rudauli;
  "In c. 1491 … migrated to Shahabad, in Ambala near Delhi" (p. 365, folio 343); invited by "ʿUmar
  Khan Sarwani, the vizier of Sultan Sikandar", a move Rizvi says "was precipitated more by expedient
  than pious motives"; "visited Ajodhan and Multan" and "became friendly with Sultan Sikandar Lodi"
  (p. 366, folio 344); settled at Gangoh after Babur's 1523-24 campaign, captured after "Ibrahim
  Lodi's defeat at Panipat on 20 April 1526", "released by Babur", then Gangoh "for the rest of his
  life" (p. 367, folio 345); "On 23 Jumada II 944/27 November 1537 the Shaikh died" (p. 368, folio
  346) — **no tomb named (not stated)**; "who, during his lifetime as today, enjoys great respect
  from all classes of Muslims" (p. 376, folio 354). Doctrine: he identifies "sufi beliefs based on
  the Wahdat al-Wujud with the philosophy and practices of Gorakhnath" (p. 358, folio 336), "finds
  the teachings of the Naths identical to the Wahdat al-Wujud" (p. 361, folio 339), "also uses the
  name Alakh Niranjan in the same sense" (p. 359, folio 337), "expects sufis to absorb themselves in
  Onkar through zikr" (p. 359, folio 337), teaches sahaja as "ontological immortality or the sufi
  baga [OCR?] [baqa]" and meditation "on the True Name or Ism-i A'zam" (p. 360, folio 338), turns
  *Ahmad*/*Ahad* on "the intervening mim (M)" (p. 361, folio 339), and "warned the 'ulama' that they
  were selling knowledge in return for a living" (p. 360, folio 338). He "lectured on the Chanda'in
  with the same intensity as when he lectured on the works of Ibn al-*Arabi [OCR?] [Ibn al-ʿArabi],
  Fakhru'd-Din 'Iraqi and Sa'di" (p. 387, folio 365) and his Persian translation of it "was not
  available even to his son" (p. 386, folio 364); he protested "the imposition of 'ushr on Muslim
  rent-free grants" (p. 410, folio 386). Printed once "Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddas" (p. 362, folio 340);
  also pp. 21, 185, 357, 420 (folios 7, 167, 335, 396); "The Bibliography lists five of his works"
  (p. 437, folio not stated). His son **Shaikh Ruknuʾd-Din**, born "On 5 Jumada I 897/5 March 1492",
  author of the *Lataʾif-i Quddusi* "commenced a month before his father's death and completed after
  it", in which "Humayun was accused of not making a distinction between the kufr and Islam", and of
  a commentary on the *Rushd-Nama* whose "most difficult problem was to justify the contents of his
  father's above work"; "His successor was Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din who died in AD 1575-76" (pp. 365,
  368–369; folios 343, 346–347), cited as "Lata 'if-i Quddusi, Delhi, 1311;1894" — **the semicolon in
  the AH/CE pair is as printed. He is not the Multan Ruknuʾd-Din; see Traps.** His khalifa **Shaikh
  Jalal Thaneswari**, "who died in 1581-82" (pp. 368, 370, 387, 420; folios 346, 348, 365, 396).
- **Kabir** — the largest subject of chunks 023–024 and the principal figure of no archive row
  (confirmed against the index by the chunk 027 worker). "The only reliable facts about his life are
  that he lived in Banaras about the fifteenth century and was a weaver"; "Of various dates for his
  birth 1425 is the most acceptable" (p. 398, folio 374); "Of the many dates given for Kabir's death
  1505 is the most reliable" (p. 399, folio 375); **the *Khazinatuʾl-Asfiyaʾ* date "Kabir died in
  1003/1594-95" is reported and rejected** (p. 398, folio 374); "Kabir Muwahhid lived during the
  reign of Sikandar Lodi" (p. 397, folio 373). Adoptive parents "'Ali, popularly known as Niru" and
  "his wife Nima"; "The local qazi gave the child the name Kabir" (p. 398, folio 374); his guru
  disputed between "a pir called Pitambar", "the Hindu god, Rama" and "Shaikh Taqi" (p. 398, folio
  374), and "Although Kabir's earthly guru is unknown" (p. 404, folio 380). Verse: "The Hindi verses
  called sakhis, dohas and doctrinal poems, jointly known as Ramai'ni"; the collections "the Adi
  Granth, the Kabir Granthawali and the Bijak (Treasury)" and "The earliest authentic collection of
  his hymns and s/okas [OCR?] [slokas] was compiled in the Granth Sahib" (pp. 398–399, folios
  374–375); his list of names for Reality at p. 401 (folio 377). "Being a member of a lowly caste of
  weavers was a source of great pride to Kabir" (p. 405, folio 381); "K: bir [OCR?] [Kabir] lived
  far from the Lodi capital" in a Banaras "plagued with civil war and political struggle" (p. 406,
  folio 382); the Banaras complaint by **Shaikh Taqi**, the Brahmans, and the conflicting fatwas of
  **Qazi Piyara and Shaikh Budh** (p. 406, folio 382); **Shaikh Ruzquʾllah Mushtaqi**'s father
  **Shaikh Saʿduʾllah (d. 928/1522)** judged him "a muwahhid" (p. 397, folio 373). Tombs in section
  A. Index headword "Kabir (b. 1425, d. 1505), 373-84, 393-94" (p. 471, folio 447), cited to the PDF
  page it is printed on. **Two Shaikh Taqis are distinguished at p. 398 (folio 374), one of Kara
  Manikpur "a disciple of Shaikh Salim Chishti (1479-1572)" who "died in 984/1576-772 [OCR?]
  [984/1576-77, the 2 being a fused footnote marker]", and one at Jhusi "although nothing else is
  known of him".**
- **Lal Ded / Lalla** — "A Kashmiri Shaivite, popularly known as Lal Ded, Lal Didi, and Maʾi Lal
  Diddi … also known by her Sanskritized names, Lalla Yogishwari or Laleshwari. Her family were
  Brahmans from Pompur and she appears to have been born sometime in the middle of the fourteenth
  century"; married young, "spurning family life she became a Shaivite yogini"; her sayings are "The
  Lalla-Vakyani", which "had a strong effect on local sufis"; "According to legend she met Mir Saiyid
  ʿAli Hamadani on several occasions" (p. 371, folio 349); she influenced Nuruʾd-Din Rishi per
  Mishkati's *Asraruʾl-Abrar* of 1065/1654-55 (p. 28, folio 14). **No death date, no tomb — (not
  stated).** Index "Lalla, 14, 349-50" (p. 472, folio 448), cited to the PDF page it is printed on.
- **The Kashmir cast** — **Shaikh Nuruʾd-Din Rishi** (section A), whose disciples **Bamuʾd-Din,
  Zainuʾd-Din and Latifuʾd-Din "were Brahmans by birth"**, "Shaikh Zainuʾd-Din invented a distinctive
  dress for Rishis", with **Nasruʾd-Din and Qiyamuʾd-Din** (p. 373, folio 351), and who "and his
  disciples preferred to call themselves Rishis not sufis" (p. 373, folio 351). **Baba Dawud Khaki**,
  who "Before his death in 994/1586 … completed a short biography, entitled Wirdu'l-Muridin, written
  in metre, of his pir, Shaikh Hamza Kashmiri (d. 1576). The work in the main is highly eulogistic
  and fails to give many biographical details" (p. 25, folio 11; also p. 321, folio 299, with its
  commentary the *Dasturuʾs-Salikin*). **Baba Nasib Kashmiri (d. 1047/1637)**, who "In 1045/1635 …
  wrote a Rishi Nama" and "is strongly hostile to Shi'is", and **Bahaʾuʾd-Din Baha Mattu**, whose
  Rishi Nama "was completed in 1248/1832" (p. 26, folio 12). **Shaikh Hamza Makhdum** (section A).
  **Shaikh Yaʿqub Sarfi**, "Another outstanding Kashmiri sufi of the seventeenth century … the
  grammarian" — **the book's own chronology is inconsistent, since it then dates his death to 1595**
  — initiated "at Samarqand by Makhdum Kamalu'd-Din Shaikh Husain of Khwarazam", friend of "Shaikh
  Salim Chishti", "a respected figure at the courts of Humayun and Akbar", his Qurʾan commentary "has
  not survived", "A frequent visitor to the Mughal court in Lahore, in 1594 he finally retired to
  Srinagar, where he died on 8 Zu'lqa'da, 1003/15 July 1595" (pp. 321–322, folios 299–300). **Mir
  Saiyid ʿAli Hamadani**, "born … in Hamadan, on 12 Rajab 714/22 October 1314", initiated by "Shaikh
  Sharafu'd-Din Mahmud Nizamu'd-Din Muzdaqani", further trained by three named shaikhs, who "reached
  Srinagar in 783/1381" and then "left Srinagar a frustrated man … died on 6 Zu'l-hijja 786/19
  January 1385 after having passed through Pakhli, near Kunar. His body was taken to Khuttalan, now
  part of the U.S.S.R., where it was buried" (pp. 313–314, folios 291–292); "The authorship of 170
  books have been attributed to Saiyid 'Ali and undoubtedly this is an exaggeration … about fifty
  survived; his most famous contribution … the Zakhiratu'l-Muluk"; he "translated the Fusus al-Hikam
  into Persian" (p. 315, folio 293); his *Risala-i Futuwwa* holds that "all stages of the mystic
  journey depended on Futuwwa" and that "'Ali bin Abi Talib … had been made custodian of the
  knowledge of the Futuwwa by the Prophet Muhammad", with four pillars, "forgiveness when one could
  inflict revenge, patience at the time of anger, wishing an enemy well and a preference for the
  needs of others rather than one's own" (pp. 315–316, folios 293–294); "Unlike the Chishtis, Mir
  Saiyid 'Ali Hamadani was imbued with the searing missionary zeal of 'Ala'u'd-Dawla Simnani"
  (p. 314, folio 292); his biographer **Jaʿfar Badakhshi**'s *Khulasatuʾl-Managib* [OCR?] [Manaqib]
  was "started in 787/1385, however, the date of its completion is unknown" and "does not refer to
  the Mir's activities in Kashmir" (p. 24, folio 10). **Mir Muhammad**, his son, "Born in 774/1372 at
  Khuttalan", who came in 796/1393, for whom Sultan Sikandar "built an imposing khangah [OCR?]
  [khanqah]", who had "accepted from the veteran sufi, a khilafat-nama", and who "after a stay of
  twelve years, like his father he too decided to leave" (pp. 318–319, folios 296–297), with his
  convert **Suha Bhatta / Saifuʾd-Din** (p. 319, folio 297). **Saiyid Hisari**, who "in about 1390 …
  migrated to Kashmir from Hisar", "a very liberal mystic who became a favourite of Sultan Sikandar
  (1389-1413) and settled in Srinagar, where he remained for the rest of his life" (pp. 318–319,
  folios 296–297) — **no death date, no tomb (not stated)**. **Mir Shamsuʾd-Din ʿIraqi**, who
  introduced Shiʿism "in 1481 … as an envoy of the Timurid Sultan, Husain Mirza (1469-1506)",
  disciple of "Shaikh Isma'il Kubrawiyya", who worked "under the name of a prominent Irani sufi, Nur
  Bakhsh", re-entered "In 1501", "began initiating sufis into the order of Saiyid Muhammad Nur
  Bakhsh, although in reality he made them Isna 'Ashari Shi'is", and left for "Skardu in Ladakh"
  (pp. 320–321, folios 298–299) — **on the "Shamsuʾd-Din" collision see Traps**. **Bulbul Shah**
  (section A). **The Bayhaqi Saiyids**, "descendants of Taju'd-Din, a Saiyid sufi from Bayhaq",
  patronised by "Sultan Zainu'l-'Abidin (1420-70)", "rightly remembered by the Kashmiris as Bud Shah,
  the Great King" (p. 320, folio 298). **Saiyid Tajuʾd-Din, Saiyid Husain, Saiyid Ahmad of Kirman,
  Saiyid Muhammad Isfahani, Saiyid Jamaluʾd-Din and Shaikh Hamza**, the migrants and the one
  Kashmiri disciple named (pp. 312–313, folios 290–291).
- **Shaikh Safiuʾd-Din Kaziruni, founder of Uch** — "The first sufi to settle in the region … a
  native of Kazirun near Shiraz, in Iran. His uncle was Shaikh Abu Ishaq Kaziruni, who died in
  426/1035. After appointing Safiu'd-Din his khalifa, according to tradition, Shaikh Abu Ishaq
  ordered him to mount a camel and travel in whatever direction the animal led him; he was then to
  remain where the camel finally halted. Although this happened to be in the middle of a desert, the
  Shaikh founded a town later called Uch. Although this story would appear mythical, it is typical
  of medieval tales relating to sufis"; "No account of Shaikh Safiu'd-Din's activities at Uch
  remains" (p. 129, folio 111); his yogi encounter is back-referenced at p. 348 (folio 326). **The
  man has no archive row, but the town he is said to have founded carries five; the sentence is
  carried to the Misfiled section and is the most consequential unmapped line in this part of the
  book.**
- **The Uch Suhrawardi line** — **Saiyid Raju Qattal (Saiyid Sadruʾd-Din)**, younger brother and
  successor of Makhdum-i Jahaniyan, nicknamed "Raju, and Qattal (Slayer), for his militant
  evangelism", whose pir was his own father Saiyid Ahmad Kabir, and of whom Makhdum Jahaniyan said
  God "had chosen for Raju the life of a recluse", a judgement that "belies his militant
  evangelism"; **"His whole life was spent at Uch and it was also where he died"** (pp. 301, 303;
  folios 279, 281) — **no tomb, no date (not stated)**; his descendants' branch "the
  Qalandariyya-Suhrawardiyya Murtazawi, settled in eastern U.P." (p. 328, folio 306). **Shaikh
  Kabiruʾd-Din Ismaʿil**, "the most prominent" of his disciples, who "studied the 'Awarifu'l-Ma'arif
  under him. Like his pir Shaikh Kabiru'd-Din spent his whole life at Uch" (pp. 304, 307; folios 282,
  285). **Saiyid Mahmud Nasiruʾd-Din**, who "lived in Uch", "had a very large family and is believed
  to have died in 847/1443-44"; **Saiyid Hamid Kabir**, "known for his kindly disposition"; and
  **Saiyid Ruknuʾd-Din Abuʾl-Fath**, "an equally great sufi" (p. 304, folio 282) — **his name
  collides with `shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam`'s principal figure and he is a different man; see
  Traps.** **Saiyid Ahmad Kabir**, son of Saiyid Jalaluʾd-Din Surkh and pir of both brothers
  (pp. 299, 303; folios 277, 281) — **his father is an archive principal figure and is carried to
  the Misfiled section.**
- **The Multan Suhrawardi circle** — **Shaikh Sadruʾd-Din ʿArif**, son and khalifa of Bahaʾuʾd-Din
  Zakariyya, whose malfuzat the **Kunuzuʾl-Fawaʾid** compiled by Khwaja Ziyaʾuʾd-Din are "no longer
  extant", who taught that "No breath should be inhaled or exhaled without zikr", and who "died on
  23 Zu'lhijja 684/19 February 1286" (pp. 220, 223; folios 202, 203) — **no tomb (not stated), and
  the book does not distinguish him by any further name from Shaikh Sadruʾd-Din of Quniya (pp. 225,
  290; folios 205, 270).** **Shaikh Fakhruʾd-Din Ibrahim ʿIraqi** (tomb in section A), "from the
  neighbourhood of Hamadan", who "built a beautiful madrasa in Hamadan", followed a qalandar party
  "with shaved beard and eyebrows", took the khirqa and married at Multan, then Mecca, Quniya, where
  he heard "Shaikh Sadru'd-Din on the Fusus al-Hikam" and composed "the Lama'at (Flashes or
  Effulgences)", then Tuqat, Egypt, Syria and Damascus (pp. 224–225, folios 204–205); his son
  **Kabiruʾd-Din** "who had come from Multan, joined his father" (p. 225, folio 205); his **Diwan**,
  **'Ushshag-Nama [OCR?] ['Ushshaq-Nama]** and *Lamaʿat*, on which Jami wrote the **Ashiʿatuʾl-Lamaʿat**
  (p. 225, folio 205); "even after his initiation by Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya he continued to
  live as a wandering dervish" (p. 328, folio 306); he "introduced Ibn al-'Arabi to Indian sufis"
  (p. 122, folio 104); the plate "'Iraqi with the Qalandars from the Majalisu'l-'Ushshaq / Bodleian,
  dated 959/1552, Ms. Ouseley Add. 24, f. 79 R." (p. 222, folio not stated) — **the same manuscript
  as the samaʿ plate of chunk 006, there at f. 119 R; the rest of p. 222 is unreadable OCR of the
  image.** **Amir Husain (Husaini)**, born "about 671/1272-73" by Rizvi's reckoning, "birth place …
  at Ghizv, a village in Ghur", a disciple of Sadruʾd-Din who "stayed in Multan and wrote verses in
  praise of Sultan Jalalu'd-Din Firuz Khalji (1290-96)" then "migrated to Herat" (p. 226, folio
  206), his eight works listed at p. 229 (folio 209), three of them "quoted in various texts but
  have not yet been discovered"; he wrote "Early in 1311" to **Saʿduʾd-Din Mahmud Shabistari "(d.
  720/1320)"**, "the celebrated mystic of Tabriz", "listing the following fifteen questions on
  sufism" (pp. 226–229, folios 206–209; also pp. 360–361, folios 338–339). **Maulana Husamuʾd-Din**,
  who "migrated to Bada'un … the people of Bada'un gave him the title, Maulana of Multan" (p. 230,
  folio 210) — **two other Maulana Husamuʾd-Dins stand near him, one who died in Gujarat after 1327
  (pp. 198–199, folios 180–181) and one whose Qutuʾl-Qulub sentence is cut off at a chunk boundary
  (p. 197, folio 179); the book does not say whether they are one man.** **Shaikh Ahmad-i Maʿshuq**,
  "a native of Qandahar … an alcoholic", converted by sherbet, who "even abandoned obligatory
  prayers" (p. 230, folio 210). **Shaikh Salahuʾd-Din Dervish**, who "migrated to Delhi from Multan"
  and "unlike Shaikh Nasiru'd-Din, was not humble and forbearing* towards the political powers of
  his time" (p. 230, folio 210). **Hasan Afghan**, the illiterate disciple whose rebuke to the Imam
  is at pp. 223–224 (folios 203–204). **Shaikh Hamiduʾd-Din Suwali**, who cursed Bahaʾuʾd-Din's son
  (p. 220, folio 202).
- **The Delhi khalifas of Nizamuʾd-Din** — **Maulana Fakhruʾd-Din Zarradi** of Samana, who converted
  after a disputation, "shaved his head, adopted celibacy", "rented a house in front of the
  jama'at-khana", whose "fame as a scholar spread to Baghdad", who moved to Daulatabad "about 1327",
  studied Hadis at Baghdad "becoming the acknowledged authority in that field", and whose "ship,
  overloaded with pilgrims, sank and Maulana Fakhru'd-Din was drowned" (pp. 199–201, folios 181–183;
  also p. 181, folio 163). **Shaikh Qutbuʾd-Din Dabir**, who carried Zarradi's shoes "on his head"
  and did "not falter" when the Sultan "ordered him to give up such blasphemous beliefs or he would
  be killed" (pp. 200–201, folios 182–183). **Maulana Shamsuʾd-Din Yahya**, who accepted "a woollen
  garment and a purse of tankas" (pp. 200, 203; folios 182, 185). **Maulana ʿAlaʾuʾd-Din Nili** of
  Awadh, who "found the associated responsibilities too onerous" and spent his last years "reading
  the Fawai'du'l-Fu'ad [OCR?] [Fawa'idu'l-Fu'ad] which he himself had transcribed" (p. 201, folio
  183). **Maulana Burhanuʾd-Din Gharib**, who "invented a peculiar style of dancing … known as the
  Burhani", was denounced for "sitting on cushions like a great Shaikh", expelled and restored, and
  "forced to leave Delhi for Daulatabad" (pp. 21, 201, 271; folios 7, 183, 251) — **on the
  `langer-makhdoom` collision see Traps** — with his khalifa **Shaikh Zainuʾd-Din** (section A).
  **Qazi Muhiʾuʾd-Din Kashani**, "One of the most senior khalifas", whose khilafat-nama the Shaikh
  "wrote … with his own hand" and from whom he "remained estranged … for about a year" after a royal
  grant (pp. 194–195, folios 176–177). **Maulana Wajihuʾd-Din Yusuf**, sent to Chanderi "to give him
  spiritual protection" and kept there (p. 195, folio 177) — **distinct from "Maulana Wajihu'd-Din
  Pa'ili" (p. 192, folio 174).** **Maulana Shamsuʾd-Din, son of Yahya**, of Awadh, who "had no
  respect for sufism" until he converted, then "even refused to see people who called", and who died
  of a lanced boil rather than go to Kashmir "and preach Islam in the temples there" (pp. 195–196,
  folios 177–178). **Shaikh Qutbuʾd-Din Munawwar of Hansi**, grandson of Jamaluʾd-Din of Hansi, who
  left Hansi "Putting his prayer carpet on his shoulders, and carrying his stick … accompanied by
  his son, Nuru'd-Din", and who told Sultan Firuz "to stop drinking and refrain from continual
  indulgence in hunting, as the killing of animals should be necessitated only by human needs"
  (pp. 196–197, 264; folios 178–179, 244). **Shaikh Jamaluʾd-Din of Hansi**, "The most senior
  disciple of Baba Farid", formerly "the khatib of Hansi, owning villages and extensive property",
  author of "the Mulhimat … and his Diwan", who "tore the paper containing the khilafat-nama to
  pieces" (p. 171, folio 153; also p. 329, folio 307). **Maulana Badruʾd-Din Ishaq**, steward of the
  jamaʿat-khana and Baba Farid's son-in-law, who came to Ajodhan "carrying a large number of books",
  retired to "the Jami' mosque at Ajodhan", "died soon after the Baba's death", and wrote "an Arabic
  grammar called Tasrif-i Badri, but unfortunately it has not survived" (pp. 170–171, folios
  152–153; also p. 160, folio 142). **Shaikh Badruʾd-Din of Ghazna** (grave in section A), "it was
  his skill as a poet and orator that made him famous … at a ripe old age Shaikh Badru'd-Din still
  danced like a boy of ten", **probably the same man as "Badru'd-Din Ghaznawi" (p. 158, folio 140);
  the book does not say** — his khanqah was built by **Nizamuʾd-Din Kharitadar (The Treasurer)**, who
  "was charged with embezzlement and lost his position. His loss of power tended to undermine the
  prestige of the Shaikh" (p. 156, folio 138), **the clearest patronage-risk passage in the book.**
  **Shaikhuʾl-Islam Najmuʾd-Din Sughra**, who invited the Khwaja to Delhi (p. 143, folio 125), grew
  jealous of Qutbuʾd-Din over samaʿ (p. 154, folio 136), and "was dismissed by the Sultan" over the
  Tabrizi affair (pp. 218–219, folios 200–201). Plus **Maulana Fasihuʾd-Din** (p. 191, folio 173);
  **Iqbal**, the servant (pp. 181, 232; folios 163, 212); **Sadr-i Jahan Qazi Kamaluʾd-Din** and his
  "farman of two villages" (p. 196, folio 178); **Khwajgi Khujandi**, the cloth merchant who "used to
  give something to the beggars he met and drop sugar and sesame seeds into ant holes" (p. 241, folio
  221); **Muhammad Katib, a hajib** (p. 198, folio 180); **Wahiduʾd-Din Quraishi** (p. 239, folio
  219); **Tajuʾd-Din Multani**, whose child died when the amulet could not be found (p. 240, folio
  220); **Maulana Burhanuʾd-Din Nasafi** and his three conditions (p. 239, folio 219); **Shaikh
  ʿAli**, "a bare given name with no nisba, place or date" (p. 243, folio 223); **Shaikh Piyara**
  (p. 276, folio 256); **Hamiduʾd-Din Dihlawi**, named once in a legend, **"whether this is the
  Nagauri Shaikh is not stated"** (p. 135, folio 117); **Ibrahim Qunduzi** (p. 137, folio 119);
  **Maulana Minhajuʾd-Din Tirmizi** (p. 157, folio 139); **Shaikh ʿAli Sijzi** (p. 155, folio 137);
  **Malik Sharafuʾd-Din Kubra** (pp. 170–171, folios 152–153); **Maulana Nizam Madani** (pp.
  249–250, folios 229–230).
- **The Firdausiyya in India** — **Khwaja Badruʾd-Din Samarqandi**, "the founder of the si/sila
  [OCR?] [silsila] in India", sent by Bakharzi to Delhi, "very fond of sama'", whose relations with
  the Chishtis were "amicable" and whose death date is **explicitly unknown to the book** — "probably
  it was during the reign of Sultan 'Ala'u'd-Din Khalji" (pp. 246–248, folios 226–228); his two
  khalifas **Khwaja Ruknuʾd-Din** "(who lived near Kilukhari)" and **Khwaja Najibuʾd-Din**, who
  "preferred to lead the quiet life of an ascetic, however, his disciples made him famous" (p. 248,
  folio 228); **Shaikh ʿImad**, whose two sons "were so envious of Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din Auliya' that
  they even condemned him publicly" and both drowned near the langar (p. 248, folio 228); **Maulana
  Fariduʾd-Din**, who "in 777/1375—76, compiled a book on Sunni jurisprudence called the Fatawa-i
  Tatar Khaniyya" (p. 248, folio 228); **Shaikh Najibuʾd-Din Firdawsi** (p. 249, folio 229); **Shaikh
  Husain Muʿizz Balkhi**, "a later Firdausiyya saint from Bihar", whose discourses were collected as
  the *Ganj-i La Yakhfa* (pp. 22–23, folios 8–9). **Shaikh Muzaffar** and his party (section A), whose
  ancestors "came from Balkh" and whose father followed "Shaikh Ahmad Charm-Posh (One Who Wears
  Skins), a cousin of Shaikh Sharafu'd-Din" (p. 257, folio 237). **Shaikh ʿIzz Kakuʾi and Shaikh
  Ahmad Bihari**, executed under Firuz after a mahzar; "The people of Delhi believed they were
  crazy"; the *Futuhat-i Firuz Shahi* calls them atheists and says "Some people in Bihar considered
  Ahmad to be God" (p. 251, folio 231; also p. 256, folio 236).
- **Masʿud-i Bak** — "The pioneer of Delhi's Wahdat al-Wujud movement"; "His real name was Sher Khan
  and his ancestors had migrated from Bukhara to Delhi"; "a relative of Sultan Firuz Tughluq" made
  "fantastically wealthy" by office; disciple of "the Chishti, Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din, son of Shaikh
  Shihabu'd-Din" though he "believed Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din Auliya' to be his real teacher"; "a later
  authority writes that Sher Khan was beheaded, on a fatwa from the 'ulama.'" (p. 261, folio 241);
  works an untraceable *Tamhidat*, the *Mirʾatuʾl-ʿArifin* "divided into fourteen chapters called
  Kashfs or Revelations", and "his Diwan, entitled the Nuru'l-Yagin [OCR?] [Nuru'l-Yaqin]" (p. 262,
  folio 242); his verses "helped to soften many earlier bitternesses" (pp. 327–328, folios 305–306).
  **No death date, no tomb — (not stated).**
- **Saiyid Muhammad Husaini bin Jaʿfar al-Makki (Jaʿfar-i Makki)** — father "Amir Ja'far … a mugta'
  [OCR?] [muqta'] in Cambay and an officer of 1,300 horsemen"; initiated by Nasiruʾd-Din Chiragh-i
  Dihli, whom he served "even cleaning with his beard the istinja clay of his preceptor"; at Ghazna
  "in 789/1387-88", twenty years travelling, "In 809/1406—7 he was in Balkh", settling in Delhi
  "about 820/1417-18"; his *Bahruʾl-Maʿani* letters run "10 Safar 824/14 February 1421" to "27
  Muharram 825/21 January 1422", thirty-six in all; he "claimed to have met at least 380 sufi saints"
  (p. 264, folio 244) — **the Pass 1 worker records that the book's own dating of his life is
  internally implausible.** Doctrine at pp. 265–267 (folios 245–247), including "the Righteous
  Caliphate ended with 'Ali, therefore, his successors were usurpers", and Rizvi's correction of his
  Harun ar-Rashid error (p. 267, folio 247). Also p. 422 (folio 398).
- **The Jaunpur and Kalpi circles** — **Qazi ʿAbduʾl-Muqtadir**, "The Delhi sufi and scholar whose
  disciples and descendants made Jaunpur an important Chishti centre", who "died on 26 Muharram
  791/25 January 1389" (p. 281, folio 261); **Qazi Shihabuʾd-Din Daulatabadi**, made "the
  Qaziu'l-Quzat (Chief Qazi)" and "Maliku'l-'Ulama' (Prince of the 'Ulama')", author of the *Bahr-i
  Mawwaj*, an incomplete *Usul* commentary, the *Usul-i Ibrahim Shahi* and the *Managibuʾs-Sadat*
  [OCR?] [*Manaqibuʾs-Sadat*], who "died in 848/1444—45" (pp. 281–282, folios 261–262; also pp. 291,
  341; folios 271, 319); **Shaikh Abuʾl-Fath**, "born on 14 Muharram 772/8 August 1370", who "did not
  accept a government office and led a life of poverty and near-starvation" and "died on 13 Rabi' I
  858/13 March 1454" (pp. 282–283, folios 262–263) — **not the same man as Shaikh Abuʾl-Fath ʿAlaʾi
  Qureshi of Kalpi**; **Shaikh Ahmad ʿIsa and Shaikh Muhammad ʿIsa** (pp. 282–283, folios 262–263);
  **Shaikh Bahaʾuʾd-Din of Jaunpur** (p. 283, folio 263) — **one of three Bahaʾuʾd-Dins in that
  chunk**; **Shaikh Adhan Jaunpuri** (section A); **Saiyid ʿAli / Shaikh ʿAli**, who "behaved like a
  majzub", "had four wives and received regular futuh", "had no servants and he himself waited on
  other dervishes", and "died in 905/1499-1500" (p. 283, folio 263); **Maulana Ilahdad, known as
  Sharih (Commentator)**, and his disciple **Shaikh Maʿruf** (p. 286, folio 266); **Shaikh Hasan
  Tahir** (grave in section A), son of Tahir "who migrated from Multan", born in Bihar, forbidden
  the *Wahdat al-Wujud* by his father, disciple of Raji Hamid Shah, whose disciple Barbak Shah is
  said to have been, invited to Agra "where the court had been located since 1502" (pp. 285–286,
  folios 265–266); **Maulana Khwajgi**, **Maulana Ahmad Thaneswari** and **Shaikh Abuʾl-Fath ʿAlaʾi
  Qureshi**, all buried at Kalpi (pp. 293–294, folios 273–274); **Khwaja Ikhtiyaruʾd-Din ʿUmar of
  Iraj** and his disciple **Shaikh Yusuf Budh**, who "translated Ghazali's Minhaju'l 'Abidin into
  Persian" and "In 834/1430-31 … died while engrossed in a performance of sama'" (pp. 294–295, folios
  274–275), whose own leading disciple was "Muhammad Bihamad Khani, the author of the Tarikh-i
  Muhammadi" (p. 295, folio 275).
- **The Lucknow and Rudauli circles** — **Shaikh Qiwamuʾd-Din of Lucknow**, through whom double
  initiation "seems to have been introduced" (pp. 292–293, folios 272–273); **Shaikh Sarang** and
  **Shaikh Mina** (section A); **Saʿduʾd-Din Khairabadi**, who wrote on "the Futuhat al-Makkiyya of
  Ibn al-'Arabi" and "completed the Shaikh Mina's malfuzat", and **Allahdiya Khairabadi**, "dying in
  993/1585" (p. 293, folio 273); **Shaikh Ahmad ʿAbduʾl-Haqq of Rudauli** (section A), "born in
  Delhi", brother "Shaikh Taqiu'd-Din", disciple at Panipat of "Shaikh Jalal Panipati, who in turn
  traced his spiritual genealogy from Shaikh Shamsu'd-Din Turk also from Panipat, a khalifa of Shaikh
  'Ala'u'd-Din 'Ali Ahmad Sabir", so "through the Sabiri branch of the Chishtis"; Sunam, then Bengal
  after "Timur's invasion in 1398-99 devastated both the Panjab and Delhi", then Bihar, Awadh and
  Rudauli; "Twice he visited Jaunpur but Qazi Shihabu'd-Din conspired to prevent him from being
  granted an audience" (pp. 290–292, folios 270–272; also pp. 366, 374; folios 344, 352); his
  disciple **Shaikh Bakhtiyar of Rudauli**, "Formerly the slave of a jewel merchant", who "continued
  to trade" (p. 292, folio 272); **Shaikh Jamal, a Muslim Gujar**, "the only real Muslim he had found
  between Pandua and Awadh" (p. 292, folio 272).
- **The Mandu and Nagaur circle** — **Khwaja Muʿinuʾd-Din Khwurd**, "the eldest son of Shaikh
  Husamu'd-Din Sukhta and the grandson of Shaikh Fakhru'd-Din, who in turn was the son of Khwaja
  Mu'inu'd-Din Chishti", whose sons moved to Mandu "after the occupation of Ajmer by the Rana of
  Mewar" (p. 295, folio 275); **Shaikh Qutbuʾd-Din "Chisht Khan"**, "a commander of twelve thousand
  soldiers", who refused Ajmer and then engineered his cousin's removal there (pp. 295–296, folios
  275–276); **Shaikh Bayazid**, that cousin, married to daughters of "Shaikh Mahmud Dihlawi" and of
  Khwaja Husain Nagauri, whose "devotee" Sultan Mahmud became (pp. 295–296, folios 275–276); **Shah
  Miyanji of Malwa**, whose pir "lived for 150 years and Shah Miyanji survived for 120", who
  "would remain without any human contact" for six months at a time and refused the Qazi of Mandu
  (p. 296, folio 276); **Shaikh Ahmad Majid Shaibani**, "born at Narnol. By the age of eighteen he
  was an accomplished scholar", who criticised "the humiliating custom of prostration before rulers,
  which the Sultans of Mandu had introduced", left Ajmer "in 922/1516 … because of its occupation by
  Rana Sanga of Mewar" and "finally died on 25 Safar 927/4 February 1521" at Nagaur (p. 296, folio
  276); **Shaikh Khizr, Shaikh Mubarak and Shaikh Abuʾl-Fazl**, three generations drawn to Nagaur
  (p. 149, folio 131) — **this Shaikh Khizr is a historical man and must not be confused with
  "Khwaja Khizr" of the miracle stories (p. 152, folio 134).**
- **The Gujarat line of Makhdum-i Jahaniyan** — **Burhanuʾd-Din Qutb-i ʿAlam** (tomb in section A),
  "Born either in Uch or Multan in 790/1388—89" — **the alternative is the book's own and is not
  resolved** — who "settled near the new capital, Ahmadabad" (p. 304, folio 282); his son **Saiyid
  Muhammad, "Shah-i ʿAlam"/"Shah Manjhan"**, "born on 9 Zu'lqada 817/20 January 1415 and … trained
  by Shaikh Ahmad-i Khattu as well as by his father", who "died on 20 Jumada II 880/21 October 1475"
  (p. 305, folio 283); **Malik Dawaruʾl-Mulk**, "A prominent and pious official of Sultan Mahmud I
  Begarh's (1459-1511)", who "fell fighting on 13 Zu'lqa'da 915/22 February 1510. Long remembered by
  the people of Gujarat and the Deccan after his death, they continued to seek Dawaru'l-Mulk's
  blessings" (p. 305, folio 283) — **a layman whose posthumous blessing is sought: a shrine-type cult
  with no shrine named**; **Shaikh ʿAli Khatib, Shaikh Sirajuʾd-Din, Malik Muhammad and
  Aminuʾl-Mulk**, of whom Malik Muhammad drew "the Shaikh's water from the River Sabarmati,
  unflinchingly walking through the bazaar with the pot on his head" (pp. 305–306, folios 283–284).
  **Saiyid Muhammad of Jaunpur (1443-1505)**, who "In 1496 … reached Gujarat and a year later at
  Ahmadabad declared himself the Mahdi", was "banished from Gujarat in 1500", and after whom "many
  people withdrew from the world to a life of asceticism" (p. 306, folio 284); and **Rukn**, an
  earlier Mahdi claimant beheaded under Firuz Shah (p. 289, folio 269) — **a one-word name that
  collides with an archive row's short form; not mapped.** **Shaikh Rajgiri "Akhi"**, "A native of
  Daryabad in Awadh", who moved to a Ganges village "Finding his constant stream of visitors
  irksome" (p. 306, folio 284).
- **Shaikh Samaʿuʾd-Din and Shaikh Hamid bin Fazluʾllah "Jamali"** — the two who made Delhi a
  Suhrawardi centre. Samaʿuʾd-Din (grave in section A) was four steps from Makhdum-i Jahaniyan, also
  taught by "a disciple of the celebrated Mir Saiyid Sharif Jurjani", and "Leaving Uch and Multan
  after his initiation … visited Nagaur, Gujarat and Bayana" (p. 307, folio 285); his
  "Miftahu'l-Asrar (Key to the Divine Secret)" is said to be "based on the writings of Shaikh
  'Azizu'd-Din Nasafi (d. 661/1263)" (p. 308, folio 286). Jamali, "a member of the Kanbo Sunni
  merchants", changed his pen name "from Jalali (Awe-inspiring) to Jamali (Lovable)", travelled to
  Mecca, Medina, "the Maghrib, the Yemen, Palestine, Turkey, Syria, Iraq, Iran and Khurasan" and
  "visited Ceylon to see the footprint of Adam", met Jami at Herat "before Jami's death, on 9
  November 1492", and "died on 10 Zu'lqa'da 942/1 May 1536" on Humayun's Gujarat campaign
  (pp. 308–309, folios 286–287); his masnawi "Mihr wa Mah" at pp. 309–310 (folios 287–288); the
  *Siyaruʾl-Arifin*, "written between 937/1530 and 942/1536", is "the most important work for this
  study" (p. 25, folio 11) — **spelt "Kanbo" in the sigla key and "Kambo" in the text.** His sons
  **Shaikh ʿAbduʾl-Haʾi "(b. 923/1517-18)"**, who "died in 959/1551-52", and **Shaikh Gadaʾi**, made
  "Sadru's-Sudur", who "died in 976/1568-69 or a year later" and whose "memory was vilified by his
  enemies who inscribed to him such phrases: 'You are dead you great hog.'" (pp. 310–311, folios
  288–289). **On the `shah-jamal` and `shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani` collisions see
  Traps.**
- **The qalandars and Haidaris in India** — **Shah Khizr Rumi**, "a historical personality" who
  "arrived in Delhi from his native Anatolia during the lifetime of Shaikh Qutbu'd-Din Bakhtiyar
  Kaki", was allowed "to maintain the dress and customs of a qalandar", initiated **Saiyid
  Najmuʾd-Din Ghausuʾd-Dahr** "at Surhurpur, near Jaunpur", and "died in 750/1349-S0 [OCR?]
  [1349-50]" in Anatolia (p. 326, folio 304). **Shaikh Sharafuʾd-Din Abu ʿAli Qalandar of Panipat**
  (grave in section A), whose discipleship ʿAbduʾl-Haqq calls "unauthentic" and which ʿAbduʾr Rahman
  Chishti compromises by "asserting that Shaikh Abu 'Ali Qalandar had been initiated into the
  Chishtiyya order by the spirit of Shaikh Qutbu'd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki"; "As a qalandar he refused to
  observe the rules of the Shari'a. Nevertheless he approved when Maulana Ziya'u'd-Din Sunnami, an
  orthodox 'alim, clipped his moustache to the length required by holy law"; father "Salar
  Fakhru'd-Din, had migrated from Iraq" (p. 327, folio 305; also pp. 249, 374, 409; folios 229, 352,
  385). **Shaikh ʿAbduʾl-ʿAziz Makki**, who at Ajodhan "disappeared into a grotto (sardabad) on the
  outskirts of the town" and "prophesied he would reappear at the same time as the last Imam Mahdi
  before the Day of Resurrection" (p. 326, folio 304) — **Ajodhan with a saint attached who is NOT
  Baba Farid; carried to the Misfiled section.** **Saiyid Shah ʿAbduʾr-Rasul** and the Rasuli branch
  "in the hills near Alwar" (p. 328, folio 306). **Shaikh Abu Bakr Tusi Haidari**, "The most
  prominent Indian Haidari… settled in Delhi in the mid-thirteenth century", visited by Nizamuʾd-Din
  Auliyaʾ and by Jamaluʾd-Din of Hansi, who gave him the title "Baz-i Safid (White Falcon)", who
  backed "Arkali Khan, rather than his rival, the Khan-i Khanan", and whose "followers declined owing
  to a lack of leadership. However, the wandering Haidaris survived for many centuries" (pp. 329,
  331; folios 307, 309), with **Nuruʾd-Din Malik Yar Parran**, "a native of Lar in Fars … attached to
  the silsila of Shaikh Abu Ishaq Kaziruni", who earned the title "Parran (Flier)" (pp. 182, 330;
  folios 164, 308). **Sidi Maula**, who "came from what was known as the Upper Region (presumably the
  Helmand river region)", whose devotees included "The Sultan's eldest son, the Khan-i Khanan", "An
  eminent qazi, Jalal Kashani Balbani", "Kotwal Biranjtan and Hathiya Payak", and whose execution
  Barani and ʿIsami report differently — "'Isami … argued that khirga-poshan-i kham [OCR?]
  [khirqa-poshan-i kham] (sufi impostors) falsely accused Sidi Maula of being an alchemist"
  (pp. 330–331, 422; folios 308–309, 398). **No death date, no tomb — (not stated).**
- **Baba Ratan (Hajji Baba Ratan, Abuʾl-Riza)** — "said to have hailed from Tabarhinda or Bhatinda in
  the Panjab … Born before the birth of Muhammad", who became "a Muslim and a companion of the
  Prophet"; "Muhammad personally related Ahadis to Baba Ratan and subsequently a treatisc [OCR?]
  [treatise] entitled Risala-i Rataniyya was composed"; "He returned to the Punjab and died in
  700/1300 where he was buried. However the Ahadis attributed to Baba Ratan were considered by
  Saghani as apocryphal" (p. 342, folio 320). The endorsement chain runs through **Shaikh Raziuʾd-Din
  ʿAli Lala of Ghazna**, who "Before his death on 3 Rabi* I 642/9 August 1244 … met Baba Ratan who
  handed him the comb which the Prophet had given him", then Simnani and Hamadani, "a part of the
  Kubrawiyya tradition", and **Ibn Hajar al-Asqalani (773/1372-852/1449)**, who "gave an account of
  Baba Ratan in his large biographical dictionary al-Isaba fi Tamyiz al-Sahaba" (pp. 342–343, folios
  320–321). **A parallel figure: ʿAbduʾllah Chingal**, "the Gujarati", who "observed Muhammad's
  miracle of the splitting of the moon" and "survived for several centuries before he died in
  Gujarat" (p. 343, folio 321). **The book never writes "Nath" of Baba Ratan and never mentions
  Peshawar; see Traps.**
