- **The women of Appendix A** — **Bibi Fatima Sam** (section A); **Bibi Sara**, "the mother of Shaikh
  Nizamu'd-Din Abu'l Mu'id, an important contemporary of Khwaja Qutbu'd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki" (p. 425,
  folio not stated, sourced "24A, p. 46" [OCR?] ["2AA, p. 46"]); **a second Bibi Fatima**, who "died
  on the fortieth day of a fast" — **the book does not say whether she is Bibi Fatima Sam** (p. 427,
  folio 403); **Bibi Auliyaʾ**, who "lived at the time of Sultan Muhammad bin Tughluq, who was a
  great devotee … Her sons and grandsons apparently also became saints" (p. 427, folio 403). Shaikh
  ʿAbduʾl-Haqq "wrote a separate chapter in the Akhbaru'l-Akhyar on women saints" (p. 425, folio not
  stated).
- **The poets and Hindi masnawi writers** — **Amir Khusraw**, "India's most famous Persian poet",
  born "651/1253" at Patiali into "the Lachin tribe", who "learnt Persian, also some Turkic dialects,
  and Hindawi from the Hindu chiefs who would call on his grandfather. But he failed to develop a
  taste for Arabic"; patrons in sequence Malik Chajju, Bughra Khan, "Prince Muhammad, and governor
  of Multan" killed "in Zul'hijja 683/March 1285", then Awadh and Delhi; "In Multan Khusraw
  encountered the Mongols and was, for a short time, imprisoned by them"; he died "on 18 Shawwal
  725/27 September 1325", **with a rival date in the footnote, "Other dates ure [OCR?] [are] 29
  ZuIqa'da [OCR?] [Zu'lqa'da] 725 6 November 1325", and both stand**; "not a khalifa … but important
  communications of the khalifas to their Shaikh were made through the sufi poet"; title "Tuti-i Hind
  (Parrot of India)"; fourteen works with dates at pp. 187–189 (folios 169–171) (pp. 186–190, folios
  168–172; also pp. 176, 191, 243, 333; folios 158, 173, 223, 311). **Amir Najmuʾd-Din Hasan Sijzi**,
  "Born in Bada'un in 652/1254—55", who "began writing poetry at the age of thirteen", "excelled even
  his friend and fellow sufi, Khusraw, in writing ghazals", became a disciple "sometime before
  707/1307 … then more than fifty-two", "remained unmarried" and "died at Daulatabad either in 737 or
  738/1336 or 1337", "known as the Sa'di of India" (p. 191, folio 173; also pp. 19–20, 235, 239–240,
  243; folios 5–6, 215, 219–220, 223) — **the book does not say whether the qawwal of p. 235 and the
  disciple are the same man.** **Amir Khwurd** (section D). **Ziyaʾuʾd-Din Barani**, "both an 'alim
  and a sufi, but above all he was an ambitious courtier", "A bonvivant who lived extravagantly …
  endowed with a naive sense of his own intellectual and religious superiority", who "urged the
  Sultans to reserve high office for only eminent Saiyids and Shaikhs" and "died some time after
  1357"; his lost *Hasrat Nama* "was probably the most interesting study in sufism" (pp. 30, 192;
  folios 16, 174). **Maulana Dawud ("Mulla Dawud")**, author of the *Chandaʾin*, "The earliest known
  masnawi written in Hindi", "from Dalmau in the Rae Bareli district", "Probably the work was started
  in 772/1370—71 and completed in 781/1379-80" (p. 386, folio 364). **Shaikh Qutb ʿAli Qutban**,
  author of the *Mrigawati*, "the disciple of Makhdum Shaikh Budhan", who "preferred to call himself
  a Suhrawardi" and "completed his masnawi in Muharram 909/June-July 1503, after working on it for
  just over two months" (p. 389, folio 367). **Malik Muhammad Jaʾisi**, "also known as Muhaqqiq-i
  Hind (Researcher of Indian Truth). Born in 900/1494-95", who "became a Mahdawi under the influence
  of his Mahdawi pir, Shaikh Burhan of Kalpi", with six further works named and, of his death, only
  the inference "It would seem he died sometime at the end of that period" — **(not stated)**
  (pp. 394–395, folios 370–371). **Mir ʿAbduʾl-Wahid Bilgarami**, "born in c.915/1509-10", of
  "Bilgaram in Hardoi, near Lucknow", married in Kanauj, who "died on 3 Ramazan 1017/11 December
  1608", author of the *Sabaʿ-i Sanabil* "Written in 969/1562" and, "In 1566", of the *Hagaʾig-i
  Hindi* [OCR?] [*Haqaʾiq-i Hindi*], "a Persian dictionary of Hindi songs … divided into three
  sections" (pp. 350, 381, 384; folios 328, 359, 362). **The Bengali and Arakanese poets** — Shaikh
  Zahid (1498-99), Shaikh Chand, "'Abdu'l Hakim (1620-90) of Svandvip [OCR?] [Sandvip]", "Saiyid
  Murtaza (1590-1662) from Murshibabad [OCR?] [Murshidabad]", "Saiyid Sultan (1550-1648) of
  Chittagong" (pp. 374–375, folios 352–353), Daulat Qazi, Alaol, whose *Padmavati* was "composed in
  1651", Gharibuʾllah, Razzaq Nandan ʿAbduʾl-Hakim, Rasti Khan and ʿAbduʾn-Nabi, who "In 1684…
  composed the Dastan-i Amir Hamza" (pp. 395–396, folios 371–372); **Yusuf Gada**, who completed the
  *Tuhfatuʾn-Nashaʾih* "on 10 Rabi' II 795/23 February 1393" and "was a disciple of Shaikh
  Nasiru'd-Din Chiragh-i Dihli" (p. 396, folio 372). **Qazi Ruknuʾd-Din Samarqandi and Bhojar
  Brahman**, the *Amrita-Kunda* translators, with **Shaikh Muhammad Ghaus Shattari
  (906/1500-01-970/1562-63)**, who "re-translated [it] into Persian", and **Shaikh Sulaiman**, to
  whom ʿAbduʾl-Quddus "imparted its essence" (p. 357, folio 335). **Shaikh Ahmad from Naharwala in
  Gujarat**, a musician "who gave expert renditions of Hindawi ragas", "said to have been a disciple
  of Faqir Madhu, the Imam of the Jami' mosque in Ajmer, who retained his Hindu name even after
  conversion" (p. 348, folio 326).
- **Other named figures with little attached** — **Shaikh Sajjan**, who "relinquished his life as a
  robber and became a sufi saint" en route to Panipat (p. 409, folio 385); **Bodhan or Lodhan**, a
  Brahman who "declared Islam and Hinduism as both true religions", "imprisoned, instructed in Islam
  and after he refused to convert, was executed" — **the book gives him two names and two possible
  home towns and does not choose** (pp. 406–407, folios 382–383); **Maulana Ziyaʾuʾd-Din Hakim of
  Balkh**, the philosopher who "threw his philosophy books into the river" (p. 139, folio 121);
  **Muhammad Yadgar** of Sabzwar, "a scoundrel and a Shi'i" who after conversion "freed his slaves
  and sought the forgiveness of those he had previously victimized" (pp. 138–139, folios 120–121);
  **Shamsuʾl-ʿArifin ʿAbduʾl-Wahid and Shaikh Nizamuʾd-Din Abuʾl-Muyʾid**, whose descendant "Shaikh
  Jamal became very famous at Kol (Aligarh), where he also died" (p. 139, folio 121) — **the same
  name is printed "Abu’l-Muy’id" in the text and "Abu'l-Mu'yid" in the footnote on one page**;
  **Shaikh Salim of Sikri**, "the famous Chishti saint", "(1479-1572)" (pp. 144, 398; folios 126,
  374); **Ram Deo**, "head of the priests of Ajmer" — **quoted from Yusuf Husain, not asserted by
  Rizvi** (p. 136, folio 118); **Jaipal**, "his prime minister … who was also a magician" (p. 135,
  folio 117); **Mahesh**, the childless Brahman (p. 428, folio not stated); **Shaikh Sadruʾd-Din of
  Meerut**, "a son-in-law of the Tughluq Shah, Ghiyasu'd-Din" (p. 430, folio 406); **Jai Ram**, "Guru
  Nanak's brother-in-law … a steward of Daulat Khan in Sultanpur" (p. 409, folio 385) — **Guru
  Nanak's sister is implied and not named; (not stated), which is the whole of what this book offers
  `gurdwara-babay-nanki`.**

### C. Saints and Sufis of the wider Islamic world — none with an archive row

*Chapters One–Two are a history of early Sufism outside India. Entries are compressed to name,
dates as the book prints them, and page references; the book's own contradictions and hedges are
kept, because they are facts about the source.*

- **Shaikh Abu Saʿid bin Abiʾl-Khair of Mayhana** — "born on 1 Muharram 357/7 December 967 in
  Mayhana, the present Me'ana, between Abiward and Sarakhs, in Khurasan"; father "Babu Bu'l-Khair"
  [OCR? name at risk], "a druggist as well as a sufi"; first guide "Abu'l-Qasim Bishr-i Yasin of
  Mayhana", who died 380/990; then "Abu'l-Fazl Muhammad bin Hasan al-Sarakhsi"; "Around 415/1024 …
  settled in Nishapur"; dissuaded from hajj by "Abu'l-Hasan Khargani" [OCR?] [*Kharqani*]; died "on
  4 Sha'ban 440/12 January 1049" (pp. 84–88, folios 68–72). **"Not only in his literary role but
  also in his reorganization of khanqah life did the Shaikh prove himself a pioneer"** (p. 82, folio
  66); "the Shaikh was opposed to a fixed source of income—nothing was accumulated for future use"
  (p. 88, folio 72); "From the time of the Khurasanian, Abu Sa'id, kKhanqahs [OCR?] [khanqahs] were
  rendez-vous for artisans and merchants" (p. 422, folio 398); shocked al-Qushairi by "extravagant
  spending and entertainments involving music and dancing, which were often attended by young boys"
  (p. 87, folio 71); "the first known sufi to have practised" *namaz-i maʿkus* (pp. 358, 364; folios
  336, 342); one of three with "a profound interest in qalandars" (p. 323, folio 301). His two
  malfuzat and their dated composition at pp. 17–18 (folios 3–4). **"Which Abu Saʿid is meant" at
  p. 78 (folio 62) is ambiguous in the book itself.**
- **Shaikh ʿAbduʾl-Qadir Jilani** — founder of the Qadiriyya; "born in 470/1077—78 in the village of
  Jilan"; father "Abi Salih Jangi Dust"; Baghdad at eighteen; sufism "sparked off by Abu'l-Khair
  Hammad al-Dabbas who died in 523/1129, however, he obtained his khirga [OCR?] [khirqa] from
  al-Mukharrimi"; preacher "at Baghdad in 521/1127" after twenty-five years wandering, "the last
  eleven, in complete seclusion"; "became highly popular as a theologian, rather than as a sufi"; "At
  fifty-one he married"; "died an earthly death in 561/1166"; sermons "a/ [OCR?] [al] Fath
  al-Rabbani, comprising sixty-two of them and the Futuh al-Ghaib, containing seventy-eight"
  (pp. 100–102, folios 84–86); also pp. 138, 152, 413 (folios 120, 134, 389). **No tomb named — (not
  stated).** The Javanese material rests on Drewes's summary of the "Hikayat Abdul-Qadir Jilani"
  (p. 102, folio 86).
- **Shaikh Najmuʾd-Din Kubra and the Kubrawiyya** — "'the Carver of Saints' (Shaikh-i Wali Tarash)";
  "born in Khwarazm in 540/1145-46"; disciple of "Shaikh Isma'il Qasri (d. 589/1193)", then "Shaikh
  'Ammar ibn Yasir al-Bidlisi (died about 597/1200-01)", then "Shaikh Ruzbihan al-Wazzan of Kazirun
  (d. 584/1188)", whose daughter he married; killed in "618/1221, the Mongol invasion of Khwarazm"
  (pp. 111–112, folios 93–94). Branches: "the Firdawsiyya and the Hamadaniyya; the Baghdadi branch
  was the Nuriyya and the Khurasani branches were the Rukniyya, the Ightishashiyya and the
  Nurbakhshiyya" (p. 111, folio 93). Disciples "Majdu'd-Din Baghdadi, (died either in 606/1209-10 or
  in 616/1219-20), 'Attar's pir"; "Sa'dud'd-Din Hamawi (d. 650/1253)"; "Najmu'd-Din Daya (d.
  654/1256)"; "Saifu'd-Din Bakharzi"; and "Raziu'd-Din 'Ali Lala" (pp. 111–112, folios 93–94). Three
  works at p. 112 (folio 94).
- **The Khwajgan / Naqshbandi chain** — **Khwaja Abu Yaʿqub Yusuf al-Hamadani**, "(d. Muharram
  535/August 1140)", educated at Baghdad, "lived at Marw and Herat, dying in Marw" (pp. 113, 138;
  folios 95, 120); **Khwaja ʿAbduʾl-Khaliq Ghujduwani** of "Ghujduwan, modern Gizduvan", "the true
  originator of the unique features of the Silsila-i Khwajgan", whose "eight principles of sufi life
  and the rituals he advocated were largely based on yogic practices, current in the Bukhara region",
  succeeded "by four Ahalifas [OCR?] [khalifas], all from Bukhara" (pp. 113–114, folios 95–96); the
  chain "'Arif Riwgari (died 657/1259-60)", "Mahmud Anjir Faghnawi (d. 643/1245 or 670/1271-72)—
  'Azizan 'Ali ar-Ramtini (d. 705/1306 or 721/1321-—22)—Muhammad Baba as-Samasi (d. 740/1340 or
  755/1354)—Saiyid Amir Kulal al-Bukhari (d. 772/1371), Khwaja Baha'u'd-Din Naqshband" (p. 114,
  folio 96) — **three of the five links carry two alternative death dates and none is preferred**;
  **Khwaja Bahaʾuʾd-Din Naqshband**, "born in Muharram 718/March 1318", trained "from the spirit of
  Khwaja 'Abdu'l-Khaliq Ghujduwani", "died on 3 Rabi* I, 791/1 March 1389", after whom "the order
  became known as the Naqshbandiyya" (pp. 115, 287; folios 97, 267). **No tomb for any of them —
  (not stated).**
- **ʿAinuʾl-Quzat of Hamadan** — "the sufi martyr, Abu'l-Ma'ali 'Abdu'llah ibn Abi Bakr Muhammad ibn
  'Ali ibn al-Hasan ibn al-Miyanji"; "Born in 492/1098"; family from "Miyana in Azerbaijan";
  imprisoned at Baghdad "by the Saljugqid [OCR?] [Saljuqid] vizier of Iran, al-Dargazini"; his
  defence "translated into English by A.J. Arberry and called Apologia"; "On 6-7 Jumada II, 525/6-7
  May 1131, 'Ainu'l-Quzat was martyred at the age of thirty-three"; "the order he founded did not
  long survive"; his "TYamhidat [OCR?] [Tamhidat]" (pp. 102–103, folios 86–87). **The three dates
  are internally consistent (525 − 492 = 33) and they agree exactly with "(492/1098-525/1131)" at
  p. 262 (folio 242), where the *Tamhidat* "was written in 521/1127" — a rare internal
  cross-check.** Also pp. 256, 321 (folios 236, 299).
- **The Suhrawardi founders** — **Ahmad al-Ghazali**, who "In 488/1095 … succeeded his brother as a
  professor at the Nizamiyya seminary in Baghdad", "Initiated into sufism in Tus by Abu 'Ali
  al-Farmazi", who "defended the monotheism of Iblis" and "migrated to Qazwin where he died in
  520/1126" (pp. 102, 149; folios 86, 131); **Shaikh Ziyaʾuʾd-Din Abuʾn-Najib as-Suhrawardi**, "born
  in 490/1097 at Suhraward, to the west of Sultaniya, in the province of al-Jibal (the Mountains)",
  which "The Greeks called … Media" and which "From the ninth century … became known as Iraq 'Ajami
  or Iranian Iraq", author of the Arabic *Adab al-Muridin*, who "died in 563/1168" (p. 102, folio
  86); **Shaikh Shihabuʾd-Din Abu Hafs al-Suhrawardi**, "born in Rajab 539/January 1145", who "learnt
  theology from Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Qadir Jilani … but was initiated into sufism by his uncle", was the
  Caliph's ambassador to three courts and "died in 632/1234-35" (pp. 103–104, folios 87–88); and
  **ʿIzzuʾd-Din Mahmud bin ʿAli of Kashan**, "(d. 753/1352-53)", who rearranged the
  *ʿAwarifuʾl-Maʿarif* as the *Misbahuʾl-Hidaya wa Miftahuʾl-Kifaya* (p. 104, folio 88).
- **Abu Hamid Ghazali** — "(450/1058—505/1111), from Tus near Mashhad. Educated at Tus, Gurgan and
  Nishapur, sometime before 1091 Ghazali underwent a period of deep scepticism"; professor at the
  Baghdad Nizamiyya 1091–1095; resigned 1095 and "until 1106, he lived in Syria", travelling to
  Mecca, Alexandria and Tus, "All this time he lived like a sufi"; "In 1106, he accepted the post of
  lecturer at another Nizamiyya seminary, this time in Nishapur" (p. 80, folio 64) — **the fullest
  dated itinerary in chunk 004 and the only one wholly in CE years.** "the principal mission of his
  later years was to reconcile the life of the madrasa, or seminary, to that of the khanqah or
  monastery" (p. 80, folio 64); "His principal contribution to sufism was in the great emphasis he
  laid on the observance of the outward form of religious activities"; on hypocrisy "which he
  considered to be a form of polytheism"; on kingship, monarchs' "special kind of divine light
  (farr)" (pp. 82–84, folios 66–68). **The chunk's opening sentence is the tail of a quotation whose
  subject "is in the previous chunk and cannot be recovered here" — (not stated)** (p. 82, folio 66).
  Also pp. 69, 80, 295 (folios 53, 64, 275).
- **Ibn al-ʿArabi** — "Shaikh al-Akbar, 'the Greatest Shaikh'"; "Born at Murcia in the south-east of
  Spain on 27 Ramazan 560,7 [OCR?] [560/7] August 1165 into the ancient Arab tribe of Tayy"; father
  a friend of "Ibn Rushd or Averroes (1126-98)"; itinerary Tunis 590/1194, Fez, Marrakesh 597/1200,
  Mecca, Baghdad 601/1204, Quniya, Mecca again 611/1214, "nine years at such places as Siwas, and
  Malalya in Anatolia", Damascus from 629/1232 "until his death on 28 Rabi‘ II 638/16 November 1240"
  (pp. 121–122, folios 103–104); four works at pp. 122–123 (folios 104–105); transmitters
  "Sadru'd-Din Al-Qunawi, who was initiated at Quniya in 607/1210", Rumi, ʿIraqi, "Maulana Nuru'd-Din
  'Abdu'r-Rahman Jami of Herat (817/1414-898/1492)" and "'Abdu'l-Karim al-Jili (767/1365-832/1428)",
  who "lived for some time in Yaman, later travelling through India in 790/1388, wrote about thirty
  books on Ibn al-'Arabi's philosophy", chief work "al-Insan al-Kamil (The Perfect Man)" (p. 122,
  folio 104). Opposition: "In Bukhara, the works of Ibn al-'Arabi were burned" (p. 270, folio 250);
  also pp. 265, 269, 274, 315, 363 (folios 245, 249, 254, 293, 341).
- **al-Hallaj** — "born about 244/857-S8 [OCR?] [857-58] at Tur, in Fars, to the north-west of
  al-Bayza. His father was a wool carder, who later settled in the textile centre of Wasit";
  educated at Wasit and Basra; Junaid at Baghdad, hajj, Khurasan "dressed in a soldier's uniform",
  "about four hundred disciples", a second pilgrimage, then "India and Turkistan, where he
  acquainted himself with Buddhism and Manichaeism. About 290/903 he again went to Mecca, this time
  wearing only an Indian loin-cloth"; "Ana'l-Haqq (I am [God] the Truth)"; imprisonment "in
  301/913"; "Imprisoned for nine years"; "Ultimately on 29 Zu'l-Qa'da 309/1 April 922 Hallaj was
  hung on a gibbet after various revolting and merciless tortures had been inflicted" (pp. 73–74,
  folios 57–58); works at p. 74 (folio 58); "his visit to Sind" discussed (p. 345, folio 323); also
  pp. 228, 256, 265 (folios 208, 236, 245). **No tomb — (not stated).**
- **Junaid of Baghdad** — "Sari's nephew"; "Junaid's father was a glass merchant"; "Junaid died in
  Baghdad in 298/910"; "only his treatises, his Rasaʾil (Epistles) and a series of letters have
  survived"; "the founder of the sober (sahw) school of sufism", titled "Saiyid al-Taʾifa (Lord of
  the Sect), Taʾus al-Fugara' [OCR?] [al-Fuqaraʾ] (Peacock of the Dervishes) and Shaikh al-Mashaʾikh
  (Director of the Directors)" (pp. 70–71, folios 54–55) — **the densest line of titulature in the
  book**; also pp. 59, 75, 105, 315 (folios 43, 59, 89, 293).
- **al-Muhasibi** — "Born at Basra in 165/781-82, Muhasibi migrated to Baghdad early in his life";
  persecuted by Ibn Hanbal; "Muhasibi's life became so endangered that he fled to Baghdad. So
  secretly had his existence been kept that when he died in 243/857 only four people attended the
  funeral. Although the study of Muhasibi's writings was banned, succeeding generations of sufis
  recognized his deep contribution" (p. 68, folio 52) — **the flight sentence contradicts the
  migration sentence and both stand.** His title, his doctrine of repentance and *munajat*, and
  Margaret Smith's assessment at pp. 69–70 (folios 53–54).
- **The Baghdad school** — **Maʿruf al-Karkhi**, founder, "Abu Mahfuz Ma'ruf ibn Firuz al-Karkhi",
  with "Christian parents", who "died in 200/815-16" (p. 65, folio 49) but for whom **chunk 004
  gives no death year — (not stated)** (p. 66, folio 50), educated under "Abu Sulaiman Dawud ibn
  Nusair al-Ta'i of Kufa who died between 160/777 and 165/782", his teaching governed by "Hagq
  [OCR?] [Haqq] (Truth) and Sidg [OCR?] [Sidq] (Truthfulness)" (p. 66, folio 50; also p. 399, folio
  375); **Bishr al-Hafi**, named both as Maʿruf's "brother" (p. 66, folio 50) and as "Abu Nasr Bishr
  ibn al al-Haris [OCR?] [ibn al-Haris] al-Hafi", "In his youth he was an alcoholic", who "died in
  227/841-42" (pp. 54–55, folios 38–39); **Sari al-Saqti**, "Originally … a merchant of spices and
  seasonings", who "In 253/867-68 … died at the ripe old age of ninety-eight" and was "the 'first in
  Baghdad to teach unification (Tawhid) through the path of mysticism'" (p. 66, folio 50); **Abu
  Saʿid al-Kharraz**, "a cobbler by trade", who defined *fanaʾ* and "baga' [OCR?] [baqaʾ]", whose
  "Kitab al-Sidg [OCR?] [al-Sidq] … has been published with an English translation by A.J. Arberry"
  and lists "fear, hope, trust, love, shame, longing, intimacy", and whose death date is **the book's
  own admission of indeterminacy** — "uncertain, but it appears to have been sometime between
  279/892 and 286/899" (pp. 67–68, folios 51–52); **al-Shibli**, "of Khurasanian origin but had been
  born in Baghdad or Samarra" — **an unresolved alternative** — former "governor of Damawand", who
  begged "in the streets of Baghdad", was "committed to a mental asylum because of his ideas" and
  "died in 334/946 at the age of eighty seven" (p. 75, folio 59; also pp. 76, 382; folios 60, 360);
  **Nuri**, named only in the masters list (p. 77, folio 61).
- **Zuʾn-Nun al-Misri** — "A native of Ikhmim in Upper Egypt, he was born about 180/796. He made a
  study of medicine and alchemy"; "In 214/829 he was accused of heresy, arrested and sent to
  Baghdad. After examination the Caliph had him released"; "He died at Jiza in 246/860-61" (p. 63,
  folio 47) — **the Caliph of the trial is not named (not stated)**; also pp. 64, 67 (folios 48,
  51).
- **The Khurasan and Transoxiana masters, with the dates the book prints** — **Ibrahim bin Adham**,
  "born about 112/730 in the Arab community settlement in Balkh", migrated to Syria "Some time
  before 137/754", "died in c. 161/777-78", "married and left a child in Balkh, but advocated
  celibacy for mystics" (pp. 49–50, 275, 344; folios 33–34, 255, 322); **Abu ʿAli Shaqiq bin
  Ibrahim**, printed "Abu ‘Ali Shagiq [OCR?] [Shaqiq] bin Ibrahim al-Azali [OCR?] [no confident
  reading; do not normalise]", died "as a martyr … in 194/810" (pp. 50, 52; folios 34, 36); **Hatim
  al-Asamm (the Deaf)**, "author of a number of works on ethics" (printed "onethics"), who "died at
  Washjard near Tirmiz in 237/851-52" (pp. 52–53, 56; folios 36–37, 40); **Abu Hamid Ahmad ibn
  Khazruya**, "Married to the daughter of the local governor", who "wore the clothes of a soldier"
  and "died in 240/854 at the age of ninety-five" (p. 53, folio 37); **Fuzayl ibn Iyaz**, "Born in
  Samarqand", "a gallant and discriminating bandit, he robbed neither women nor the poor", who "died
  in Mecca in 187/803" (pp. 53–54, 132, 315–316, 409; folios 37–38, 114, 293–294, 385);
  **ʿAbduʾllah ibn al-Mubarak al-Hanzali**, "Born in 118/736—37 of a Turkic father and an Iranian
  mother", "a very rich merchant", who "died at Hit on the Euphrates in 181/797" (p. 54, folio 38);
  **Abu Turab al-Nasafi of Nakhshab** (printed "of Nakshab"), who "died in 245/859-60" (p. 56, folio
  40); **al-Hakim al-Tirmizi**, "the most eminent saint of Tirmiz", whose influence "extended from
  Ghazali of Tus to Ibn al-ʿArabi of Spain", five works named, who "retired to Nishapur where he
  died some time after 285/898" (pp. 56–58, folio 40–42; also p. 115, folio 97, **which names an
  archive principal figure and is carried to the Misfiled section**); **Abu Hafs al-Haddad**, "By
  profession a blacksmith, he was converted to sufism through an encounter with a Jew", who "died in
  his home town in 265/879" (p. 58, folio 42); **Hamdun al-Qassar**, "The founder of a unique path
  in sufism", "He died in 271/884-85"; "The path of Hamdun was that of malamat (blame)" (p. 58,
  folio 42) — **the archive's `madho-lal-hussain` row carries the silsila "Malamati"; the book makes
  no such connection and none is asserted here**; **Abu ʿUsman al-Hiri**, who "died at Nishapur in
  298/910—-11" (p. 59, folio 43); **Yahya ibn Muʿaz al-Razi**, who "After losing everything in a
  robbery … settled in Nishapur … died in Nishapur in 258/871-72" (p. 59, folio 43); **Abu Yazid of
  Bastam (Bayazid)**, whose "grandfather had been a Zoroastrian", who "met one hundred and thirteen
  spiritual teachers during his thirty years of roaming. This would, however, seem to be based on
  legend", and who "died in **261/874 or 264/877-78**" (p. 59, folio 43) — **two death dates side by
  side, neither preferred**; also pp. 67, 75–76, 80, 184, 256, 265, 344–348 (folios 51, 59–60, 64,
  166, 236, 245, 322–326), **printed in three spellings, "Bayazid Bastami", "Abu Yazid" and
  "Abu-Yazid Albistami"**; **Abu ʿAli Sindi**, from whom "Some hagiologists suggest that Bayazid
  learnt the doctrine of fanaʾ" (p. 61, folio 45) — **the nisba is the whole of the Indian-origin
  controversy**; **Abu Yaʿqub Yusuf ibn al-Husain of Rayy**, who "died in 304/916-17" and whose
  "lectures generally failed to attract an audience" (p. 61, folio 45); **Abuʾl Fawaris Shah ibn
  Shujaʿ of Kirman**, "said to have been a scion of a princely family", author of "several books on
  sufism, none of which have survived" (pp. 59, 61; folios 43, 45) — **no death date (not stated)**;
  **Muhammad ibn Khafif of Shiraz**, "born in 270/882. Like Abuʾl Fawaris he was of royal blood, but
  for him the prayer carpet was infinitely preferable to the coronet", who made "pilgrimages to
  Mecca at least six times" and died "at Shiraz in 371/982" (pp. 61–62, folios 45–46); **Sahl
  al-Tustari**, "born in c. 200/815 and studied first with Sufyan al-Sawri and later with Zuʾn-Nun
  Misri", who "died in Basra in 282/896" (pp. 62–63, 315–316; folios 46–47, 293–294) — **the Pass 1
  worker flagged the chronology of his teacher as a live doubt**; **Abu Sulaiman al-Darani**, who
  "retired to Daraya, near Damascus, where he died in 215/830" (pp. 64–65, folios 48–49); and the
  masters list in one sentence, "al-Hakim in Tirmiz, Abu'l-ʿAbbas Sayyar in Marw, Qassar in
  Nishapur, Bayazid in Bastam, Khafif in Shiraz, Sahl in Tustar, and around Kharraz, Muhasibi,
  Junaid and Nuri in Baghdad" (p. 77, folio 61).
- **The first generations** — the **Ahl al-Suffa**, "called Ahi al-Suffa [OCR?] [Ahl al-Suffa] or
  Ashab-i Suffa (The People of the Verandah)", who "observed incessant prayer and fasting, as did
  the Prophet himself" (pp. 37–38, folios 21–22); **Abu Zar al-Ghifari**, "died in 652 or 653", whose
  "revolutionary outspokenness led him into court exile" (p. 38, folio 22); **Abu Darda ʿUwaymar bin
  Zaid**, "one hour of reflection was better than forty nights of prayer" (p. 38, folio 22); **Uways
  al-Qarani**, sought out "in Qaran, an oasis habitation in the Najd desert", a casualty at Siffin
  "between June and July 657" (p. 41, folio 25); **Hasan al-Basri**, "(642-728)", "Basra's pride",
  "too young to become the disciple of 'Ali bin Abi Talib (656-61), yet the sufi orders trace their
  spiritual descent through him from Muhammad and 'Ali", in hiding "in 705 … until Hajjaj's death in
  714" (p. 43, folio 27) — **"Hasan's discipleship with 'Ali and his conversion by him, would appear
  to be pious myths composed by later writers"** (p. 43, folio 27); also pp. 45, 132, 315–316 (folios
  29, 114, 293–294); **Habib ibn Muhammad**, "Prior to being Hasan's disciple, Habib had been a
  usurer, known for his evil habits" (p. 45, folio 29); **Malik bin Dinar**, "(d.c. 127/744) … had
  led an evil life before converting to sufism" (pp. 45–46, folios 29–30); **Abu Hashim**, "the
  first spiritualist to be known as a sufi", died "at the end of the eighth century" (p. 47, folio
  31); **Sufyan al-Sawri**, "born at Kufa in 97/715-16", who fled "to Mecca in 158/774-75" and "died
  in 161/777-78", whose "school of jurisprudence … survived for about two centuries" (pp. 46–48,
  folios 30–32).
- **Rabiʿa bint Ismaʿil al-ʿAdwiya** — printed "Rabi‘a bint Isma‘il al--Adwiya" [OCR?]; "sold into
  slavery as a child, having been seized by a man in the street and sold for six dirhams", footnoted
  "A coin worth less than a penny"; "The date of her birth is unknown, but she appears to have died
  in 135/752 or 185/801" — **two death dates, neither preferred**; "She remained celibate throughout
  her life"; visited by "Hasan Basri, Malik Dinar, Sufyan Sauri and Shaqiq Balkhi"; her retort that
  "at least no woman had ever been a hermaphrodite"; two prayers quoted in full (pp. 46–47, folios
  30–31; also pp. 425, 427, folio not stated and 403). Her biographer **Fariduʾd-Din ʿAttar** "(d. c.
  1220)" "apparently gained access to a lost work on Rabi'a" (p. 46, folio 30).
- **The Persian Sufi poets** — **Sanaʾi**, "born in Ghazna, or Balkh in the fifth/eleventh century
  and believed to have died in Ghazna in 525/1130-31. Browne, however, has suggested he died later,
  around 1150" — **birthplace and death year both left open by the book** — a court poet who
  "relinquished his post, retiring to the life of a dervish", author of the "Hadigatu'l-Hagiga wa
  Shari'atu't-Tariga [OCR?] [Hadiqatu'l-Haqiqa wa Shari'atu't-Tariqa]" (pp. 34–35, 95, 229; folios
  not stated/19, 79, 209); **Fariduʾd-Din ʿAttar**, "born in 537/1142-43, either in Nishapur or its
  neighbourhood … died there in 617/1220, the year the Mongols invaded the town", with **an
  attribution problem stated outright: "during this period there were two writers called 'Attar in
  Nishapur, and a large number of works ascribed to Faridu'd-Din 'Attar were in fact written by the
  other 'Attar"**, and the *Mantiquʾt-Tayr*'s "seven dangerous valleys: Quest, Love, Mystic-Knowledge,
  Detachment, Unification, Bewilderment and Fulfilment in Annihilation" (p. 96, folio 80; also
  pp. 75, 217–218, 256; folios 59, 199–200, 236) — **not Baba Farid; see Traps**; **Jalaluʾd-Din
  Rumi**, "born at Balkh in Rabi' I 604/September 1207", father "Baha'u'd-Din Walad" forced out
  "in 614/1217", the family settling in Quniya "In 626/1228"; Shams of Tabriz's arrival "In
  642/1244", flight "to Damascus in 643/1246" and murder "in 645/1247 … his body later being thrown
  down a well"; "Jalalu'd-Din Rumi died in Quniya in Jumada II, 672/December, 1273"; the *Masnawi*
  "divided into six books and contains about 26,660 couplets … begun after urgings from the
  Mawlana's favourite disciple, Husamu'd-Din Chelebi", dictated "whenever he was in the grip of
  ecstasy"; "There is no clear structure in the Masnawi" (pp. 97–98, folios 81–82) — **no tomb and no
  shrine at Quniya is mentioned (not stated)**; **Baba Tahir ʿUryan**, whose life "was spent in the
  area between Hamadan and Luristan", who reproached Tughril at Hamadan "In 447/1055" — "Oh Turk,
  how are you going to act towards the Muslims?" — and whose "al-Kalimat al-Qisar (Brief Sayings)"
  V. Minorsky selected from (pp. 92, 323; folios 76, 301) — **no birth or death date, no tomb, no
  silsila (not stated)**; **Saʿdi of Shiraz**, "(b. between 610—5/1213-19, d. 691/1292)" (p. 229,
  folio 209); **Nizami of Ganja**, who "died on 4 Ramazan 605/12 March 1209", his *Khamsa* and its
  parts dated at p. 385 (folio 363); **Hafiz (c. 1325-89)** (pp. 258, 384; folios 238, 362);
  **Jami**, "(817/1414-898/1492)", whose *Nafahatuʾl-Uns* was "completed in 883/1478-79" and who
  died "on 9 November 1492" (pp. 25, 111, 115, 122, 225, 246, 309, 395; folios 11, 93, 97, 104, 205,
  226, 287, 371).
- **The Qalandariyya founders** — **Yusuf**, "a Spanish Arab from Egypt", the Qalandariyyas first
  appearing "in Damascus in c. 610/1213-14" per al-Maqrizi (p. 324, folio 302); **Shaikh Jamaluʾd-Din
  of Sawa**, a student at Damascus "Between 1210 and 1225", then "Damietta on the Nile in Egypt",
  whose shaving of beard and eyebrows was "later adopted as the distinguishing feature of the
  Qalandariyya", his town identified by Gibb "with Sawaq or Sawaj, a city between Hamadan and
  Tehran" (p. 324, folio 302); **Hasan al-Jawaliqi** (section A). The distinction from the malamatis
  at pp. 323–324 (folios 301–302).
- **Shaikh Ahmad bin ʿAli al-Rifaʿi (1106-82)** and the Rifaʿiyya, "of Egypt, Iraq, Syria and
  Turkey", who "were significantly influenced by wandering yogis" (pp. 332, 345, 428; folios 310,
  323, not stated).
- **The early Sufi authors** — **Abu Saʿid ibn al-ʿArabi**, "born in Basra, but moved to Mecca, where
  he remained until his death in 341/952-53 … Junaid's disciple and for about thirty years a Hadis
  teacher in Mecca", his *Tabaqat al-Nussak* "not survived" (p. 78, folio 62) — **not the Ibn ʿArabi
  of the Futuhat; the book makes no such claim**; **Jaʿfar al-Khuldi**, "died in 348/959-60", whose
  *Hikayat al-Awliyaʾ* "has not survived" (p. 78, folio 62); **al-Sarraj al-Tusi**, "(d. 378/988-89)",
  whose *Kitab al-Lumaʿ* "still exists and has been critically edited" (pp. 76, 78, 104; folios 60,
  62, 88); **Abu Talib al-Makki**, "(d. 386/996—97)", author of the *Qut al-Qulub* (pp. 45, 66, 70,
  78, 80, 198; folios 29, 50, 54, 62, 64, 180); **al-Kalabazi**, "a native of Bukhara who wrote in
  Arabic. He died in 388/998-99", and **Mustamli**, who translated him and "died in 434/1042-43"
  (p. 78, folio 62); **al-Sulami of Nishapur**, "He died in 412/1021-22", whose *Tabaqat* "laid the
  foundations of a genre" and whose "Haq'iq al-Tafsir [OCR?] gives an important insight into the
  sufi understanding of Qurʾanic teaching" (pp. 78–79, folios 62–63); **Abu Nuʿaim**, "who died in
  430/1038—39" (p. 79, folio 63); **al-Qushairi of Nishapur**, "who died in 465/1072", whose *Risala*
  "was undertaken between 437 and 438/1045 and 1046" — **a dated composition window, one of only two
  in that chunk** — translated by **Abu ʿAli ʿUsmani** "before Qushairi's death in 465/1072", and
  quoting **Abu ʿAli ad-Daqqagq [OCR?] [ad-Daqqaq]** (pp. 79, 87, 93, 271, 273; folios 63, 71, 77,
  251, 253).
- **Ibn al-Jawzi** — "(510/1126-597/1200)", "the greatest Hanbali theologian in Baghdad", given by
  the Caliphs "what amounted virtually to inquisitorial powers", author of the *Nagd [OCR?] [Naqd]
  al-ʿIlm waʾl-ʿulamaʾ* and *Talbis Iblis*, "translated as 'The Devil's Delusion' by Margoliouth"
  (pp. 103–104, folios 87–88).
- **Khizr / Khwaja Khizr** — "A mysterious figure who appeared in Islamic legends, there was some
  controversy as to whether Khizr was a prophet"; "he had drunk the fountain of life, had been
  rendered immortal … a contemporary of every age"; the guide of Moses in "verses 59 to 81 of
  Chapter 18 of the Qur'an"; "His name was invoked in times of danger especially by merchants and
  travellers"; "In India Khwaja Khizr is recognized as a river God or a spirit of wells and streams"
  (p. 118, folio 100); in Bengal "believed to travel by fish, a counterpart of the fish incarnation
  of Vishnu" and worshipped with Pir Badr (p. 339, folio 317); one of the Panch Pir (pp. 339–340,
  folios 317–318); a mosque named after him at Quqa (p. 434, folio 410); also p. 152 (folio 134).
  **Not the historical Shaikh Khizr of Nagaur (p. 149, folio 131).**
- **Ibn Battuta** — born at Tangier "on 17 Rajab 703/24 February 1304", left "on 13 June 1325",
  reached the Indus "on 12 September 1333", the Deccan "in July 1342", Fez "in November 1349",
  Sijilmasa "at the end of 1353", and "died in Morocco in either 1368-69 or 1377" — **two death
  dates, neither preferred**; he dictated to **Ibn Juzayy (1321-1357)**, provided by **Abu ʿInan
  (1349-58) of Fez**; "The work was completed in December 1357" (p. 30, folio 16); he supplies the
  Sawa shaving story (p. 324, folio 302), the Haidaris' iron rings (p. 329, folio 307), the Amroha
  party "in c. 1342 ap [OCR?] [AD]" **whose quotation is cut off at the chunk boundary** (p. 331,
  folio 309), the Ceylon temple and the Quqa mosque (pp. 411, 434; folios 387, 410), and the two
  Shah Jalal confusions (pp. 220, 337; folios 202, 315).
- **Other named figures of the wider world** — **al-Harawi**, author of "the Jsharat ila ma'‘rifat
  al-ziyarat [OCR?] [Isharat ila maʿrifat al-ziyarat]", "who died in 611/1214" (p. 33, folio 17) —
  **the one work in the book explicitly about visiting shrines**; **Husain**, "massacred on 10
  Muharram 61/10 October 680" at Karbala, and **Hasan**, "b. 624-25", "poisoned in 669-70" (pp. 38,
  42; folios 22, 26); **Zayn al-ʿAbidin (d. 712)** and **al-Baqir (d. 731)**, printed "al-Bagir
  [OCR?]" (p. 42, folio 26); **Jaʿfar al-Sadiq**, "born 699-700 or 702-03", "killed in 765", from
  whom Abu Hanifa, Malik bin Anas, "Wasil bin 'Ata" and "Jabir bin Hayyan, the founder of Arabic
  alchemy" learnt (pp. 42, 267; folios 26, 247); **ʿAli al-Riza**, "born in Medina in 148/765 …
  summoned to Marw by Caliph al-Ma'mun (813-33) in 201/816 and was killed at Tus, two years later in
  818" (p. 65, folio 49); **al-Muqannaʿ, the Veiled Prophet of Khurasan** (p. 55, folio 39);
  **al-Biruni**, "(b. 973, died after 1050)", who "by 1000 ab [OCR?] [A.D.]" wrote the "A/-Asar
  al-Bagiyya'an il Qurun al-Khaliyya" [OCR?], with his authority **Abuʾl-ʿAbbas Iran Shahri** and
  **Zurkan** (pp. 48–49, 345–348; folios 32–33, 323–326); **Ibn Sina (Avicenna)**,
  "(370/980—428/1037)", in conversation with Abu Saʿid (pp. 80, 88, 186; folios 64, 72, 168);
  **Nizamuʾl-Mulk Tusi**, "(b. c. 1018, died 1092)" (pp. 80, 83; folios 64, 67); **Ibn Taymiyya
  (1263-1328)** and "Maulana 'Abdu'l-'Aziz Ardbili", who "probably reached India from Syria sometime
  after 1330" (p. 194, folio 176); **Zamakhshari**, "1075-1144, an Iranian from Khwarazam [OCR?]
  [Khwarazm]", whose *al-Kashshaf* was "completed in 1134" and *al-Mufassal* written "between
  1119-21" (p. 266, folio 246); **Shaikh ʿAbduʾllah al-Yafeʿi al-Yamani**, "the most respected saint
  in Islam's holiest city" (p. 299, folio 277); **Nasir-i Khusraw**, "born in 1004 … died between
  1072 and 1077" (p. 186, folio 168); **Khwaja Muhammad bin Sirin** (pp. 250–251, folios 230–231);
  **Saiyid Ahmad Yasawi** (p. 336, folio 314); **Shaikh Burhanuʾd-Din Sagharji of China** (p. 337,
  folio 315); **Shaikh ʿAzizuʾd-Din Nasafi (d. 661/1263)** (p. 308, folio 286); **Mir Saiyid Sharif
  Jurjani** (p. 307, folio 285); **Shaikh Auhaduʾd-Din Kirmani** (pp. 138, 153; folios 120, 135);
  **Nuruʾd-Din ʿAbduʾr Rahman al-Isfaraʾini al-Kasirqi, "(d. 717/1317)"**, and **Saʿduʾllah**
  (pp. 268–269, folios 248–249); **Sadruʾd-Din Ibrahim, son of Saʿduʾd-Din al-Hamuya** (p. 269, folio
  249); **Abu Madyan Shuʿaib bin al-Husain (1126-98)** (p. 428, folio not stated); **al-Maqrizi**
  (p. 324, folio 302); **Daulat Shah Samarqandi** and **Kashifi** (p. 269, folio 249).
- **The jurists, theologians and philosophers with dates** — "Abu Hanifa who died in 767", "Malik bin
  Anas (d. 795)", "Al-Shafi'i who died in 820", "Ahmad bin Hanbal whose death occurred in 855"
  (p. 38, folio 22); "Abu'l Hasan al-Ash'ari (b. 873-74, d. 935-36)" (p. 39, folio 23); "al-Kindi
  (d. c. 850), Razi (865-925), Farabi (d. 950) and Ibn Sina or Avicenna (980-1037)" (p. 40, folio
  24); "Ahmad ibn Hanbal (780-855) … the founder of the puritanically orthodox Sunni school of
  jurisprudence, called Hanbali" (pp. 66, 68; folios 50, 52) — **dates in CE only, the one bare-CE
  lifespan in that chunk**; "Imam Abu Hanifa (699-767)" (p. 180, folio 162); **Maulana Raziuʾd-Din
  Saghani**, "the eminent Bada'un-born Indian" author of the *Mashariquʾl-Anwar*, "a selection of
  2,253 Hadises", "a most critica] [critical] author" who "rejected many Hadises, long regarded as
  correct" (pp. 177, 214, 342; folios 159, 196, 320); **Maulana Burhanuʾd-Din Marghniyani**, author
  of the *Hidaya*, and his grandson, "Timur's Shaikhu'l-Islam" (p. 294, folio 274); **Maulana
  Muʿinuʾd-Din ʿUmrani**, "an enemy of sufism" (p. 293, folio 273); **Mulla ʿAbduʾl-Malik ʿAdil of
  Jaunpur** (p. 286, folio 266); **Imam Abuʾl-Lais Samarqandi** (p. 163, folio 145); **Qazi Saʿd and
  Qazi ʿImad** (p. 214, folio 196); **Ikhtisan Dabir** (pp. 200–201, folios 182–183).
- **Christian, Jewish and non-Muslim religious figures** — "Isaac of Nineveh in the seventh century";
  "Aphraates, the monk, who lived in Iran during the fourth century"; "Ephraim, the Syrian, also of
  the fourth century"; "Simon of Taibutheh, an East Syrian who died in 680"; "Abraham bar Dashandad,
  an East Syrian, who lived sometime around 720 and 730" (p. 65, folio 49); "St. John of the Cross:
  'Two natures (God and man) in a single spirit and love of God!'" (p. 74, folio 58); "the Nestorians
  who held that Mary, through self-mortifica-tion, annihilated all human qualities… and Jesus was the
  result of this union" (p. 67, folio 51); **Iblis**, "whose monotheism, Hallaj believed, prevented
  him from prostrating before Adam" (pp. 73–74, folios 57–58).

### D. Hagiographers, biographical dictionaries and their authors — the archive's own source base

*Chapter One is a survey of the literature this archive's shrine descriptions ultimately rest on.
None of these authors is an archive principal figure; the list is kept complete because the
provenance question it answers is the archive's own.*

- **Shaikh ʿAbduʾl-Haqq al-Dihlawi**, *Akhbaruʾl-Akhyar fi Asraruʾl-Abrar* — "born in January-February
  1551 and died in 1642-43", "famous as a muhaddis", studies "both in India and Hejaz", library
  "extensive"; the work "incorporated 225 biographies of Indian sufis", "began with a note on Shaikh
  'Abdw't-Qadir Jilani"[OCR?] "and ended with an account of the author's own ancestry", "written
  before 996/1588 but was later revised in 999/1590-91" (pp. 25–26, folios 11–12); its tabaqat heads
  at p. 26 (folio 12); its chapter "on women saints" (p. 425, folio not stated); cited as an
  authority at pp. 170, 172, 217, 233, 271, 278–279, 283, 308, 326, 334, 427–428 (folios 152, 154,
  199, 213, 251, 258–259, 263, 286, 304, 312, 403, not stated).
- **The malfuzat Rizvi calls spurious** — the *Anisuʾl-Arwah*, the *Dalilu'l-'Arifin* and the
  *Fawaʾiduʾs-Salikin*; "the spurious malfuzat of the great Indian Chishtis were embroidered by
  anonymous authors" (p. 18, folio 4), and they "were not available while Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din
  Auliya' was alive" (pp. 18–19, folios 4–5). **The two Firdausiyya titles "Ganj-i La Yafna" and
  "Ganj-i La Yakhfa" differ by two letters and the book calls the second "a similar title"** (pp.
  22–23, folios 8–9). Others: *Ahsanuʾl-Aqwal*, *Jawamiʿuʾl-Kilam*, the *Siyar-i Muhammadi* "In
  831/1427-28" by "Muhammad 'Ali Samani", and the *Rafiquʾl ʿArifin* (pp. 21, 24, 28; folios 7, 10,
  14).
- **The dictionaries in the order Chapter One gives them** — **Jami**, *Nafahatuʾl-Uns*, "completed in
  883/1478-79 … an enlarged version of Shaikh 'Abdu'llah Ansari's Tabagat al-Sufiyya"[OCR?] (p. 25,
  folio 11); **Kashifi**, "Rashahat-i 'Ainu'l Hayat", "written in 909/1503-04", "valuable for the
  information it gives on the Naqshbandis" (p. 25, folio 11); **Abuʾl-Fazl ʿAllami "(1551-1602)"**
  and **ʿAbduʾs-Samad**, the *Akhbaruʾl-Asfiyaʾ* "in 1015/1606—07" (p. 26, folio 12); **Muhammad
  Ghausi Shattari**, *Gulzar-i Abrar*, "the biographies of 575 sufis", "A resident of Mandu",
  "dedicated to the Emperor Jahangir" (p. 27, folio 13); **Ilah-diya**, "Saiyaru'l-Aqtab"[OCR?],
  "completed in 1056/1646—47", "legendary and fantastic … particularly of the Sabiriyya branch"
  (p. 27, folio 13); **Muhammad Hashim**, "Zubdatu'l-Mugamat"[OCR?] [Maqamat], "compiled in
  1037/1627-28", "a biographical dictionary of the Naqshbandis" (p. 27, folio 13); **Muhammad Sadiq
  Kashmiri Hamadani**, "the biographies of 125 sufis who were all buried in Delhi … the Kalimatu
  's-Sadigin"[OCR?], plus the *Tabaqat-i Shah-Jahani* (pp. 27, 445; folios 13, 421); **Mir ʿAli Akbar
  Husaini Ardastani**, *Majmaʿul-Auliyaʾ*, "about 1,400 sufis, both Indian and non-Indian" (p. 27,
  folio 13); **Dara Shukoh "(born 1615, killed 1659)"**, *Safinatuʾl-Auliyaʾ* "completed on 27
  Ramazan 1049/21 January 1640", and **Jahan Ara Begum**, *Munisuʾl-Arwah* (p. 27, folio 13) — **the
  name is spelt "Dara Shukoh" and "Dara Shikoh" three lines apart**; **Shaikh ʿAbduʾr-Rahman
  Chishti**, who "succeeded to the leadership of the Chishti order in 1032/1622", "A scholar of
  Sanskrit", whose *Mirʾatuʾl-Asrar* "was started in 1045/1635 and not completed until 1065/1654",
  and whose *Mirʾat-i Madari* and *Mirʾat-i Masʿudi* are in section A (pp. 28, 334–335, 340; folios
  14, 312–313, 318); **Shaikh ʿAlaʾuʾd-Din Muhammad Chishti of Barnawa**, "Chishtiyya-i Bahishtiya or
  Firdausiya-i Qudsiya", "around 1076/1665-66" (p. 28, folio 14); **Ghulam Muʿinuʾd-Din ʿAbduʾllah
  Khweshgi of Qasur**, printed "Ghulam Mu'inu'd-Rin"[OCR?], whose *Maʿarijuʾl-Wilayat* of
  1094/1682-83 "quotes at great length excerpts from the writings and letters of sufis and of their
  documents which are not to be found elsewhere" (p. 28, folio 14) — **"Qasur" is Kasur, an archive
  location, and the sentence is carried to the Misfiled section**; **Muhammad Bulaq Dihlawi**
  (*Matlubuʾt-Tabin*, "In 1111/1699-1700"), **Muhammad Habibullah Akbarabadi** (*Zikr-i Jamiʿ-i
  Auliyaʾ-i Dihli*, "completed in 1140/1727-28"), **Muhammad Akram** (*Sawatiʿuʾl-Anwar*, "completed
  by the author in 1142/1729") (p. 28, folio 14); **Mir Ghulam ʿAli Azad Bilgarami**,
  *Rauzatuʾl-Auliyaʾ*, "written in 1161/1748 … ten saints buried at Khuldabad" (p. 29, folio 15);
  **Gul Muhammad Maʿrufi Karkhi Chishti Ahmadpuri**, "who died in 1827", author of a continuation of
  the *Siyaruʾl-Auliyaʾ* and "a valuable record of the lives of seventeenth and eighteenth century
  Chishtis" (p. 29, folio 15).
- **Mufti Muhammad Ghulam Sarwar of Lahore** — **the most important figure in Chapter One for this
  archive's source base.** *Khazinatuʾl-Asfiyaʾ*, "completed in 1281/1864-65", "the most widely
  read" of the nineteenth-century dictionaries; "a prolific author"; his "vast library of books on
  sufism which, at the end of the nineteenth and in the early twentieth century, were dispensed
  amongst a large number of Panjabi book lovers and scholars"; Rizvi's verdict, "Using unreliable
  sufi family trees, Ghulam Sarwar accepted every date given in these texts and composed verses of
  chronograms", "giving scholars an opportunity to glorify the mission of sufis and the impact of
  their miracles in the Islamization of the sub-continent" (p. 29, folio 15); his book is cited
  twice more and rejected both times (p. 398, folio 374). **"of Lahore" is an archive location and
  is carried to the Misfiled section.**
- **Minhaj Siraj** — "an 'alim and a qazi but he was also a friend of sufis" (p. 29, folio 15), yet
  head of "another group amongst the 'ulama'" who "certainly did not approve of sufis dabbling in
  the political arena" (p. 30, folio 16); "Despite his official position as a Qazi, Minhaj Siraj was
  a great supporter of sama'" (p. 215, folio 197); also pp. 153, 242, 247, 276 (folios 135, 222,
  227, 256).
- **The manuscript collections and repositories** — "such extensive collections as those of Sir Salar
  Jang of Hyderabad, Nauwab"[OCR?] [Nawwab] "Habibu'r-Rahman Khan Shirwani of Habibganj, Aligarh, and
  of Professor Hafiz Mahmud Shirani in Lahore, have now been deposited in various libraries and
  museums and can therefore be researched by scholars" (p. 16, folio 2); "Muhammad Bashir Husain,
  Fihrist makhtuta-i shirani, Lahore, 1968." (p. 449, folio 425), **the only Pakistani manuscript
  catalogue in the bibliography**; "India Office", "Bodleian, 1263", "Ethé, 1839/1844/1854/1867",
  "Rieu, 632", "Mashhad MS., 7879", "Manchester manuscript", "Habibganj collection, Aligarh
  University manuscript", "Panjab University manuscript", "British Museum Rieu", "Bodleian, 262",
  "India Office, D.P. 1107" (pp. 134–147, 149, 252–264, 448; folios 116–129, 131, 232–244, 424). The
  named works of the Ajmer chapter, with their variant spellings, at pp. 134–147 (folios 116–129).
- **The Bibliography in bulk, as the Pass 1 worker characterised it** — part one, pp. 442–448 (folios
  418–424), "roughly two hundred and thirty entries of Arabic and Persian primary sources", about a
  third manuscripts; part two, pp. 448–449 (folios 424–425), "roughly eighty entries in Sanskrit,
  Hindi and Urdu", plus "seven of Rizvi's own Hindi titles and an Urdu trilogy by Shaikh Muhammad
  Ikram from Lahore and Karachi"; part three, pp. 450–456 (folios 426–432), "roughly three hundred
  entries in English, French and German". **"These counts are approximate; I counted by page and did
  not tally line by line."** The bibliography's own rule: "The articles published in journals are
  not listed." (p. 456, folio 432). **Publishers' cities are imprints, not toponyms.** The Sikh
  shelf (eleven titles, pp. 451–455, folios 427–431), the Nath shelf (six titles, pp. 448–450, folios
  424–426), the Kashmir cluster (pp. 442–455, folios 418–431, including "Rafiqi, A.Q., Sufism in
  Kashmir from the fourteenth to the sixteenth century, Australian National University Thesis,
  1972.", indexed at "Australian National University, 353", p. 460, folio 436) and seven
  Pakistan-subject titles (pp. 450–455, folios 426–431) are recorded in full in the Misfiled section
  where they bear on archive rows, and otherwise here.

### E. Rulers, dynasties and officials named — none is an archive principal figure

*The one royal name that IS an archive principal figure, Sultan Qutbuʾd-Din Aibak, is in the
Misfiled section.*

- **Caliphs and Middle Eastern rulers** — "Harun al-Rashid (786-809)" (pp. 51, 53, 267; folios 35,
  37, 247); "Caliph Mutawakkil (847-61)" (p. 64, folio 48); "al-Ma'mun (813-33)" (p. 65, folio 49);
  "al-Mansur's (754-75)" (p. 267, folio 247); "al-Walid I (705-15)" (pp. 127, 311; folios 109, 289);
  "'Usman (644-56)" (p. 38, folio 22); "'Abdu'l Malik (685-705) and 'Umar II (717-20)" (p. 43, folio
  27); "Abu Bakr the Trusty", "'Umar the Discriminator", "'Usman of the Two Lights", "'Ali the
  Well-Approved" (p. 51, folio 35); "al-Nasir (575/1179-622/1225)", "al-Malik al-Adil 1 [OCR?] [I]
  Saifu'd-Din (596/1200-615/1218)", "'Ala'u'd-Din Muhammad (596/1200—-617/1220)", "*Ala'u'd-Din
  Kay-Qubaz [OCR?] [Kay-Qubad] I (616/1219-634/1237)" (p. 104, folio 88); "al-Qa'im
  (422/1031-467/1075)" (p. 91, folio 75 corrected); "al-Musta'sim Bi'llah (640/1242-656/1258)"
  (p. 337, folio 315); "al-Malik al-'Adil Ketbogha (1294-96)" (p. 325, folio 303); "Hajjaj, Walid's
  governor in Iraq" (p. 127, folio 109); "'Aziz known as al-Khammar" (p. 332, folio 310).
- **The eastern dynasties with the book's dates** — Tahirids "from 205/821 to 259/873"; Saffarids
  "Founded in Sistan in 253/867, by Ya'quub [OCR?] [Ya'qub] bin Lays al-Saffar, a coppersmith", the
  Mongols ruling Sistan "until 885/1480"; Samanids "Between 204/819 and 395/1005 … capital at
  Bukhara"; Qarakhanids "between 382/992 and 607/1211"; Ghaznawids under "Sebuktigin
  (366/977-387/997)" and "Mas'ud (1030-40)"; Ghurids under "'Ala'u'd-Din … Jahan Suz (Incendiary of
  the World)", "Bahram Shah (1118-52)", "Ghiyas'ud-Din Muhammad of Ghur (558/1163—599/1203)" and
  "Shihabu'd-Din (later Mu'izzu'd-Din Muhammad 599/1203—602/1206)"; Saljuqs, "In 429/1038 their
  leader, Tughril, (1038-63) in his capital Nishapur, proclaimed himself Sultan of Khurasan"
  (pp. 82–83, folios 66–67); Buwayhids "From 320/932 to 454/1062" from "the Daylamite region of the
  highlands of Gilan" (p. 92, folio 76); Mongols, "In 1221 the last Khwarazm-Shah, Jalalu'd-Din, was
  driven by Chingiz across the Indus, and in 1256 the Il-khanid branch of the Mongols seized
  Baghdad, assassinating the last 'Abbasid, al-Musta'sim, two years later. The capital … was Tabriz"
  (p. 84, folio 68); Ilkhanids "Hulagu (1256-65)", "Arghun (1284-91)", "Ghazan Khan Mahmud
  (1295-1304)", "Uljaytu (1304-16)" (pp. 268–270, folios 248–250); "Berke (1257-67)" (p. 247, folio
  227); "Tarmashirin, the Chghta'i [OCR?] [Chaghta'i] king of Transoxiana", beaten "in the Ghazna
  region in 1326" (p. 193, folio 175); "Chingiz Khan", spelt "Chinghiz Khan" on the facing page
  (pp. 183–184, folios 165–166); "Jalalu'd-Din Mingburnu", defeated "November 1221" (pp. 151–152,
  folios 133–134); "Timur" (pp. 294, 430, 432; folios 274, 406, 408).
- **Mahmud of Ghazna** — "Yamain [OCR?] [*Yamin*] al-Dawla Mahmud (388/998-421/1030) established
  Ghaznawid rule over Khurasan, Afghanistan and the Panjab" (p. 83, folio 67); the Multan and
  Somnath campaigns and the Jats of Mansura (p. 128, folio 110); "the annexation of the Panjab by
  Mahmud of Ghazna … prompted many sufis to settle in the area" (p. 130, folio 112); "it was the

  (folios 70–72, 76, 289, 313). **"Malik Ayaz is not named anywhere" — (not stated) (p. 128, folio
  110) — and "He is not the principal figure of any archive row" (p. 83, folio 67): the book's whole

- **The Delhi Sultans** — Iltutmish "(1210-35)" (pp. 29, 143, 151, 153, 209–218, 243, 285; folios 15,

- **The provincial rulers** — **Bengal**: "in 1338 the Delhi Sultanate lost Bengal completely", "Ilyas

  the port of Chittagong and established cultural and diplomatic relations with China" and whose

### F. Orders, groups, castes and categories with no archive row

- **The Shikaftiyyah** — "a class of Khurasan sufis who lived in caves and were called Shikaftiyyah
  (from the word shikaft, cave)", implicitly "based on the life-pattern of a section of Buddhist
  monks" (p. 104, folio 88). **A named ascetic group with a dwelling type and no site — (not
  stated).**
- **The Karramis** — "A Sunni sect which was very popular in Khurasan around 370/980-81 … Their main
  centre was in Nishapur"; "The founder of the sect was Abu 'Abdullah Muhammad bin Karram of Sistan
  who died in Jerusalem in c. 255/869" (pp. 86–87, folios 70–71) — **the one instance in chunk 005 of
  a sufi being opposed by a named sect rather than by the ʿulamaʾ alone.**
- **The Haidariya, the Jwaliqis and the Madaris** — "the corporation known as the Haidariya", whose
  "main centres were in Jaunpur and the Bengal Sultanate" (p. 332, folio 310), wearing iron rings
  "through their ears, hands and other parts of their bodies" (p. 329, folio 307); **the Madaris**,
  described "By the time of the Mughal Emperor Shah Jahan (1628-1658)" — "his followers, the
  Madaris, wore no garments and smeared their bodies with ashes. The most perfect among them went
  naked even in the severest winters of Kashmir and Kabul … Their hair was tangled, and they wound
  iron chains around their heads and necks … they wore black turbans and carried black flags. They
  refused to observe traditional prayers and fasting and ate a large amount of hemp leaves, proudly
  relating stories of their excessive consumption of this intoxicating drug", with the sub-branch
  "Be Qaid wa Be Nawa (Without Ties and Material Concern)", who "made their khirgas [OCR?] [khirqas]
  from shreds which they collected from the streets. Whenever they wanted a gift they reviled the
  reluctant donor until their demands were fulfilled" (p. 341, folio 319) — **the fullest
  description of a living mendicant order in the book, and the archive has no Madari row.**
- **The Ismaʿilis of Sind and Multan, and the conversion of Sind** — the Fatimid dependency of Multan
  "Between 977 and 985", Mansura's acceptance of "the over-lordship of the Egyptian Caliphs", the
  doctrine of "batin", "ta'wil (esoteric exegesis)", "khass (élite)" and "tagiya [OCR?] [taqiya]",
  and the Sumira dynasty's rule of "the Lower Sind until the middle of the fourteenth century"
  (p. 128, folio 110); the named agents of conversion, the Qurʾan "translated into local regional
  dialects", scholars "sent to the 'Abbasid court", and "Abu Ma'‘shar¢ [OCR?] [Abu Maʿshar] Najih (d.
  170/787) … and the poet Abu'l-‘Ata> [OCR?] [Abuʾl-ʿAta] (died after 158/774)" as "leading literary
  figures from Sind" (p. 127, folio 109). **Multan and Sind are archive territory and this material
  is carried to the Misfiled section.**
- **The Naths, Siddhas and yogis** — "The term 'jogi' or 'yogi' in sufi literature, is never precisely
  defined, however the yogis referred to in later anecdotes are the Nath Yogis or Siddhas who
  acquired supernatural powers through hatha-yoga" (p. 130, folio 112); "From the time of the
  fifteenth century, Bengali sufism was strongly fused with indigenous mystic elements, mainly from
  Naths" (p. 280, folio 260); "The Naths and the Siddhas spread from the Panjab to Bengal" (p. 376,
  folio 354); "According to Nath esoteric practices, the human body is a fort in its own right"
  (p. 390, folio 368); "Discussions on the conception of the Ultimate Reality enhanced the mutual
  respect of the Naths and the sufis" (p. 355, folio 333). **The Nath figures the book names and the
  archive does not carry**, all p. 354 (folio 332) unless noted: **Matsyendra** (also
  "Matsyendranath", "Minanath", "Lui-pa", "meaning fish Lord in Tibetan"), "the first human guru of
  the cult … reported to have lived in the tenth century AD" (p. 353, folio 331); **Lakshinkara**, "a
  legendary princess of the mythical kingdom of Indrabhuti … compared to Eleanor of Aquitain"
  (p. 353, folio 331); **Nimnath**, "probably Nemi, the twenty-second Jain tirthankara";
  **Parasnath**, "probably Parsava, the twenty-third Jain tirthankara" — **carried to the Misfiled
  section**; **Bhutanath**, "'Ghost Lord,' probably the Buddha"; **Dayanath**; **Nagarjunanath**,
  "the Buddhist philosopher Nagarjuna (c. AD 100-200)"; **Bharatinath**; **Dandanath**, "founder of
  a cult of staff-bearing yogis"; **Puranbhagat**, who "with his half-brother Rasula, has inspired
  hundreds of miraculous tales"; **Charpati (Charpatinath)**, "a rasavada or alchemist and poet,
  some of whose verses in Panjabi are extant"; **Guga (Guganath)**, "whose power over serpents was
  phenomenal"; **Manikchandra**, "a bania by caste"; **Gahininath**; **Dharamnath**;
  **Jalandhari-pa**; **Kanu-pa**; **Mainamati**; **Gopi-Chand**. Index headwords "Adi Nath, 331",
  "Alakh, 366", "Alakh-Nath, 337", "Alakh Niranjan, 337", "Bhartinath, 332", "Bhutanath, 332"
  (pp. 458–462, folios 434–438), cited to the PDF pages they are printed on. **A concentration of
  Nath material at folios 331–344 that a `tilla-jogian` or `gorakhnath-goraknath-temple` entry would
  want; the Pass 1 worker could not see those pages and did not follow the numbers, and neither have
  I.**
- **The Sahajiya and Siddha poets** — **Tillo-Pada** and **Saraha-Pada**, "who flourished between the
  eighth and ninth centuries AD" (pp. 352–353, folios 330–331; p. 377, folio 355); **Kanha**, quoted
  in a footnote (p. 360, folio 338).
- **The Vaishnava teachers** — "Sandilya who lived around aD [OCR?] [AD] 100" and the "Pancharatra
  Agamas"; "Nathamuni, Yamuna and … Ramanuja"; "Shankaracharya" (p. 376, folio 354); "the Alvars",
  of whom "the greatest … was Namalvar (c. AD 800), himself a lowly Sudra", and "Trimulisari"
  (p. 377, folio 355); "Ramanuja, whose dates are believed to be 1050 and 1137, was born near what
  is now Madras and was educated at Conjeeveram … he settled in Srirangam"; "Madhava (1197-1280) …
  a Kanarese Brahman"; "Nimbarka (c. 1130-1200), a Telugu Brahman" (p. 377, folio 355); "the Sanskrit
  poet Jayadeva (c. 1100) a protege of the Sena court in Bengal" (p. 378, folio 356); "The great
  Marathi poet, Jnanadeva (1275-96)" and "Born around 1270, Namdeva came from a low caste family of
  Pandharpur tailors" (p. 378, folio 356); "Ramananda (c. 1360-1470)" (pp. 379, 397; folios 357,
  373); "Chaitanya (1485-1534), a native of Navadvipa (Nadia)" and "Chandidas (c. 1350—1430)",
  footnoted "A Bengali Shakta poet" (p. 379, folio 357); "Nabhaji, the author of the Bhagat Mal …
  written at the end of the sixteenth century" (p. 381, folio 359). **The Bauls**, "a popular group
  of Muslim and Hindu singers in Bengal … The movement began in Nadia"; "Bhirbhadra, a son of
  Nityananda (1473-1544) was their first guru and received the Baul faith from a Muslim woman called
  Madhava Bibi" (p. 380, folio 358).
- **The Indic sources named** — "the Sanskrit work entitled Suka-Saptati by Chintamani Bhatta"; "the
  Panchtantra and Hitupdesha [OCR?] [Hitopadesha]" (p. 150, folio 132); "the Rati Rahasya (Mysteries
  of Passion) by Kokapandita or Kukkoka who flourished in the eleventh or twelth [OCR?] [twelfth]
  centuries"; "Raja Indra" and "Gandharb", footnoted "A demigod" (p. 151, folio 133); "Patanjali" and
  "the Yoga Sutra" (pp. 345, 347; folios 323, 325); "Samkhya, one of the six schools of classical
  Hindu philosophy" and "the Bhagavad Gita" (pp. 345, 348; folios 323, 326); "Halayodha Misra, the
  author of Shek Subhodya", "said to have been the court poet of Lakshmansena … but the work is of a
  later date" (p. 220, folio 202).
- **The orders as index headwords with their folio runs** — "Chishtiyya, 1-2, 4. 7, 9-11, 13-14, 101,
  114-89, 197, 244, 272, 275-76, 367, 392, 398, 402", "Chishti sufis, 192-93, 212, 216-26, 230, 259,
  289, 308", "Chishtiyya centres, 241.-77 [OCR?] [241-77]", "Chishti-Sabiri order, 154, 343"
  (p. 463, folio 439); "Firdausiyya, 8, 92, 226-40, 399" (p. 465, folio 441); "Kubrawiyya, 1, 92,
  248, 289, 296, 299. 320, 350, 399" (p. 472, folio 448); "Nagqshbandiyya [OCR?] [Naqshbandiyya], 2,
  11, 13.96 97. 101 [OCR?] [13, 96-97, 101?]" (p. 478, folio 454); "Qadiriyya, 2, 84-85, 396"
  (p. 480, folio 456); "Jalaliyya order, 260", "Jalaliyya, 82" (p. 470, folio 446); "Qalandariyya,
  352", "Qalandariyya-Chishtiyya, 304-5" (p. 480, folio 456); "Madari, 319-20" (p. 474, folio 450);
  "Rishi movement, 14, 350-1" (p. 482, folio 458); "Sabiriyya-Chishtiyyas, 13, 154" (p. 483, folio
  459); "Shattariyya, 1, 12", "Suhrawardiyya, 1, 11, 87-88, 190-240, 272, 277-300, 315, 367,
  398-400" (pp. 484–490, folios 460–466). All cited to the PDF pages the index is printed on. **The
  Naushahia and the Azeemia are absent from the ranges covered.**
- **Categories in the index that touch archive rows without naming any of them** — "Sikhs, 383-84,
  388, 394-95", "The Sikh religion, 356", "Sikh-i muhr, 307", "Siri Guru-Granth Sahib, 330-94"
  (p. 486, folio 462) — **"330-94" is a sixty-five-folio span for a single locator, quoted as
  printed and not repaired; no Guru is named in that range**; "Sant, 357, 372-73, 382, 388, 395"
  (p. 484, folio 460) — **the generic term only**; "Yogis, 5, 16, 110-11, 143-44, 189, 216, 233,

- **The bakhshis** — "the Buddhist monks, bakhshis or bhikshus (those who beg) had started missionary
  activity under state patronage"; "the Irani Buddhism of this period was Mahayanist", the evidence
  being "the summary of some important works incorporated in the Jami'u't-Tawarikh of Rashidu'd-Din
  Fazlu'llah" (p. 268, folio 248); after 694/1295 Ghazan Khan "began a campaign of ruthless
  persecution of Buddhists" (p. 269, folio 249).

### G. Deities, divine names and non-Muslim divine figures with no archive row

*Deities that ARE archive principal figures — Shiva, Krishna, Kali, Rama, Jagannath, Varuna,
Narayana and Parshvanatha — are in the Misfiled section and are not listed here.*

- **Indra, Mitra, Agni and Garuda** — inside the quoted Rigveda hymn, "They call Him Indra, Mitra,
  Varuna, Agni, and even the fleet-winged celestial bird Garuda. The One Reality, the learned speak
  of in many ways" (p. 34, folio not stated, cited "Rigveda, |, p. 64"); "Rishis and gods such as
  Indra, Brahma, Vishnu, Murari and Gandharva" (p. 387, folio 365).
- **Brahma, Om, Niranjan, Kartar, Saʾin, Hari, Govind, Murari, Visambhar, Gopinath, Madhava, Allah,
  Rahim, Karim and Khuda** — Kabir's list of names for Reality (p. 401, folio 377); Qutban's list
  "Niranjan, Kratar [OCR?] [Kartar], Vidhata, Pramesh, Ek-Onkar, Alakh" (p. 389, folio 367).
- **Shakti and the sun-and-moon pairing** — "Shiva and Shakti" (p. 389, folio 367); "the sun (Shakti)
  and the moon (Shiva)" (p. 390, folio 368); index "Shakti, 34, 337, 366, 368-69", "Shaivite, 355",
  "Shaivite Nath cult, 331", "Shaivite yogini, 14, 349-51" (p. 485, folio 461).
- **Vishnu and the avatars** — "The Saguna bhaktas worshipped Vishnu and his different avarars [OCR?]
  [avatars] (incarnations)" (p. 397, folio 373); index "Vishnu, 109, 354-55, 377", "Vishnu-Pad, 359",
  "Vishnu- Purana, 354" (p. 490, folio 466; **the intruded space is as printed**).
- **Braj, Gokul, Jasodha** (pp. 383–384, folios 361–362); **Sawi Deva**, the Anasagar idol (p. 135,
  folio 117); **Mount Sumeru or Maru**, which "Rizvi treats … as a literary device, not a place"
  (pp. 411–412, folios 387–388).
- **Other index headwords in the deity range that are not archive principal figures** —
  "Tirthankara, 332" (p. 489, folio 465), **the category, singular**; "Sivanabh, 387", "Sumeru, 368,
  387", "Tryambaka, 332", "Sat-Cit-Ananda- Murti, 333", "Shankaracharya, 354-55", "Shakyamuni, 248",
  "Siddhartha Gautama (the Buddha), 322", "Parameswara, 333", "Kali Yuga, 34, 394", "Mahesh, 354,
  404-5", "Yamuna, 354", "Jaina, 355", "Jaina Kasapanaka-Y ogins [OCR?] [Kasapanaka-Yogins], 330"
  (pp. 458–491, folios 434–467), cited to the PDF pages they are printed on. **"Mahesh" is also the
  name of the childless Brahman of Appendix B (p. 428, folio not stated); the book does not
  distinguish the two uses.**
- **The Kaʿba at Mecca** — "always figuratively or as a target of accusation" in chunk 004: the
  Qaramati accusation (p. 74, folio 58), Shibli's "'am running to set fire to the Ka'ba'" (pp. 75–76,
  folios 59–60), and "Thou maketh the Ka'ba an idol temple and converteth a tavern into a mosque"
  (p. 263, folio 243). **A named pilgrimage centre with no observance, endowment or custodian
  described — (not stated).**
- **Unnamed Hindu shrines visited by a Muslim ruler** — Sultan Qutbuʾd-Din of Kashmir "visited Hindu
  shrines, dressed like a Hindu and celebrated Hindu festivals; on one occasion he even performed a
  yajna in order to avert a famine", footnoted "The performance of a sacrifice" (p. 314, folio 292).
  **No shrine is named — (not stated).**

### H. Toponyms with no archive row

*Listed in bulk by chunk range, as the Pass 1 notes list them. **Archive-location toponyms — Lahore,
Multan, Uch, Sehwan/Siwistan, Peshawar and Gorakhattri, Karachi, Sialkot, Qasur/Kasur, Nankana
Sahib, Kartarpur, Saiyidpur, Talwandi, Baluchistan, Ajodhan and the province-level Sind, Panjab and
Kashmir — are in the Misfiled section under ruling (a).** Nothing below corresponds to a row.*

- **Chunk 001 (pp. 16–29, folios 2–15)** — Delhi/Dehli, Daulatabad, Bihar, Jaunpur, Bengal, Orissa,
  Mandu, Kichaucha, Rudauli, Nagaur, Barnawa, Khuldabad, Shikohabad, Kirman, Mayhana, Hejaz, Mecca,
  Medina.
- **Chunk 002 (pp. 30–49, folios 16–33)** — Tangier, North Africa, Egypt, Syria, Mecca, Iran, Iraq,
  Asia Minor, Transoxiana, Afghanistan, the Indus valley, Delhi, the Deccan, the Maldives, Ceylon,
  Bengal, Assam, Sumatra, China, Malabar, the Persian Gulf, Hejaz, Fez, Granada, Morocco, the
  Sahara, Sijilmasa; Ghur and Ghazna; Medina, Kufa, Damascus; Baghdad, Azarbaijan, Gilan,
  Tabaristan, Gorgan, Khurasan, Marw, Bastam, the upper Oxus, the Pamir; Alexandria, Antioch,
  Gondeshapur; Ramla, **where a khanqah IS attached** (p. 47, folio 31).
- **Chunk 003 (pp. 50–65, folios 34–49)** — Balkh, Khurasan, Syria, Mecca, Marw, Abiward, Samarqand,

### I. Modern scholars and translators named in the text — recorded for provenance, not as figures

- **Chapters One–Two** — "Ibrahim al-Geyoushi, a modern authority on Tirmizi" (p. 56, folio 40; **the
  footnotes give "M.I. Al-Geyoushi" — a live doubt**); "Ritter" (p. 60, folio 44); "Nicholson and
  R.C. Zaehner support this theory, while Arberry rejects it" (p. 61, folio 45); "Ibn Khaldun", from
  "The Mugaddimah [OCR?] [Muqaddimah] … tr. F. Rosenthal, New York. 1958, pp. 156-200" (pp. 57–58,
  folios 41–42); "Guy Le Strange. The lands of the eastern Caliphate, London, 1966" (p. 56, folio
  40); "A.M. Schimmel Tari, Ibn al-Khafif, Ankara, 1955" (p. 62, folio 46); "Margaret Smith, An
  early mystic of Baghdad, p. 26" (p. 69, folio 53); "A.H. Abdel-Kadar [OCR?] [Abdel-Kader], The
  life, personality and writings of Al-Junayd, London, 1962" (pp. 66, 71–73; folios 50, 55–57);
  "R.A. Nicholson, Studies in Islamic mysticism, Cambridge. 1967" and "The idea of personality in
  Sufism, Cambridge, 1923" (pp. 74, 84; folios 58, 68); "J.S. Trimingham, The Sufi orders in tslam
  [OCR?] [Islam], Oxford, 1973" (p. 79, folio 63); "W.M. Watt", translator of "al Mungiz min
  az-zalal [OCR?] [al-Munqiz min az-zalal]" (p. 81, folio 65); "E.G. Browne, A literary history of
  Persia" (pp. 88, 94–96; folios 72, 78–80); "Jogendra Singh's translation of Ansari's Munajat"
  (p. 94, folio 78); "V. Minorsky" (pp. 92, 132; folios 76, 114); "Muhammad bin Munawwar,
  Asraru't-Tawhid, Tehran, 2nd edition, 1348/1969" (pp. 86–91, folios 70–75 corrected); "Irij Afshar
  ed. Halat wa Sukhanan …, Tehran, 1349, Iranian era/1970" (p. 88, folio 72); "Rawandi,
  Rahatu's-Sudur, London, 1921" (pp. 84, 92; folios 68, 76 — **spelled two ways, a live doubt**);

  catalogue of Indian miniatures … library of A. Chester Beatty, London, 1936" (p. 357, folio 335);
  "W.H. McLeod, Guru Nanak and the Sikh religion, Oxford, 1968", **the chunk 024 antagonist**
  (pp. 407–419, folios 383–395); "E. Trumpp, The Adi Granth, 2nd edition, New Delhi, 1970"
  (pp. 411–413, folios 387–389); "A.H. Dani" and "Dr. V.L. Menage, Reader, School of Oriental and
  African Studies, University of London" (p. 413, folio 389); "A.E. Affifi" (p. 414, folio 390);
  "Gopal Singh" (pp. 414, 417; folios 390, 393); "Macauliffe" throughout (pp. 402–411, 418; folios
  378–387, 394), **the source chunk 023 flagged as unsourceable, whose title is supplied at p. 453
  (folio 429)**; "R.F. Burton, A pilgrimage to al-Madinah and Mecca; J.F. Keane, Six months in the
  Hejaz" (p. 412, folio 388); "Canon Kenneth Gragg [OCR?] [Cragg?]" (p. 417, folio 393);
  "Shihabu'd-Din al-'Umari, Masalik ul-Absar …, tr. Otto Spies. Muslim University Journal, Aligarh"
  (p. 421, folio not stated); "R.C. Majumdar", quoted at length against the importance of sufism,
  "The Delhi Sultanate, second edition, Bombay, 1967, p. 555", with "'Aziz Ahmad also endorses
  Majumdar's view in Studies in Islamic culture, p. 134" (pp. 421–422, folio not stated / 398).
  **The index's modern-scholar headwords in the S–Z range** — "Sharif, M.M., 105", "Shastri, H.,
  329", "Shirani, Professor Hafiz Mahmud, 2", "Shirref, A.G., 369", "Shlomo Pines, 323",
  "Srivastava, A.L., 289", "Stace, W.T., 18", "Stern, M.A., 289", "Stern, S.M., 318", "Tarafdar,
  M.R., 352", "Temple, R.C., 317, 349", "Trimingham, J.S., 63", "Tritton, A.S., 295", "Trumpp,
  387-89", "Watt, W.M., 23, 65", "Zaehner, R.C., 44-45, 57, 323" (pp. 486–491, folios 462–467), all
  cited to the PDF pages the index is printed on.
### J. Name collisions and traps — figures and headwords that look like archive principal figures and are not

*Every one of these was checked against `shrine_index.tsv` and rejected. They are recorded so that a
future worker does not make the mapping this file deliberately withholds.*

- **"Qutbuʾd-Din" — five distinct men, none of them Sultan Qutb ud-Din Aibak.** (i) **Khwaja
  Qutbuʾd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki**, the Chishti shaikh of Mahrauli, d. 1235 (pp. 18, 152–156, 166, 170,
  193, 196–199, 215, 217, 219–220, 226, 239–240, 246, 304–305, 326, 348, 394, 401; folios 4,
  134–138, 148, 152, 175, 178–181, 197, 199, 201–202, 208, 219–220, 226, 284–285, 304, 326, 374,
  377). (ii) **Qutbuʾd-Din Muhammad**, the Ghurid king who founded Firuzkuh (p. 133, folio 115).
  (iii) **Sultan Qutbuʾd-Din Mubarak Shah Khalji** (pp. 178, 213, 242; folios 160, 195, 222).
  (iv) **Sultan Qutbuʾd-Din of Kashmir (1373-89)** (pp. 313, 314, 318; folios 291, 292, 296) — "This
  is not Qutbuʾd-Din Aibak", the Pass 1 worker states. (v) **Shaikh Qutbuʾd-Din, titled Chisht
  Khan**, of Mandu (pp. 295–296, folios 275–276), and a sixth, **Qutbuʾd-Din**, a son of Sharafuʾd-Din
  Munyari (p. 260, folio 240), and a seventh, **Shaikh Qutbuʾd-Din Dabir** (pp. 200–201, folios
  182–183), and an eighth, **Qazi Qutbuʾd-Din Kashani** (p. 194, folio 176), and a ninth,
  **Qutbuʾd-Din Kashani** the scholar (p. 216, folio 198), and a tenth, **Shaikh Qutbuʾd-Din
  Munawwar of Hansi** (pp. 196–197, folios 178–179), and an eleventh, **Sultan Qutbuʾd-Din Ahmad of
  Gujarat (1451-59)** (p. 431, folio 407), and a twelfth, **Khwaja Qutbuʾd-Din Maudud** of Chisht
  (p. 23, folio 9). **Aibak himself IS named, twice, and is carried to the Misfiled section; none of
  the twelve above is him.** **The Qutb Minar is a monument, not a shrine, and is not
  `tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak` either** (pp. 155, 267; folios 137, 247).
- **"Ruknuʾd-Din" — at least six distinct men, of whom one is probably an archive principal figure
  and is carried to the Misfiled section.** (i) **Shaikhuʾl-Islam Shaikh Ruknuʾd-Din Multani**, who
  "led Shaikh Nizamu'd-Din's funeral prayer" (p. 181, folio 163) and whose khanqah received "one
  hundred villages" (p. 233, folio 213) — **the Pass 1 worker called this "the chunk's one serious
  unmade archive mapping"; see Misfiled.** (ii) **Khwaja Ruknuʾd-Din**, Firdawsi khalifa "who lived
  near Kilukhari" (p. 248, folio 228) — "a different man from the Multan Shaikh Ruknuʾd-Din".
  (iii) **Saiyid Ruknuʾd-Din Abuʾl-Fath of Uch**, son of Saiyid Hamid Kabir (p. 304, folio 282) —
  **his name collides exactly with `shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam`'s principal figure "Shah Rukn-e-Alam
  (Rukn-ud-Din Abul Fath)", and he is a different man: a fifteenth-century Uch Bukhari, not the
  Multan Suhrawardi who died in 1335.** (iv) **Shaikh Ruknuʾd-Din, son of ʿAbduʾl-Quddus Gangohi**,
  d. AD 1575-76 (pp. 365, 368–369; folios 343, 346–347) — "this man is not the Multan Ruknu'd-Din".
  (v) **Shaikh Ruknuʾd-Din, son of Shaikh Shihabuʾd-Din**, the Chishti who was "the Imam of Shaikh
  Nizamu'd-Din Auliya'" and Masʿud-i Bak's pir (p. 261, folio 241, footnote 2). (vi) **Ruknuʾd-Din
  Ibrahim, a son of Iltutmish** (p. 223, folio 203). (vii) **Qazi Ruknuʾd-Din Samarqandi**, the
  *Amrita-Kunda* translator (p. 357, folio 335). (viii) **Maulana Ruknuʾd-Din Samarqandi**, the
  opponent of samaʿ (p. 215, folio 197). (ix) **Ruknuʾd-Din Muhammad of Sanjan in Khwaf** (p. 133,
  folio 115). (x) **Shaikh Ruknuʾd-Din ʿAlaʾuʾd-Dawla Simnani** (p. 342, folio 320) — the same man as
  ʿAlaʾuʾd-Dawla Simnani, named with a third element.
- **"Farid" / "Fariduʾd-Din" — at least five distinct men, only one of whom is the archive's Baba
  Farid.** (i) **Shaikh Fariduʾd-Din Mahmud of Nagaur**, called "Shaikh Farid" by the book (p. 148,
  folio 130) — **"a direct collision with Baba Farid"**; he is also the "Shaikh Farid of Nagaur"
  whose Hindawi verse is discussed at p. 349 (folio 327), where **"the book keeps them apart on the
  same line"**. (ii) **Fariduʾd-Din ʿAttar** of Nishapur, the poet (pp. 46, 96, 217–218, 256; folios
  30, 80, 199–200, 236) — **"a third distinct Farid in this book"**. (iii) **Maulana Fariduʾd-Din**,
  the Firdausi jurist of 777/1375—76 (p. 248, folio 228). (iv) **Farid Sani**, a successor of Baba
  Farid (p. 351, folio 329). (v) **Farid bin Salar**, the compiler of the *Rafiquʾl-ʿArifin* (p. 284,
  folio 264). **And the Panch Pir "Shaikh Farid" (pp. 339–340, folios 317–318), which may or may not
  be Baba Farid — carried to the Misfiled section as a query, not resolved here.**
- **"Jamal" / "Jamali" — four distinct men, none of them `shah-jamal`'s Syed Shah Jamal Uddin Naqvi
  Bukhari and none of them `shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani`'s Hafiz Muhammad Jamal
  Multani.** (i) **Shaikh Hamid bin Fazluʾllah "Jamali" Kanbo Dihlawi**, d. 942/1536 (pp. 25,
  308–310; folios 11, 286–288) — the Pass 1 worker names the two collisions and withholds both.
  (ii) **Shaikh Jamaluʾd-Din of Hansi**, Baba Farid's senior disciple (p. 171, folio 153).
  (iii) **Shaikh Jamal**, a descendant of Nizamuʾd-Din Abuʾl-Muyʾid, "very famous at Kol (Aligarh),
  where he also died" (p. 139, folio 121). (iv) **Shaikh Jamal, a Muslim Gujar**, in Awadh (p. 292,
  folio 272). Plus **Shaikh Jamaluʾd-Din of Sawa**, a Qalandariyya founder in Damascus (p. 324, folio
  302), and **Saiyid Jamaluʾd-Din Bukhari**, who initiated Shaikh Hamza Makhdum (p. 321, folio 299),
  and **Saiyid Jamaluʾd-Din** among the Kashmir migrants (pp. 312–313, folios 290–291).
- **"Chiragh" — Shaikh Nasiruʾd-Din Mahmud Chiragh-i Dihli is NOT `shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh`.**
  That row's principal figure is Syed Abdul Razzaq (Shah Chiragh) of Lahore, d. 1657; this man is a
  Delhi Chishti who died in 1356 (p. 205, folio 187). The Pass 1 worker flagged the collision
  explicitly at p. 234 (folio 214). **Nor is `darbar-sakhi-shah-chan-charagh` reached by it.**
- **"Burhanuʾd-Din" — four distinct men, none of them `langer-makhdoom`'s Makhdoom Burhan-ud-din of
  Chiniot.** (i) **Maulana Burhanuʾd-Din Gharib**, the Chishti of Daulatabad (pp. 21, 201, 271;
  folios 7, 183, 251). (ii) **Maulana Burhanuʾd-Din Nasafi** (p. 239, folio 219). (iii) **Burhanuʾd-
  Din, called Qutb-i ʿAlam**, of Batwa (pp. 304–305, folios 282–283). (iv) **Burhanuʾd-Din Kaziruni**
  of the Zaytun khanqah (p. 434, folio 410). Plus **Maulana Burhanuʾd-Din Marghniyani**, author of
  the *Hidaya* (p. 294, folio 274), **Shaikh Burhanuʾd-Din** the father of Qutbuʾd-Din Munawwar
  (p. 196, folio 178), **Shaikh Burhan of Kalpi**, Jaʾisi's Mahdawi pir (p. 395, folio 371), and
  **Shaikh Burhanuʾd-Din Sagharji of China** (p. 337, folio 315).
- **"Tirmizi" — three distinct men, none of them `shrine-of-pir-baba-syed-ali-tirmizi`'s Syed Ali
  Tirmizi (Pir Baba), d. 1583.** (i) **al-Hakim al-Tirmizi**, d. after 285/898 (pp. 56–58, 77;
  folios 40–42, 61), named once as a bare surname with "no first name, no dates and no place"
  (p. 115, folio 97) — the Pass 1 worker flagged the collision there. (ii) **Maulana Minhajuʾd-Din
  Tirmizi** of Multan (p. 157, folio 139). (iii) **Abuʾl-ʿAbbas al-Tirmizi**, in the masters list as
  "al-Hakim in Tirmiz" (p. 77, folio 61).
- **"Baba Ratan" is not Baba Pir Ratan Nath of `dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee`.** The book styles him
  "Hajji Baba Ratan" and "Abuʾl-Riza", places him at Tabarhinda/Bhatinda in the Panjab, dates his
  death 700/1300, and **gives no Nath, no Peshawar and no Jhanda Bazar** (p. 342, folio 320; index
  p. 461, folio 437).
- **"Bari Dargah" is not `bari-imam`.** The index headword "Bari Dargah, 317" (p. 462, folio 438)
  sits on the same folio 317 as "Astana, 317", "Arakan, 316-17", "Bengal, … 314-18", "Bihar, …
  316-17" and "Buddhist, … 316-18" (pp. 460–462, folios 436–438) — **so this dargah is in the book's
  Bengal–Arakan material, not near Islamabad. Recorded as a trap, not a find.**
- **"Shah ʿAbduʾl-Latif" the 1917 Tippera author is not Shah Abdul Latif Bhittai of
  `bhit-bhit-shah`.** The bibliography entry reads "Shah 'Abdu'l-Latif, Silsila-i 'Aliya-i
  Chishtiyya-i Nizamiya-i Fakhriyya-i Sulaimaniyya-i Latifiyya, Tippera, 1917." (p. 447, folio 423) —
  **a Chishti silsila-nama printed at Tippera in 1917; the book gives no Bhit, no Sindh, no *Risalo*
  and no dates, and the archive's Bhittai died in 1752. The Pass 1 worker withheld the mapping and so
  do I; the coordinator may wish to see it noted.**
- **"Bhai Gurdas Bhalla" is neither `sevapanthi-darbar-bhai-gurdas-gandava`'s Bhai Gurdas
  (Sevapanthi) nor `khatwari-darbar-shikarpur`'s Bhai Gurdas Singh (Kanhiya Lal).** "the account
  written by Bhai Gurdas Bhalla, who assisted in the compilation of the Adi-Granth. The account known
  as Bhai Gurdas' Var I was written in the first quarter of the seventeenth century" (p. 408, folio
  384); index "Bhai Gurdas Bhalla, 384" and "Bhai Gurdas' Var, 304" (p. 462, folio 438). **The book
  gives the patronymic "Bhalla", which neither archive row's principal figure carries. A three-way
  name collision, deliberately unmapped.**
- **"Shamsuʾd-Din" — Mir Shamsuʾd-Din ʿIraqi of Kashmir is not Shah Shams-ud-Din Sabzwari of Multan
  and not Shams Ali Qalandar of Shamsabad** (p. 320, folio 298). The "Sabzwar" of pp. 138–139
  (folios 120–121) is an Iranian town where a Shiʿi governor was converted, not a nisba attached to
  any archive saint — **the Pass 1 worker flagged Sabzwar as "One toponym does carry a live
  question"; it remains open and is not mapped.** Plus **Sultan Shamsuʾd-Din Iltutmish**, **Qazi
  Shamsuʾd-Din** (p. 29, folio 15), **Maulana Shamsuʾd-Din son of Yahya** (p. 195, folio 177),
  **Maulana Shamsuʾd-Din Yahya** (p. 200, folio 182), **Shamsuʾd-Din Firuz of Bengal** (p. 337, folio
  315), **Shamsuʾd-Din Muhammad Tabrizi** (p. 98, folio 82), **Shamsuʾd-Din Halwaʾi** (p. 145, folio
  127), **Shamsuʾd-Din Turk of Panipat** (p. 290, folio 270), **Qazi Shamsuʾd-Din of Chausa**
  (p. 260, folio 240), **Malik Shamsuʾd-Din** (p. 210, folio 192) and **Shamsuʾl-Malik** (p. 173,
